Z is Diophantine in Q
Version 14
November 7, 2026
Abstract
We construct an integer-coeﬃcient polynomial in 292 890 231 905 variables whose rational
zero projection is exactly Z. Its degree is less than 2↑↑ 7, and the absolute value of every
coeﬃcient is less than 2↑↑8.
We show the minimum degree of a Diophantine deﬁnition for Z in Q is 4 and specify two
smaller quartics. The ﬁrst has 314 variables, 6 672 nonzero monomials, and coeﬃcients of
magnitude below 26496. Its complete decimal coeﬃcient table occupies 264 430 bytes. The
second has 299 variables, 6 630 nonzero monomials, and largest absolute coeﬃcient 186624· 3226 .
Its table writes coeﬃcients relative to the ﬁxed integer C = 3 225 . Both rational zero projections
are exactly Z.
Well-known consequences of Z being Diophantine in Q include a refutation of Mazur’s con-
jecture that the topological closure of the rational points of a variety over Q has ﬁnitely many
components, and existence of a universal rational Diophantine polynomial. Moreover, the set
HTP(Q) of integer polynomials with a rational zero is many-one equivalent to the halting set
0′.

1 The main theorem and proof structure
This construction builds on OpenAI’s preprint Hilbert’s tenth problem over the rational num-
bers [78].
The strategy of using rank-one elliptic curves and existential local-divisibility conditions to
approach undecidability over Q goes back to Pheidas [ 94]. His work also developed partial valuation
predicates anticipating the pole conditions used here.
Earlier deﬁnability results provide the logical context. Robinson gave a ﬁrst-order deﬁnition of
Z in Q [71, Theorem 3.1]. Poonen gave a deﬁnition with universal quantiﬁers followed by existential
quantiﬁers [ 72, Theorems 3.1 and 4.1]. Koenigsmann gave a universal deﬁnition and proved that
Q\ Z is Diophantine in Q [83, Theorem 1 and Corollary 2]. These are historical context, not
additional premises of our construction.
5We use N = {0, 1, 2,... }. For a reduced rational number a/b, with b > 0, put h(a/b) =
log max(|a|,b ). Deﬁne 2↑↑1 = 2 and 2↑↑(r + 1) = 2 2↑ ↑r; every tower is associated to the right.
Theorem 1.1 (A polynomial deﬁnition of the integers) . There exists one polynomial
P∈ Z[X0,X 1,...,X 292890231904]
such that
degP <2↑↑7,
|[Xα]P|< 2↑↑8 for every multi-index α,
q∈ Z ⇐⇒ ∃(w1,...,w 292890231904)∈ Q292890231904, P (q,w 1,...,w 292890231904) = 0 ( q∈ Q).
All conclusions concern the same polynomial. Here q∈ Z means the ordinary embedded integers.
The ambient variable count does not assert that every variable occurs essentially.
The ﬁle ordinary-integer-definition.json speciﬁes the polynomial P∗. The argument ﬁrst
represents integer indices by points on an elliptic curve over Q(
√
2). A separate value relation
identiﬁes the ordinary rational value of an index. The point coordinates and the integer index have
distinct roles throughout.
Theorem 1.1 refutes Mazur’s conjecture that the closure of the rational points of a variety
in its real locus has ﬁnitely many connected components. Mazur proposed this in 1992 [ 70]; his
observation that it obstructs a Diophantine deﬁnition of Z is recalled in [ 76, p. 166]. It also gives a
universal rational Diophantine polynomial, with HTP(Q) many-one equivalent to 0′. Section 13.1
proves these three consequences.
Version 14 gives a quartic in 299 total variables and an ordinary decimal-coeﬃcient quartic in
314 total variables. Section M proves the reductions and speciﬁes their exact coeﬃcient tables.
The Version 13 constructions and their arithmetic inputs remain as proof baselines. Version 11’s
index-set and retained-class identiﬁcation in the published Kato application is unchanged.
Version 10 clariﬁed determinant specialization, the published prime-two Euler-system input,
the local valuation deduction, and the identiﬁcation of the saved templates, following the review
in repository Issue 2. It also corrects and expands the historical attribution. The polynomial
speciﬁcations and numerical bounds are unchanged. We thank manvirjaswal for the detailed
review 1.
Version 9 incorporated Gabriel Conant’s odd-index product-height argument and asymmetric
formal-depth reﬁnement, developed with ChatGPT. Section J speciﬁes the resulting 523- and 540-
variable quartics and proves their connection to the arithmetic inputs established in this manuscript.
The earlier quartics below are retained as proof and source baselines; they are not the current
variable records.
Section 15 gives the explicit 4 973-variable quartic. Its arithmetic source revises the base residual
compiler of Section 14, with a separate proof of soundness and completeness. The complete coef-
ﬁcient table and exact algebraic conversion certiﬁcate are included with this manuscript. A third
polynomial, in 962 variables, is constructed in Section 17. Its complete monomial table records
the large integer coeﬃcients using exact powers. The 4 973-variable polynomial is retained with its
ordinary, fully expanded integer coeﬃcients. Section 18 proves that a single polynomial deﬁning Z
in Q must have total degree at least four, following the agentic notes cited there. Thus the degree
of a quartic deﬁnition is optimal.
The ﬁnal composition uses the following precise statements.
1https://github.com/elliotglazer/diophantine-z-q/issues/2
6G Canonical point codes form exactly the inﬁnite cyclic group 2E(Q(
√
2)) = ZP0. The
membership polynomial types arbitrary inputs; raw addition describes the group law on
canonical codes.
M The actual guarded multiplication template has rational witnesses exactly for triples
with indices a,b,ab .
H The six-contact height theorem holds with the constants used by the precision computa-
tion and, separately, by multiplication.
PC The explicit pole polynomial is soluble for every input in Theorem 5.2.
C The actual ﬁve-input natural polynomial and its rational translation have the exact
computation and substitution semantics of Section 10, including the power template.
B The templates, ﬁxed constructors and private witness substitutions specify the same
saved expression P∗. The quantitative syntax calculation applies to that expression.
Lemma 1.2 (Assembly). Assume G, M, H, PC, C, B. Then P∗ satisﬁes Theorem 1.1.
Proof. Section 11 proves the exact ordinary-value relation using one shared shift and disjoint private
witness blocks. The literal substitutions in B identify its existential polynomial with P∗. Section 12
proves the stated bounds for that same expression.
Proof of Theorem 1.1. Section 3, including Proposition 3.1, proves G with its canonical membership
and addition formulas. Theorem 4.1 proves H for each of the two sets of constants used below.
Sections 7–9, with Appendices E–G, prove the restricted converse in Theorem 5.3. The simultaneous
descent in Section 5 then proves PC. Using these results, Section 6 proves M on all rational input
codes, including the ﬁnite false-triple guard.
Section 10 proves C for the ﬁxed ﬁve-input natural polynomial and its rational model translation.
Its witness variables remain natural before that translation. Sections 2 and 12 and Appendix C
prove B, including the ﬁxed scalars and private witness substitutions. All six statements therefore
hold, and Lemma 1.2 applies to the speciﬁed P∗.
2 A ﬁnite speciﬁcation of the polynomial
The delivered speciﬁcation is construction/ordinary-integer-definition.json, a 23 179 783-
byte JSON expression with SHA256
4d639d4c44e69ea360d36d4591b8fdc82269d68b9363af0157d1e5c1174c7af6.
It contains 23 library templates and seven named recipes. The root recipe is integers; its only
parameter is the rational variable at index zero. Every other variable is existentially quantiﬁed
over Q. Sources S01–S03 ﬁx the construction and its semantics. Their historical speciﬁcation had
23 180 023 bytes and SHA256
a86790bbf1095d6b1cea9c66067ad8432dd35adc320254eecde21f9f98574714.
The delivered expression replaces two diagnostic sourceFile strings and the path key in
sourceHashes by portable relative paths. Direct comparison shows that every polynomial-deﬁning
ﬁeld is unchanged. Since these three provenance ﬁelds contribute no equations, both byte repre-
sentations denote exactly the same integer polynomial, with the same variables and bounds. The
shipped construction/SOURCE_MANIFEST.json records both identities and the normalization; the
companion SOURCE_MAP.json retains the historical source references.
72.1 Literal operations and witness scope
A scalar node is a variable, integer constant, sum, product, or a call to an earlier template. A
call substitutes its parameter expressions and a private consecutive witness block into the callee’s
polynomial. Shared nodes represent repeated subexpressions, not new equations. A recipe sums
squares of constraint polynomials or multiplies branch polynomials. Over Q,
∑
i
f 2i = 0 ⇐⇒ ⋀
i
fi = 0,
∏
i
fi = 0 ⇐⇒
⋁
i
fi = 0.
Private witness blocks can be moved to the front because the rational domain is nonempty. A whole
inactive product-call block may receive arbitrary values. This does not discard ﬁxed equations
inside an active callee. A rational constant p/q, q⁄= 0, is represented by a fresh c and the globally
conjoined equation qc =p. In particular the cached coordinates of P0 satisfy

These equations remain active outside internal disjunctions in the multiplication, signed-encoding
and exception templates. Inverses are equations uv = 1, never partial evaluation operations.

2.2 Fixed ﬁnite constructors
Three node types abbreviate ﬁnite polynomials. A fixed_integer inserts a ﬁxed integer expression;
psi_component inserts one coordinate of a division polynomial; finite_guard_product inserts a
product over a ﬁxed box of integers. Their bounds never depend on the free rational input.
For example, on y2 =x3 +Ax, write ψn =fn for odd n and ψn = 2yfn for even n. The bases
are
f0 = 0, f1 =f2 = 1,
f3 = 3x4 + 6Ax2−A2, f4 = 2(x6 + 5Ax4− 5A2x2−A3).
For m≥ 3, f2m = fm(fm+2f 2m−1−fm−2f 2m+1). For m≥ 2, put L = fm+2f 3m, R = fm−1f 3m+1,
and D0 = 16(x3 +Ax)2. Then f2m+1 = D0L−R for even m and L−D0R for odd m. Negative
indices satisfy ψ−n = −ψn. This is a terminating recursion. These are the short-Weierstrass
normalizations of [ 47, §§5.5–5.6, Lemmas 5.20 and 5.24, Theorem 5.21]. In its Jacobian-coordinate
convention,x([n]P ) = φn(P )/ψn(P )2 andy([n]P ) = ωn(P )/ψn(P )3. The numerator formula has a
minus sign:
ωn = ψn+2ψ2n−1−ψn−2ψ2n+14y , ψ 2n = 2ψnωn.
Thus the ﬁxed-scalar ordinate equation is 2y([n]P )ψn(P )4 =ψ2n(P ). For n⁄= 0 and a nonidentity
point in the torsion-free carrier, ψn(P )⁄= 0, so clearing these denominators is reversible.
8The ﬁxed prime catalogue consists of the ﬁrst 22 primes above 324 in each unit residue class
moduloW = 4∏p≤324p. Dirichlet’s theorem ensures termination. Put Ns = (∏p≤324p)Hcat, where
Hcat is the catalogue product. Assign inverse abscissa zero at the identity. For the normalized
representation 1/x([m]P0) = (a +bs)/c with a,b,c ∈ Z, c >0, and gcd(a,b,c ) = 1 , deﬁne M0 to
be the least m≥ 1 such that N 4s | a,b and gcd(c,Ns) = 1 (S31). This is a ﬁxed integer, with
a successful index bounded in Section 12. The ﬁxed point coordinates in the guard have nonzero
denominators by the inﬁnite order of P0. Thus ﬁnite expansion gives one integer polynomial without
requiring a monomial expansion. Its arithmetic zero set remains a separate issue. In particular,
bounding the integer T does not prove that it excludes all false multiplication triples.
3 The elliptic model of integer indices
The rank-one model and its generator calculation adapt [ 78, Proposition 2.1 and its proof].
Let
K0 = Q(s), s 2 = 2, d = 75− 53s, E :y2 =x3−d2x,

The identity has code (1, 0, 0, 0, 0). An aﬃne point has code (0,x 0,x 1,y 0,y 1) with x = x0 +x1s,
y =y0 +y1s. Linear independence of 1,s makes this unique. Write ι(n) for the code of [n]P0.
Proposition 3.1 (Generator certiﬁcate) . The point P0 has inﬁnite order and 2E(K0) = ZP0.
Proof. We give the argument of S05–S07, making its ﬁnite certiﬁcate explicit. The ring of integers
Z[s] has class number one because its Minkowski bound is
√
2< 2. It has four unit squareclasses.
The sign vectors of −1, 1 +s are independent, so every totally positive unit is a square.
Thex-squareclass map in the two-isogeny descent on E has image contained in the eight classes
generated by−1, 1 +s,d . Away from (d), odd v(x) makes the minimum of 3v(x) and v(x) + 2v(d)
odd, impossible for y2. The torsion points give 1,−1,d,−d. Put ϵ = 1 + s, δ = 3−s. The change
(x,y )↦→ (ϵ4x,ϵ 6y) takes E to C : y2 = x3−δ2x, and R = (1 + s, 2s) provides the remaining unit
class. Thus the ﬁrst image has order eight.
OnE′ :y2 =x3 + 4d2x, a nonzero x is positive in both real embeddings. Its valuations are even.
At (s) the two terms have valuations 3m and m + 4, with equality only at m = 2. At (d) the only
odd case would require u2 =−4 in F7. Thus the second image is trivial. The exact two-isogeny
sequence gives [E(K0) : 2 E(K0)] = 8 ; its possible extra kernel term vanishes since a nonkernel
rational two-torsion point maps to the dual kernel.
The good reductions at (17,s− 6) and (23,s− 5) have 20 and 24 points. Prime-to-residue-
characteristic torsion injects, so the torsion group has order at most four, hence is exactly the
displayed full two-torsion. Mordell–Weil now gives rank one. The x-class of R has opposite real
signs, whereas all torsion classes have equal signs. Its free index is therefore odd.
The duplication forms on C are
F2(a,b ) = (a2 +δ2b2)2, G 2(a,b ) = 4ab(a2−δ2b2).
At either real embedding their maximum lies between max(|a|,|b|)4 and 676 max(|a|,|b|)4. For
primitive a,b their common ideal has valuations at most six at (s) and four at (δ), and none
elsewhere. For the latter bound use that −1 is nonsquare modulo seven. For the former, a local
9unit is A +Bs with A odd, and vs((A +Bs)2 + 1) is two or three according to the parity of B.
Hence
4hx(T )− log 392≤hx(2T )≤ 4hx(T ) + log 676.
With 2ˆh(T ) = lim m 4−mhx(2mT ) this gives hx(T )≤ 2ˆh(T ) + log 392/3. Direct doubling gives
x(2R) = (57− 28s)/8, with height log 41. If R =mS +T for torsion T and|m|≥ 3, it follows that
hx(S)≤ log(413· 676)108 + log 3923 < log 9.
The strict inequality is the exact integer comparison 413· 676· 39236< 9108.
A nonrationalx∈K0 in this height range has primitive polynomial aX 2+bX+c with 1≤a≤ 81,
|b| ≤162, |c| ≤81, and discriminant 2k2, 1 ≤ k ≤ 162. Both roots are included. Rational
possibilities have coprime numerator of absolute value at most nine and denominator from one to
nine. The exact ﬁnite square test of x3−δ2x gives precisely
0, δ, −δ, 1 +s, 23− 17s, 2 + 4s, (5− 4s)/2.
The certiﬁcate covers 7,004 primitive quadratics, their 14,008 roots, and 111 rational candidates.
To test whether u +vs is a square, take both signs of a rational square root t of u2− 2v2, test
y20 = (u +t)/2, then set y1 = v/(2y0) if y0⁄= 0 . The y0 = 0 case requires v = 0 and y21 = u/2.
Substitution veriﬁes each result. This is a necessary and suﬃcient test, including zero. The last
four listed coordinates are those of R +T forT∈C[2]. Every surviving nontorsion point therefore
has height ˆh(R), contradicting ˆh(R) = m2ˆh(S). This ﬁnite computation is an explicit part of the
proof, with a recorded independent replay; it is not replaced by a rank oracle.
Thus 2C(K0) = Z(2R). The inverse coordinate change sends 2R to P0, as asserted.
3.1 Polynomial membership and addition
Apart from the identity branch, membership in 2E(K0) is expressed by choosing X,Y,H ∈ K0,
u∈ Q, and imposing
Y 2 =X 3−d2X, u N(Y ) = 1, 2YH = 3X 2−d2, x =H 2− 2X, y =H(X−x)−Y.
Every nonidentity double has a preimage with nonzero ordinate. Each ﬁeld equation is two rational
equations. Membership is an all-input statement. Raw addition has the narrower exact contract
Addraw(ι(a),ι (b),R ) ⇐⇒ R =ι(a +b).
Its branches cover both identities, opposite points, distinct abscissas and doubling with nonzero
ordinate. Every later use supplies canonical input codes. Multiplication ﬁrst imposes membership
of all three roots and is sound on arbitrary rational tuples. In the natural predicate, four initially
untyped tuples enter multiplication calls, so are typed before their squared values are added. La-
grange’s theorem then gives exactly ι(N). This uses multiplication completeness; the generator
proposition alone does not supply the natural predicate.

Theorem 4.1 (Six-contact estimate) . For every such S,R and every u∈ Q\C ,
h(u)≤H6(S;R)MS(u)24.
Proof. TakeB = (2,−3), the maximal order O = Z[i, (1 +j)/2], and µ = 2j +ij. With its positive
sign choice, the form Trd(µ−1xy) has matrix

and determinant one, in the ordered basis (1,i, (1 + j)/2, (i +ij)/2). The Riemann form is the
convention of [ 21, equation (3) and Lemma 3]; its Rosati involution is a↦→ µ−1aµ. This is the
principal-polarization datum whose normalizer quotient is P1j , with branch values 0,∞,−16/27
and indices 4, 6, 2 [19, 21]. Put K0 = ˆO ∗ and K3 = ker(K0−→ (O/3O)∗). Let N be the ﬁnite-
adelic normalizer of ˆO. It normalizes K3. At a split prime the local normalizer is the center times
the maximal-order units; at each of the two division primes its quotient by these groups has order
two. Thus |N/(A∗fK0)| = 4 . A central ﬁnite idele acts trivially on the level-three curve: multiply
it by a rational scalar to remove all ﬁnite valuations, and choose the scalar’s sign so that the
residue at three is one. The remaining idele lies in K3, and the rational central scalar acts trivially
on the symmetric domain. Consequently the entire level-three curve Y3, with all its determinant
components, admits the action of N/(A∗fK3) with quotient P1j , and
deg(Y3−→ P1j )≤ 4|(O/3O)∗|≤ 4· 81 = 324.
We construct its integral model without changing this degree. Apply [ 20, Theorem 2.1 and
Corollary 2.3] to V1(5) over Z[1/30]. Its determinant image is full, its level is maximal at the
11division primes, and 5≥ 4 is prime to six. First add a full ﬁve-marking reﬁning the V1(5)-marking.
This gives the full-ﬁve moduli scheme, including every determinant component, as a ﬁnite étale
cover. Next add the ﬁnite étale sheaf
IsomO((O/3O),A [3]).
This sheaf is nonempty on every geometric ﬁber. Indeed B3 is a division algebra and the rational
Tate module is a rank-one left B3-module. Every O3-stable lattice in that module is principal:
choose a nonzero lattice element of least division valuation and divide every other element by it on
the right. Thus the Tate lattice is free of rank one over O3. One can also check this in characteristic
zero and extend it to all ﬁbers by local constancy of the ﬁnite étale A[3] with itsO-action.
The resulting smooth projective scheme X35 carries full markings at three and ﬁve. The group
G5 = (O/5O)∗ changes only the ﬁve-marking. Its action is free: a stabilizer would give a polarized
automorphism preserving the full three-marking, hence a ﬁnite-order element of 1 + 3M4(Z3). That
group is torsion-free, as follows by expanding a hypothetical prime-order element at its ﬁrst nonzero
3-adic term. The automorphism is therefore the identity, and the full ﬁve-marking forces the group
element to be the identity. The quotient X35/G5 is smooth projective, its covering map is ﬁnite
étale, and the universal abelian scheme, its canonical principal polarization, its O-action and its
three-marking descend. This quotient represents exactly the full-three moduli problem, so its
generic ﬁber is Y3. The auxiliary ﬁve-marking has contributed no degree factor to Y3→ P1j .
Each geometric component of Y3 is a torsion-free cover of the norm-one orbifold of Euler charac-
teristic−1/3, and hence has genus at least two. The normalizer action extends to its smooth proper
model by uniqueness of the stable model, including permutations of components. Fix p> 324 and
work after strict henselization. The action is tame. On each component use its eﬀective action.
A ﬁnite-order automorphism of order prime to p cannot specialize to the identity, by considering
its ﬁrst nonzero term modulo a power of the uniformizer. At a ﬁxed point the same power-series
argument shows that the tangent character of the stabilizer is faithful. Thus the stabilizer is cyclic.
For a nonidentity generator the tangent eigenvalue minus one is a unit. Its ﬁxed-point equation
therefore has a unique lift by Hensel’s lemma. All powers of the generator ﬁx that lift. It follows
that no new branch orbit appears and that distinct branch orbits cannot collide: their stabilizers
at a purported common specialization would belong to one cyclic group, whose nontrivial elements
have the same unique ﬁxed lift. A veraging a parameter gives a local invariant ring R[[ze]]. Hence
the quotient is smooth and retains the three disjoint branch sections and their indices 4, 6, 2.
Its genus-zero model with these three ordered sections is isomorphic to P1Zp carrying the ordered
triple (0,∞,−16/27), which is disjoint since 2 and 3 are units. The isomorphism agrees with j on
the generic ﬁber, by uniqueness of a projective coordinate on three points. Thus it is the integral
model in the stated j-coordinate; no further denominator prime has been discarded.
Pull back by J(u) =−57600F (u)2/G(u)6. If

replaces e by e/ gcd(e,a ) and leaves 2, 1, 1. The discriminants and resultants have prime support

By [ 18, Part I, Theorems 2.8–2.9], polarizable complex tori are abelian varieties and their
analytic homomorphisms are algebraic. After clearing lattice denominators, the same assertion
identiﬁes rational Hodge endomorphisms with algebraic quasi-endomorphisms. These endomor-
phisms are deﬁned over Q: every automorphism of C/Q ﬁxes their action on all torsion points, and
torsion is Zariski dense. On rational homology the complex structure is right multiplication by an
element J∈ B⊗ R with J 2 =−1. In EndQ(B) = B⊗Bop, commutation with J requires each
right coeﬃcient to commute with J. Its rational centralizer is either Q or an imaginary quadratic
ﬁeldk. Hence the geometric endomorphism algebra is either B orM2(k). We treat these two cases
separately. Throughout, put M =MS(u), d = [L : Q]≤D, and Q =QR.

there. Take µ1 = 1 and inﬂate this ﬁnite choice to GQ. The discrepancy

commutes with B, hence belongs to Q×. Set aσ = (degµσ)−1/4> 0 andνσ =aσµσ in the category
with algebraic coeﬃcients. Throughout these coeﬃcient extensions Galois acts on the varieties

linearly. In particular, the aσ are constant coeﬃcients in the products below. Multiplicativity of

Thusε is a normalized sign cocycle inﬂated from Gal(L/Q). Only ﬁnitely many algebraic coeﬃcients
aσ occur.
We need a splitting with controlled ramiﬁcation, not merely existence of a projective lift. Identify
H 2(GQ,{1,−1}) with Br(Q)[2]. At a ﬁnite prime outside T the class of ε is zero: its restriction
is unramiﬁed and the unramiﬁed local quotient has cohomological dimension one. Let T− be the
ﬁnite primes where its invariant is 1/2. For each odd p∈T− choose an odd character of (Z/pZ)×;
at 2∈ T− use the nontrivial character modulo four. Their product is a Dirichlet character χ,

Here the product over an empty set is one.
To check that χ has the required square-root obstruction, use the exact sequence of constant
discrete Galois modules 1→ {±1} →µ∞ z↦→z2−−−→µ∞ → 1. By local reciprocity, a ﬁnite-order
14character of GQp has a character square root exactly when its value on −1∈ Q×p is 1. Indeed this
is the only obstruction on the torsion part of Q×p ; the valuation factor and the procyclic principal-
unit factors admit such roots. Hence the boundary of χ and [ε] agree at every ﬁnite place. Brauer
reciprocity makes them agree at the real place, and the injective global-to-local Brauer map makes
them equal globally [ 42, Chapter I]. Choosing a continuous pointwise square root of χ and correcting
it by a sign cochain consequently gives a continuous cochain β :GQ→µ2m such that
β2 =χ, β (σ)β(τ )β(στ )−1 =ε(σ,τ ).
Its restriction to GL is a character invariant under GQ. Outside T , its inertia restrictions are
quadratic and nontrivial at only ﬁnitely many primes. Twisting by the quadratic character of the
product of those odd primes kills all these restrictions: at an odd prime there is a unique nontrivial
quadratic character on inertia. This introduces ramiﬁcation only at those primes and possibly at
2∈T , and changes neither displayed identity. We henceforth use this choice of β.
Let L′ be the kernel ﬁeld of β|GL. Conjugacy invariance shows that L′/Q is Galois, and

The last inequality follows from R≥ max(D, 1012) and E0 = 10(DG0)2.
Modularity and a uniform conductor bound. Choose one number ﬁeld C containing all
aσ, the values of β, and a splitting ﬁeld of B. For each coeﬃcient prime 𝓁 choose an embedding
of C into Q𝓁 and extend scalars on V𝓁(A) to a ﬁnite extension containing its image. With the
constant-coeﬃcient convention above, deﬁne
ρ𝓁(σ) = β(σ)−1aσµσ◦σ.
The cocycle identities make this a representation of GQ. It commutes with B, so matrix units give
ρ𝓁≃ r𝓁⊕r𝓁 with dimr𝓁 = 2 . On GL′ it is the actual Tate action of A. Faltings’ semisimplicity
and endomorphism theorems [ 33] show that r𝓁|GL′ is absolutely irreducible: after splitting B, its
commutant is the scalars. Lemma H.1 below shows that the representation is de Rham at 𝓁, by
proper-smooth comparison and ﬁnite descent [ 15, 16], and that its two Hodge–Tate weights are 0,−1
in the Tate convention. Berger’s p-adic monodromy theorem [22] makes this de Rham representation
potentially semistable, as required by Pan’s theorem. It is odd. To see this without an assumption
on a residual representation, compare with Betti homology at a complex embedding. Take the map
on rational Betti homology induced by antiholomorphic conjugation followed by the holomorphic
quasi-isogenyµc, and extend it C-linearly. It exchanges the two Hodge summands. Each summand
is a simple module for B⊗ C =M2(C). Consequently its action on the two-dimensional multiplicity
space is not scalar; since r𝓁(c)2 = 1, its eigenvalues are 1,−1. It is unramiﬁed outside T∪{𝓁}, since
there L is unramiﬁed, A has good reduction, and β is unramiﬁed.
Use the Tate convention in which the cyclotomic character has Hodge–Tate weight −1. Apply
[23, Theorem 1.0.4] at 𝓁 = 5 ; there is no residual-image proviso at this prime. It gives a cuspidal
eigenform up to character twist. The twisting character is a de Rham subrepresentation of the Hom
representation and is unramiﬁed outside ﬁnitely many primes. Local algebraicity [ 24, Appendix
B, pp. 32–33] and class ﬁeld theory over Q make it a ﬁnite character times an integral power of
the cyclotomic character: removing its integral local Hodge–Tate weight leaves ﬁnite inertia at ﬁve
and at every other prime, hence a ﬁnite ray-class character. Comparing {0,−1} with the modular
weights{0, 1−k} after that power forces k = 2 and power zero. Absorb the ﬁnite Dirichlet character
into the eigenform and take its primitive newform f . Li’s Fricke formula [ 25, p. 296, before Lemma
156] makes the span of its coeﬃcient conjugates Fricke-stable. Shimura’s quotient construction and
Euler-factor identity [ 26, Theorem 1] therefore show that r5 is a constituent of the Tate module of
the modular abelian variety Af , or equivalently r∨5 is its cohomological constituent. This duality
preserves the conductor.
The representations at ﬁve and three belong to this same system. Enlarge C to contain the
coeﬃcients of f , retaining the chosen embedding at ﬁve. For p /∈ T∪{ 3, 5}, choose a place v of
L and a lift σ∈GQp of the residue ﬁeld map x↦→xp in its decomposition group. Since Lv/Qp is
unramiﬁed and A has good reduction, relative p-Frobenius maps the reduction Av to the reduction
of σA at v. Composing with µσ gives an endomorphism of Av. The characteristic polynomial of
an abelian-variety endomorphism on rational Tate modules is independent of the coeﬃcient prime.
Thus half its trace, multiplied by β(σ)−1aσ, is one element of C whose images are the traces of every
r𝓁. Away also from the ﬁnite set of primes dividing the level of f , the equality with its modular
trace at ﬁve is an equality of algebraic numbers. Transporting it through an embedding at three,
Chebotarev and semisimplicity identify r3 with the corresponding modular Tate representation. We
may therefore use r3 to compute the conductor at ﬁve and r5 at all other primes.
Here are uniform local bounds, including the small primes. For a ﬁnite extension of Qp of
degree at most a, its normal closure has degree at most a!. For a ﬁnite Galois local extension with
ramiﬁcation index e and diﬀerent exponent δ, let f be the Eisenstein polynomial of a uniformizer
π over the maximal unramiﬁed subﬁeld. Then δ ≤ e− 1 + evp(e). The derivative terms have
distinct valuations modulo e, so there is no cancellation at the smallest valuation. The identity
f ′(π) =∏σ⁄=1(π−σπ) over the inertia conjugates gives δ =∑i≥0(|Gi|− 1). Changing variables by
the Herbrand function yields δ/e = 1− 1/e +

At p, take F = Lv and adjoin A[3], using A[5] if p = 3 . This extension has degree at most
DG0 over Qp. Raynaud’s criterion [ 31, Theorem 3.5] gives semistable reduction there. Its wild
inertia acts trivially on each V𝓁(A) with 𝓁⁄=p. The preceding break bound and the subgroup rule
for upper numbering therefore show that all absolute ramiﬁcation groups above U0 = 4(DG0)2 act

5 The pole predicate and its converse input
The pole construction and simultaneous-descent strategy adapt [ 78, Section 3].

Write Φ(B) for rational solubility. For B⁄= 0,m is a signed divisor of 2730, and the equations give
points (A,r 1)∈ Ee and (Az2,zr 2)∈ EeH with eHz⁄= 0 , where E𝓁 : y2 = x(x−𝓁)(x + 3𝓁). The
polynomial has total degree 75.
Lemma 5.1 (One-sided obstruction) . If B⁄= 0 and Φ(B), then vp(B) is not negative and odd for
any p /∈S0.
Proof. If k = vp(B) < 0 were odd, then vp(A) = k, vp(H) = 3 k. Exactly one of e,eH has
even valuation. Square scaling makes its parameter a unit, preserving the parity of the abscissa’s
valuation. For a unit parameter the cubic has valuation vp(x) if vp(x)> 0 and 3vp(x) if vp(x)< 0.
Its being a square forces even valuation. But both points have abscissa in the squareclass A, a
contradiction.
Theorem 5.2 (Pole completeness, PC). For every rational B with square reduced denominator,
0<B < 1/2 and vp(B)≥ 4 for every p∈S0 imply Φ(B).
The following statement is the E =E𝓁,s2(E) = 1 case of [ 74, Theorem 1.1], and also follows as
a special case of [ 75, Theorem 1.1]. We give the restricted argument here; these preprints are cited
for attribution, not assumed as additional converse inputs.

corankZ2 Sel2∞(E𝓁/Q) = 1 = ⇒ ords=1L(E𝓁,s ) = 1.
Here c4 = 208𝓁2, ∆ = 2304𝓁6, and j = 35152/9. The nonintegral rational j shows these curves
are non-CM. They are diﬀerent from the CM generator curve. Theorem 5.3 is proved in Sections 7–
9, with its coeﬃcient and normalization arguments in Appendices E–G. We now prove Theorem 5.2
from this restricted converse.
5.1 The exact descent reduction
The signed divisors of 2730 realize all real, 2-adic and 3-adic squareclass signatures. After −1, 2, 3
ﬁx sign and valuation parities, the remaining unit vectors of 5, 7, 13 are (0, 1, 1), (1, 0, 0), (0, 1, 0) and
form a basis. Choose positive m making A =mB square at two and a nonsquare unit squareclass
at three. Let β,θ be the positive squarefree representatives of A,H . Then β≡ 17 (mod 24) ,θ≡ 1
(mod 24) , with disjoint supports, β >1, and θ = 1 permitted. Each is a local square at the support
of the other. At a prime of β, the relevant odd valuation of B is positive, so 1−B/m is a square
unit. At a prime of θ, v(B) must be zero, since otherwise v(H) is even; there B/m≡ 1, so A is a
square.
The simultaneous construction in S11–S12 gives n,nθ positive squarefree and congruent to
eleven modulo twelve, both with two-Selmer dimension three and with extra Kummer class (β, 1).
Its matrix conditions can be stated explicitly. For support primes qi, set si = [−1/qi], λi = [3/qi],

Lemma 5.4 (Dimension three gives corank one) . For an elliptic curve with full rational two-torsion,
dimF2 Sel2 = 3 implies corankZ2 Sel2∞ = 1.
19Proof. The ﬁnite Kummer sequence gives r + dim Sha[2] = 1 . Write Sha[2∞] = ( Q2/Z2)t⊕F
with F ﬁnite. The Cassels–Tate pairing on the quotient by the maximal divisible subgroup is
nondegenerate and alternating for an elliptic curve [ 37]. Its ﬁnite primary cyclic factors are paired,
so dimF [2] is even. Thus F = 0 and r +t = 1. The restriction of the pairing to F [2] need not be
nondegenerate and is not used.
Lemma 5.5 (Forward analytic rank zero and one). LetA/Q be an elliptic curve. If ords=1L(A,s ) =
r≤ 1, then rankA(Q) = r and Sha(A/Q) is ﬁnite. Consequently corankZ2 Sel2∞(A/Q) = r.
Proof. Modularity follows from [ 50, Theorem A]. Apply [ 54, Theorem 1, pp. 4–5], whose statement
includes both r = 0 and r = 1 . Its Theorems 3–5 provide the auxiliary imaginary quadratic ﬁeld,
the nonzero Heegner height, and ﬁniteness over that ﬁeld; the restriction kernel for Sha is contained
in the ﬁnite group Sha(A/Q)[2]. The ordinary Kummer sequence
0−→A(Q)⊗ Q2/Z2−→ Sel2∞(A/Q)−→ Sha(A/Q)[2∞]−→ 0
gives the corank assertion. Independently, the r = 0 ﬁniteness assertion follows directly from [ 55,
Corollary 14.3], applied to the modular quotient J1(N ) ↠A over Q and the trivial character. This
result includes the prime two and imposes no residual irreducibility hypothesis.
Applying Theorem 5.3 to both n and nθ, Lemma 5.5 gives rank one and ﬁnite Sha, hence
Sha[2] = 0 . The Kummer class is then represented by actual rational points. Their abscissas have
squareclass β, by the cubic equation. Write H =θc2 and rescale the second point to EnH . If the
ﬁrst abscissa is Aq2, divide both parameters by q2 and both coordinates by q2,q 3. The abscissas
become A,Az 2, giving precisely the eleven witnesses, with w = (eHz )−1. If θ = 1 , one can reuse
a point on the same curve. This proves Theorem 5.2, with both restricted-converse applications in
their stated domain.
6 Multiplication and the ﬁnite false-triple guard
The local-test construction and prime-pattern strategy adapt [ 78, Section 2 and Lemma 6.1].
The actual multiplication template ﬁrst forces all three input codes to be members of 2E(K0).
These membership calls are conjuncts inside the height certiﬁcate; they are not assumed caller
preconditions. Thus even untyped rational inputs, if accepted, have unique indices. The template
has 35,134 rational witnesses. Its construction is S03 and its mathematical speciﬁcation is S13.
We use the following local conventions for the displayed integral Weierstrass equation. Put
z =−x/y, and put w = 1/x with w(O) = 0 . This w diﬀers from the auxiliary variable −1/y used
in [ 48, IV.1]. The formal expansions give w = z2 times an integral unit, so v(w) = 2v(z). For a
complete discrete valuation with e = v(p), the formal logarithm converges on v(z) > 0 and is an
isometry with inverse exponential on v(z)>e/ (p− 1) [48, IV.5.5, IV.6.3–6.4, pp. 129–132]. Indeed
its degree-r coeﬃcient has denominator dividing r, so each relative error has valuation at least
(r− 1)v(z)−evp(r)> 0 in this range. Thus log([n]Q) = n log(Q) and v(z([n]Q)) = v(z(Q)) +v(n)
for nonzero Q and n there. These statements concern the formal neighborhood of our integral
equation. When identifying it with the full reduction kernel of a minimal model, we use [ 48,
VII.2.1–2.2, pp. 188–191].

This veriﬁes exactly PC’s hypotheses, including all nonzero guards. Negative indices, zero indices
and coincident points are handled by the group-law branches. Therefore Theorem 5.2 supplies the
required pole witnesses. This proves the full multiplication statement M, including completeness
for every integer triple (n1,n 2,n 1n2) and soundness on arbitrary rational input codes.
7 The restricted converse and its dependency order
The organization of the restricted-converse argument follows [ 74], particularly its ring-class inter-
polation, determinant transfer, coeﬃcient systems and ﬁxed-curve assembly. The broader converse
is stated in [ 75]. Our specialization uses the same full Selmer-corank convention, including any
divisible part of Sha, as those sources.
For a positive squarefree 𝓁≡ 11 (mod 12) put
E𝓁 :y2 =x(x−𝓁)(x + 3𝓁).
Its invariants are c4 = 208𝓁2, ∆ = 2304𝓁6 and j = 35152/9. Thus it has full rational two-torsion
and is not a CM curve, since a rational CM j-invariant is an integer. The curve in this section is
diﬀerent from the ﬁxed CM curve used to represent integer indices.
The assertion needed in Theorem 5.3 is
corankZ2 Sel2∞(E𝓁/Q) = 1 = ⇒ ords=1L(E𝓁,s ) = 1. (RC)
We prove this assertion in this section and the next two sections. Appendices E–G give the ﬁxed-
form, theta, nonvanishing and normalization constructions used in this deduction. By [ 50, Theorem
A and Introduction, conditions (1)–(6)], E = E𝓁 is modular. Here N denotes its conductor. The
modular realization gives a rational weight-two newform f of level N and an elliptic quotient
qf : J0(N ) ↠ Af in the rational isogeny class of E; see [ 51, Theorem 11.19 and Remark 11.18].
Choose a Q-isogeny α :Af→E and put ϕ =αqf and π(x) = ϕ([x]− [∞]). Thus π(∞) = 0 and,
for q -N , ϕTq = [aq(f )]ϕ. Fix the integral Tate lattice and the local squareclasses allowed at the
primes of 2N . Constants depending on this data are chosen before any conductor, companion ﬁeld
or binary dimension varies. Quotienting by a rational point of order two gives a rational two-isogeny.
The Selmer parity theorem [ 49, Theorem 1.8] therefore gives root number −1 under the left side of
(RC). This application does not replace Selmer corank by Mordell–Weil rank and does not assume
ﬁnite Sha.
29Remark 7.1 (Why this family is the input to the converse) . The congruence 𝓁≡ 11 (mod 12) comes
from the pole-completeness construction. In the proof of Theorem 5.2, the two parameters n,nθ are
positive squarefree numbers in this congruence class; their two-Selmer groups have dimension three.
Lemma 5.4 gives full two-primary Selmer corank one, so these are exactly the two applications
required of Theorem 5.3.
In the converse argument we ﬁx one such 𝓁. In particular 𝓁 is odd, is prime to three, and is
greater than one, hence is not a square. We use the displayed full rational two-torsion and non-CM
properties, the resulting rational two-isogeny, and the explicit roots 0,𝓁,−3𝓁. The last roots enter
theK-split seed argument below. The constants may depend on this ﬁxed curve; there is no bound
onω(𝓁) and no assertion that they are uniform as 𝓁 varies. The statements about coeﬃcient graphs
below are the rational, full-two-torsion case used by E𝓁 and its quadratic twists.
Here is the dependency order of the proof. A ﬁxed-form Euler-system calculation gives the
even coeﬃcient bound. A varying-companion arithmetic estimate, proved by the two limits below,
then gives the genus-point lower bound. Only after that lower bound do we normalize the odd
coeﬃcients. Their ﬁnite-stage forms and the even forms give one common binary family. Finally
a ﬁxed-ﬁeld determinant argument excludes a zero derivative at the base. The even estimate does
not use that ﬁnal determinant argument on Heegner classes.
7.1 The even coeﬃcient calculation
Use normalized central values L∗(h) with the real and imaginary periods ﬁxed by π and the
integral lattice. Only their valuations are used; every subsequent comparison uses this same
normalization. For every twist whose two-primary Tate–Shafarevich group is ﬁnite, let s(h) =
lengthZ2 Sha(E(h)/Q)[2∞].
Proposition 7.2 (The ﬁxed-form even scalar) . Use the ﬁxed E,π , integral lattice and allowed
old local types speciﬁed above. Put Λ = Z2[[t]]. For a ﬁnite elementary abelian two-group G, let
χx =χ0(x◦ε) (x∈ ˆG) be a family of quadratic characters with a common real sign and the same
restrictions at every place dividing 2N . There is one scalar uev∈ Λ[1/2][G] satisfying (3.2). Its
clearing exponent and the bounded error in that formula depend only on the ﬁxed data, not on G
or the good-prime conductor support. The central identity is used only at corank-zero constituents
with nonzero central value; at a corank-zero constituent the same central coordinate is zero exactly
when the central value is zero.
Proof. The following construction is expanded in Propositions E.1 and E.2, with its central calcu-
lation in Proposition E.3. The ﬁxed-form construction uses one integral symbol combination with
nonzero projections to both real signs. Kato’s integral maps, with j∗ cohomology taken modulo
each power of two before passage to the limit, give classes whose inertia restrictions vanish integrally.
Indeed the low-degree Leray edge map gives this vanishing before rationalization. Restriction of
transfer is the Mackey sum of the corresponding upper inertia restrictions. Hence unnormalized
transfer preserves the vanishing even when the intervening extension is ramiﬁed. No character
projector divided by a growing group order is used.
For a ﬁnite binary character group, let F be the least common multiple of the odd parts of
all its character conductors, including the base character. Write ZF for the compatible Iwasawa
class obtained by pushing the retained-smoothed Kato classes at levels F 2n through the integral
coeﬃcient map. Here and throughout the common-scalar construction, the binary family is required
to have one real sign and ﬁxed local squareclasses at 2N . Writing its characters as χx =χ0(x◦ε),
this means ε(c) = 1 for complex conjugation c, and ε|GQv = 1 at every v | 2N . Indeed every
character of G evaluates these elements to one, and the characters separate G. The lower bound
30uses G = 1 ; the upper-transfer cubes lie in the ﬁxed signed unit ﬁlter of their coeﬃcient source.
The ﬁnal cubes have the same property. At a character χ its unused-prime factors are
Eq(χ) = 1− aqχ(q)q σ−1q + 1qσ−2q . (3.1)
They are the actual determinants of the unused good local terms. Active ramiﬁed good primes
have rational inertia−1, so have no such rational local term. The ﬁxed bad and dyadic corrections
form a factor FE with nonzero central value.
The rational divisibility input is Kato’s Theorem 13.4(2), together with the nonzero-class input
in 13.5(2) [ 55]. At two we justify the divisibility through [ 73, Theorem 0.8]; Appendix E checks its
stronger abelian-extension hypothesis and compares the two cohomology conventions. We use the
height-one primes not containing two. Rational irreducibility is preserved on a quadratic subgroup
of the non-CM open image. That subgroup contains a nontrivial unipotent, which supplies the
required one-dimensional ﬁxed space. Oddness supplies the two real eigenspaces, and purity supplies
the common Frobenius weight. This application does not use residual irreducibility at two or the
stronger hypotheses of Theorem 13.4(3).
The smoothing constants c,ds can be chosen to satisfy
c,ds≡ 1 (mod 3 NoddF ), c,d s≡ 1 + 2e (mod 2e+1),
where e≥ max{3,v 2(N )} is ﬁxed by the original dyadic data. In weight two and cohomological
degree one the two smoothing exponents are one. Kato’s explicit reciprocity formula therefore
contributes valuation 2e, independent of F . For the central normalization, ﬁrst descend to the
primitive character conductor and then use the invariant basis
√
heχh of the quadratic character
line. The resulting varying scalar 1/h is a two-adic unit. The complete calculation in Appendix E
includes discriminant 4h; a Gauss basis there has the extra factor
√
∆h/h, which must also be
included in its change of basis. The numerical sizes of c,ds may grow, while their relevant valuation
remains ﬁxed.
For completeness, the integral calculation at two uses the positive complex. Finite continuous
cochains are ﬁltered unions of ﬁnite free modules and hence ﬂat. Splitting the residual complex into
cohomology and disks, then using the nilpotent perturbation calculation of Section 9, gives ﬁnite
free models with their maps. In the ﬁxed real cyclotomic tower the ordinary two-primary class
group vanishes. One proves this inductively: only the prime above two ramiﬁes in each quadratic
step; the abelianization of a possible Hilbert two-class tower is generated by that inertia of order
two. A ﬁnite two-group with cyclic abelianization is cyclic, and inertia excludes a larger cyclic
extension. The unit-signature calculation is separate. A norm-minus-one unit in positive layers,
and−1 at the base, has signature vector a unit in the characteristic-two cyclic group algebra. For
a scalar coeﬃcient the resulting residual dimensions are
dimH 1 = 2n +sn− 1, dimH 2 =sn− 1.
For each ﬁxed support sn is bounded. Universal coeﬃcients give generic residual rank one in degree
one; the two trivial elliptic factors give rank two. Unit cancellations thus identify the positive
complex overO[G] withO[G]2[−1]. This last rank is independent of support, although preliminary
models need not have a uniform size.
A generator of the ﬁxed real invariant lattice gives a connecting class a. Its central image need
not be saturated. Twice an ordinary class lifts because the relevant real H 1 is killed by two. Wedge
a with a lift of 2FEZF in the original determinant line. This produces a common scalar uev. The

constituent of Selmer corank zero with L∗(h)⁄= 0:

Here Λ = Z2[[t]] andO = ˆΛ(2). The error and Cev are ﬁxed before the binary group and support.
To justify membership in Λ[G], ﬁrst use rational characterwise divisibility and Fourier inversion
for membership in Λ[1/2][G], without estimating the Fourier denominator. Intersect coeﬃcientwise
with the integralO[G] result. The explicit coeﬃcient construction also covers ﬁeld discriminant 4h,
with its dyadic character retained before the coﬁnal norm limit, as in Appendix E. At a corank-zero

At an active good odd prime gv = 2, since AIv =E[2]. Unused terms cancel (3.1); all other terms

7.2 Nonvanishing with all support restrictions
Proposition 7.3 (Nonvanishing in a prescribed unit ﬁlter) . The curve E is the ﬁxed curve of this
section. The following assertion allows any nonempty signed unit ﬁlter and any additional ﬁnite
avoidance set; its bound on the number of prime factors does not depend on either set. A signed
unit ﬁlter prescribes a real sign and unit squareclasses at a ﬁnite set S containing 2, 3 and the bad
primes. Let B be any additional ﬁnite set of forbidden primes. If its root number is even, there
is a squarefree parameter in this ﬁlter, coprime to B, with at most ﬁve prime factors and nonzero
central value. In an odd ﬁlter there is a simple-zero parameter, with no asserted prime-count bound.
Proof. Here is the reduction to the precise classical statements. Choose a signed prime q0 in the
prescribed unit classes by Dirichlet’s theorem, avoiding S∪B. Apply Hoﬀstein–Luo [ 57] in the
even case, or Bump–Friedberg–Hoﬀstein [ 56] in the odd case, to the actual curve E(q0). Require
their positive discriminant d to split at S∪B∪{|q0|} and at the conductor primes. Then q0d has
the prescribed original classes. Hoﬀstein–Luo gives at most four prime factors of d, so at most ﬁve
in total. The BFH clause used in the odd case is the simple-zero clause.
The root-number check includes ramiﬁed dyadic quadratic characters. At the primes in S equal
squareclasses give isomorphic local curves. At a good odd prime newly ramiﬁed in the parameter,
the root factor is (−1/p) [58, Proposition 3.3]. Its product outside S is ﬁxed by the real sign and
the dyadic unit class. Thus unit classes 3 or 7 modulo eight are allowed; the ﬁeld discriminant
may be four times the parameter. We do not invoke an odd-discriminant root formula on those
parameters. See also Proposition G.1 for the same ﬁve-prime bound and its subsequent use in the
companion construction.
7.3 Coeﬃcient symbols and the ﬁnite address lemmas
We state the ﬁnite interface before using it for the even upper bound. The graph language records
quadratic residue data of the coeﬃcient indices. It does not replace a Selmer group by an abstract
graph. Throughout this subsection all vertices are in the rational full-two-torsion case. Thus there
is one independent residue bit per pair; the split/simple distinction needed for other two-torsion
ﬁelds does not occur here.
32Deﬁnition 7.4 (Compatible typed graphs) . Fix the ﬁnite support S of a coeﬃcient source, contain-
ing two, and the ﬁnite quadratic characters used to read its coeﬃcients. The type of a prime p /∈S
records its values under these characters and its unit squareclasses at S, including its class modulo
eight. We write the resulting ﬁnite elementary two-group additively as L. We retain all relations
among these coordinates; a tuple of arbitrary character values need not be a type. Additional ﬁxed
splitting conditions restrict us to their actual realizable types. For an odd theta stage, its reduction
prime is part of this ﬁxed support during that stage. Let Slab also contain the ramiﬁcation of any
additional stationary label tests. Enlarging Slab records labels and prime exclusions; it does not
change the coeﬃcient source or add relaxed places to a Selmer complex.
A compatible typed graph consists of a ﬁnite ordered vertex set V , an individually realizable
type 𝓁v∈L for each vertex, and quadratic residue bits. For prime representatives, write zvw = 0
or 1 according as (pv/pw) = 1 or−1. For each unordered pair just one direction is freely speciﬁed;
the other satisﬁes
zvw +zwv =ϵvϵw, ϵ v = (pv− 1)/2 (mod 2) ,
where ϵv is part of the type. There are no self-residue bits. Only these compatible, realizable
data are used below. A ﬁxed ﬁlter prescribes the total type ∑v𝓁v needed for the coeﬃcient index,
including the signed parameter convention of the source. If that convention is h = σD, then
D =∏vpv > 0 is the Fourier index and σ is ﬁxed by the source. The signed factors p∗v = (−1/pv)pv
will be used when the same graph deﬁnes a family of quadratic characters.
Contracting two vertices replaces their type by its sum and every incidence to an external vertex
by the sum of the two incidences. Equivalently its template is the product pvpw; it is represented
by a fresh prime with those data. Reverse incidences are determined by reciprocity. No bit internal
to the contracted cluster is retained. A vertex is primary if its generator is a square unit at the
ﬁxed support and its type is neutral in every retained quadratic and stationary coordinate. It is
neutral relative to a graph if in addition all its incidences to the other vertices are zero.
Two symbols may read projections of one common type group. The projections retain the
relevant coordinates, preserve primary types, and commute with the product and external-incidence
rules just given. We require each indicated projection to be surjective onto its own realizable
alphabet; we do not require the map to their product to be surjective.
Deﬁnition 7.5 (Coeﬃcient symbols) . A symbol is the function A with values in F2 obtained from
one ﬁxed coeﬃcient source and one ﬁxed normalized depth δ. On an allowed positive squarefree
index D it is
A(D) =
{
1, v 2(a(D)) = δ +ω(D),
0, v 2(a(D))>δ +ω(D),
and it is zero oﬀ that source’s ﬁlter. For the even source, exact phase removal makes the relevant
coeﬃcient rational. For the odd source, (3.8) gives the required phase removal modulo the retained
power 2Mθ ; use its integer representative at that precision. A coeﬃcient zero modulo 2Mθ is
invisible. Whenever a unit is tested, the precision exceeds δ +ω(D), so the test is independent of
the representative. No exact rationality of an arbitrary lifted odd theta coeﬃcient is asserted. The
lower bound making these alternatives exhaustive is part of the coeﬃcient construction, not part
of this deﬁnition. For the even source it follows from (3.2) and the coeﬃcient-square formula of
Proposition E.4.
For an odd source this deﬁnition at one ﬁnite stage is used only through a speciﬁed ﬁnite
squarefree-weight cutoﬀ and at a stage where the lower bound holds through that cutoﬀ. The chosen
cutoﬀ and all required divisions must be included in the operator budget. The ﬁxed minimum and
the compatible ﬁnite tables are constructed in Propositions G.3 and G.5. The resulting limit symbol
33is a function on ﬁnite compatible typed graphs. It is not a valuation rule at all weights of one ﬁnite
form. The same source and depth are kept when a vertex is omitted.
Lemma 7.6 (The coeﬃcient rules) . After the stated coeﬃcient lower bounds have been proved, the
even symbol and the stabilized odd symbol satisfy the following rules on compatible typed graphs. At
an odd stage a rule is asserted only when all its inputs, outputs and coeﬃcient divisions lie in the
chosen ﬁnite budget. Conﬁgurations in these rules, and outputs of contractions, are nonempty.
1. Graph dependence and whole-prime omission. For a new vertex q adjoining a list U , its
reading factors through the elementary quadratic extension unramiﬁed outside S∪U . It is
determined by its type and its incidences with U . The reading after omission of p∈U uses the
same original form, phase characters and depth, with varying support U\{p}. That extension
is unramiﬁed at the whole rational prime p. Changing inertia at the omitted prime has no
eﬀect on this reading.
2. Contraction. For the independent bit of a pair v,w , write ∆vwA =A|zvw =0+A|zvw =1, changing
the reverse bit as required by reciprocity. Then
∆vwA(Γ) = A(Γ/(v,w )).
The graph on the right has the summed type and every summed external incidence from
Deﬁnition 7.4. Its fresh prime representative is read using the omitted-prime source in the
ﬁrst rule.
3. Primary isolation. If a conﬁguration contains a primary vertex neutral relative to all its other
vertices, its symbol is zero.
These are identities of the actual coeﬃcient sources, rather than axioms imposed on an arbitrary
binary function.
Proof. Proposition F.5 proves the rules from the integral shell recurrence, the unary subtraction and
the weight-two trace and inertia identities. Divisibility by the integer two precedes reduction modulo
the residue ﬁeld. In the present full-two-torsion case every new vertex is split in Q(E[2]) = Q; the
contraction coeﬃcient is one. The omitted form is constructed from the same original source, so its
unramiﬁedness removes the entire prime and does not choose a new minimum. The ﬁnal fresh-copy
comparison in that proof shows dependence on the whole typed graph, including overlapping prime
realizations. The same argument proves the rules for the even source; no odd normalization is
needed in that application.
Lemma 7.7 (Finite realization) . Keep ﬁnitely many old prime vertices and all their mutual data
ﬁxed. Given individually realizable types for ﬁnitely many new vertices, every prescription of the
independent forward bits involving a new vertex can be realized by distinct fresh primes outside any
prescribed ﬁnite avoidance set. This includes primary vertices and extra ﬁxed splitting conditions.
A product template can likewise be replaced by a fresh prime with the same type and all external
incidences whenever that output type is one of the permitted realizable types.
Proof. For rational quadratic characters the ﬁxed type is a reduced residue condition at a modulus
supported on Slab, including eight. Choose the new primes successively. A desired residue bit at
an old prime is a nonzero residue class there. The Chinese remainder theorem combines these
classes with the ﬁxed type, and Dirichlet’s theorem supplies inﬁnitely many primes in the resulting
reduced residue class. At each step avoid all previously chosen primes and the ﬁnite avoidance set.
Reciprocity gives the required reverse bits. Apply the same construction to the external residues
34and support type of a product template. The graph dependence in Lemma 7.6 makes replacement
by this fresh representative legitimate for the symbol.
Deﬁnition 7.8 (Unit seeds and partitionable lists) . A unit seed is a ﬁnite compatible typed graph
with symbol one. A minimum nonempty seed has the least positive vertex count among such seeds.
This terminology does not exclude the possibility that the empty conﬁguration is also a unit. A
nonempty prime list is partitionable if its vertices admit a partition into nonempty clusters, with a
contraction tree in each cluster, whose contracted graph is a unit seed. These trees and the resulting
types and incidences constitute a contraction certiﬁcate. This condition makes no assertion about
a Selmer group.
Lemma 7.9 (Minimum seeds and contraction certiﬁcates) . At the least positive vertex count of
a unit seed, its value is independent of all internal pair bits and depends only on its vertex types.
A contraction certiﬁcate for a nonempty list makes its polynomial in the independent pair bits
nonzero. In particular, a list with the same total type as a minimum nonempty seed is partitionable
if it contains anchors for the ﬁrst r− 1 seed types and a nonempty remainder, where r is the seed’s
size.
Proof. If r≥ 2, a nonzero derivative in an internal bit would, by contraction, give a unit on r− 1
nonempty vertices. This contradicts minimality. If r = 1 there is no internal bit. Iterating the
contraction identity along a forest gives its contracted symbol; an output equal to one therefore
proves that the original polynomial is nonzero. For the last assertion keep the r− 1 anchors and
merge the whole remainder into the last cluster. Equality of total types forces the last seed type.
The internal bits of the minimum seed do not matter. All contractions have nonempty output.
Deﬁnition 7.10 (Addresses and terminal polynomials) . For an integer b≥ 1, an address isx∈ Fb2.
A vertex with activation λ∈ (Fb2)∗\{ 0} is present at x exactly when λ(x) = 1 . Stationary vertices
are present at every address. At a ﬁxed address, retain all vertex types and any ﬁxed external
incidences, and regard a symbol as a function of the independent pair bits between the active
terminals. Its unique multilinear polynomial over F2 is the terminal polynomial . Its variables
describe possible changes of those bits; the initial prime realization already has ﬁxed bit values.
Finite diﬀerences in these variables are formal derivatives.
Lemma 7.11 (One symbol with a stationary base) . Fix a coeﬃcient symbol satisfying Lemma 7.6,
a nonempty stationary terminal list, its types and any stationary external data. Suppose its terminal
polynomial is nonzero and its total data lie in the source’s ﬁlter. For every b≥ 1 and every ﬁxed
assignment of the list’s pair bits, ﬁnitely many new primary vertices with nonzero linear activations
can be adjoined, with only their new incidences chosen, so that the symbol is one at every x⁄= 0 .
The original vertices and all their mutual data are unchanged. No new vertex is present at zero.
Fixed extra splitting and neutral external-incidence requirements may be included in the primary
types. For a ﬁxed even source the original prime representatives can be kept throughout. For a
stabilized odd symbol, the ﬁxed data in this statement are the abstract types and bits; its actual
theta stage is chosen before its prime representatives, as speciﬁed in the proof.
Lemma 7.12 (Two symbols on one address family) . Fix one or two coeﬃcient symbols satisfying
Lemma 7.6, reading compatible projections of one common realizable alphabet. Fix b≥ 1 and termi-
nals with nonzero linear activations, including at least one terminal for each nonzero linear form.
Stationary external data may also be ﬁxed. Assume that at every x⁄= 0 the active conﬁguration
is nonempty, its total data satisfy every required ﬁlter, and each symbol’s terminal polynomial is
separately nonzero. Then, starting with any ﬁxed assignment of these compatible types and bits,
35ﬁnitely many new primary vertices with nonzero linear activations can be adjoined so that all the
symbols are one at every x⁄= 0. Only incidences involving the new vertices are chosen. All original
data and the zero address are preserved, and the same ﬁxed splitting and neutral external-incidence
conditions may be imposed as above. If an odd limit symbol is present, this is ﬁrst a statement about
the ﬁnite typed graphs. It does not ﬁx rational prime representatives before the eventual theta stage.
Proof of the two address lemmas. The forest and network calculation in Proposition G.6 proves
both statements. For the stationary case its grounded-network columns attach to a ﬁxed terminal,
so no terminal activation anchors are required. For variable terminals the stated anchors give the
row-sum identity on every active restriction. Singular networks isolate prescribed derivatives at one
nonzero address; varying networks supply the corresponding diﬀerence when two symbols share a
variable. The two-symbol proof takes the product of the top forms in the ordinary polynomial
ring before Boolean reduction. Its nonzero coeﬃcient forces a single assignment that works at all
nonzero addresses. It does not require the two symbols to have a common nonzero evaluation before
the auxiliary vertices are added.
Each construction uses a ﬁnite number of vertices and coeﬃcient readings once b and the
terminal polynomials are ﬁxed. For an odd symbol, include all of them in one ﬁnite weight and
operator budget, then use Proposition G.5 to choose one actual theta stage carrying those tables
and divisions. Only afterward use Lemma 7.7 to choose its primes. Any old prime representatives
already chosen at that stage may be retained when they have its full prescribed types and bits, by
the same realization lemma. In the even stationary application all original primes can be retained
from the outset. The number of auxiliaries and the required theta precision may depend on b and
the terminal data. The source’s depth and the valuation constants attached to a unit do not change.
No common ﬁnite stage for all values of b is asserted.
The extra upper-bound argument. Nonvanishing alone does not bound the valuation of L∗,
and (3.2) by itself gives a lower bound. The needed upper bound uses a separate even-symbol
selection and transfer argument, which we now give.
Proposition 7.13 (The partitionable even upper estimate) . Fix one even coeﬃcient source, its
signed unit ﬁlter and its depth. If a parameter d in that ﬁlter has a nonempty partitionable prime
list and E(d) has full two-primary Selmer corank zero, then (3.3b) holds. Its constant depends only
on the ﬁxed source, not on the number or sizes of the primes of d or on later ﬁnite exclusions.
Proof. For the even coeﬃcient system described below, a unit symbol at a parameter d gives
L∗(d)⁄= 0, v 2L∗(d)≤ 2ω(d) +C0. (3.3a)
This follows from its same-ﬁlter coeﬃcient-square formula and its ﬁxed normalization. That even
normalization uses the lower bound from (3.2), not the as-yet undeﬁned odd minimum. Parti-
tionability is the nonempty contraction-certiﬁcate condition in Deﬁnition 7.8. By Lemma 7.9, its
iterated ﬁnite diﬀerence is nonzero and hence so is the terminal polynomial. An empty unit, when
present, satisﬁes its coeﬃcient bound directly and does not require the stationary address lemma.
For a partitionable parameter d already known to have Selmer corank zero, keep all its prime
factors stationary. Lemma 7.11 gives arbitrarily large binary cubes based at d, with no old prime
removed, whose other addresses satisfy (3.3a). The ﬁxed-form common scalar in (3.2) has one
uniformly cleared denominator on every such cube. Choose a two-adic precision larger than the
nonzero-address scalar valuation bound and its ﬁxed clearing exponent. Its sign-evaluation coeﬃ-
cient is a Boolean function with the bounded bit degrees proved in Section 9. A cube dimension
36larger than their sum therefore supplies a nonzero address congruent to the base at this precision.
If the base scalar were zero or had larger valuation than the bound, so would that nonzero address,
a contradiction. The corank-zero central comparison in (3.2) consequently gives
L∗(d)⁄= 0, v 2L∗(d)− 2ω(d)−s(d)≤C1. (3.3b)
The constants are ﬁxed by the even system. The dimension needed may depend on d; the valuation
bound does not. This is the cyclotomic missing-address argument on the ﬁxed-form Kato scalar,
not the later Heegner transfer.
Proposition 7.14 (A bounded companion in the prepared ﬁlter) . For (3.3), restrict h to positive
squarefree parameters in the prepared odd-sign unit ﬁlter containing one, prime to its ﬁxed support.
Thus h≡ 1 (mod 8) and w(E(h)) =−1. The simple-zero parameters used in (3.4)–(3.6) lie in this
ﬁlter. There are constants A,CE ﬁxed before h varies such that, outside any prescribed additional
ﬁnite avoidance set, one can choose a negative odd fundamental discriminant k′ which is a square
at every place dividing 2Nh , avoids the extra-unit ﬁelds, and has L∗(hk′)⁄= 0. It satisﬁes (3.3), in
particularω(k′)≤A. The valuation estimate uses the ﬁxed even sources of the original E, although
the analytic nonvanishing theorem will be applied to an auxiliary twist of E.
Proof.
Here is why the actual rough companion is partitionable. Prepare an even symbol for every
possible total type at the ﬁnite ﬁxed support. There are ﬁnitely many. In our full rational two-
torsion case the alphabet is all split. A least nonempty unit seed has no dependence on internal
edge bits: an edge derivative would be a smaller nonempty unit conﬁguration. Its individual labels
determine it. The negative-discriminant parity at two makes the total label nonzero, so this seed
is nonempty. A list with the same total label and enough copies of the seed labels contracts to it:
keep the ﬁrst r− 1 seed anchors and merge all remaining vertices into one nonempty cluster. Its
label is forced to be the last seed label. Thus the list is partitionable, regardless of the number or
labels of the remaining vertices.
Before h varies, ﬁx a bounded multiset of dummy labels supplying the required anchors and
reserves for every prepared total type. For each h, realize these labels by distinct primes avoiding
the ﬁxed support, the support of h, and all already ﬁxed exclusions. Put in B the entire ﬁxed
support, the support of h, the chosen dummy primes, and every other already ﬁxed forbidden
prime. Include any already ﬁxed reduction prime in B as well. Prescribe the total k′ to be a
negative odd fundamental discriminant, square at every place of 2Nh and outside the extra-unit
cases. Absorb h and the dummy product into the curve and use the preceding nonvanishing theorem
in its even ﬁlter. It adds at most ﬁve new primes. For the actual resulting total type, the already
chosen dummy supply makes hk′ partitionable. Its analytic nonvanishing gives Selmer corank zero
by the forward rank-zero theorem. Apply (3.3b), obtaining
ω(k′)≤A, v 2L∗(hk′)≤ 2ω(h) + 2ω(k′) +s(hk′) +CE. (3.3)
Here A is the ﬁxed dummy count plus ﬁve. This is the argument of the partitionable-even and
rough-companion lemmas, with their stationary primes and order of choices retained. Neither prime
sizes nor a uniform analytic threshold are asserted. Excluding a later coeﬃcient prime does not
enlarge the old Selmer support. The ﬁnite source preparation and the distinction between ﬁxed
dummy labels and their later prime realizations are also detailed in Proposition G.2.

7.5 A ﬁxed nonzero witness and actual odd forms
Choose one positive squarefree Dw in the odd ﬁlter containing one, after adjoining the primes of h∗
and their compatible unit tests. Section 7.2 supplies L′(E(Dw), 1)⁄= 0. The explicit height formula
makes P genDw nontorsion. Its unwanted h∗ component has rank zero. One ﬁxed multiplier L kills
the bounded-degree torsion and the projection denominator, so Q =LP genDw is a rational nontorsion
point on E(Dw).
Let C be complex conjugation on its Tate module and choose a nonzero integral row b with
bC = b. The Kummer translation image of Q is a full lattice. Indeed, if it vanished, its rational
cocycle would descend to the Tate image. A nonidentity central scalar z gives
(z− 1)c(g) = (g− 1)c(z),
38so that descended cocycle is a coboundary, contrary to Kummer injectivity. Irreducibility makes a
nonzero translation span the Tate space, and intersection with any ﬁxed ﬁnite kernel preserves full
rank. Twisting preserves open image by restriction to an index-at-most-two subgroup.
Choose one such translation v with 2b(v)⁄= 0. Multiplying conjugation by this translation gives
square translation (1 +C)v, detected by 2b(v) with one ﬁxed valuation cw. The element is a single
actual Galois element on the compositum. It is trivial on √Dw and nontrivial on √h∗ because
Dw > 0> h∗. Its restrictions therefore already agree on intersections. Finite Chebotarev, with a
place chosen above the resulting prime, gives arbitrarily advanced stages (Mθ,𝓁 0) such that
ρ(Fr𝓁0)≡C (mod 2Mθ ), (Dw/𝓁0) = 1, (h∗/𝓁0) =−1, v 2λMθ (Q) = cw <M θ. (3.7)
Here λMθ =bκMθ is the actual ﬁnite-ﬁeld Kummer map on E(F𝓁20). It has Frobenius sign +1. Allreductions use that same place. This is a ﬁxed-witness statement, not a bound for arbitrary points
or arbitrary reduction primes.
The weighted ternary theta construction gives actual holomorphic forms at these stages. At
original level primes the root weight uses the summand selected by the cyclic level structure. At
two, the integral spectral projectors of (1 +x)/2 specify that summand; the operator itself is not in
general idempotent. The nilpotent weight at h∗ and the division-algebra residue-line weight at 𝓁0
have the prescribed conjugation and uniformizer signs. Their product is even. The root-character
ﬁeld is ﬁxed before 𝓁0 and Mθ.
Appendix F gives these functions, their exact transformation rules, and the direct extraction
using the form Q/m. Poisson summation for the resulting compactly supported local functions gives
the scalar transformation law and holomorphy at every cusp. A ﬁxed stabilizer multiple, for example
one divisible by| GL4(F3)|, clears the ﬁnite automorphism groups. For the Hecke recurrence, a split
or irreducible nonzero reduction has 1 + (−n/p) invariant lines, a nonzero nilpotent has one, and
zero has p + 1. The unique integral primitive nilpotent neighbor supplies the remaining square
term. Tracking the scaling characters gives the recurrence with the same character convention as
the coeﬃcient comparison.
For an admissible D with (D/𝓁0) = 1 , the oriented embedding count [ 62, Theorem 3.1], with
changed set{𝓁0}, gives
aMθ (D) = c0 2ω(N )ζMθ (D)λMθ (LP genD ) (mod 2 Mθ ), (3.8)
where c0⁄= 0 is ﬁxed and ζ is a permitted root-of-unity phase. For admissible D with (D/𝓁0)⁄= 1,
including 𝓁0| D, the local Schwartz support instead gives aMθ (D) = 0 . Formula (3.8) is asserted
only on the ﬁrst support. There is no division by a class number. Formula (3.7) makes Dw visible
with ﬁxed upper valuation in (3.8). It remains on every stage’s support, including the extra test
(Dw/𝓁0) = 1 .
After phase removal, the coeﬃcients used for valuation are integers. Equation (3.6) gives a
uniform lower bound for v2aMθ (D)−ω(D) on visible bounded-weight sequences. Take the ﬁnite
integer values constant on a set belonging to the chosen nonprincipal ultraﬁlter. This set of values
is nonempty by Dw, is bounded below, and hence has an attained minimum δ. If the resulting lower
inequality failed for a ﬁxed weight bound on an ultraﬁlter-large set, choosing a violating index and
then one of ﬁnitely many smaller integer values would contradict minimality. For any ﬁxed ﬁnite
operator budget, the coarse lower bound handles the large weights and stabilization handles the
bounded weights. Consequently every required division is valid at one suﬃciently advanced actual
stage. No inﬁnite-precision modular form or arbitrary-ring base-change assertion is needed.
397.6 One common family and the ﬁnal order of choices
The even source is a ﬁxed algebraic vector in the full good-Hecke eigenspace. Waldspurger’s Corol-
lary 2, including its same-dyadic-squareclass removal of the auxiliary condition, compares the actual
squarefree Fourier functionals [ 63]. Local upper-unipotent projections retain a nonzero initial func-
tional; smoothness supplies lower-unipotent invariance. The algebraic linear conditions choose a
vector over a number ﬁeld. Galois conjugation followed by the matching ﬁnite character twist shows
that each normalized coeﬃcient ratio is a rational number times a two-power root of unity. This
supplies the valuation and nonvanishing comparison on the whole ﬁlter. Only ﬂat ﬁeld extension of
the algebraic model is used. The comparison is proved at the original support S0 before any extra
coprimality sieve B. The sieve is applied last, and every later good recurrence prime avoids the full
support S0∪B.
For both coeﬃcient systems the integral operators have the required omission and contraction
rules. At a new prime Vp raises the level exponent only to one, with ramiﬁed quadratic character.
The recurrence makes the relevant diﬀerences divisible by the integer two before residue reduction.
Multiplying by ordinary theta, then taking a fresh trace, removes the integral unary corrections.
Two fresh traces vanish. The matrix trace identity gives t(gu2) = t(g), hence elementary quadratic
labels. At a new odd level prime the weight-two local representation has one unramiﬁed and one
ramiﬁed line. The normalized-induction and Whittaker calculation below identiﬁes the Fourier Up
eigenvalue with arithmetic Frobenius on the unramiﬁed line. The diagonal-matrix calculation gives
the two inertia diﬀerences. These identities prove omission and primary isolation for the same
original form and depth, without choosing another minimum.
If the intrinsic odd limit symbol is a unit at the empty conﬁguration, choose an actual stage
carrying that value with Mθ >δ o. The index D = 1 lies in every moving support and then satisﬁes
v2aMθ (1) = δo < Mθ. The visibility implication (O.8) gives ords=1L(E,s ) = 1 , which is exactly
(RC). No ﬁnal companion or determinant bound is needed in this case. For the rest of the ﬁnal-
family and missing-address argument, assume that the empty conﬁguration is not a unit. The
bounded-weight witness then supplies a least nonempty odd seed, as in Appendix G.
Fixing the ﬁnal companion. Use a separate even symbol on negative discriminants square
at 2N , excluding the ﬁxed odd support, the ramiﬁcation support of E[8], and the exceptional
imaginary ﬁelds. Choose a unit k of this symbol. Then L(E(k), 1)⁄= 0 . Fix k and K = Q(√k)
before choosing the binary dimension. At least one prime ramiﬁed in K is outside all the old ﬁxed
label extensions and torsion ramiﬁcation. Choose every later moving theta prime 𝓁0 away from k.
The intrinsic odd symbol continues to omit the primes of k.
At any such stage, the normal closure of the old odd label extension is unramiﬁed outside its
old support and 𝓁0. The fresh ramiﬁed prime of K lies outside this set, so its intersection with K
is Q. Thus each old odd label still has a lift split in K. The added stationary tests at the primes
of k may have relations; include those relations in the common alphabet, rather than requiring
independent arbitrary prescriptions. Stabilize its separate projection maps on the same ultraﬁlter.
This preserves the old odd minimum. Fresh ramiﬁcation also makes K independent of Q(E[8]) and
of the old ﬁelds Q(i) and Q(

The forward theorem at this known nonzero central value gives s2(E(k)/Q) = 0 . The sum-and-
diﬀerence isogeny over Q gives
s2(E/K ) = s2(E/Q) +s2(E(k)/Q) = 1.
Indeed its kernel and cokernel on the relevant restriction and corestriction maps are killed by a
ﬁxed power of two. Tensoring the compact Selmer modules, equivalently the Pontryagin duals of

Lemma 7.16 (The nonempty seed for the present curve) . ForE =E𝓁 and the k,K just chosen,
the positive even source for E(k), normalized at index one, has a nonempty unit seed consisting of
K-split primes.
Proof. This is the specialization to the roots 0,𝓁,−3𝓁 of the coeﬃcient calculation in Proposition G.4.
If no such nonempty unit existed, its integral p3 coeﬃcient and a fresh trace would force ap(E(k))≡
p + 1 (mod 8) at every fresh good K-split prime outside the support, whether or not it lies in the
coeﬃcient ﬁlter. Indeed, oﬀ the ﬁlter a(p) = 0 in (G.4a); the second fresh prime p′ matches the old
labels so that pp′ lies in the ﬁlter, as used in (G.4d). The selected p below need not lie in the ﬁlter.
The cited calculation uses positive Fourier indices and the positive twist character convention; it
derives this congruence inside the assumed absence of nonempty units.
Choose such a prime with p ≡ 1 (mod 4) , (𝓁/p) = −1 and (k/p) = 1 , avoiding the ﬁnite
support. In fact this p is outside the square ﬁlter: quadratic reciprocity and p≡ 1 (mod 4) give∏
r|𝓁(p/r) = ( 𝓁/p) = −1, so p is a nonsquare at some odd r | 𝓁, and r | N . This is why the
oﬀ-ﬁlter scope of the preceding calculation is needed. The fresh ramiﬁcation of K permits these
simultaneous conditions. Because k is a square modulo p, the reductions of E(k) and E𝓁 are
isomorphic over Fp. None of the three nonzero rational points of order two can be doubled: their
necessary square diﬀerences include, respectively, −𝓁,𝓁,−4𝓁. Each is a nonsquare under the chosen
conditions. The two-primary reduction group therefore has order exactly four, contradicting the
asserted congruence. This proof uses the particular curve E𝓁; no nonempty-seed theorem for an
arbitrary elliptic curve is being asserted.
We record the ﬁnite combinatorial realization used here. Each label space is a ﬁnite elementary
two-group; the common alphabet projects onto each separately. Product surjectivity is unnecessary.
Take suﬃcient repeated copies of every label, with total label zero. Keeping the seed anchors,
contract the unused copies into the last anchor. Minimal positive seed size makes the internal bits
irrelevant, so each of the two terminal polynomials is nonzero. Grounded Laplacian minors express
the constant and pair-contraction operators, including singular matrices. A cyclic singular network
isolates its target radical and a path network isolates its selected pair coeﬃcient. In the product
of the two top forms, taken in the ordinary polynomial ring before Boolean reduction, a variable
of exponent two uses one singular program in each factor; exponent one uses one varying program.
The total degree kills the other contractions and leaves coeﬃcient one. Thus one choice works
for both forms. CRT and Dirichlet realize the prescribed forward residue bits by distinct primes;
quadratic reciprocity supplies the reverse bits.
