8 Actual classes, local conditions and central comparison
LetK = Q(
√
k) be imaginary, split at 2N , and diﬀerent from Q(i) and Q(√−3). Active primes are
good odd primes split in K. Derivative primes are good inert primes outside all current supports.
The actual ﬁnite coeﬃcients are
Ai = (Z/2iZ)[t]/ti, M i = (T2E⊗Ai)(χhΨi).
For a ﬁxed binary group G, use Ai[G] and the universal quadratic character. The character Ψi
is ﬁnite, trivial at 2N , ramiﬁed at a moving conductor prime ri, and has prescribed odd scalar
exponents at the ﬁnite active list. In a binary family let Q be the union of its prime supports.
Apply Proposition 8.5 to an orientation above every q∈ Q, prescribing ξi(Frq) = 1 at each ﬁnite
stage. This prescription includes the primes unused by an individual character, including every
graph prime at augmentation. The same Ψi is used for all characters of the family. The ﬁnite set
Q is ﬁxed before choosing the sequence of moving primes ri; the trace bound cE does not depend
on its size. The prime ri varies with precision. There is no assertion that one ﬁxed odd prime
supports an inﬁnitely ramiﬁed Z2 character.
Deﬁnition 8.1 (Coeﬃcient and local-condition conventions) . In Sections 8 and 9, retain the ﬁxed
non-CM curve E, parametrization π, Tate lattice, and old local types of Section 7. Put Λ = Z2[[t]]
andO = ˆΛ(2). A binary family uses one ﬁnite elementary abelian two-group G, one real sign and
common local squareclasses at 2N . Its universal varying quadratic character is therefore trivial at
the real place and at 2N . The active primes are good odd primes split in the indicated imaginary
quadratic ﬁeld K; the moving character is the ﬁnite character speciﬁed above, trivial at 2N . The
primitive and relaxed complexes use the actual derived Kummer, unramiﬁed, full and orthogonal
local conditions speciﬁed below, with their maps and homotopies. At derivative primes the four
local conditions are those of Lemma 9.5. Every determinant coordinate uses the retained integral
determinant basis.
Lemma 8.2 (Conjugate coeﬃcients and local duality) . For the ﬁnite coeﬃcient module Mi and
coeﬃcient ring Ri = Ai or Ai[G] above, let τ be an actual complex conjugation. Pullback of the
Galois action gives an Ri-linear identiﬁcation τ ∗Mi≃ M ∗i (1) through (v,w )↦→ e2(v,ρT (τ )w); it
ﬁxes t and the group-ring basis. With the conjugate places, actual orthogonal local conditions and
their isotropy homotopies, ﬁnite Selmer duality gives
CU (Mi)≃R HomRi(CU ⊥(M ∗i (1)),Ri)[−3].
The local Kummer and derivative comparisons used here are proved below and in Lemma 9.5.
Proof.
42The dual coeﬃcient system. Fix an actual complex conjugation τ∈GQ, so that τ 2 = 1. The
moving character is constructed on the ring-class group:
ξi : Gal(Hri/K)−→ Z/2niZ, Ψi = (1 +t)ξi,
where ni is large enough that (1 +t)2ni = 1 in Ai. Conjugation acts on this ring-class group by
inversion. Consequently
Ψi(τgτ −1) = Ψi(g)−1 (g∈GK).
LetRi =Ai in the scalar construction and Ri =Ai[G] in the binary construction. Write αi =χhΨi,
respectively αi = χ0εΨi, where ε : GK→ G is the restriction of the universal rational quadratic
character. Then ετ =ε =ε−1 and ατi =α−1i . Here τ ∗Mi denotes pullback of the Galois action; it
does not act on Ri.
The Weil pairing gives the following Ri-linear perfect pairing:
Mi⊗Riτ ∗Mi−→Ri(1), (v,w )↦−→e2(v,ρT (τ )w).
Indeed ρT (τ )2 = 1, the two scalar characters multiply to one, and e2 transforms by the cyclotomic
character. Thus τ ∗Mi≃ M ∗i (1). We do not identify Mi with its ordinary dual when Ψi is non-
quadratic. In this convention t and every element of the group-ring basis are ﬁxed. Inverting 1 +t
is already accounted for by pullback of the character.
At a split place v, the dual local condition is transported from the conjugate place τv . At a
ﬁxed place it is transported by the actual local representative of conjugation. Kummer conditions
at 2N are preserved by this transport. A full condition is paired with the zero condition; the full
local complex is replaced by zero only at the localizations where its displayed contraction exists.
At a derivative prime the ﬁnite and singular planes are checked with this transported pairing. In
the chosen coordinates this is the pairing β(x,y ) = ϵ⟨x,Jy⟩. The J is therefore part of the duality
convention, not an extra identiﬁcation of an arbitrary twist with its dual.
Use the coeﬃcient-valued pairing in the ﬁnite Selmer diagrams, with the conjugate local places
and their isotropy homotopies. The resulting duality is
CU (Mi)≃R HomRi(CU ⊥(M ∗i (1)),Ri)[−3].
These are the maps and homotopies retained in both limits.
8.1 Finite Heegner descent and the derivative identities at two
Fix the non-CM elliptic curve E/Q, its conductor N , its Tate module T = T2E, and a modular
parametrization π : X0(N )−→E. Normalize π(∞) = 0 . Let K be imaginary quadratic, split at

character. Its construction is separate from the descent proved here.
43Proposition 8.3 (Finite Heegner descent with one multiplier) . With E,π,K,f,I,M I,A and the

There is no multiplier depending on K,f,I,m , the truncation length, the number of active primes
or|G|.
Proof.

This bounds the whole invariant torsion group, with no restriction on the number of conductor

each of them.
For the matrix-span statement used elsewhere, square preimages of I + 2nE11, I + 2nE12 and
I + 2nE21. The squares belong to every quadratic subgroup, and their images, together with I, are
I, I + (2n+1 + 22n)E11, I + 2n+1E12, I + 2n+1E21.

I−E11. This bound also depends only on E.

yd𝓁 =F𝓁yd. (H4)
Here F𝓁 is rational arithmetic Frobenius on the reduced elliptic curve. This is the usual CM

(d,𝓁 ) = 1 makes the local CM order the maximal unramiﬁed quadratic order. The CM elliptic

also proves (H4) after every old ideal-class translate, with its place transported. Howard, Section

and residual-surjectivity theorem are not used here.

5. Integral local descent at the old places. We record the local fact needed to put da(cI,a )
in the old Kummer conditions. If F ′/F is a ﬁnite unramiﬁed extension of nonarchimedean local
ﬁelds and B/F is an elliptic curve, then
H 1(Gal(F ′/F ),B 0(F ′)) = 0, c BH 1(Gal(F ′/F ),B (F ′)) = 0, (H10)
wherecB = #ΦB(kF ) is the geometric component group order. To prove the ﬁrst assertion, reduce
a cocycle to the connected smooth special ﬁber. Lang’s theorem [ 80, Theorem 1 and its corollary]
makes its H 1 over the ﬁnite residue ﬁeld zero; inﬂation–restriction then makes its H 1 for the ﬁnite
residue extension zero. Lift the resulting coboundary through the smooth reduction map. The
remaining cocycle takes values in the formal group. For a uniformizer of F , ﬁxed by the unramiﬁed
Galois group, each successive formal quotient is an additive copy of the residue ﬁeld of F ′ [48,
IV.3.2(a), p. 124]. A normal basis makes this an induced module, so its positive-degree cohomology
vanishes. Correct the coboundary successively in these quotients. The corrections converge in
B1(F ′), and the group is ﬁnite, so their limit solves all the cocycle equations. The component exact
sequence proves the second assertion of (H10). No division by [F ′ :F ] was used.
If a class v∈H 1(F,B [2a]) becomes Kummer over F ′, its image in H 1(F,B )[2a] belongs to the
kernel of restriction. That kernel is the image of H 1(Gal(F ′/F ),B (F ′)). Thus cBv is Kummer over
F , by the Kummer exact sequence and (H10).
All ring-class extensions in use are unramiﬁed at 2N , since the conductors are prime to 2N .
The base ﬁelds there are unramiﬁed extensions of Kv = Qp. Geometric component groups are
unchanged under unramiﬁed extension. Let Cbad be a common multiple of their orders for the
ﬁnitely many allowed local quadratic types at 2N . The same integer works for every conductor,
every place above 2N and every K. Choose a ﬁxed positive integer m0, allowing the other ﬁxed
multiplier requirements in the argument to be included, and deﬁne
L =m0CπCbad22e, b I,a =m0Cbadda(cI,a )∈H 1(Hf,E [2a]). (H11)
Thus any common ﬁxed multiple of this descent multiplier and the detection multiplier is permitted;
every class is scaled by the same m0. Then bI,a is Kummer at 2N and restricts to δa(LDIyfn I ). At
a good place away from 2fnI , the same assertion has component order one. It gives the unramiﬁed
local condition, since at good odd places the ﬁnite Kummer image equals the unramiﬁed image. At
active and moving primes the required condition is full. The integer L is now ﬁxed, independently
of K,f,I,m,b and G.

sign character.

Localizing (H21) separates the cosets into decomposition-group orbits: this is the local Mackey
formula, a sum of the local corestrictions from the transported places. At 2N , the deformation
character Ψ is trivial locally; the ﬁxed quadratic character need not be trivial. Under the ﬁxed
quadratic-twist identiﬁcation, the local map is the ordinary integral norm on Kummer classes. One
can see this without a projector: the sum ∑δχ(δ)δQ is a point of the quadratic twist over the lower
ﬁeld, and Kummer of this sum equals the cohomological transfer. The extra A coeﬃcients take
48ﬁnite linear combinations of such maps. Thus the old Kummer conditions proved in paragraph 5
survive (H20), also in the universal family with its common local quadratic type. Local Kummer
corestriction is natural even for a ramiﬁed ﬁeld extension; in the present old-place descent the
extensions used in (H10) are unramiﬁed.
At a derivative prime, Hf/K splits completely and θ is trivial on the local Galois group. Each
Mackey term uses a transported place and a transported Frobenius. Transporting the Tate basis
transports F as well, so (H19) is equivariant, not an assertion that an arbitrary change-of-place
matrix commutes with a ﬁxed untransported J. The same weights occur on the upper and lower
class. Summing gives, in the common target coordinates,
f (PI∪{𝓁},m) = 0, s (PI∪{𝓁},m) = Jf (PI,m ). (4.2)
Active and moving conditions are full; all other good conditions are unramiﬁed and preserved by
the same maps.
The split norm formula in (4.1) also survives this construction. For a new conductor prime the
class-ﬁeld squares obtained by retaining the derivative factors are Cartesian, by (H5) and the same
local-unit calculation at that prime. Restriction and corestriction commute in these squares. If
b lifts 2ec in (H9), its corestriction lifts 2e times the corresponding corestricted class; uniqueness
after the second multiplication by 2e proves the required compatibility of da. The weighted sums
are transitive ﬁnite sums. Thus the two horizontal Artin terms in (4.1) become θ(σq) and θ(σq)−1,
givingaq−θ(σq)−θ(σq)−1 on the class. For I = ?, the class is precisely the Kummer image of the
L-multiplied weighted point, so the same statement gives the central split-norm relation with that
exact L.

8. Actual ﬁnite Selmer-cone witnesses. RepresentPI,m by a continuous global cocycle p. At
each constrained place, membership in the indicated local condition gives a local cycle uv and a
degree-zero cochain qv with
locvp−ιvuv =dqv. (H22)
These entries give a cycle in Cone(C•(GK,S,M )⊕⨁Uv→⨁C•(Kv,M ))[−1]. For (H19), choose
in the same way a degree-zero cochain whose boundary is the diﬀerence of the two representatives
being compared. At the derivative primes use the already speciﬁed D0-retaining ﬁnite/singular
local complexes, not full/zero conditions. Their coordinate equality is the ﬁnite local cohomology
equality just proved. All coeﬃcient groups and coset sets at this stage are ﬁnite; the cocycles and
the ﬁnite list of witnesses factor through ﬁnite quotients.
Apply the coeﬃcient push to these witnesses at the same time as to the cocycle, including the
natural Kummer comparison homotopies. If (i,p,h ) is a retained contraction to a ﬁnite target
model, a relation z−z′ =dq becomes pz−pz′ =d(pq). For a comparison F to another model with
projectionp′, the correction between p′Fz andp′Fipz is the boundary of p′Fhz (with the consistent
contraction sign). Thus the ﬁnite arithmetic provides actual cycles, boundaries and comparison
entries before compression. Adding a derivative prime extends this same ﬁnite diagram. Existence
and size bounds for those target contractions, and the subsequent two limits, are separate inputs;
this proof makes no inference from abstract isomorphism of cohomology groups to those bounds.
The resulting ﬁnite arithmetic uses the single multiplier (H11) and the precision rule a≥m +e,
(H12). No factor depending on [Hf :K],|G|,|I| or the number of active primes occurs.
8.2 The derived Kummer condition
Lemma 8.4 (The actual derived Kummer condition) . For the actual local twist B = E(h) over
F = Kv, the condition UF = B(F )∧2 [−1]→ RΓ(F,T 2B) includes the ﬁnite two-primary torsion.
50Its reduction modulo 2a has B(F )[2a] in degree zero and B(F )/2aB(F ) in degree one. Its Kummer
image is its exact local annihilator, and the comparison has integral isotropy homotopies. At v| 2N ,
triviality of the moving character and the common old quadratic type identify the scalar and binary
versions by derived coeﬃcient extension. These are the local maps used in the Selmer-cone duality
above.
Proof. For the actual local twist B =E(h) overF =Kv, put
UF =B(F )∧2 [−1]−→RΓ(F,T 2B).
The completion includes ﬁnite two-primary torsion. Derived reduction modulo 2a has B(F )[2a] in
degree zero and B(F )/2aB(F ) in degree one. At v| 2N , Ψi is trivial, so derived coeﬃcient extension
deﬁnes the scalar Ai condition for this actual twist. In the universal binary family every hx has
the same local squareclasses at 2N as h0 = 1 . Hence every sign character evaluates the universal
character ε trivially on GKv , and therefore ε|GKv = 1 in G, before reduction modulo two. The
Ai[G] Kummer condition and its null-homotopy are consequently derived scalar extensions of the
ﬁxed local data. Omitting the scalar twist, the universal local-squareclass condition, or degree-zero
torsion changes this argument.
The local ﬁnite dualities [ 42, I.2.3, I.3.2–I.3.5] make the Kummer map adjoint to H 1(F,B [2a])→
H 1(F,B )[2a]. The latter has Kummer image as kernel, so the Kummer image is its exact annihilator.
To obtain the integral chain statement, write M = B(F )∧2 . The restricted cup map M [−1]⊗L
M [−1]→ Z2[−2] has homotopy class determined by Hom(M⊗M, Z2). Finite isotropy makes
every value divisible by every power of two, hence zero. A projective resolution therefore supplies
a null-homotopy. The possible ambiguity group in degree minus one vanishes. Reduction modulo
two, including the torsion in degree zero, gives the perfect quotient-to-dual comparison; derived
Nakayama lifts it. The global Selmer duality theorem [ 44, Sections 5.1, 5.2.4, 5.4.3 and 6.3.4] is
applied to these actual local maps and homotopies.
For clarity the Selmer complex is the cone

Frobenius ring’s perfection is not used as a coeﬃcient-reduction-compatible arithmetic trace.
8.3 Actual support comparisons
At a tame split prime the local free model has
d0(v) = (Av,Cv ), d 1(v,w ) =−Cv +Dw, A = Φ− 1, C =g− 1, D = Φ−Nq(g),
Nq(g) = q + 12 + q− 12 g, DC =CA. (4.4)
At augmentation g = 1 , this is the direct sum of the unramiﬁed complex [Φ− 1] in degrees zero
and one and the singular quotient [Φ−q] in degrees one and two.
The support-change theorem [ 44, Proposition 7.8.8(ii)] retains arbitrary old local conditions
and puts the unramiﬁed condition at new places. Its ﬁnite-module proof applies at two. Use it
only when the actual coeﬃcient representation is unramiﬁed at the removed place. There are ﬁve
uses here: universal augmentation at Q; an unused scalar prime q - h; characteristic-two residual
removal after χh = 1; the moving prime after t = 0; and a new good inert derivative prime outside
51h,ri and the old support. In the last case the coeﬃcient module is unramiﬁed and the chosen
ﬁnite local plane is the unramiﬁed condition. It is not applied to an active ramiﬁed scalar prime
or to ri before central specialization. Replacing the unramiﬁed condition by the full condition then
gives the singular quotient by a cone of Selmer cones. In particular the old complex is an actual
subcomplex of an upper triangular model, not just a complex with the same Euler characteristic.

8.4 The same functional at every localization
Proposition 8.6 (The paired functional and its integral representatives) . Use the retained inner-
limit diagrams of Lemma 9.1, with the local conditions of Deﬁnition 8.1, and let z be the actual
retained class. Transport z by conjugation, lift it through the orthogonal comparison at the generic
point, and pair by the retained duality. This deﬁnes one generic closed degree-two functional λz.
It has an integral representative after multiplication by 2a overO[G], for a ﬁxed old-support ex-
ponent a independent of the active list and G. For a scalar constituent with its primitive local
conditions, its paired functional has an integral representative at every height-one prime p⁄= (2) of
Λ, including (t) and (t + 2). These representatives have the same generic value for that primitive
constituent. The universal relaxed functional is asserted integral after the ﬁxed power of two over
O[G]; scalar regularity of its corrected coordinate is obtained instead from the actual primitive com-
parison of Lemma 8.11. All these assertions concern the inner limit and the indicated localizations,
not inverses of t in a ﬁnite Artin ring.
54Proof. After the inner limit, put O = \Z2[[t]](2). In (4.4), the residual active Frobenius is (1 +t)aI
with a odd. Thus A,D are invertible overO[G]. The formulas
h1(v,w ) = A−1v, h 2(u) = (0,D −1u)
contract the complex; the identities follow from DC =CA.
Which functional is paired with the class. Let z denote the retained class and transport it
by τ and the preceding Weil identiﬁcation to the conjugate coeﬃcient system. At a localization
where the full local complexes at active and moving primes are acyclic, the comparison from the
orthogonal condition to the transported condition is a quasi-isomorphism. Apply its inverse in the
derived category to the transported class, and call the resulting class z⊥. Deﬁne
λz(w) = PD(w,z ⊥), w ∈H 2(CU ).
Thusλz is a closed degree-two functional, with a cochain representative obtained using the displayed
local contractions and the retained global duality map. The inverse is unique in the derived category,
so representatives built at distinct localizations deﬁne the same generic functional. Integrality still
requires the stated integral contractions and ﬁxed old-support correction; it does not follow from
abstract duality alone. The coordinate deﬁnition below uses this same λz.
Here the representatives must be constructed separately. Over O[G], the active Frobenius
operators A,D are units, and moving-prime inertia minus one is t times a unit; the displayed
homotopies are integral. For the height-one assertion take the scalar primitive constituent. Its
active primes are precisely the primes ramiﬁed in that constituent, so inertia minus one is −2, a
unit at p⁄= (2) . At the moving prime inertia minus one is invertible unless p = (t); at (t) the
nonzero central Frobenius determinants give integral adjugate contractions. In particular (t + 2)
is treated by the inertia contraction. Unused good primes in the primitive constituent retain the
unramiﬁed condition and its orthogonal comparison; no inverse of a full local Frobenius complex at
such a prime is required. This proves the scalar assertion only for the primitive functional. In the
universal relaxed family an unused prime can specialize to trivial inertia, so the −2 contraction is
not available there. Its correction is the determinant identity of Lemma 8.11. The common generic
value alone would not prove any of these integrality statements.
In the inertia contraction the maps are
h1(v,w ) = C−1w, h 2(u) = (−C−1u, 0).
The ﬁxed old-support lattice and the exponent. Here is the integral content of the old-
support comparison. For each p| 2N and each of the ﬁxed allowed local quadratic types δ, choose
once a bounded free Z2-model of the diagram
Up,δ−→Lp,δ, U p,δ = (E(δ)(Qp)∧2 )[−1], L p,δ =RΓ(Qp,T 2E(δ)),
together with its Kummer map, cup pairing, isotropy homotopy, and quotient-to-dual comparison.
Finite torsion is retained in Up,δ; it is represented by a free resolution, not removed. The local
duality and Kummer exact sequence identify that comparison integrally:
Cone(Up,δ→Lp,δ)≃R HomZ2(Up,δ, Z2)[−2].
This is the chain-level comparison of Lemma 8.4, with its speciﬁed isotropy homotopy. In particular
it is not a rational isomorphism of free parts of cohomology. A quasi-isomorphism between these
55bounded free models has an integral homotopy inverse: its bounded acyclic free cone is contractible,
by splitting from its last nonzero term and proceeding downwards. Consequently neither torsion
lengths nor inverse determinants of torsion diﬀerentials are denominators in this comparison.
Every allowedK splits at 2N . Its two completions above p are therefore Qp, with precisely two
copies of this same diagram, interchanged by conjugation. The moving character and the universal
varying quadratic character are trivial on their decomposition groups. All old-place diagrams over
Ai[G] are thus derived scalar extensions of this ﬁnite ﬁxed list. Tensoring the chosen free models and
their homotopy identities preserves all these identities. The remaining good unramiﬁed conditions
use the integral unramiﬁed comparison, and the derivative conditions use their integral orthogonal
planes. Neither requires a new lattice comparison when a prime is added.
For completeness, there is also no hidden group-order denominator in duality. The coeﬃcient
pairing is the Ai[G]-valued Weil pairing. If one checks its perfection over Ai, the auxiliary pairing
on the group algebra is
(a,b )↦−→ [1](ab), ([1](gh))g,h∈G = (1h=g−1)g,h∈G.
Its matrix is a permutation matrix, with integral inverse. It is not the normalized trace |G|−1 Tr.
The global cup-product and trace maps are integral as well. The hypotheses of Selmer duality here
are a perfect coeﬃcient pairing and the actual orthogonal local diagrams with their isotropy homo-
topies [44, Sections 6.2.5–6.2.8 and Theorem 6.3.4]; the old Kummer diagrams just described supply
those hypotheses. Since K is imaginary there is no real-place Tate correction in this argument.
Over S =O[G] the only remaining diﬀerences between the transported conditions and their
orthogonal conditions occur at the active and moving places. Their full local complexes have
the integral contractions displayed above. Their direct sum, of any ﬁnite size, is still an integral
contraction. The resulting comparison j : CU ⊥→ τ ∗CU is an integral homotopy equivalence over
S. Write j−1 for such a homotopy inverse and c for conjugate transport. On the retained bounded
free models,
z⊥ =j−1c(z), λ z = PD(−,j −1c(z))
are integral cochains, and the second is closed. Every map in this formula is integral; no inverse
on a torsion cohomology quotient is taken. The ﬁnite-model contractions and their retained limits
preserve these identities. In detail, represent the comparison on the bounded free models and
contract its acyclic cone over S. The cone contraction yields a chain map j−1 and homotopies
jj −1≃ 1 andj−1j≃ 1 overS. Thus d(j−1c(z)) = j−1c(dz) = 0 as a cochain identity, and its image
diﬀers from c(z) by an integral boundary. Applying the integral duality chain map gives a closed
element of HomS(C2U,S ), with the claimed generic value. This proves the existence of an actual
integral closed lift, rather than only a lift after rationalization.
With the conventions of this paper the actual class z already contains the ﬁxed multiplier L.
Thus the preceding construction permits a = 0 . One may keep any larger ﬁxed nonnegative a
in the subsequent inequalities. This does not change z, divide out L, or change the determinant
basis. It proves the existence and independence of the exponent from G, the active graph, and the
allowed varyingK; a numerical computation of a new denominator is unnecessary because no such
denominator occurs in these integral diagrams. This conclusion concerns S. The separate primitive
height-one argument above, and the corrected relaxed coordinate comparison, retain exactly their
stated scopes.
Deﬁnition 8.7 (The class-functional coordinate) . For a constituent whose generic cohomology has
dimensions (1, 1) in degrees one and two and vanishes otherwise, use the determinant line
D = det −1C≃H 1(C)⊗H 2(C)∗.
56The class-functional coordinate u is the coordinate of [z]⊗ [λ]∈D in the retained integral deter-
minant basis. Here z is the actual class and λ is the closed functional just constructed. For a
generically acyclic constituent set u = 0 . The primitive and relaxed subscripts specify the corre-
sponding local conditions on C.

Kummer local objects at these changed odd places are also rationally zero: local points have ﬁnite

Thus the full/Kummer quotients are rationally acyclic as well. At the ﬁxed old places the Kummer
maps and their duality homotopies are retained unchanged.

Lemma 8.9 (Determinant specialization with no dimension jump) . LetA be a discrete valuation
ring with uniformizer t, fraction ﬁeld F , and residue ﬁeld k. Let C be a bounded ﬁnite free complex
over A. If
dimFHi(C⊗AF ) = dimkHi(C⊗Ak) for every i,
then its cohomology is free and commutes with reduction modulo t. In particular, when the only
nonzero dimensions are (1, 1) in degrees one and two, the coordinate of an actual cycle and an
actual closed functional in det−1C is regular at t = 0 and specializes to the coordinate of their
central tensor in the specialized retained determinant basis.
Proof. Smith reduction splits the bounded free complex into free summands with zero diﬀerential
and two-term summands [A tru−−→A], whereu is a unit and r≥ 0. A summand with r> 0 contributes
one extra residue-ﬁeld cohomology dimension in each of two adjacent degrees and contributes none
overF . The dimension equalities therefore exclude every such summand. The remaining two-term
summands are contractible over A. On the zero-diﬀerential summands both cohomology and the
class-functional tensor commute with base change. The determinant identiﬁcations include the
same contractible summands before and after reduction, with their unit diﬀerentials; there is no
independent rescaling of the retained basis.

58Proof. First we justify the specialization of the generic coordinate. The forward theorem,
Lemma 5.5, gives rank one and ﬁnite Sha for the ﬁrst twist and rank zero and ﬁnite Sha for its

this forces a nonzero generic kernel. Semicontinuity bounds its dimension by one. The Euler
characteristic is zero, so the generic dimensions are also (1, 1). Lemma 8.9 now identiﬁes the
specialization of the generic coordinate with the central tensor computed in Lemma 8.8, in the
same retained determinant basis. This step uses neither the numerical calibration (4.6) nor the
later uniform determinant bound.

possible divisible part of Sha.

Lemma 8.11 (Unused-prime comparison and one corrected coordinate) . At an unused good split

their homotopies are retained. At q these are the maps from the unramiﬁed condition to the full
condition and from zero to the unramiﬁed condition. Naturality of the cup pairing [ 44, Sections
6.4.1–6.4.3] gives
βR(v,iw ) = βP (jv,w ).

To see the unit assertion, retain the ﬁnite mapping-cone bases. Their comparison is a sign; replacing
them by the retained homotopy equivalent free models contributes only determinants of unit disks.
These are Ai-units. Their limits and inverse limits are Λ-units, and q−4 is one also. An arbitrary
O-unit would not suﬃce.
We check the integrality needed for the universal correction. The graph-wide prescription at
the start of this section applies also to this unused prime. Modulo the maximal ideal (2,IG) of
O[G], every group element and ﬁxed quadratic sign becomes one, and the scalar z becomes 1 +t.
Since E[2] is rational, aq is even at every good odd prime. Thus

61Simultaneous prescriptions and local lifts. We spell out why the preceding calculation ap-
plies to the whole graph. First choose its actual ﬁnite set Q by Proposition G.7. Its primes are
good, odd, split in K, and outside 2NDK. The theta prime has already been chosen and is an
exclusion. Choose one prime of K above each q∈Q. In Proposition 8.5, “active list” can be this
entire list of orientations: the construction uses only these splitting and avoidance properties, not
ramiﬁcation in any particular quadratic constituent.
Here is the compatibility check in its class-group presentation. Adjoin orientations above 2N
and auxiliary split primes generating Cl(K), all in distinct conjugate pairs. Assign weight one to
every member of Q and weight zero to the other primes. For the relation basis m(a) andαa =βa/βa
used there, a relation ∏aαbaa ∈K(µ2∞)×2n implies
2n|∑
a
bam(a)j (j corresponding to Q).
Indeed, a root belongs to a ﬁnite cyclotomic layer, which is unramiﬁed at each of these odd primes,
and its valuation is integral. Multiplying by the weights and summing proves that the prescribed
Kummer multipliers respect every relation. At a resulting split prime r, their power-residue values
therefore satisfy every relation in the presentation of Pic(Z +rOK). The resulting character has
value one at every chosen orientation in Q, value zero at 2N , and is surjective on the residue-ratio
kernel, hence on inertia at r. The conjugate orientation has the negative value because the product
ideal is rational principal. The radical-ﬁxing subgroup (4.4c) preserves all these prescriptions while
imposing the two trace bounds. Its level depends only on the Tate image. Enlarging Q consequently
changes the ﬁnite Chebotarev ﬁeld, but not cE.
Perform this construction separately at each coeﬃcient stage, excluding Q, the theta prime, the
old support and the previously chosen moving primes. Taking ni≥ 2i gives (1 +t)2ni = 1 in Ai,
so these are actual ﬁnite characters. They need not be restrictions of one character of GK: the
retained inner limit is the limit of the ﬁnite diagrams of Lemma 9.1. At each ﬁxed graph orientation
their values are already exactly 1 +t, so that identity survives every coeﬃcient reduction and that
limit. The norm identities (4.1), their coeﬃcient maps, and their boundary witnesses hold before
character evaluation. This is the same construction for all constituents. At t = 0 the moving
character is trivial, so its added prescriptions do not alter the central quadratic family.
There is a local-lift issue only for the universal quadratic character. Write gq∈G for its inertia
generator at q; thus x(gq) = (−1)λq(x). Fix a lift of arithmetic Frobenius at the chosen orientation
and deﬁne zq using that lift. The moving character is unramiﬁed there, and its value is 1 + t.
The ﬁxed quadratic character is unramiﬁed there also. Every other graph prime contributes an
unramiﬁed quadratic character at q. Changing the Frobenius lift can therefore only replace zq by
gqzq. For an inactive character x, x(gq) = 1 , and x(zq) is the actual unramiﬁed Frobenius scalar.
For an active character its value is immaterial to the correction, which is one. Thus we never assign
an unramiﬁed Frobenius eigenvalue to a ramiﬁed constituent.
This independence also holds inside R =O[G], before evaluation. Both choices of zq have
reduction 1 +t modulo (2,IG), so both corresponding eq are units. Put rq =lq/e2q andr′q =l′q/(e′q)2.
They agree in R/(gq− 1), hence (1 +gq)(r′q−rq) = 0 . Since R is two-torsion-free and rq = 1 + 2vq,
r′q = 1 + 2v′q, this gives
(1 +gq)(v′q−vq) = 0.
The correction 1 + (1 + gq)vq is consequently independent of the lift. Reversing the orientation
replaces zq byz−1q and leaves both eq and lq unchanged.
For clarity, write sq =zq +z−1q . Direct expansion gives
q2lq = (q− 1)2 +a2q−aq(q + 1)sq +qs2q, v q = q− 12q2e2q(−(q + 1)e2q + (q− 1)−sqeq).
62Every term in the second expression is integral in R because q is odd and eq is a unit. These
inverses are taken only after the inner limit and localization at (2), never in the ﬁnite ring Ai[G].
Reduction modulo (2,IG) giveseq =t2/(1 +t) and lq =t4/(1 +t)2. The latter identity also holds
at each sign evaluation modulo two. At augmentation the local two-place determinant is q4lq, so
its residual t-order is exactly four. Multiplying the correction factors over the ﬁnite set Q proves
the asserted universal integrality and all the characterwise identities simultaneously.
Lemma 8.12 (The unknown base has zero coordinate if its derivative vanishes) . Use the base
h0 = 1 in the ﬁnal ﬁxed- K construction, with L(E(k), 1)⁄= 0 and full two-primary Selmer corank
one for E/K . If L′(E/K, 1) = 0 , then uprim,0(0) = ~u0(0) = 0 for the same coordinates as above.
The primitive coordinate is regular at (t) by the local argument below, and its corrected relaxed
coordinate has the same property by Lemma 8.11. No ﬁnite Sha assertion at the base is required.
Proof. PutA = Λ (t), with residue ﬁeld Q2 and fraction ﬁeld F . Use the actual ﬁnite free primitive
complex and its retained class and functional over A. The latter is integral over A by Proposition 8.6.
The conductor-one height formula makes the speciﬁed point torsion when L′(E/K, 1) = 0 . Thus
its rational Kummer class vanishes, and the retained boundary (4.5) makes the specialization of
the actual cycle z zero. No new normalization of L is made here.
Full Selmer corank one gives central dimensions (1, 1) in degrees one and two and zero in every
other degree. This uses the full Selmer group, including a possible divisible part of Sha. Over
the DVR A, a bounded ﬁnite free complex decomposes, by successive Smith reductions, into free
zero-diﬀerential summands and two-term disks [A tr− →A], up to units. A disk with r> 0 contributes
one additional residue-ﬁeld cohomology class in each of its two adjacent degrees. Semicontinuity
and Euler characteristic therefore leave only two generic possibilities: the complex is acyclic, or its
dimensions are (1, 1) in degrees one and two.
In the acyclic case the speciﬁed coordinate is zero by deﬁnition. In the second case the generic
and central dimensions agree in every degree. Lemma 8.9 applies: no disk with r >0 occurs. All
cancellable disks have unit diﬀerential, so integral determinant base change identiﬁes the coordinate
of [z]⊗ [λz] with the coordinate of its central tensor, in the retained determinant basis. That
tensor is zero because [z]0 = 0 . This proves regularity at (t) and uprim,0(0) = 0 directly. Finally
~u0 = ε0uprim,0 with ε0∈ Λ× by Lemma 8.11, so the corrected coordinate is also regular and zero
at the center. This proof uses neither the numerical lower bound of Proposition 9.6 nor ﬁnite Sha
at the unknown base.
9 The two limits and determinant transfer
This section uses the actual diagrams of Section 8. Their cohomology ranks alone would not
suﬃce: maps to original cochains, local comparisons, cup products, classes and boundary witnesses
are retained together. The same retained data include the integral duality maps, their homotopy
inverses, and the homotopies expressing compatibility of duality with localization.
9.1 Finite free models and the inner limit
Lemma 9.1 (Finite free models and retained inner-limit diagrams) . LetA be an Artin local ring
with nilpotent maximal ideal, and let a complex of ﬂat A-modules have ﬁnite bounded residual coho-
mology. This includes the actual continuous cochains with ﬁnite free coeﬃcient modules used here.
It admits a bounded ﬁnite free model with actual inclusion, projection and contraction homotopy.
63Every ﬁxed ﬁnite diagram of maps and homotopies, including the duality and local-condition data
used here, transports to these models.
For one ﬁxed active support and binary family, retain a tuple whose model ranks are bounded over
the ﬁnite coeﬃcient stages. One ﬁxed nonprincipal ultraﬁlter gives a diagram over Λ at all coeﬃcient
precisions, preserving its matrix identities and retained homotopy inverses. This assertion imposes
no rank bound uniform over all active supports.
Proof. Let A be an Artin local ring with nilpotent maximal ideal m. A ﬂat A-module is free,
including in inﬁnite rank: lift a basis modulo m, use nilpotence for surjectivity, and ﬂatness and
nilpotence for vanishing of the kernel. A continuous ﬁnite-coeﬃcient cochain module is a ﬁltered
union of modules of functions on ﬁnite clopen partitions, hence is ﬂat.
Suppose the residual cohomology of a complex of such modules is ﬁnite and bounded. Split
the residual complex into cohomology and disks, and lift graded bases. Let d0 be the lifted disk
diﬀerential andd =d0 +ϵ, where ϵ has entries in m. Use the graded decomposition into cohomology,
disk sources and disk targets. Let i0,p 0 include and project the cohomology summand, and let h0
invertd0 from disk targets to disk sources, zero on the other summands. Then
p0i0 = 1, h 20 =h0i0 =p0h0 = 0, 1−i0p0 =d0h0 +h0d0.
With this normalized contraction, the ﬁnite geometric series give
dC =p0ϵ(1 +h0ϵ)−1i0, i = (1 +h0ϵ)−1i0,
p =p0(1 +ϵh0)−1, h =h0(1 +ϵh0)−1.
Substitution using d2 = 0 givespi = 1 and 1−ip =dh+hd. The resulting bounded ﬁnite free model
carries actual maps and homotopies to the original cochains. Transport any ﬁxed ﬁnite collection
of arithmetic maps by f ↦→ p′fi . The identity 1−ip = dh +hd transports their compatibility
homotopies as well, including the second homotopies in the cup-product diagrams.
Fix one nonprincipal ultraﬁlter on coeﬃcient stages. Bounded ranks can be ﬁxed on a set in
that ultraﬁlter. Each ﬁnite tuple of matrix entries then has a compatible limit at every coeﬃcient
precision, giving matrices over Λ. Every polynomial identity between the matrices survives. Re-
tained homotopy inverses remain homotopy inverses. Mere stagewise quasi-isomorphisms without
those maps would not justify a nonﬂat coeﬃcient change.
Lemma 9.2 (The reduced generic residual rank bound) . FixE, an integer A≥ 0, and a number j
of derivative labels. For the ring instances with ω(k)≤A, reduction to characteristic two followed
by the stated support comparison and inversion of t gives a generic residual model of rank at most
N (E,A,j )≤ 56ω(2N ) + 8A + 56j + 70.
This bound is independent of the number and sizes of active primes and of v2(ri− 1). It bounds the
reduced generic model, not the whole retained support-comparison tuple before t is inverted.
Proof. All ranks in this proof are sums of the ranks in all degrees of one complex. They are not
the size of the diagram retaining all comparison maps. Put v = ω(2N ) and m = v + 1 + j. Let
S0 be the ﬁnite places of K above 2N , the moving prime, and the derivative primes. Let T be the
set of rational primes below S0, together with the primes ramiﬁed in K/Q, and put s =|T|. Since
2| 2N ,
s≤v +A + 1 +j. (5.1a)

impose additional Selmer conditions.
We ﬁrst give the global estimate at the residue ﬁeld Bi/(t) = F2, where Bi = F2[t]/(ti). The
residual coeﬃcient module is the trivial module W = E[2] = F22. Write ST for all ﬁnite places of

the exact sequence

Here 2∈T and µ2 = F2 as a Galois module. The unit term has dimension 1 +|ST|: the unit rank
is|ST| and the roots of unity contribute one dimension. Moreover
|ST|≤ 2s−r, dimF2 Cl(OK,ST )[2]≤r− 1. (5.1b)
For completeness, the class-group inequality follows directly from the imaginary quadratic genus
argument. Conjugation acts by inversion on the ideal class group. If [a] has order dividing two,
write a = (α)a. Taking ideal norms gives NK/Qα = 1 , since this norm is positive. Hilbert 90
gives α = β/ ¯β (take β = 1 + α unless α = −1, and take β = √k in that case), so βa is a
conjugation-invariant fractional ideal. Modulo rational principal ideals such ideals are generated
by the ramiﬁed prime ideals. Their classes have exponent two, and the principal ideal of

d, where

of a quotient of a ﬁnite abelian group cannot increase. This proves (5.1b), including the possible
ramiﬁcation at two. The bound on |ST| counts one prime over each ramiﬁed rational prime and at
most two over each other prime.
Inﬂation from GK,S0 intoGK,ST is injective on H 1 with trivial coeﬃcients. Consequently

Hence h1(GK,S0,W )≤ 4s + 2, giving total dimension at most 8s + 2. In either computation the
H 2 estimate is supplied by the global Euler characteristic, not by a generator estimate alone.

66For clarity, a universal binary character is not trivial merely after reduction modulo two. In

are the dimensions after reduction to F2((t)), so the same estimate bounds the scalar O-minimal
models used below.
9.2 Evaluation and the outer valuation ring
Lemma 9.3 (Evaluation, coeﬃcient change and the outer DVR) . For the retained global models and
for the primitive and derivative Selmer models, evaluations at sequences of Galois elements satisfy
(5.2). A cycle deﬁnes an abstract crossed homomorphism of the indicated product group. The
induced map on H 1 is injective, and the ﬁnite detecting-list comparison remains injective after ﬁeld
extension. Direct evaluation of other Selmer models is asserted only when their local maps induce
isomorphisms on residual H 0 and injections on residual H 1. For orthogonal zero/full comparisons,
the assertion may instead be transported at a test ring where the actual local comparison complexes
are contractible.
For a sequence of ring instances with ω(kj)≤ A and a ﬁxed number of derivative labels as in
Lemma 9.2, the quotient O∞ in (5.3) is a DVR with uniformizer two. The bounded minimal-model
matrices, evaluations, duality maps and their compatibility homotopies pass to it. Sequences whose
diﬀerentials tend to zero give actual crossed homomorphisms there. No continuity assertion on a
single original Galois group, or completeness of O∞, is required.
Proof. For ﬁxedK let ΓK =∏iGK and include one simultaneous complex conjugation. Evaluation
of retained cochain maps at arbitrary sequences g = (gi),h = (hi) gives
e1gd = (ρ(g)− 1)e0, e 2g,hd =ρ(g)e1h−e1gh +e1g. (5.2)
One ﬁxed ultraﬁlter supplies these identities for all sequences. A cycle therefore deﬁnes an abstract
crossed homomorphism of the product group. We do not assert that its restriction to one original
Galois group is a continuous pointwise limit.
Evaluation remains injective after ﬁeld extension. At each ﬁnite stage take representatives V
for H 1 and coeﬃcient space W . Evaluate (c,w ) by bc(g)− (ρ(g)− 1)w. The intersection of all
kernels is (0,W Γ). A ﬁnite list of at most dimV + dimW evaluations already has that intersection.
Choose and pad these lists at the ﬁnite stages before taking their common limit. The comparison
to [W→Wd] is an isomorphism on H 0 and injective on H 1. Its cone has no residual cohomology in
degrees at most zero. Cancel unit disks there so that the free cone starts in degree one; the same is
true after every coeﬃcient extension. The long exact sequence gives the claimed injection. For the
primitive and derivative Selmer complexes the required residual comparison with global cochains
follows from their actual local conditions. At an old Kummer place, derived reduction retains
B(F )[2] in degree zero, isomorphically onto the local invariants, and has the Kummer injection in
degree one. The unramiﬁed condition at a good place has the same degree-zero invariants as the
full local complex and injects in degree one. A full condition has the identity map. Every derivative
condition retains D0 and includes its stated degree-one subspace. The Selmer-cone exact sequence
therefore gives an isomorphism on global H 0 and an injection on global H 1.
67This does not apply directly to the zero conditions in the retained orthogonal complexes: at
(2,t ) their local degree-zero invariants need not vanish. Indeed, two zero local conditions with
residual invariant space W can contribute (W⊕W )/ diag(W ) to Selmer H 1, and global cochain
evaluation kills this contribution. Such orthogonal complexes are retained for duality, without
asserting residual evaluation injectivity. If an actual zero/full comparison is contractible at a
chosen test ring, its retained contraction identiﬁes the two Selmer models there and transports the
evaluation injection. This use requires that contraction at the chosen ring; it is not an assertion
for arbitrary unused relaxed primes at every scalar height-one localization.
Now allow Kj,hj to vary with ω(kj)≤A. For copies Oj ofO, form
O∞ =
(∏
U
Oj
)
/{(xj) : v2(xj)→U∞}. (5.3)
A nonzero element has valuation one ﬁxed nonnegative integer on a set in U and is that power of
two times a unit. Every nonzero ideal has an element of least valuation and is principal. Thus (5.3)
is a DVR with uniformizer two and characteristic-zero fraction ﬁeld. Completeness is unnecessary.
Integral minimal-model matrices and (5.2) pass to (5.3). So do the retained duality maps,
inverse homotopies and duality-to-localization compatibility homotopies. After minimal cancella-
tion an integral homotopy equivalence is degreewise invertible: its residual matrices are invertible,
and Nakayama applies. These inverse matrices and all compatibility identities pass to the same
outer quotient. Thus perfect duality and exact orthogonality are preserved there; they are not
inferred merely from isotropy. Approximate cycles suﬃce: if v2(dwj)→∞ , the degree-two term
in (5.2) tends to zero, so w∞ is an actual crossed homomorphism. The ﬁnite detecting lists have
bounded length by (5.1); retain their cone contractions to preserve injection. One must not replace
approximate cycles by unexplained exact cycles.
9.3 One new prime detects two classes
Lemma 9.4 (The common kernel and ﬁnite realization of a rank-two test) . Use the exact kernel
of sequences acting trivially on the full Tate module, the relevant ring-class ﬁelds and quadratic
characters. On the characteristic-zero tests used below, restriction of the evaluated cohomology to
this kernel is injective. When the moving scalar character is nonquadratic, two independent classes
have a rank-two localization at a single actual kernel element. Finite Chebotarev realizes the required
conjugation coset and basis-cochain evaluations, with exact inner-limit matrix identity (5.4) before
anyO-minimal-model denominators are applied.
At the residual test only scalar-inertia vanishing, bounded cohomology and ﬁnite detecting lists
are asserted. The quadratic character exception at characteristic-zero height-one tests is treated in
Proposition 9.6.
Proof. The exact kernel consists of sequences trivial on the full Tate module, all relevant ring-class
ﬁelds and quadratic characters. The scalar element of Section 8 is central modulo this kernel,
though not in the kernel itself. For a cocycle zero on the kernel,
(u4− 1)b(g) = (g− 1)b(z)
makes it a coboundary over characteristic zero. The same ﬁxed scalar survives the outer quotient.
To eliminate residual invariants, use moving-prime inertia: its scalar diﬀerence is t, invertible in
F2((t)). These are two separate arguments.
68On the characteristic-zero tests used for rank reduction, the ﬁxed ﬁnite matrix-span witness gives
absolute irreducibility of each rank-two summand. When the scalar character is not quadratic, mov-
ing inertia has distinct characters on the two summands and simultaneous conjugation exchanges
them. The induced rank-four representation is therefore absolutely irreducible with scalar endo-
morphisms. The outer fraction ﬁeld is one such characteristic-zero test; the quadratic character
exception at an inner test is treated separately below.
For two independent cohomology classes on these tests, the span of their joint values on the exact
kernel projects onto both copies of this absolutely irreducible representation. A proper submodule
with both projections onto would be the graph of a scalar isomorphism, contradicting independence.
Thus the span is the full sum. If the determinant of their two ﬁnite localizations vanished on
every actual kernel element, additivity and polarization would make it vanish on the entire span,
impossible. Hence one actual element supplies a rank-two localization on these characteristic-zero
tests.
The polarization identity itself is also valid in characteristic two, but no residual irreducibility
or residual rank-two localization is asserted. Here E[2] is trivial of rank two, so its scalar twist
is isotypic and the induced residual representation has multiplicity two. At the residual test we
use only scalar-inertia vanishing of H 0, the bounded residual cohomology dimensions, and ﬁnite
detecting lists from intersections of linear evaluation kernels. Those lists need not consist of one
rank-two localization. The rank-two selection for the uniform valuation bound occurs only after
passage to the characteristic-zero fraction ﬁeld of the outer DVR.
Chebotarev is applied at ﬁnite stages, not to the product group. Each of the ﬁnitely many
original basis cochains in degrees zero through two factors through a ﬁnite quotient. Include
their quotient, the required Tate precision, ring-class and quadratic ﬁelds and old diagrams in one
compositum. The element just found, multiplied by conjugation, is a single actual automorphism
of that compositum. Its restrictions agree on intersections. Choose the new prime and a place
above it to realize that representative.
This also resolves the precision issue caused by localization. Choose the new prime to match
all original basis-cochain evaluations at a common retained precision n(i)→∞ . Taking the same
inner ultraﬁlter gives an exact equality of Λ-matrices
Lj =Eσ2j. (5.4)
Only afterward compose with the inclusion Ij from an O-minimal model: LjIj = Eσ2jIj. The
denominators in Ij may depend on j; equality (5.4) makes this irrelevant. Long t-jets are never
treated as approximations in the two-adic valuation of O, where t is a unit.
9.4 Switches and the varying-companion estimate
Lemma 9.5 (Derivative local conditions and determinant-preserving switch) . At a derivative prime
use Lv = D0⊕ (Fv⊕Sv)[−1]⊕D2[−2] with the transported pairing, and retain D0 in all four
conditions. Take Ustr =D0, Urel =D0⊕ (Fv⊕Sv)[−1], UF =D0⊕Fv[−1], and US =D0⊕Sv[−1].
Then strict and relaxed are orthogonal, the two plane conditions are self-orthogonal, and their cone
triangles are the ones below.
Over the stated fraction or test ﬁeld, suppose the actual old class spans generic H 1 and has
nonzero ﬁnite localization v. Its derivative from Proposition 8.3 has ﬁnite coordinate zero and
singular coordinate Jv . The strict generic H 1 is zero, the new generic H 1 is spanned by this
derivative, and the two class-functional determinant coordinates have equal valuations, even when
v is not primitive in the local integral lattice.
Proof.
69The strict and relaxed conditions at a derivative prime. The words strict and relaxed
have a diﬀerent local meaning at a derivative prime from zero and full at an active or unused split
prime. At a derivative prime use the actual split local model
Lv =D0⊕ (Fv⊕Sv)[−1]⊕D2[−2]
with zero diﬀerential at the retained precision, and retain its pairing and isotropy homotopies. Here
D0 and D2 are dual, and Fv,Sv are the ﬁnite and singular isotropic planes. Put
Ustr =D0, Urel =D0⊕ (Fv⊕Sv)[−1],
UF =D0⊕Fv[−1], U S =D0⊕Sv[−1].
Thus U ⊥str = Urel and the two plane conditions are self-orthogonal under the transported duality.
All four maps retain local degree-zero cohomology. In particular, the relaxed condition here omits
D2[−2] and is not the full local complex. The actual cone triangles are consequently
Cstr−→CF−→Fv[−1]−→Cstr[1], C str−→CS−→Sv[−1]−→Cstr[1].
These are the triangles used in the determinant switch. Zero/full conditions at the other primes
retain their previously stated meaning. Exact annihilator and dimension assertions in the switch
argument are made after passage to the stated fraction or test ﬁeld, using these integral diagrams
and their retained dualities. At a new inert prime the ﬁnite and singular planes are free of rank
two, with cross pairing β(x,y ) = ε⟨x,Jy⟩. Suppose the old actual class A spans generic H 1 and
has f (A) = v ⁄= 0 . Its derivative B satisﬁes f (B) = 0 , s(B) = Jv . The strict H 1 vanishes.
The transported duality and localization identities identify the relaxed localization image with the
kernel of its dual localization map, hence with its exact annihilator [ 44, Sections 6.3.3–6.3.4]. This
image contains (v, 0) and (0,Jv ), so these span it. Hence the new generic H 1 is spanned by B.
The determinant comparison is exact even if v is divisible. In the fraction ﬁeld choose x withv∧x
of unit volume, and similarly y withJv∧y of unit volume. The ﬁnite and singular determinant exact
sequences have the same strict factor, so their class-functional coordinates have equal valuations.
All strict torsion remains in that common factor. Requiring x integral would be wrong when v is
divisible.
Proposition 9.6 (Uniform ring lower bound and height-one regularity) . Fix E,π , the old local
types and a bound A. For every ring instance satisfying the hypotheses of Proposition 8.10 and
ω(k)≤A, the actual primitive coordinate satisﬁes
v2uprim≥−C(E,A ), u prim∈ Λ[1/2],
where the ﬁrst valuation is that of O and the constant is independent of the active support and
companion ﬁeld. Consequently its central specialization and (4.6) imply (3.5).
The regularity assertion also holds at the unknown full-corank-one base used in Lemma 8.12.
The uniform numerical lower bound here is asserted only for the known simple-zero ring instances;
it is not asserted at that unknown base.
Proof. The starting generic kernel has rank one. Indeed, at a known simple zero the ordinary
class is nonzero. Equation (4.5), with its bounded nonzero multiplier, makes the retained central
class nonzero. Over Λ(t) cancel the degree-zero and degree-three disks. If the generic kernel of the
resulting two-term free complex vanished, the actual lifted cycle would vanish, contradicting its
central value. Semicontinuity bounds that kernel by the central dimension one.
70Suppose no uniform lower bound exists. Choose instances with v2uj→−∞ , ω(kj)≤ A, and
write their minimal completed models as [Onj Dj− − →Onj ], ﬁxing the bounded n on an outer-large set.
In each generic one-dimensional kernel choose a primitive vector vj. Its outer image is nonzero, so
the outer kernel dimension s is at least one. If s > 1, one new derivative prime gives rank-two
localization including that vector. The relaxed localization image is self-annihilating and contains
the whole ﬁnite plane, hence equals that plane. The strict kernel has dimension s− 2 and the
singular switch adds no localized vector, so the new outer dimension is s− 2.
The actual inner class is a nonzero scalar multiple of vj. Even if this scalar tends to zero, its
generic localization is nonzero on an outer-large set. Exact switch preservation therefore keeps each
inner determinant valuation. The new inner kernels again have primitive vectors. Thus their outer
dimension remains at least one; a proposed drop to zero is already a contradiction. There can
be only ﬁnitely many drops. For each ﬁxed number of them (5.1) bounds the models; no bound
uniform in an unbounded number of switches is needed.
At terminal outer dimension one a maximal boundary minor ∆ is nonzero. Its inner minors have
one ﬁnite valuation b on an outer-large set. Determinant-one Schur operations in a square two-term
rank-one-kernel complex give u =±αβ/∆, where α is an integral surviving cycle coordinate and β
has valuation at least −a. Consequently v2uj≥−a−b, the required contradiction. We obtain
v2uprim≥−C(E,A ). (5.5)
Here (5.5) is asserted for the known simple-zero ring instances used to obtain the varying-companion
estimate. Their nonzero actual central class supplied the rank-one start. It is not a uniform
valuation assertion at the unknown corank-one base. Bounded matrix size alone would not prove
it: [2j] is a counterexample to that inference.
Height-one regularity , including quadratic specializations. Fix a height-one prime p⁄= (2),
put R = Λ p and k = Frac(Λ/p), and use the actual primitive scalar Selmer complex over R. Here
R is a DVR and k has characteristic zero. All subsequent switches use the derivative conditions of
Lemma 9.5, retaining D0. The primitive old local conditions and these derivative conditions have
the evaluation injection of Lemma 9.3. Cancel the degree-zero and degree-three disks and then the
unit disks. Write C = [Rs d− →Rs] in degrees one and two, with d⊗k = 0 . Its generic kernel has
dimension one. Choose a primitive vector v∈ kerd. Such a vector exists because kerd is saturated
inRs: divide a nonzero kernel vector by its largest common power of a uniformizer. Thus ¯v⁄= 0 in
V =H 1(C⊗Rk) and dimkV =s≥ 1. The actual class z is a nonzero fraction-ﬁeld multiple of v.
We will only make switches whose ﬁnite localization is nonzero on ¯v. Consequently their generic
localization of z is nonzero, which is the hypothesis needed for the actual-class determinant switch.
If the scalar character specialized to k is nonquadratic, the rank-two detection of Lemma 9.4
applies to ¯v and an independent vector whenever s> 1. The ﬁnite localization then has rank two.
Exact duality makes the relaxed localization image equal to the ﬁnite plane, so switching to the
singular plane lowers s by two. We give the quadratic case in detail.
Let α be the specialized character on the index-two group. Since the moving inertia generator
has value 1 +t, quadraticity implies t(t + 2) = 0 in k. The only possible primes are therefore (t)
and (t + 2). The equality ατ =α−1 =α uses the actual conjugation τ ; it involves no automorphism
of R or substitution for t. The action
cτ (g) = Jc(τ −1gτ ), J =ρT (τ ),
on cocycles gives a k-linear involution on cohomology. Extend α to the index-two overgroup by
choosing its value on τ to be +1. The other extension diﬀers by the character of K. The induced
71rank-four representation is consequently W+⊕W−, with each W± of rank two. The matrix-span
witness in (H1)–(H2) and the paragraph following them makes their restriction to the index-two
group absolutely irreducible. Twisting its matrices by nonzero scalar character values does not
change their linear span. The summands are nonisomorphic: an intertwiner restricts to a scalar,
whereas their actions of τ are J and−J. A scalar intertwining these two actions is zero since 2 is
invertible. We write V =V+⊕V− for the resulting cohomology signs.
At a new inert derivative prime write F,S for the ﬁnite and singular planes. The action on
F is J, and that on S is−J. Indeed, the local rational Frobenius acts on the Tate module by J;
it ﬁxes its square, the ﬁnite Frobenius generator, and conjugates tame inertia by its 𝓁-th power.
The retained congruences give 𝓁 =−1 in the limit, so pullback on the tame coordinate contributes
𝓁−1 =−1. These assertions hold at every previously chosen derivative prime as well. A common
scalar sign would simply interchange the names + and−. The transported cross pairing is exactly
β(x,y ) = εe2(x,Jy ), ε ∈k×,
from Lemma 9.5. The ﬁnite and singular planes are isotropic. These are the retained local cup-
product identities, including the extra ﬁnite precision in (H12), not a residual alternatingness
assertion. Since detJ =−1 and J 2 = 1 , e2(x,Jy ) = e2(y,Jx ). Choose nonzero J-eigenvectors
e+,e −. They form a basis and e2(e+,e −)⁄= 0. The two local cohomology signs are therefore
H+ =F (e+)⊕S(e−), H − =F (e−)⊕S(e+).
They are orthogonal nondegenerate symmetric hyperbolic planes. In either plane the self-pairing
of aF (e±) +bS(e∓) is 2ab times a nonzero constant. Its only isotropic lines are thus its ﬁnite and
singular axes.
All old conditions are stable under this involution. Conjugate old Kummer conditions are
transported into each other; primitive ramiﬁed full conditions occur at the conjugate pair; unused
good conditions are unramiﬁed; and both F andS at a previous derivative prime are stable under
the actions just computed. At the new prime let Cstr andCrel have the actual truncated conditions
D0 and D0⊕ (F⊕S)[−1]. Put
U = im
(
H 1(Crel⊗k)−→F⊕S), K 0 =H 1(Cstr⊗k).
The retained localization-duality diagrams give U =U ⊥. Speciﬁcally, localization’s connecting map
is its dual under the strict/relaxed duality, so its kernel is both U and U ⊥; this is the exactness in
[44, 6.3.3–6.3.4] used in Lemma 9.5. Isotropy alone would not suﬃce. The cone triangles, with D0
retained, give exact sequences
0−→K0−→V −→U∩F−→ 0, 0−→K0−→V ′−→U∩S−→ 0, (5.5a)
where V ′ is the switched ﬁber cohomology. There is no preceding local degree-zero term, since the
quotient conditions are F [−1] and S[−1]. The space U is stable under signs. Each U±⊂H± is its
own annihilator in H±, hence is one of that plane’s two isotropic axes.
We also need one prime detecting prescribed classes of opposite signs. Choose nonzero c+∈V+
andc−∈V−. Their restrictions to the exact kernel H of Lemma 9.4 are nonzero additive equivariant
maps. Their joint k-linear evaluation span in W+⊕W− surjects to each summand. Simplicity and
nonisomorphism force this span to be the full direct sum. At an element h∈H, evaluation at (τh )2
on a sign-δ cocycle is
cδ((τh )2) = (1 + δJ )cδ(h) ( δ = +1,−1). (5.5b)
72This follows from additivity on H and the sign relation; a coboundary is zero on H. Thus the two
ﬁnite-coordinate maps on the joint span surject respectively onto ke+ andke−. There is an actual
h for which both coordinates are nonzero. For completeness, if their product vanished at every
actualh, additivity and the identity Q(x +y)−Q(x)−Q(y) for that product would make its polar
form zero on all pairs of actual evaluations. It would then vanish on their linear span, contradicting
surjectivity onto the two axes. Finite Chebotarev and the exact matrix identity (5.4) realize this
choice by a new inert prime, avoiding every previously used prime. All character, Tate, cochain
and old-diagram constraints are included in the same ﬁnite quotient as in that lemma. No residual
irreducibility or inﬁnite-level Chebotarev assertion is used. The same argument for one nonzero
sign class gives nonzero localization in its ﬁnite axis.
Suppose ﬁrst that both V+ and V− are nonzero. Choose the class in one sign to be a nonzero
component of ¯v and choose any nonzero class in the other. Detection as above puts both ﬁnite
axes in U , so U =F andU∩S = 0. Equation (5.5a) gives dimV ′ =s− 2. The localization of ¯v is
nonzero: its two sign components lie on diﬀerent ﬁnite axes, so cannot cancel.
Suppose instead that only one sign occurs, say V =V+, with s> 1. Detect ¯v in its ﬁnite axis.
Then U+ = F (e+). Since V− = 0 , (5.5a) gives U−∩F (e−) = 0 . The isotropic-axis calculation
forces U− =S(e+). Consequently K0 has sign dimensions (s− 1, 0), while V ′ has sign dimensions
(s− 1, 1). This proves that the ﬁrst switch introduces the other sign and preserves total dimension.
Sinces− 1≥ 1, the next switch uses the preceding mixed-sign case and lowers the total dimension
by two. After the ﬁrst switch choose the primitive vector in the new integral generic kernel; include
one of its nonzero sign components in the second detection. Thus the actual-class nonvanishing
requirement holds at both steps. The case V =V− is identical with signs interchanged.
At each step the new class is the actual derivative PI∪{𝓁} of Proposition 8.3, with the same ﬁxed
multiplier L. Equation (4.2) gives ﬁnite coordinate zero and singular coordinate Jf (PI ), which is
nonzero generically. The construction retains the old Kummer conditions and all earlier singular
conditions, by the ﬁnal assertion following (H19). Lemma 9.5 therefore makes this class span the
new generic kernel and preserves the valuation of its class-functional determinant coordinate. In
particular we do not replace the actual class by the chosen primitive vector. That vector is used
only to select the prime. The paired functional is the one transported from the same actual class.
The determinant comparison uses the common strict determinant factor; fractional complements
to f (PI ) and Jf (PI ) have unit exterior volume. Consequently a divisible localization creates no
extra valuation, and all strict torsion factors remain present on both sides.
Each new generic kernel has rank one and has a primitive integral vector, so its ﬁber dimension
is at least one. A proposed drop from dimension two to zero is therefore a contradiction; that
conﬁguration cannot occur. Whenever the dimension exceeds one, one or two switches give a
strict drop by two. Thus ﬁnitely many switches leave dimension one. After cancellation the
terminal complex has one free generator in each of degrees one and two; its diﬀerential is zero
because its generic kernel has rank one. The actual class is integral. To apply Proposition 8.6
after these switches, we check its orthogonal comparison on the added local conditions. In the
integral derivative model of Lemma 9.5, D0 pairs with D2 and S pairs perfectly with F , while
the pairing on S is zero with its retained isotropy homotopy. Hence (D0⊕S[−1])⊥ = D0⊕
S[−1] as the speciﬁed local condition, with the retained integral comparison maps. Conjugate
transport preserves this condition as well. At a derivative prime the comparison from the orthogonal
condition to the conjugate condition is therefore the identity under those integral maps. It requires
no inverse of a localization vector or of a local Frobenius operator. Using this identity in the
Selmer-cone comparison, together with the same old-place comparisons as before, constructs the
integral orthogonal lift of the new transported class. Composing with the retained integral global
duality gives a closed integral degree-two functional. Generically this is exactly the functional of
73Proposition 8.6: both use the inverse of the same orthogonal comparison, which is unique in the
derived category. Thus its primitive integrality proof extends to every ﬁnite list of these derivative
conditions. At the active ramiﬁed primes this uses the unit −2; at the moving prime it uses t
except at (t), where the central Frobenius determinants are nonzero and hence units in R. Unused
good primes retain their unramiﬁed condition. No inverse at an unused relaxed prime is used. The
terminal coordinate is the product of the two integral coeﬃcients, up to a determinant-basis unit, so
belongs to R. Valuation preservation transports this conclusion through every switch. This proves
regularity at every p⁄= (2) , including both quadratic specializations. Finally the normal-domain
intersection formula for Λ[1/2] givesuprim∈ Λ[1/2].
The same proof applies at the unknown full-corank-one base. If its actual generic class is zero,
its coordinate is zero. Otherwise its nonzero generic cycle and the central dimension bound give the
rank-one start used above. This requires no uniform outer valuation bound at that base. Combining
(5.5), for the known simple-zero instances, with (4.6) and ∑p|ktp≤ 2A proves (3.5). No ﬁxed-ﬁeld
class number is absorbed into the varying-ﬁeld bound.
9.5 Actual augmentation and the ﬁxed-ﬁeld count
Lemma 9.7 (Augmentation and the ﬁxed-ﬁeld rank budget) . Fix the ﬁnal ﬁeld K and the family
of Section 7, in the branch where the odd empty conﬁguration is not a unit. There are no derivative
labels. At augmentation all graph primes are unramiﬁed. The actual old-support subcomplex gives
(5.6). With dK = dim F2 Sel2(E/K ),tK = lengthE(K)[2∞] andhK = lengthH 2(CK)tors, it has the
rank, Smith-disk and torsion bounds (5.7). These are ﬁxed- K bounds, independent of the binary
dimension and graph size.
At every sign character, the inverse new local blocks and the resulting old-rank Schur complex
have no Laurent terms below −4M modulo 2M , uniformly in the number of new blocks. This is a
bound on sign evaluations, not on coeﬃcients in the group basis. The ﬁnite number hK does not
assume ﬁniteness of the whole Sha at the base.
Proof. We now use the ﬁnal K in the remaining branch of Section 7, where the intrinsic odd
empty conﬁguration is not a unit. There are no derivative labels in this last binary family. At
augmentation every graph prime is unramiﬁed, and the actual support triangle gives
Cold−→ (Cb)0−→
⨁
q∈Q
Qq−→Cold[1], (5.6)
with Cold an actual subcomplex of an upper triangular model. The graph-wide prescription gives
scalar 1+t at each chosen orientation, even though the quadratic character is trivial at augmentation.
The Euler-factor calculation in Lemma 8.11 therefore gives
lq,0 =e 2q,0 = t4(1 +t)2.
The two-place local determinant diﬀers only by the unit q4. Its residual t-order is consequently
four, uniformly in q and the number of graph primes.
Put T = T2E and let CK be the ordinary compact Kummer Selmer complex over the ﬁxed
imaginary quadratic ﬁeld K, with the derived Kummer condition, including its degree-zero torsion,
at every ﬁnite place. Lengths in this paragraph are lengths over Z2. Write
dK = dim F2 Sel2(E/K ), t K = lengthE(K)[2∞], h K = lengthH 2(CK)tors.
74The ﬁnite Selmer group deﬁnes dK, and perfection of CK makes its cohomology ﬁnitely generated,
so hK is ﬁnite. This does not assert that the full two-primary Tate–Shafarevich group is ﬁnite.
The already ﬁxed companion has L(E(k), 1)⁄= 0, so the sum-and-diﬀerence isogeny and the forward
corank-zero theorem give full Selmer corank one for E/K . This includes any divisible part of the
Tate–Shafarevich group.
Here are the cohomology calculations underlying the budget. At ﬁnite coeﬃcients, the local
degree-zero maps are isomorphisms onto local invariants and the local degree-one maps are the
Kummer injections. The Selmer cone therefore has H 0 =E(K)[2] andH 1 = Sel 2(E/K ). The Weil
pairing and the exact orthogonality of the Kummer conditions give the ﬁnite self-duality with shift
three [ 44, 5.1.1–5.1.6 and 6.3.4]. Since K has no real places and E[2] is rational of rank two, this
gives
dimF2Hi(CK⊗L F2) = (2,dK,dK, 2) ( i = 0, 1, 2, 3), (5.7a)
with zero cohomology in other degrees. In particular a minimal ﬁnite free Z2 model of CK has total
rank 2dK + 4.
For the integral torsion calculation, H 0(CK) = TGK = 0 : a compatible system of rational
points of unbounded two-power order cannot lie in the ﬁnite group E(K)tors. The exact sequence
for 0→T→T⊗ Q2→E[2∞]→ 0, and the local Kummer conditions, identify
H 1(CK)tors =E(K)[2∞].
Indeed this ﬁnite group injects as torsion global cohomology, all of its elements satisfy the Kummer
conditions, and a torsion class maps to zero with rational coeﬃcients, so is in this image. Integral
Kummer self-duality CK≃R HomZ2(CK, Z2)[−3] now gives
H 3(CK)≃ Ext1Z2(H 1(CK), Z2), lengthH 3(CK) = tK. (5.7b)
It also identiﬁes the free ranks in degrees one and two. They both equal one: universal coeﬃcients
with Q2/Z2 give
0−→H 1(CK)⊗ Q2/Z2−→ Sel2∞(E/K )−→H 2(CK)tors−→ 0,
and the middle group has full corank one. Thus the total integral torsion length of CK ishK + 2tK.
At t = 0 the moving character is trivial. At either place over the good split moving prime ri,
the unramiﬁed condition is the ordinary Kummer condition. Replacing it by the full condition adds
the singular complex
Qri,w = [ Z22 Φri −ri−−−−→Z22 ] in degrees one and two .
Here Φri is arithmetic Frobenius on T , with trace ari and determinant ri. In particular
det(Φri−ri) = ri(ri + 1−ari) = ri det(1− Φri),
so this determinant is nonzero and has valuation at most cE. The same bound holds at the conjugate
place. The singular complexes have zero degree-one cohomology and ﬁnite degree-two cokernels of
total length at most 2cE.
This comparison is retained before the inner limit. The ﬁeld K andCK are ﬁxed, while the two
moving Frobenius matrices and the comparison maps may vary with i and with the graph. Pass
to their limit at every coeﬃcient precision using the same retained ﬁnite diagram. A matrix whose
75determinant has valuation at most cE cannot acquire a zero determinant in this limit: the property
is determined modulo 2cE +1. Consequently, if Dc =Cold⊗LΛ Z2, the resulting triangle is
CK−→Dc−→Qr−→CK[1], (5.7c)
whereQr is the sum of two rank-two singular disks and H 2(Qr) = Jr has length at most 2cE. The
notationr in this limiting complex does not designate a ﬁxed rational prime supporting characters
of unbounded order.
Reducing (5.7c) modulo two, the two singular disks contribute at most eight to total cohomology
dimension. Equation (5.7a) thus gives a ﬁnite free model of Cold over Λ of total rank at most
2dK + 12, by cancelling unit entries at its maximal ideal (2,t ). The integral long exact sequence of
(5.7c) gives
H 1(Dc)≃H 1(CK), 0→H 2(CK)→H 2(Dc)→Jr→H 3(CK)→H 3(Dc)→ 0. (5.7d)
In particular H 3(Dc) is a quotient of H 3(CK). The image of the torsion of H 2(Dc) inJr has length
at most lengthJr, and its kernel is H 2(CK)tors. Hence, deﬁning the symbol used below by

Finally, a ﬁnite free complex over Z2 decomposes into free cohomology generators with zero

The model and its matrices may vary with the moving characters, but these bounds depend only
on the ﬁnal ﬁxed K and ﬁxed old data. They do not depend on the binary dimension or graph size.
The varying-ﬁeld estimate (5.1) is not used with j = b, and hK is not treated as a varying-ﬁeld
constant.
The lift is made over R = Λ[G] before passing to O[G]. Let C be the retained ﬁnite free family

diﬀerential is upper triangular. This follows by representing the connecting map between these
bounded free complexes by a chain map and forming its cone.
Cancel unit disks in C0. The same pivots and elementary operations lift to C overR, since the
augmentation ideal is contained in the Jacobson radical of R. Let C′ be the resulting family model,
so that C′0 is minimal. Decompose D over Λ into a minimal complex Dmin and unit disks E. The

its maximal ideal and Nakayama. Add to C′ the same unit disks ER and lift the resulting graded

exactly the diﬀerential of D. All matrices and their inverses at this step lie in Λ[G]; thus their sign

Proposition 9.8 (A common denominator for the corrected coordinate) . Under Lemma 9.7, use

The reference program S21 uses exact quadratic-ﬁeld arithmetic and capped integer operations.
The ideal register intermediate representation S22 computes the same predicate on every ﬁve-
natural tuple. Its independent all-input argument covers normalized signed arithmetic, origin and
zero-ordinate cases, equal-abscissa addition, and every early rejection. Nine exact polynomial
identities verify the nonexceptional projective formulas and preservation of Y 2 = X 3 +AXZ 4.
Scalar loops decrease by halving; the precision loop divides by four; factorial iteration is bounded.
This is a proof about the ideal intermediate representation, rather than an assertion that a budgeted
simulator always ﬁnishes.

This permits a ﬁnite cap. For positive L, σ(x) = min(x,L + 1) commutes with saturated addition
and multiplication. Powers and factorials can be computed with this saturation. Capping an
exponent is safe for base at least two because an exponent above L already makes the value exceed
L. Base zero, base one and exponent zero are separate cases. This proves equality with the ﬁxed-
height expression without requiring its full evaluation when the available precision is too small.
10.1 The natural polynomial and the power interface
The saved precision polynomial has 1 305 811 natural variables, the ﬁrst ﬁve being inputs. Its
SHA256 is
f3e163f5c60b972c98b48647274f956dd7d78428e27ddc0543574a5f18120b8c.
81Source S23 specializes a ﬁxed universal natural halting polynomial at the code of a wrapper. The
wrapper inverts four Cantor pairs and then runs the unchanged precision machine. Its pairing
equations are
2ti = (ci +ti+1)(ci +ti+1 + 1) + 2ti+1 (0≤i< 4), t 4 =c4.
Their triangular intervals prove unique natural unpairing. The new polynomial sums the square of
the old halting polynomial and the four squared diﬀerences above.
The universal compiler packs a ﬁnite run of a ﬁxed m-state increment/decrement/branch pro-
gram into base-B integers. Acceptance means reaching the unique ﬁnal halt with all registers zero;
the ﬁxed interpreter includes a ﬁnite cleanup stage. Choose
c> 0, K = 2c, B = 4K >m, T =Bq, F q = 1 +B +··· +Bq, D = (K− 1)Fq,
and require every input to be below K. For natural integers, say X is masked by Y when every
set binary bit of X occurs in Y . Require each register history Rj to be masked by D and satisfy
Rj <T . Each state history Si is masked by Fq, and
m−1∑
i=0
Si =Fq, S halt =T.
Thus register digits are below K, terminal register digits are zero, and state digits are zero or one.
Since B >m, the state sum has no carries and selects exactly one state at each time.
The nonzero mask Nj is determined by
KNj = (Rj +D) AND (KFq).
Indeed each digitr+K−1< 2K <B has itsK-bit set exactly when r> 0. For a decrement/branch
instruction at state i testing register j, impose
Vi =Si ANDNj, S i =Vi +Wi.
These are the nonzero and zero branch histories. Form the packed increment indicator Aj from
states incrementingj, the decrement indicator Cj from the appropriate Vi, and each incoming-state
indicatorIi from the states or branches directed to i. They have zero-one digits, since there is just
one active instruction and branch at each time. The transition equations are
Rj +BCj = initialj +BRj +BAj, S i = 1i=0 +BIi.
Every register coeﬃcient on either side is at most K < B; uniqueness of base- B digits gives the
initial conditions and each exact transition. The possible extra ﬁnal slot vanishes because halt has
no outgoing instruction and the terminal registers are zero. For q = 0 the same equations require
the initial state already to be halt and the inputs to vanish. Conversely, any ﬁnite accepting run
ﬁts after choosing c suﬃciently large, and supplies all these masks and equations.

Every natural solution of x2 +y2 = 1 + bxy, b >2, is an unordered adjacent pair. To prove
