10.3 Interpreter, lowering and ﬁve-input specialization
The universal program numbering is e + 1 = 2 w(2D + 1) . At w = 0 it halts. Otherwise the
four base- 2w digits at positions 4p,..., 4p + 3 give the opcode, register and two targets at state p.
Opcode one increments, opcode two decrements or takes its zero branch; every other opcode halts.
Missing digits are zero, so decoding is total.
The interpreter stores a ﬁnite tape as 0 or cons(h,t ) = 2 h(2t + 1). Repeated halving decodes
a nonempty head and tail; the tail is smaller than the code. To update a register, save the ﬁnite
reversed preﬁx, replace the entry and restore the preﬁx. Absent entries are zero. The reserved jump
register remains zero. Copies restore their source; aliased addition snapshots its operand before
overwriting it. Division maintains x = qy +r and subtracts only when r≥ y; the interpreter’s
divisors are at least two. These invariants prove that one interpreter loop executes exactly one
decoded instruction, with ﬁnite work. A simulated halt enters cleanup and clears all registers.
An inﬁnite simulation never enters cleanup. Thus its all-zero halt is equivalent to virtual halting,
without requiring the virtual ﬁnal tape itself to be zero.
The precision IR uses normalized signed pairs for integer arithmetic and pairs over the basis
1,
√
2 for ﬁeld arithmetic. Norm zero implies the ﬁeld element is zero: after clearing denominators,
a primitive solution of a2 = 2b2 would have both entries even. Every division is guarded by that
exact test. The Jacobian point formulas satisfy the curve equation and the aﬃne chord or tangent
formulas after multiplying denominators. Equal abscissas split into doubling and opposite-point
branches; zero ordinate doubles to the identity. The ﬁxed projective point is the same aﬃne P0.
These are polynomial identities, independent of the generator theorem.
The scalar loop halves its remaining count. The precision loop divides its remainder by four.
The capped arithmetic computes exactly min(x,L + 1); zero bases, unit bases and zero exponents
are separate. For bases at least two an exponent exceeding L already exceeds the cap. Thus the
ideal IR terminates and agrees with the stated rational predicate on all ﬁve natural inputs, including
every early rejection.
Each primitive lowering uses fresh auxiliary registers, snapshots overlapping sources, and pre-
serves the live-register map at the end of each ﬁnite block. Saturating decrement gives natural
84subtraction, and repeated addition gives multiplication. All variable divisions are guarded. In-
duction on completed loop iterations proves the same claim for loops. Acceptance enters cleanup;
rejection enters a permanent self-loop. Hence the primitive machine halts exactly on acceptance,
although the ideal decision program is total.
For pair(a,b ) = (a +b)(a +b + 1)/2 +b, unpairing maintains remainder = input−s(s + 1)/2
and subtracts s + 1 while s< remainder. At exit 0≤ remainder≤s, so b = remainder, a =s−b.
Four repetitions put the ﬁve codes into registers zero through four. Fresh scratch lies above the old
register range and is cleared; old work registers remain zero. Relocating every target by the preﬁx
length therefore preserves the precision machine.
The polynomial specialization ﬁxes the universal program input to this wrapper code, maps
the old packed input to variable ﬁve, and maps every old variable i≥ 2 and private-block start
by i↦→ i + 7. The four pairing variables occupy ﬁve through eight, disjoint from the ﬁve inputs.
The appended four squared equations force their unique Cantor values. Thus, for the exact saved
polynomial C,
∀c∈ N5, ∃z∈ N1305806 C(c, z) = 0 ⇐⇒ accepts_naturals(c) = True. (C.4)
The natural compiler proof has no height, restricted-converse or rational-model premise. Its use of
the height expression concerns only computation of that ﬁxed integer expression.
10.4 Translation to point codes
The typing order is independent of the natural predicate. First, member forces a canonical code
in D, and raw addition on two canonical elliptic inputs forces their canonical sum, including the
origin and two-torsion cases. The ﬁxed scalar graph gives [m]Q on D. In multiply, the three
root membership calls are forced conjuncts inside height_certificate. Thus the four scalar
shifts are in D before they are passed, unchanged, to the 27 slope calls and the six test-group
calls within each slope. This supplies the hypotheses of every raw addition in those groups. Only
after globally typed multiplication is established do the four square products and three successive
additions justify natural. Their outputs are canonical by the raw-addition postcondition, so no
separate output membership assumption is needed. The signed-encoding and exceptional branches
start their addition chains at this natural code, the literal origin, or the ﬁxed signed generator. An
inactive product branch is not assumed true. The ﬁxed generator equations are conjuncts outside
the internal products in all three templates that use them. These observations give the base cases
for the source-DAG induction below. Apply (C.4) using the exact signed index model. Induction
on the actual source DAG gives each node the code of its signed integer value. Natural guards
constrain source variables, not intermediate sums or products. Raw addition receives canonical
codes; globally typed multiplication justiﬁes the natural guards. Fixed generator equations and
scalar templates supply constants.
The power-value recipe has three signed code inputs, seventy-nine private natural witnesses, and
an output code for the value of the power-template polynomial. That value is not independently
required to vanish. Its saved root equality is [17751579, 15]. The precision parent has 54056
explicit variables and 15845 disjoint private blocks of seventy-nine variables. No parent variable
node accesses a private coordinate. Replacing each block by its private power-value call therefore
preserves the full existential assignment. Only the ﬁnal parent root is equated to the identity.
At each primitive formula, equality is a diﬀerence, conjunction is a sum of squares, and dis-
junction is a product. A topological induction, including the globally conjoined ﬁxed equations,
proves equality of polynomial values under substitution, not merely equisatisﬁability of an unrelated
formula. This is the required link to the saved AST.
8511 A variable-shift relation for ordinary values
We prove the ordinary-value deduction from the exact index model, the compiled precision relation,
the height theorem and PC. These are supplied by Sections 3, 10, 4 and Theorem 5.2, respectively.
Capital letters below denote ﬁve-coordinate point codes; their lower-case indices are ordinary inte-
gers. The external variable t is any rational number.

The ﬁrst follows from |t−n| < 2ϵl < 4−B/2 < e−B. For the second, positive multiples of P0

Lemma 11.1 (Divisibility). If Test(ι(n),ι (κ),t ) holds, then MSht(t)|κF (n).

denominators are nonzero by the guards on H and V0. For a nonzero point in either formal group,
log(Q) = z(Q)(1 +eQ), v (eQ)≥ 1.
Here p> 324 and v(p) = 1 . By [ 48, IV.5.5 and IV.6.4, pp. 129–132], the logarithm is an injective
isometry on v(z)≥ 1. The integral invariant diﬀerential gives in relative degree r− 1 the bound
(r− 1)v(z)−vp(r)≥ 1 for every r≥ 2. Thus the guarded nonzero points H,V 0 have nonzero
logarithms. The ﬁfth test ﬁrst makes z(U )/z(H) integral and congruent to t; multiplication by the
units 1+eQ preserves that congruence. The relation [s0]H = [h]U then gives log(U )/ log(H) = s0/h.
Likewise the sixth gives (s0−κn)/(h−κ)≡ t. Multiplying by the integer denominators and
subtracting yields vp(κ(n−t))≥ 1. Since t≡ c, we get p| κ(n−c), hence p| κF (n). The
squarefree product divides as asserted. No unit assumption on κ was used.
Lemma 11.2 (One suitable shift) . For every n∈ Z\C there is κ⁄= 0 with Test(ι(n),ι (κ),n ).
Proof. Choose the sign so R0 = η27306F (n) > 0 and let S′ be its ﬁnite prime support. Each

neighborhood. Take

The formal parameter and the derivative of [n] make each Cj integral above S′ after suﬃciently

Choose rational q integral at S′ and arbitrarily close to 1/√R0 in R, without equality. Denom-

13.1 Three corollaries
We apply the exact equality between the rational zero projection of P∗ and ordinary Z proved in
Theorem 1.1.
Corollary 13.1 (Negation of Mazur’s ﬁnite-component conjecture) . Mazur’s ﬁnite-connected-
components conjecture for the real-topological closure of rational points, Conjecture 3 of [ 70, 76], is
false. Its stronger Conjecture 2 is false as well.
Proof. We recall Mazur’s projection argument, originating in [ 70] and repeated in [ 76, p. 166].
Conjecture 3 in [ 76, p. 165] asserts that the closure of the rational points of any variety over Q in
its real locus has ﬁnitely many connected components. In that source a variety means a reduced
scheme of ﬁnite type; irreducibility is not required. Put r = 292 890 231 904 and
V =
(
{P∗(t,w 1,...,w r) = 0}⊂ Ar+1Q )red, C =V (Q)⊂V (R).
Reduction changes neither the rational nor the real points. Let π :V (R)→ R be projection to t.
Theorem 1.1 givesπ(V (Q)) = Z. Since Z is closed in R andπ is continuous,C⊂π−1(Z), soπ(C)⊂
Z. Every integer already occurs on V (Q), hence π(C) = Z. The image of any connected component
ofC is a connected subset of Z, therefore a singleton. Points projecting to distinct integers belong to
distinct components. Thus C has inﬁnitely many connected components, contradicting the stated
conjecture. Conjecture 2 in the same source would give the homotopy type of a ﬁnite simplicial
complex and hence only ﬁnitely many connected components, so it implies Conjecture 3 and is
contradicted too.
Corollary 13.2 (A universal rational Diophantine polynomial) . There exist m≥ 1 and a ﬁxed
polynomialU∈ Z[e,n,x 1,...,x m] such that, for every e,n∈ N,
∃x∈ Qm U (e,n, x) = 0 ⇐⇒ machine e halts on input n.
Here e and n are ordinary integer parameters, with N embedded in Q, and are not elliptic-curve
coordinates. In particular, every computably enumerable subset of N is represented by a suitable
ﬁxed value of e in this one polynomial.
91Proof. Fix an eﬀective enumeration of machines. The Davis–Putnam–Robinson–Matiyasevich the-
orem, [ 77, Basic Theorem, p. 3], applied to the universal halting relation gives a ﬁxed D ∈
Z[e,n,z 1,...,z s], with s≥ 1, such that, for e,n∈ N,
machinee halts on n ⇐⇒ ∃ z∈ Zs D(e,n, z) = 0.
To use integer witnesses in this statement, replace each nonnegative witness by a sum of four integer
squares. In the positive-integer convention of the cited source, apply the theorem to the predicate
that machine a− 1 halts on b− 1, substitute a =e + 1, b =n + 1, and use one plus that sum for
each witness; see also [ 77, p. 2]. A dummy witness ensures s≥ 1 if needed. For each zi take a
separate block wi of r rational variables, where r is the witness count of P∗. Deﬁne
U (e,n, z, w1,..., ws) = D(e,n, z)2 +
s∑
i=1
P∗(zi, wi)2.
A rational zero makes every summand zero. Theorem 1.1 then forces every zi to be an integer and
yields the displayed halting condition. Conversely, integer witnesses for D have separate rational
witness blocks for P∗, giving a zero of U . All blocks are private and the polynomial is ﬁxed
independently of e,n . No variable, degree or coeﬃcient bound proved for P∗ is asserted for this
new polynomial U .
OpenAI already obtained Turing degree 0′ for this set in [ 78, Corollary 7.1, pp. 56–57]; that
paper explicitly does not assert many-one completeness. The stronger direction below is 0′≤m
HTP(Q), obtained from the integer deﬁnition through the universal polynomial. The reverse many-
one reduction is the standard enumeration of rational solutions.
Corollary 13.3 (The many-one degree of HTP(Q)).
HTP(Q)≡m 0′, 0′ ={e∈ N : machine e halts on input e}.
Here HTP(Q) is the set of codes of integer-coeﬃcient polynomials, of arbitrary ﬁnite arity, having
a rational zero, under a ﬁxed eﬀective coding.
Proof. Givene, specialize the ﬁxed polynomial of Corollary 13.2 at its ordinary integer parameters
to obtain U (e,e, x). Computing its coeﬃcient list and code is eﬀective, and
e∈ 0′ ⇐⇒ ⌜U (e,e, x)⌝∈ HTP(Q).
This proves 0′≤m HTP(Q).
Conversely, from the code of a polynomial f construct a machine that ignores its input and
enumerates all rational tuples of the appropriate arity. It evaluates f using exact rational arithmetic
and halts as soon as it ﬁnds a zero. Its index j(f ) is computable from the ﬁnite code of f , and
f∈ HTP(Q) ⇐⇒ j(f )∈ 0′,
where the left side uses the code of f . Thus HTP(Q)≤m 0′. Invalid polynomial codes, if present in
the coding convention, are sent to a ﬁxed nonhalting index. Using rational-coeﬃcient polynomial
codes gives the same result, since clearing their ﬁnitely many denominators is eﬀective and preserves
rational zeros. The two reductions prove the assertion.
92More generally, an integer Diophantine problem translates to a rational one by adjoining P∗
for every integer witness, with private witness blocks. Natural witnesses can ﬁrst be expressed as
sums of four integer squares. Bounds for the resulting polynomial require their own composition
calculation; the bounds for P∗ do not automatically transfer.
For example, a machine that enumerates ZFC proofs and halts upon ﬁnding a contradiction has
a rational Diophantine halting condition by Corollary 13.2. This equivalence does not assert that
a contradiction exists, and no additional explicit polynomial is speciﬁed here.

Recovering the contact line from published quotients LetB/Q be the indeﬁnite quaternion

because the elliptic-point terms vanish: 5 splits in Q(i) and 7 splits in Q(√−3), so neither quadratic
ﬁeld embeds inB. The group W is (Z/2Z)4 with involutionswm indexed by square classes of divisors
of 210.
The published models of [ 84, Theorem 4.2 and Table 2, pp. 938–939] have the following coeﬃ-

Here is a deduction of all the required branch information, rather than an additional assumption
about it. Each of these four genus-one quotients has eight ﬁxed points upstairs, by Riemann–
Hurwitz. A stabilizer for the W action on a characteristic-zero smooth curve is cyclic, and therefore
has order at most two. Each nonempty ﬁxed set of an involution is a union of W -orbits of size
eight. Riemann–Hurwitz for X→X/W now gives

The four known ﬁxed sets already contribute 32. Thus g(X/W ) = 0 , the total is 40, and there
is exactly one further orbit of eight ﬁxed points. Inertia generators around the branch points
have product one. The four classes 30, 42, 70, 105 are independent in W , and their product is
the class 210. Hence there are exactly ﬁve branch points, each of index two, with distinct labels
30, 42, 70, 105, 210. Since the involutions are deﬁned over Q and each label occurs only once, each
branch point is rational. Three of them trivialize the genus-zero quotient.
Normalize b210 =∞, b105 = 0 , b70 = 1 , and write b42 = λ, b30 = µ. On a genus-one quotient
the residual group (Z/2)3 has translation subgroup of order four, necessarily the full two-torsion
group after choosing an origin. Its quotient has the same Jacobian j-invariant: it is multiplication
94by two over the algebraic closure. The remaining double cover of X/W branches at the four points
other than bm. Therefore the four quartic invariants determine the four Legendre invariants of
these four-point subsets.

Integral geometry and the degree 576 ﬁeld Chooseµ0∈O withµ20 =−210. The embedding
criterion allows this choice in a maximal order; maximal orders are conjugate here. Use the maximal
moduli stackX and the entire principal-level three scheme Y , including its geometric components.
Yuan’s integral moduli theorem [ 85, Theorem 1.1 and Sections 2.1–2.2] makes X regular, proper
and semistable over Z, with a universal abelian surface, and smooth away from 210. Its generic
stabilizer is precisely {±1}. Principal level three is ﬁne and gives a universal full three-torsion
marking.
Work ﬁrst over R0 = Z[1/210]. The eﬀective level-change group has order 36, prime to every
residue characteristic of R0. Its generic action is free. For a ﬁnite tame group acting on a smooth
relative curve, the ﬁxed locus of an element is smooth locally along its ﬁxed points; an isolated
special ﬁxed point consequently lifts. Thus no new ﬁxed point occurs here, and YR0 → XR0 is
ﬁnite etale. The W action extends to the smooth stable model of X. Its stabilizers remain cyclic,
of order at most two. Distinct involution ﬁxed loci cannot intersect; each ﬁxed locus is etale over
the base. Consequently XR0/W is a smooth genus-zero curve with ﬁve disjoint branch sections of
index two. The three rational sections already chosen identify it with P1R0. Their values are the
ﬁve elements of C.
Set K = ˆO×, U = U (3), Z = A×f , and N = NB(Af )×(ˆO). Normalization preserves 3ˆO, so U
is normal in N . The canonical adelic action is deﬁned over the reﬂex ﬁeld Q [86, 2.2.5 and 2.3.1];
this refers to the entire canonical model, not an individually selected complex component. Every
central ﬁnite idele is a rational scalar times a unit idele. At three its scalar residue is 1 or−1,
which a rational sign removes. Thus Z⊂ Q×U acts trivially on Y .
At three the quaternion algebra is ramiﬁed. Its maximal order modulo three has 81 elements,
radical of size 9, and hence 72 units. It follows that
1−→ (O/3O)×/{±1}−→ Γ = N/(ZU )−→W−→ 1, |Γ| = 36· 16 = 576.
Above an unbranched point the actions of W and of the eﬀective level-change kernel are free. The
ﬁber therefore is a torsor of size 576 for this constant group. The Galois permutations commute
with its regular action, so belong to its regular centralizer, also of order 576. The normal splitting
ﬁeldL consequently satisﬁes [L : Q]| 576: the Galois group is a subgroup of that regular centralizer.
In particular [L : Q]≤ 576.
All ﬁber surfaces and their three-torsion markings are deﬁned over L. For two such surfaces, a
homomorphism annihilating the three-torsion factors through [3]. Hence reduction embeds
Hom(A,B )/3 Hom(A,B )↪→ Hom(A[3],B [3]).
95The ﬁnite Galois image on the integral Hom lattice is trivial modulo three. The principal congruence
subgroup modulo three in an integral general linear group is torsion-free, so this image is trivial.
Thus every geometric homomorphism between ﬁber surfaces is deﬁned over L.
At a prime outside 210, tame local coordinates for the quotient are w = z2 up to a unit at a
branch section, and etale elsewhere. A base point with even contact gives no ramiﬁcation after an
unramiﬁed extension. The universal abelian scheme extends at the resulting integral point of YR0.
Therefore
rad(DLNbad)|B∗M (s). (R)
This assertion includes contact with inﬁnity. It is not a statement obtained merely from the
numerical prime support of pairwise determinants.
The normalizer correspondences give isogenies between the conjugate surfaces. In the non-CM
case the geometric endomorphism algebra is B; Skolem–Noether adjusts a conjugate isogeny to
commute with its chosen quadratic subﬁeld. All such maps lie over L by the Hom argument. These
facts establish the usual GL 2-type and compatibility hypotheses; they do not establish the separate
bounded descent to a GL 2-type variety over Q.

A level bound for the non-CM ﬁbers This argument allows the scalar character order and
coeﬃcient ﬁeld to vary. It does not assert bounded same-ﬁeld descent to a rational GL 2-type variety.
Let s be a rational point outside the ﬁve contacts, let L/Q be the normal ﬁber ﬁeld constructed
above, and let A/L be a non-CM ﬁber surface. Thus [L : Q]| 576, all geometric homomorphisms
between conjugate ﬁbers are deﬁned over L, and End0(AQ) = B, the quaternion division algebra
of discriminant 210. Put
R =B∗M (s), T ={p :p|R}.
The geometric argument gives that L is unramiﬁed outside T and A has good reduction outside
the primes above 210. All conjugate QM structures lie in the same normalizer orbit.
Good reduction after full torsion. First a QM surface with the indicated division action has
potentially good reduction at every ﬁnite place. Indeed, pass to an extension with semistable reduc-
tion, using [ 31, Theorem 3.4, p. 406]. Every endomorphism extends uniquely to the Néron model
by its mapping property. On the identity component of the geometric special ﬁber it preserves
the maximal torus T : this torus is the maximal connected aﬃne subgroup, and every homomor-
phism from a torus to an abelian variety is zero. If its rank t is positive, restriction gives a unital
homomorphism
B−→ End0(T ) = Mt(Q), 1≤t≤ 2.
The kernel is a two-sided ideal of the simple algebra B, and the map is unital, so it is injective. For
t = 1 dimensions prohibit this; for t = 2 it would be an isomorphism from a division algebra to
M2(Q), also impossible. Hence t = 0. The semistable identity component is abelian, which is good
reduction. These Néron-model and reduction conventions are those of [ 31, §3, pp. 405–406].
Set E = L(A[11]). We next justify that E/Q is Galois. For each σ, the normalizer relation
supplies a compatible isogeny σA→ A of degree prime to 11. Here is the prime-to- 11 detail. In
the analytic description Az = C2/(O(z, 1)t) of the published 2021 edition of Voight [ 89, equations
(43.1.2)–(43.1.3)], a positive normalizer element b =

maximal-order normalizer is Q∗11 GL2(Z11). Rescale b rationally to make it an 11-adic unit, then
clear the remaining denominators by an integer prime to 11. This produces the claimed isogeny.
Changes of level marking use isomorphisms. All these geometric homomorphisms are L-deﬁned by

same kernel. Thus every conjugate of E equals E.

particular it applies at each of 2, 3, 5, 7.
The scalar lift with controlled support. Choose B-compatible isogenies µσ : σA→ A over

so lie in Q∗. Put aσ = (deg µσ)−1/4 > 0 and νσ = aσµσ, with trivial Galois action on the added
algebraic coeﬃcients. Since a scalar c has degree c4, the discrepancies of the νσ form a sign cocycle
ε∈Z2(GQ,{±1}), inﬂated from Gal(L/Q).

if required. Their product χ is tame at every odd prime, has local conductor at most 4 at 2, and

For constant coeﬃcient groups, the boundary in 1→{± 1}→ µ∞ 2− →µ∞→ 1 is the obstruction to

On GL, b is a conjugation-invariant character. Outside T its inertia characters are quadratic.
Only ﬁnitely many are nontrivial; twist by the quadratic character of the product of those odd
primes to kill them. At each odd prime there is a unique nontrivial quadratic inertia character.
This changes no square or coboundary in (LR2), and introduces possible ramiﬁcation only at 2∈T .

The compatible modular representations. Choose a number ﬁeld containing all normaliza-
tion scalars, the values of b, and a splitting ﬁeld of B. For each coeﬃcient prime 𝓁 and an embedding
into Q𝓁, set
ρ𝓁(σ) = b(σ)−1aσµσ◦σ.
The cocycle identity makes this a representation commuting with B; after splitting B, matrix units
giveρ𝓁 =r𝓁⊕r𝓁 with dimr𝓁 = 2. On GL′, ρ𝓁 is the Tate action of A. Faltings’ semisimplicity and
endomorphism theorems and End0(A) = B show that the multiplicity representation is absolutely
irreducible. Proper-smooth comparison and ﬁnite descent, as in Lemma H.1, maker𝓁 de Rham with
Tate Hodge–Tate weights{0,−1}; Berger’s monodromy theorem makes it potentially semistable.
98It is odd. On Betti homology complex conjugation followed by the holomorphic isogeny µc ex-
changes the two Hodge summands, each a simple B⊗C-module. Its action on the multiplicity space
is therefore nonscalar. After the scalar correction its square is one, so its eigenvalues are 1,−1. The
representation is unramiﬁed outside T∪{𝓁}, by (LR2) and the reduction and ﬁeld properties. Pan’s
regular two-dimensional modularity theorem applies at 𝓁 = 5. We use its published formulation in
[90, Section 7, p. 624], with the deﬁnition of a geometric representation on pp. 611–612, and the
detailed author proof in [ 23, Theorem 1.0.4]. It requires no additional residual-image restriction at
this prime and identiﬁes r5 with a cuspidal modular representation up to a character twist. The
published formulation is used as stated; no byte or text identity between Pan’s author version and
the journal version is asserted.
The twist can be taken ﬁnite and the weight is two, without invoking a separate local-algebraicity
theorem for characters. The twisting character is a de Rham subrepresentation of the relevant
Hom representation. Comparing its integer weight with {0,−1} and the modular weights {0, 1−k}
forces k = 2 and twist weight zero. On GL′, the identity (detr5)2 = detV5(A) = χ2cyc shows that
detr5/χcyc has ﬁnite image, since GL′ has ﬁnite index in GQ. The modular determinant divided by
χcyc also has ﬁnite image. The square of the twisting character consequently has ﬁnite image, so the
character itself has ﬁnite image. Absorb it in the modular form and take the primitive weight-two
newform f of level N .
The conductor at 5 is not omitted. For each prime p /∈ T∪{ 3, 5}, reduction of µσ composed
with relative p-Frobenius is a quasi-endomorphism of the good reduction of A at a chosen place.
After clearing denominators its characteristic polynomial is independent of the Tate prime. Half
its trace, multiplied by b(σ)−1aσ, is therefore one algebraic number giving the trace of every r𝓁.
Enlarge the coeﬃcient ﬁeld to contain the coeﬃcients of f . The equality with the trace of f at
ﬁve is an equality of algebraic numbers; transporting it to an embedding at three and applying
Chebotarev and semisimplicity identiﬁes r3 with the three-adic realization of f . Thus use r3 to
compute the conductor at 5 and r5 at every other prime. The modular level is the conductor of
these realizations by [ 66, §§0.3–0.7, Theorem A, pp. 409–411] and newform theory [ 65, Theorems 1
and 4]. Here the totally real ﬁeld is Q, of odd degree one, so Carayol’s extra ﬁnite-place hypothesis
for even degree does not apply. His unramiﬁed normalization twist and contragredient preserve the
conductor; his theorem allows arbitrary nebentypus. Li’s Fricke relation and Shimura’s Jacobian
construction, as in the baseline modularity argument, also place r5 in the Tate module of J1(N ).
The primitive level equals the local conductor. Let f ∈ S2(Γ0(N ),χ ) be the primitive
newform obtained after absorbing the ﬁnite character twist, and let Πf be its unitary cuspidal
automorphic representation. The character χ is arbitrary; its conductor need only divide N . For
every prime p, write a(Πf,p) for the local conductor exponent. Then
a(Πf,p) = vp(N ). (CL1)
Here is the exact published newform input. In [ 65, Theorem 1, p. 302], the local conductor ideal
is the largest ideal c for which the appropriate central character eigenspace under Γ0(c) is nonzero;
at that ideal the eigenspace is one-dimensional. The remark on pp. 306–307 identiﬁes this ideal
with the exponent in the local epsilon factor. Theorem 4 on p. 313 gives the global level spaces:
they vanish unless c(Π)|M , and at M = c(Π)∏ppbp their dimension is ∏p(bp + 1). The paragraph
immediately following that theorem identiﬁes all primitive newforms with the minimal level M =
c(Π). This proves (CL1).
This statement includes nebentypus. Casselman’s classical setup on p. 309 allows every character
ϵ moduloM and usesf|kγ =ϵ(a)−1f forγ =(a bc d)∈ Γ0(M ). Since ad≡ 1 (mod M ), this is exactly
99ϵ(d)f . Thus the usual nebentypus convention is already covered. No squarefree-level hypothesis
occurs.
For a coeﬃcient characteristic 𝓁⁄=p, Carayol’s published Theorem A gives the full local Weil–
Deligne compatibility, not only an equality at unramiﬁed primes [ 66, §§0.3–0.7, pp. 409–411]. Its
base ﬁeld here is Q, its real weight is two, and its extra ﬁnite discrete-series-place condition applies
only to even degree totally real ﬁelds, so imposes no additional hypothesis here. Theorem A includes
the dyadic cases; the weaker Theorem B is not the result being used.
Carayol normalizes reciprocity by sending geometric Frobenius to a uniformizer. His Hecke
correspondence in §0.5 diﬀers from local Langlands by a contragredient and an unramiﬁed norm
twist. It is characterized there by equality of local L- and epsilon-factors, including character
twists. The conductor is unchanged by either duality or an unramiﬁed twist. In particular, the
passage to the Tate-module convention used for r𝓁 changes no conductor exponent. Equality of
good Frobenius traces identiﬁes the global representation supplied by Carayol with the already
constructed irreducible modular representation, in this convention. This uses Chebotarev and
Brauer–Nesbitt; it does not use the unpublished irreducibility observation in Carayol’s remark
after Theorem A. Since the matching representation is irreducible, its matching semisimpliﬁcation
also forces the original representation to be irreducible.
The epsilon-factor conductor in (CL1) therefore equals the Artin conductor of r𝓁|GQp :
ap(r𝓁) = a(Πf,p) = vp(N ), p ⁄=𝓁. (CL2)
The Artin conductor here includes its tame term and Swan term; equivalently one uses the full Weil–
Deligne conductor, including the monodromy contribution. One must not discard monodromy by
replacing the local representation with its semisimpliﬁcation on inertia. Carayol’s compatibility
supplies precisely the required full local object. His epsilon-factor normalization and Casselman’s
conductor-zero additive character give the same exponent; changing an additive character only
introduces the usual common dimension-dependent shift, so no such change is needed.
Apply (CL2) with 𝓁 = 5 at every p⁄= 5 and with 𝓁 = 3 atp = 5. The companion proof identiﬁes
both r5 and r3 with the realizations of this same f . Thus the asserted conductor bounds control
every prime divisor of the primitive level, even when p2|N . No compatibility theorem at p =𝓁 is
needed.

The CM height estimate over the same ﬁeld LetA/L be an abelian surface of CM type, let
B be a quaternion division algebra over Q embedded in End0L(A), and suppose that all geometric
endomorphisms of A are deﬁned over L. Assume
d = [L : Q]≤ 576, rad(|DL|)≤R, R ≥ 1.
All heights below are stable Faltings heights in Faltings’s original normalization. We prove
hF(A)< 854R< 6100R24. (10)
No assertion about the existence or degree of L is included in this lemma.
First, AL is isogenous to E2 for a CM elliptic curve E. Here is a dimension argument which
makes the alternative cases explicit. If A were geometrically simple, its division endomorphism
algebra would act faithfully on the four-dimensional rational homology. Its dimension therefore
102divides four. As it contains B, it would equal B; but B has no commutative semisimple subalgebra
of dimension four, contrary to CM type. If the two elliptic factors were not isogenous, their
endomorphism algebra would be a product of commutative ﬁelds and could not contain B. Finally,
if E had no CM, the endomorphism algebra would be M2(Q), and the embedding of the four-
dimensional division algebra B would be an impossible isomorphism.
The elliptic factors and the isogeny may be taken over L. Indeed, the rational idempotents in
End0(A) are deﬁned over L, and the image of an integral multiple of such an idempotent is an
elliptic subvariety over L. Endomorphisms and homomorphisms of the resulting factors extend to
rational endomorphisms of A by the inclusions and rational projections; these are ﬁxed by Gal(L/L).
Thus the factor isogenies, and all endomorphisms of E, are deﬁned over L.
Put F = End 0(E), an imaginary quadratic ﬁeld, and O = End(E). Diﬀerentiation gives an in-
jective unital map F−→ EndL(LieE) = L: it is nonzero, and every nonzero elliptic endomorphism
in characteristic zero is separable. Consequently F⊂L.
We next pass to the maximal order without extending L. Choose f≥ 1 withfOF⊂O , choose
an integral basis α1,α 2 ofOF , and form the ﬁnite subgroup scheme
K =
2∑
i=1
(fαi)(E[f ])⊂E.
Each fαi is deﬁned over L, so K and the quotient E′ = E/K are deﬁned over L. Under a
complex uniformization E(C) = C/Λ, this subgroup is OF Λ/Λ; thus E′(C) has lattice OF Λ and
endomorphism ring OF . The ring cannot be larger, since the rational endomorphism algebra
remains F . This is also a direct special case of the same-ﬁeld maximal-order theorem of Rémond,
but the construction just given is the proof used here. It follows that A′ = (E′)2 is L-isogenous to
A.
Write ∆F for the fundamental discriminant of F . The discriminant tower formula implies that
every rational prime dividing ∆F dividesDL. A quadratic fundamental discriminant has odd prime
exponents one and exponent at two at most three. Therefore
|∆F|≤ 4 rad(|DL|)≤ 4R.
The multiplier four includes the possible factor 23/2.

potentially good reduction. After a ﬁnite extension with everywhere good reduction, the elliptic
discriminant formula of Silverman, reproduced as equation (6.2) in [ 30], gives
hF(E′) = 1[L′ : Q] ∑σ:L′↪→C(− log(2π)− log|η(τσ)|2− 12 log Imτσ).
Here L′ is used only to evaluate the stable height; it is not the ﬁeld used in the isogeny estimate
below. The discriminant term is zero because reduction is good. We use the individual-embedding
formula (6.2), not the class-average assertion of Proposition 6.1, so no hypothesis on Weierstrass
coeﬃcients lying in Q(j) is needed.

One can see the last inequality directly from invariant diﬀerentials: after semistable base change,

multiplied by the isogeny degree. Thus the height diﬀerence is half the logarithm of that degree
minus a nonnegative normalized cokernel length.

This is a check of the number H, not a proof that H is a height constant.

Lemma 14.4 (Exact seven-variable pole statement) . Ifb⁄= 0 and Ψ(b), then vp(b) is not a negative
odd integer for every p /∈{ 2, 3, 17}. Conversely, Ψ(b) holds whenever b >0, the denominator of b
in lowest terms is a square, and b is a square in each of Q2, Q3, Q17.
Proof. These are Lemmas 14.5 and 14.7. Their proof gives the simultaneous two-twist construction
explicitly, and applies the existing Theorem 5.3 and Lemma 5.4. It introduces no new simultaneous-
realization assumption.
The lemma includes the degenerate values of the seven witnesses permitted by ( 11). No inverse
condition on e, h, s, or U may be inserted into this particular polynomial. For b⁄= 0 , the ﬁrst
equation itself implies 1 +bt2⁄= 0 . The predicate is soluble at b = 0 ; the norm argument below
accounts for that case where necessary.
14.1.1 The seven-witness parity predicate and its ﬁnite correction
Put B = 17b. The predicate used here is exactly the existence of t,U,e,p,q,s,h ∈ Q satisfying
U (1 +bt2) = b(1−bt2), (12)
p2 =B(1−e)(1 + 3e), (13)
h =e(B−U 2), (14)
Bq 2 = (s2−Bh)(s2 + 3Bh). (15)
Denote it by Ψ(b). No nonzero conditions are added to this formula.
Lemma 14.5 (The exact one-sided assertion) . If b⁄= 0 and Ψ(b), then v𝓁(b) is not a negative odd
integer for any prime 𝓁 /∈{ 2, 3, 17}.
Proof. Suppose k =v𝓁(b)< 0 is odd. Equation ( 12) forces 1 +bt2⁄= 0: if it vanished, its right side
would be 2b⁄= 0. If t = 0, then U =b. Otherwise v𝓁(bt2) is odd and nonzero, so (1−bt2)/(1 +bt2)
is a unit. Thus v𝓁(U ) = k in all cases, and v𝓁(B−U 2) = 2k.
If e = 0 , equation ( 13) gives p2 = B, impossible. Write j = v𝓁(e). If j < 0, the right side of
(13) has valuation k + 2j, which is odd. If j >0, it has valuation k, again odd. Consequently j = 0.
This includes the possibilities p = 0, e = 1, and e =−1/3. Now h⁄= 0 and v𝓁(Bh) = 3k is odd. If
s⁄= 0, its square has even valuation, diﬀerent from 3k, so the two factors on the right of ( 15) have
the same ﬁnite valuation. Their product has even valuation and is nonzero. The same conclusion
holds when s = 0 , since the product is −3(Bh)2. The left side is either zero or has odd valuation,
a contradiction.
The omitted guards therefore cause no loss of the asserted soundness. At b = 0, the equations
require U =p =h =s = 0 and permit arbitrary t,e,q . For b⁄= 0 , the six-witness source formula
is recovered exactly by eold = Be, pold = Bp, qold = Bq, with s,t,U unchanged. Its ﬁrst curve
residual is B2 times ( 13)’s residual and its second is B times ( 15)’s residual after ( 14). Division by
B reverses this transformation.

For clarity, the local descent needed here is the manuscript’s full two-descent in coordinates
(x + 3l,x−l). The two torsion classes are (l, 1) and (3,−l). At an odd good prime, the local image
consists of the four pairs with even valuations; at a prime dividing l, the two torsion classes span it
by their independent valuation vectors. At 3 it is the set of all ﬁrst-coordinate classes with square
second coordinate. At 2 both coordinate valuations are even: the only problematic case x−l = 2u,
u odd, would give (y/2)2≡ l (mod 8) , impossible for l≡ 3 (mod 4) . When both diﬀerences are
divisible by 4, their diﬀerence 4l and the cubic equation give even valuations for both. At inﬁnity
the ﬁrst coordinate is positive, and the positive-second-coordinate subgroup has index two. Its
possible representatives consequently are (3δ∏quii ,∏qvii ) with necessary equations
Ku + diag(λ)v =δλ, (K + diag(s))v = 0, (λ +s)tv = 0.
Reciprocity gives K +Kt = sst + diag(s). Hence ker(K + diag(s)) = ker Kt∩s⊥ is a line. The
functionalλt is nonzero on this line, because λ /∈ imK andλt1 = 0, whereas st1 = 1. The displayed
106equations force v = 0 , δ = 0 , and u∈ kerK. There are at most eight Selmer classes. The class
(β, 1) is actually local everywhere: it is the identity at 2 and inﬁnity, the ﬁrst torsion class at 3,
satisﬁes the torsion comparison at support primes by K1B0 = 0 , and is unramiﬁed at the other
odd primes. It is independent of the two torsion classes since B0 is nonempty and q0 /∈B0. Thus
each two-Selmer group has dimension exactly three.
Lemma 5.4 gives full two-primary Selmer corank one. Explicitly, the Kummer sequence gives
r + dim Sha[2] = 1 ; writing Sha[2∞] = ( Q2/Z2)t⊕F , the alternating Cassels–Tate pairing on the
ﬁnite quotient makes dimF [2] even, so F = 0 and r +t = 1 . Apply RC to n and nθ, and the
manuscript’s published forward analytic-rank-one theorem. Both curves have rank one and ﬁnite
Sha, hence Sha[2] = 0 . Kummer exactness now represents (β, 1) by actual rational points. These
are not two-torsion, so their abscissas are nonzero of class β. When θ = 1 the same point can be
used twice.
Lemma 14.7 (The completeness scope actually used) . Assume only the existing RC and the pub-
lished descent and forward-rank facts used above. If b> 0 has square reduced denominator and is a
square in Q2, Q3, Q17, then Ψ(b) holds.
Proof. Choose t∈ Q close to a solution of bt2 = 1 at inﬁnity and at 2, 3, 17. In ( 12) this makes

v3(U 2/B),v 17(U 2/B)≥ 1. The denominator tends locally to 2⁄= 0 and is positive globally.
Letβ,θ be the positive squarefree representatives of B,V . The number B = 17b is square at 2,
a nonsquare unit class at 3, and has odd valuation at 17. Thus β≡ 17 (mod 24) and β >1; θ≡ 1
(mod 24) and 17 - θ. At any prime 𝓁| β outside 2, 3, 17, the valuation of b is odd and therefore
positive, since its reduced denominator is square. The same quotient calculation as in soundness
gives v𝓁(U ) = v𝓁(b); hence 1−U 2/B is a square unit. This proves that V , and hence θ, is square
at every prime of β, including 17 by construction. The supports are disjoint. Conversely, if 𝓁| θ,
then𝓁 /∈{ 2, 3, 17} andv𝓁(B) is even. Odd valuation of B(B−U 2) forcesv𝓁(B) = 2v𝓁(U ) and strict
cancellation, so B/U 2∈ 1 +𝓁Z𝓁 is square. This proves the other mutual-square condition. If U = 0,
then θ = 1 and this last argument is unnecessary.
Apply the preceding lemma. Write V =θc2 and rescale the second point by (x,y )↦→ (c2x,c 3y)
to put it on EnV . If the ﬁrst point has abscissa Ba2, divide both curve parameters by a2 and the

The exact original norm argument therefore applies unchanged. If r has no relevant odd valuation,
there is no local conclusion to prove. No guard or witness has been inserted, and the original
polynomial and coeﬃcient bound are unchanged.

WriteK = Q(ρ), ρ2 = 2, and use the literal normalized curve and generator

to obtain 2E(K) = ZP . For a nonzero S =nP , put f (S) = 1/x(S) = f0 +f1ρ. Retain the exact

It forces C = 3 224, with 25 shared rational variables and 25 quadratic-or-linear equations. The
anchor introduces two variables ai, imposes Cai =fi, and imposes T (ai) for each i∈{ 0, 1}. It has

Set N = 2 24 and m =N/2 = 2 23. We claim that, for nonzero n,
the anchor holds on nP ⇐⇒ n∈ 2· 3m−1Z.
First consider the place above 3. It is unramiﬁed quadratic, with residue ﬁeld F9 and normalized
valuation v(3) = 1 . For rational a,b one has v(a +bρ) = min(v3(a),v 3(b)): after dividing by the
smaller power of 3, linear independence of 1, ¯ρ over F3 precludes cancellation. The curve has good
reduction there. Direct calculation gives
¯P = ( ¯ρ, 0), z (2P ) = 3396356159088− 4135204317756ρ3287811625151 , z =−x/y.
The reduction of P has order two. The denominator in this expression is a 3-unit and both
numerator coeﬃcients have valuation one. Consequently nP is in the formal kernel exactly when
n is even, and v(z(2P )) = 1 .
110For completeness, the valuation of every such multiple can be computed without an unproved
uniformity assertion. The integral formal group in the parameter z has inverse−z, so its multiplica-
tion series are odd. Their linear coeﬃcient is the corresponding integer. Thus, for a 3-unit integer
u, [u]z = uz +O(z3) preserves positive valuation, whereas [3]z = 3z +O(z3) increases valuation
by exactly one at depth at least one. The higher terms have valuation at least 3v(z) > v(z) + 1 .
Apply this repeatedly to [n/2](2P ), allowing negative integers by negation. It gives
v(z(nP )) = 1 + v3(n), v (f (nP )) = 2 + 2v3(n) (2 |n).
The second equality follows from f =z2(1− (11− 6ρ)f 2) on the formal branch. The integral formal-
group construction and its linear multiplication coeﬃcient are given in [ 48, IV§1, Example IV.2.2.3
and Proposition IV.2.3(a)]. No minimal-model assertion at the bad prime 2 is used below.
At the place above 2, normalize v(2) = 1 , so v(ρ) = 1/2. The two summands of a rational pair
have valuations of opposite integer parity after multiplication by 2, and hence cannot cancel. The
displayed coordinates give
v(x(P )) =−3, v (y(P )) =−9/2, v (z(P )) = 3/2.
Thus P lies on the formal branch at inﬁnity, even though reduction at 2 is bad. Every integral
multiple lies in this formal subgroup. One can see the integral branch directly by setting w =−1/y:
the equation becomes w =z3− (11− 6ρ)zw2, which has a unique solution w∈z3O[[z]]; its group
law is the integral formal group just cited. In particular f (nP ) is integral at 2. Since
v(a +bρ) = min(v2(a),v 2(b) + 1/2),
its rational coeﬃcients are both 2-integral. As C is a 2-unit, the two anchor conditions at 2 impose
no further restriction on n.
At 3, the two anchor conditions are equivalent to v(f (nP ))≥N . Positive valuation of f implies
negative valuation of x, hence membership in the formal kernel, so n is even. The preceding exact
formula makes the remaining condition v3(n) ≥ m− 1. Conversely these two conditions give
both required coordinate divisibilities at 3, and the condition at 2 already holds. The proved
characterization ofT supplies its rational witnesses, proving the subgroup claim.
In particular, anchor indices contain a nonzero ﬁnite-index subgroup and have positive represen-
tatives. They can be combined with any ﬁnite collection of requirements that indices be divisible
by ﬁxed integers: take a common multiple with 2· 3m−1. More generally, any ﬁnitely many formal-
neighborhood conditions whose preimages are ﬁnite-index subgroups can be imposed in the same
way. This statement does not claim compatibility with arbitrary nonzero residue conditions.

has fold =λ2f and (

14.3 The exact norm tests
For a nonzero rational contact r and rational tests Xi of positive orders Li, apply the binary
operation Nr(a,b ) = a2 +rb2 to the ordered leaves 1,X 1/rL1,...,X k/rLk in the following ﬁxed
order. First pair 1 with X1/r, where L1 = 1 . Group the remaining tests by increasing order,
retaining their original order within each group. Pair successive tests within each group and carry
any last unpaired test. Then pair successive resulting nodes at each level and carry any last node
until only a root W remains. The polynomial equations for the bottom pairs are
rw−r−X 21 = 0, r 2Lw−X 2i−rX 2j = 0;
an unpaired normalized leaf satisﬁes rLw−Xi = 0. Higher nodes satisfy w−a2−rb2 = 0. Powers
of r are computed by binary squaring. Add an inverse for r, a variable for W 2, and the predicate
Ψ(rW 2).
At a prime with R =vp(r) odd, the two summands in every norm node have distinct valuation
parity. There is no cancellation. The constant leaf is on the all-left path, so vp(W )≤ 0 if R <0.
Consequently vp(rW 2) > 0 ﬁrst implies R > 0. It also gives vp(W ) >−R/2. Descending any
norm node, a bound vp(Nr(a,b ))>−cR withc< 1 givesvp(a)>−cR/2 andvp(b)>−(c + 1)R/2.
Every normalized leaf therefore has valuation greater than −R. Thus vp(Xi)> (Li− 1)R≥Li− 1,
or vp(Xi)≥ Li. The root cannot vanish at such a prime. Indeed Nr(a,b ) = 0 forces a = b = 0
over Qp, and descent would make its constant leaf zero. Thus Lemma 14.4 applies even though the
polynomial has no separately imposed inverse for W .
For completeness at a positive prime r =p, tests of orders Li can be made so deep that every
nonconstant normalized leaf is p-integral and the root W is a p-adic unit. Then rW 2 has square
reduced denominator. If r is a local square at 2, 3, 17, the completeness half of Lemma 14.4 applies.

Every raw contact parameter in the complete assignment is a positive prime congruent to c
modulo W . It is therefore a square in Q2, Q3, Q17. The binary norm batch has unit root at its
contact prime, because normalized leaves are integral and its leftmost constant leaf is 1. Its root
is positive and nonzero in the real embedding. The argument rW 2 consequently is positive, has
square reduced denominator, and is locally square at 2, 3, 17. These are the exact hypotheses of the
original specialized parity-completeness input. This veriﬁes its consumption but does not constitute
a new proof of that input, which is supplied here by Lemma 14.7. In particular no integrality of
the comparison points at these three small primes is required here.

14.6 The base compiler prescription and ﬁnite veriﬁcation
Every equation above is implemented with named arithmetic intermediates so that each rational
residual has degree at most four. A ﬁeld product uses (a+b

15 An explicit quartic deﬁning the integers
This is the second ﬁnal polynomial of the manuscript. The original huge polynomial remains
unchanged. The base residual compiler in Section 14 supplies construction machinery and inherited
119arithmetic inputs; it is not a third ﬁnal polynomial.
Theorem 15.1 (An explicit quartic deﬁnition) . With precisely the inherited arithmetic hypothe-
ses and qualiﬁcations of Section 14 and Appendix B, the coeﬃcient ﬁle below deﬁnes one integer

15.1 Literal speciﬁcation and coeﬃcient ﬁle
The complete coeﬃcient table is quartics/quartic-4973/polynomial.expanded.txt. After one
comment line, each line gives a nonzero signed integer coeﬃcient followed by a nondecreasing list
of variable indices. Repeated indices give their multiplicities as exponents; a coeﬃcient alone is the
constant. Thus -6 0 2 2 denotes−6X0X 22 . The original input is X0. Like terms are collected,
and the rows are in lexicographic index-list order. The occurrence-frequency relabeling is recorded
in quartics/quartic-4973/variable-map.json and ﬁxes index zero. The gzip companion has
exactly the same uncompressed bytes. The coeﬃcient table has 1 622 053 bytes, including its
comment and line endings, and SHA-256
094f6a7ed17e7cc8d5d17a2f809127bbaffd0fe343cb2a32d18ac97e61279876
This speciﬁes every coeﬃcient without an auxiliary choice of witnesses.

Lemma 15.6 (Three-mode coverage) . Number the unchanged raw contacts from 0 to 36. Retain
full comparisons at 0,..., 4 and 21,..., 28, beta-only comparisons at 5,..., 12, and Lucas-only
comparisons at 13,..., 20 and 29,..., 36. The contacts carrying Lucas information cover the three
pointsS,T,Z , and those carrying beta information cover A,T .
Proof. The ﬁrst ﬁve positions are shared. Each of A,S,T,Z has its original block of eight ﬁnite
positions, in that order. Thus the Lucas set contains the ﬁve shared positions and all three ﬁnite
blocks of S,T,Z ; the beta set contains the ﬁve shared positions and the two ﬁnite blocks of A,T .
The unchanged two-slope rational-height argument applies separately to these sets. No position is
deleted when two slopes, raw values, or assigned primes coincide. The two maxima used below are
consequentlyBL = max(|s|,|t|,|z|) and BAT = max(|n|,|t|).
Lemma 15.7 (Doubled formal comparisons) . At a split good prime above the cutoﬀ, if v(z(R))> 0,
then either R or R +T0 is in the formal group. Whenever the relevant rational function is deﬁned,
v(logE([2]R)) = v(z(R)),
with inﬁnite valuation for R =O or T0.
Proof. For an aﬃne nontorsion point, z(R)2 = x(R)/(x(R)2−D). If v(x) = 0 , the right side
has nonpositive valuation. If v(x) < 0, the point is formal. If v(x) > 0, translation by T0 gives
x′ =−D/x and z′ =−z, so the translated point is formal with the same parameter valuation.
Doubling kills T0 and is an isometry on the formal group at an odd good prime. The asserted
equality follows from the retained logarithm isometry. The two other nonzero two-torsion points
are poles of z and are not in the domain.
Private primary points may therefore lie in E(K) rather than 2E(K). If [2]P = [2α(P )]P∗,
their coeﬃcients α(P ) may be half-integral. They are integral at each consumed odd prime. One
compares logarithms only after writing an actual integer-multiple identity between formal points;
torsion is not discarded by equating these half-indices. The primary f tests still put the primary
points themselves in the formal group. An alternate test of z(P−Q)/z(J), together with the
opposite-place f (J) test, implies v(z(P−Q)) > 0, and the lemma supplies the needed doubled
logarithm.

Lemma 15.9 (Local conic and tree deductions) . Assume the ordinary pole gives no negative odd
valuation to nonzero rW 2 outside its ﬁxed exceptional primes. At any p> 76176 with oddR =vp(r),
the equations above imply R >0 and choose one root ρ∈ Qp of 2 such that vp(ai +ρbi)≥ ei for
every retained ﬁeld.
Proof. The conic makes δ =vp(θ2− 2) = R + 2vp(sc) odd. It cannot be negative, since then vp(θ2)
would be odd; it cannot be zero. Thus δ > 0, θ is a unit, and one root ρ satisﬁes vp(θ−ρ) = δ
while the other factor is a unit. The summands in Nr(u,v ) have opposite valuation parity and
cannot cancel. The literal leaf proves W ⁄= 0 . If R <0, ascent along that leaf gives vp(W )≤ 0,
contradicting the pole conclusion. Hence R> 0. The pole conclusion gives vp(W )>−R/2. Descent
through the tree uses
vp(Nr(u,v ))>−cR =⇒vp(u)>−cR/2, v p(v)>−(c + 1)R/2,
128with 0≤c< 1, and gives vp(Wi)>−R. Consequently vp(Ti)> (2ei−1)R≥ 2ei−1, so vp(Ti)≥ 2ei.
The two summands of Ti also have opposite parity, yielding vp(Yi)≥ ei and δ + 2vp(bi)≥ 2ei. It
follows that vp((θ−ρ)bi)≥ei and hence the claimed ﬁeld order. Zero summands are treated with
inﬁnite valuation. No integrality of ai,bi and no global squareclass of Fi was assumed.
The proof uses vp(Ti)≥ 2ei, not the generally unjustiﬁed stronger bound 2eiR. It is independent
of the elliptic meaning of the ﬁelds.

Lemma 15.12 (Four-witness anchor). For rational a,b , both belong to Z(2)∩ Z(3) if and only if
9 + 3(a2 +b2) is a norm-one trace for the quaternion algebra (2, 3). Consequently the original
two-coeﬃcient anchor is equivalent to
CH− 9C2− 3f 20− 3f 21 = 0, H 2− 8U 2− 12V 2 + 24W 2a− 4C2 = 0.
Proof. By Deﬁnition 4, Observation 5 and Facts (a), (b), (c), (e) in the proof of Proposition 6 of
[83], the algebra is ramiﬁed exactly at 2, 3, the trace set is unrestricted at the other places, and it
is integral at its two ramiﬁed places 2, 3, contains integral traces with residues 1 at 2 and 0 at 3,
and is determined by these local trace conditions. These are the explicit trace facts used here, in
addition to the older sum-of-traces criterion. If m = min(vp(a),vp(b)) < 0, then at 3 the sum of
squares has valuation 2m, while at 2 it has valuation 2m for unequal valuations and 2m + 1 for
equal ﬁnite valuations. Thus 9 + 3(a2 +b2) would be nonintegral, proving one implication, with
zeros covered by inﬁnite valuation. Conversely integral a,b give a trace congruent to zero at 3. At
2 it is odd or belongs to 4Z2. For the latter case put x0 = h/2∈ 2Z2, where h is this trace. If
130x0≡ 2 (mod 4) , then (x20− 1)/3≡ 1 (mod 8) is a square, giving x20− 3v2 = 1. If x0≡ 0 (mod 4) ,
then 1−x20/6 is a square, and u = v = 1 , w2 = 1−x20/6 gives x20− 2u2− 3v2 + 6w2 = 1 . The
local trace criterion supplies rational witnesses. Finally divide the two homogeneous equations by
C2, which is allowed because the retained constant C is nonzero. This is an equality of existential
projections, not a rational formula choosing quaternion witnesses.

Integer completeness and the reverse input implication use the two height contradictions below; no
conversion of arbitrary old proxy witnesses is asserted.

15.9 Composition, completeness and ﬁnite realization
For integral input ﬁrst set β = X 2 + 2 and take A = βP∗, whose doubled index is 2β. Choose
J0,S together in the old subgroup with the required scalar and full anchor divisibilities. Their
doubled-index ratio is unchanged. The Lucas existence lemma then gives t,z for the now ﬁxed s,
and take T = tR, Z = zR. Fix the eight abscissa coeﬃcients and denominator data before using
the prime-pattern theorem. Exclude the divisors of the ﬁxed nonzero s as well as every earlier ﬁnite
exceptional set.
Next choose one common comparison point J using the stated least common multiple, then
choose the private points separately for each occurrence, using precisely the target-two CRT rule.
Construct all joint-graph, reciprocal-G, normalized-ratio and shared-square ﬁelds. Only then choose
each private conic point and its norm/pole witnesses by simultaneous approximation. Repeated
primes retain their common orientation but separate conic and private-point witnesses. The con-
structions above give every required equation. In the reverse direction the conic and pole decode
ﬁeld orders at every odd-contact prime, without an ordinariness assumption. The graph and dou-
bled logarithm arguments give integer-index congruences, and the two height contradictions give
the integer input. The exact fusions preserve the speciﬁed retained coordinates; arithmetic source
replacements use the full soundness and completeness argument rather than a claim about arbitrary
old private witness tuples.
Finally each selected source must be compiled afresh into rational quadratic residual equations
with the original input coordinate retained. For real, hence rational, values the all-weights-one
sum of their squares vanishes exactly when every residual vanishes. Clearing nonzero rational row
denominators preserves those zero sets and gives an integral polynomial of degree at most four. This
elementary last step does not prove the inherited arithmetic inputs. The exact source, complete
ﬂattening map, allowed algebraic transformations, and full coeﬃcient collection must be checked
for the single selected endpoint. None of the local identities, allocation counts or isolated coeﬃcient
bounds in this appendix is an assertion of that ﬁnal check.
Theorem 15.22 (The selected arithmetic source) . With precisely the inherited arithmetic hypothe-
ses and source-version qualiﬁcations stated above, the source consisting of the selected equations has
rational input projection Z. Its input coordinate is the original rational number X.
Proof. First suppose that rational witnesses satisfy the source. At every prime above the cutoﬀ with
an odd raw-contact valuation, the private conic, linear-unit pole and constant-left norm tree give
the required ﬁeld orders, by Lemmas 15.9 and 15.21. This argument starts at arbitrary odd-contact
primes and does not assume that they are ordinary. The joint graph supplies actual primary points
and their regular diﬀerence parameters. Positive primary- f valuations put the primary points in
the formal group; the common conjugate- f test and each alternate test allow Lemma 15.7 to be
applied to the doubled diﬀerence. Lemma 15.18, with denominator order 12 in beta-only mode and
40 otherwise, gives the integer-index congruences for each retained numerator. The beta quotient
equations and Lemma 15.14 therefore give the precise local tests used in Theorem 15.15.
136The retained scalar system gives its order- 32 divisibility, while the four-witness anchor is equiv-
alent to the original two-place condition. The general-root height and anchor estimates establish
every cutoﬀ of that theorem with H∗ = 5H. The three-mode position sets cover exactly the two
maxima needed in its proof. It follows ﬁrst that F (s,t,z ) = 0 , then that 2β = n∈ Z. Write
X =u/v in lowest terms with v >0. Since 2X 2 = 2β− 4 is integral, v2 divides 2; hence v = 1 and
X∈ Z.
Conversely ﬁx any X∈ Z. Set β = X 2 + 2 and A = βP∗. Choose the scalar pair S,J 0 in the
retained subgroup using the inherited scalar and full anchor construction, and choose t,z by the
inherited Lucas existence lemma for the resulting nonzero integer doubled index s. Set T = tR,
Z = zR. All four roots and the two common roots are nontorsion and admit the general-root
charts. Fix their rational abscissa coeﬃcients and denominator data before applying Lemma 15.3.
Its ﬁxed-system prime theorem permits the ﬁnite exclusion of divisors of s, as required by the
normalized Lucas construction.
Choose one common J by the least common multiple in Lemma 15.18; at each occurrence
choose its private points using that lemma’s three compatible congruences. No congruences between
diﬀerent occurrences are imposed. The joint graph, exact reciprocal evaluators, beta quotients and
normalized Lucas equations then have the stated witnesses and local orders. The explicit order of
choices in Section 15.9 now chooses each private conic after its ﬁeld coeﬃcients are ﬁxed. Conic
approximation supplies the integral packed leaves and a unit norm root; the ﬁxed-parameter pole
realization supplies its remaining witnesses. This remains true with the ﬁrst denominator changed
to r. The shared square and direct tangent equations are exact identities with the reconstruction
maps proved above. Thus every equation of the selected source has a rational solution at this input.
This proves both implications without identifying arbitrary old and new private witness tuples.

15.11 Quadratic reductions preserving the rational input projection
We describe the algebraic reductions applied to the new, ﬁxed source. All scalar coordinates range
over Q, and coordinate zero is the original input X. Each ﬁeld equation denotes its two rational
coeﬃcient equations. These reductions prove equality of rational input projections to that source;
its arithmetic interpretation is Theorem 15.22.
Deﬁnitions, row operations, and interfaces. An equation cv−F = 0 , with c∈ Q∗ and
F independent of v, permits substitution v = F/c in every other equation and deletion of v.
Restriction is the forward solution map, and adjoining v = F/c is its inverse. Each use below
retains degree at most two after substitution. The initial quadratic lift uses the reverse operation:
introduce targets for products of two available coordinates until the source residuals are quadratic.
Evaluating these product deﬁnitions and forgetting their targets give the two solution maps. A
previously deﬁned product can be reused only with its retained deﬁning equation.
An operation replacing Ri by aRi +bRj, while retaining Rj, has inverse Ri = (R′i−bRj)/a
when a⁄= 0 and i⁄= j. Thus every recorded linear row operation preserves the simultaneous zero
set. A shear replaces an old witness by v = v′ +cw, with ﬁxed rational c and another witness w
retained. Its forward and reverse coordinate maps are v′ = v−cw and v = v′ +cw. This is an
invertible rational coordinate change and preserves quadratic degree.
Every removed internal coordinate must have no occurrence outside the replaced rows, unless
the stated substitution is made in every such row. Retained coordinates keep their identities and
values. All new coordinates are fresh, and input coordinate zero is never a target of deletion or
redeﬁnition. These conditions apply to the current system after all preceding transformations.
Elimination modulo retained equations. For a recorded relation elimination, a current deﬁn-
ing row has the form x−N (y) = 0 up to nonzero rational scale, with N quadratic and independent of
the deleted witness x. For each other aﬀected row Fj(x,y ) the certiﬁcate gives an exact polynomial
identity
Fj(N (y),y ) = Gj(y) +
∑
k
Mjk (y)Hk(y),
up to the recorded nonzero row scales. Every Hk is an unchanged, x-free row, and every new
row Gj is at most quadratic. Forgetting x takes an old solution to a new one, since the Hk
138vanish. Conversely adjoin x =N (y): the same identities recover every aﬀected Fj = 0 . Complete
occurrence checks ensure that no other row uses x. This is an identity over Q, not a division by a
possibly zero ﬁeld value. The retained support rows are never replaced in this operation.
The recorded contact-power circuits. The circuit replacements preserve the boundary values
r2,r 24,r 80. The replacement deﬁnitions are
r2 =r2, r 4 =r22, r 8 =r24, r 16 =r28, r 24 =r16r8, r 40 =r24r16, r 80 =r240.
Their exact outputs agree with the old acyclic product deﬁnitions. For either direction retain r
and the boundary outputs, and assign each internal target its displayed power of r. These maps
work also for r = 0 and use no inverse. Each deleted internal target has no use outside the replaced
deﬁnitions; every new internal target is fresh. The certiﬁcate checks those conditions and the exact
output identities in the current system. Only circuit events with these retained powers are included
in this description.

and deﬁne Ψ0(b) by the existence of e,p,q,s ∈ Q satisfying
p2 =B(1−e)(1 + 3e), h =e(B−b2), Bq 2 = (s2−Bh)(s2 + 3Bh),

denominator, is positive and square in Q2, Q3, Q17, and
b< 17, v 2(b)≥ 3, v 3(b)≥ 1, v 17(b)≥ 2.
No additional nonzero condition on e,p,q,s,h is imposed.
Proof. Taking the preparation witnesses t = 0 and U = b in ( 11) gives exactly the displayed
equations. The obstruction therefore follows from Lemma 14.5, including its degenerate witness
cases.
For completeness put
V =B(B−b2) = B2(1−b/17).
The real inequality makes V positive. The valuation bounds make 1−b/17 a square at two, three,
and seventeen: its diﬀerence from one has valuation at least three at two and at least one at the
two odd primes. Thus V is square at these three places.
142Letµ andθ be the positive squarefree representatives of B andV . The prescribed squareclasses
of b giveµ≡ 17 (mod 24) ; in particular µ> 1 and its support contains seventeen but neither two
nor three. Also θ≡ 1 (mod 24) , and seventeen is absent from its support. At a prime 𝓁|µ outside
the three exceptions, v𝓁(b) is positive and odd, since the reduced denominator of b is square. Hence
1−b/17 is a square unit there, so V and θ are local squares. The same assertion at seventeen was
already arranged. These facts make the supports disjoint.
Conversely, if𝓁|θ, then 𝓁 /∈{ 2, 3, 17}. If v𝓁(b)> 0, the valuation of V is 2v𝓁(b); if v𝓁(b)< 0, it is

and 1−b/17≡ 0 (mod 𝓁). It follows that B = 17b≡ 172 (mod 𝓁) is a nonzero square unit. This
proves the other mutual-squareness condition.

Lemma 16.3 (Real bounds independent of later progression moduli) . LetG be the closure of the
integer orbit
{([n]P∗, [n]Pσ∗ ) : n∈ Z}⊂ E(R)×Eσ(R),
and let G0 be its identity component. Fix a ﬁnite collection of rational functions formed from
nonzero integral multiples and ﬁxed translates of either elliptic coordinate, with all denominators
nonzero as rational functions on the relevant curve. Suppose their pole set is contained in a ﬁnite
union of inverse images of ﬁnite sets under these nonzero multiplication-and-translation maps on
either factor. There is a bound C such that every nonconstant integer progression contains arbi-
trarily large positive indices at which all these functions are deﬁned and their real absolute values
are at most C. The bound is chosen from the functions before the progression modulus is speciﬁed.
If m =|G/G0|, m| L, and m| h0, the progression h≡ h0 (mod L) is dense in G0, and its
positive tail meets every nonempty open subset of G0 inﬁnitely often.
Proof. The real elliptic groups are compact Lie groups. Their closed subgroup G is a compact
abelian Lie group with ﬁnitely many components, and G0 is a torus. The projection of G0 onto
each elliptic identity circle is surjective. Indeed the projected integer orbit is inﬁnite, since P∗ has
inﬁnite order and each real embedding is injective on the group of algebraic points. If the connected
projection were trivial, the projected G would be ﬁnite; a nontrivial connected closed subgroup of
a circle is the whole circle.
Each nonzero multiplication map on a circle has ﬁnite ﬁbers. Consequently each of the pre-
scribed inverse images of a ﬁnite set has Haar measure zero in every component of G. The ﬁnite
union of pole sets cannot cover a component. Choose in each component a nonempty open neigh-
borhood whose compact closure avoids the poles. Continuity bounds all the ﬁxed rational functions
on these ﬁnitely many compact sets by one constant C.
For a progression h =r +kn, its closure is [r]P∗ + [k]G. Multiplication by the positive integer
k maps G0 onto itself, so this closure contains an entire component of G. The positive tail of the
progression is dense in its closure: compactness gives unbounded positive multiples tending to the
143identity, so each negative multiple is also a limit of positive multiples. It therefore meets the chosen
neighborhood in that component at arbitrarily large positive indices.
Ifm|k, then [k]G =G0. If also m|r, the coset is G0 itself. Apply this with k =L andr =h0
for the ﬁnal assertion.
17 The shared-point quartic in 962 variables
This section gives a new complete quadratic compilation of the shared-point construction in the pub-
lic agentic notes [ 6]. The shared comparison points, reciprocal-trace detectors, ﬁxed height scales,
direct Lucas test, and aﬃne anchor change are arithmetic and algebraic improvements recorded in
those notes. The simultaneous prime theorem used to choose the shared points is Sun’s Theorem 4.1,
in the precise form of Theorem I.1 below. Our contribution here is an independently speciﬁed gate
program, its source veriﬁcation, and further changes of coordinates and circuit identities leading to
a 962-variable polynomial after 32 certiﬁed eliminations. The deﬁnition does not depend on an un-
available coeﬃcient table. The relevant passages in the public notes are shared-point construction ,
ﬁxed height scales , and quadratic compilation and anchor translation .
We retain the arithmetic conclusions and conventions of Section 14, including the restricted-
converse input to Lemma 14.4. In this section the height estimate is Theorem 14.3, with exponent
24 and its original constant H∗; it is not the exponent-19 estimate used for the preceding quartic.
Thus
1≤H∗< 24783227.
The exact ﬁnite compilation and coeﬃcient assertions below require no arithmetic conjecture. Iden-
tifying the rational input projection with Z uses the stated arithmetic results and the simultaneous
prime theorem.
The point, pole, and correction variables below specify the arithmetic source predicate. The
ﬁnal compilation subsection gives explicit maps to its implementation coordinates.

Lemma 17.2 (The logarithm estimate at unramiﬁed places) . Let p >76176 be a rational prime

Lemma 17.3 (The three-witness anchor) . Equation (47) is equivalent to f0/C,f 1/C∈ Z(2)∩ Z(3).
For the nonzero integer label a of S = [a]P∗ it implies
0⁄=a∈ 2 3225−1Z, |a|> (21H∗)13/2> (20H∗)4.
Its permitted labels contain arbitrarily divisible positive integers.
Proof. Put R = Z(2)∩ Z(3). The quaternion algebra (2, 3)Q ramiﬁes exactly at 2 and 3. The
quadratic embedding criterion [ 89, Proposition 14.6.7] shows, for rational t⁄= 0, that 1− 2/(3t2) is
represented by 2x2 +3y2−6z2 precisely when it is nonsquare in both Q2 and Q3. This last condition
is exactlyt∈R. Indeed at 2 a positive valuation of t gives odd valuation of the expression, valuation
zero gives residue 3 modulo 8, and negative valuation gives a square in 1 + 8Z2. At 3, nonnegative
valuation of t gives odd negative valuation, while negative valuation gives a square in 1 + 3Z3. The
expression is nonzero because t2 = 2/3 has no rational solution. Multiplying by 3t2 gives
t∈R ⇐⇒ ∃u,v,w ∈ Q : u2 + 6v2− 2w2− 3t2 + 2 = 0.
147The case t = 0 has witnesses (0, 0, 1). A rational-square value of 1− 2/(3t2) is excluded by the
division-algebra criterion as well: a nonzero trace-zero quaternion cannot square to a nonzero
rational square in a division algebra.
The form A2 +AB−B2 has no nontrivial zero over F2 or F3. Scaling a pair therefore proves
vp(a2 +ab−b2) = 2 min(vp(a),vp(b)) for p = 2, 3. Apply the preceding three-witness formula to
(f 20 +f0f1−f 21 )/C2 and multiply its witnesses by C2. This gives exactly ( 47).
The local calculation in Section 14.2.2 applies with exponent 226: the reduction of P∗ at 3 has
order two and v3(z(2P∗)) = 1 . For even nonzero a it gives v3(f ([a]P∗)) = 2 + 2 v3(a). At 2 the
rational components of f ([a]P∗) are integral. The anchor is consequently equivalent to 0⁄= a∈
2 3225−1Z. Its minimum possible absolute value has binary logarithm greater than
1 + 32 (225− 1) = 50331647.5,
whereas log2((21H∗)13/2)< 132 (5 + 4783227) = 31091008. The last comparison with (20H∗)4 follows
from 2113> 208 and H∗≥ 1. The subgroup description proves the last assertion.

Lemma 17.4 (One place for every test) . LetR∈ Q∗, Dj∈K, and positive integers mj deﬁne

Proof. Set α = vp(R). The nonconstant term in each nontrivial factor has odd valuation α +

Lemma 17.5 (The two-anchor comparison) . Write the integer labels of (A,S,T,Z ) as (n,a,t,b ).
If the ﬁrst fourteen tests in (38) have their speciﬁed orders at a place p|p, p> 76176, then
Li∈O Kp, L i≡ni (mod pki), (n1,n 2,n 3,n 4) = (n,a,t,b ).
This holds at split and inert primes.
Proof. WriteH = [h]P∗ and Ui = [ui]P∗. The order-one tests put H,Ui in the formal group at p
and V0,Vi in the formal group at the conjugate place. Positive valuation of f = 1/x gives formal
membership on this good model. The guarded points give h⁄= 0, 4. Their full logarithm ratios,
after conjugating the second, are
ri =ui/h, r ′i = (ui− 4ni)/(h− 4).
148These identities follow from the homomorphism property on equal formal multiples, and do not
require P∗ itself to be formal. At least one of h,h− 4 is a p-unit. Its ratio is integral, and
Lemma 17.2 gives the same valuation to its truncated ratio. The cross test makes the other
truncated ratio integral too. Both relative errors are therefore absolute errors, and Li≡ ri≡ r′i
modulo pki. Multiplication by h,h− 4 and subtraction give 4Li≡ 4ni. Divide by four. At an inert
prime conjugation preserves the single local valuation, so the same argument applies.

Lemma 17.7 (The two reduced group cardinalities) . Letp> 76176 be prime and let π∈ Z[i] have
norm p, with π≡ 9 (mod 16) and π≡ 1 + 3i (mod 7) . The two reductions of E/K at the places
abovep have group cardinalities N (π− 1) and N (π + 1), in some order.
Proof. The congruences give p≡ 1 (mod 8) and p≡ 3 (mod 7) . Thus p splits in K and (7/p) =
−1. The reductions of d = 3−
√
2 have product 7 and opposite square classes. The two curves
y2 =x3−d2x are consequently opposite quadratic twists of y2 =x3−x and have opposite traces.
The Frobenius calculation already given in Section 14.4.1 applies: the order-four automorphism
is deﬁned over Fp, its rational centralizer is Q(i), and integrality puts Frobenius in Z[i]. Write it as
a +bi, so a2 +b2 =p and the two cardinalities are p + 1− 2a andp + 1 + 2a. Full rational two-torsion
makes both cardinalities divisible by four. Since p≡ 1 (mod 4) , this forces a odd and b even. The
real part of π is odd and its imaginary part is even. Unique factorization in Z[i] therefore makes
a +bi one of ±π,±π; the associates with factor i have the wrong parity. Hence the unordered
cardinalities are exactly N (π− 1),N (π + 1).

Lemma 17.10 (Exact point-chart maps) . The chart (48) describes precisely the ﬁnite doubles used
in the shared-point source, with the same rational witnesses up to the following invertible changes.
It preserves all required nonzero point-coordinate domains. The rational component X0 of X is
positive.
152Proof. For an old halfpoint (a,b ) withb2 =a3−Da and its tangent equation, both a,b are nonzero.
Indeed b = 0 would give a = 0 or a2 =D, whereas the tangent numerator 3a2−D would be −D
or 2D. Then a = 0 is excluded by the curve equation. Deﬁne
t =b/a, h = (a2 +D)/(2b) = a/t−t/2.
The curve equation gives t2 = a−D/a. Substitution gives t2(h2−t2/4) = D. The doubling
formulas give exactly X =h2 and Y =h(t2/2−h2).
Conversely, a chart solution has t⁄= 0 because D⁄= 0. Set
a =th +t2/2, b =at, m =h +t.
If a = 0, then h =−t/2, contradicting the chart equation. Thus b⁄= 0. Direct expansion gives

andm(a−h2)−b =h(t2/2−h2). The substitutions are inverse, so they recover exactly the old ﬁnite

true for the unchanged guarded diﬀerences and subsequent doublings.
Both real embeddings of D are positive. Since t⁄= 0 , the chart equation implies h2 = t2/4 +
D/t2 > 0 under both embeddings. Hence X0, the average of these two positive values, is positive.
The ﬁve aﬃne subtraction formulas keep their original inverses of the diﬀerences of abscissas.
Neither a subtraction guard nor a point occurrence is deleted. Relative to the direct halfpoint
doubling circuit, the chart saves two rational arithmetic coordinates for each of the nine points.

Proof of Theorem 17.9. For a source solution apply the point-chart, correction-chain and pole sub-
stitutions, and evaluate all new assignment and quotient coordinates in dependency order. The
identities and domain proofs above show that every structural residual vanishes. Conversely apply
the inverse substitutions, restore every deleted quotient by its proved nonzero denominator, and
evaluate the source assignments. Every source point, logarithm, test, detector input, scalar value
and anchor value is recovered. These maps ﬁx X0. The aﬃne anchor change, certiﬁed elimina-
tions and nonzero row normalizations preserve the same projection. Over Q, ( 57) vanishes exactly
156when all 919 residuals vanish. This proves the projection statement, and Theorem 17.8 gives its
conditional arithmetic interpretation.
