18 Why a single deﬁning polynomial must have degree at least
four
The argument in this section comes from the agentic notes [ 6, section on unconditional lower
bounds]. We give its specialization to Q, including the treatment of singular cubics, cones and
geometric reducibility. It is independent of the arithmetic inputs to our constructions.
ForF∈ Q[T,Y 1,...,Y r], write
SF ={t∈ Q :∃y∈ Qr, F(t,y ) = 0}.
All degrees below are total degrees, including the input variable T .
Theorem 18.1 (Degree obstruction) . If SF = Z, then degF≥ 4.
We use two published results. The integral form of Hilbert’s irreducibility theorem says that an
irreducible polynomial g(T,Y )∈ Q(T )[Y ] admits inﬁnitely many specializations T =t∈ Z which
preserve its degree in Y and are irreducible over Q. Deleting ﬁnitely many values also avoids its
coeﬃcient poles and any prescribed nonzero polynomial in t. This integral formulation is recalled
in the introduction of Müller [ 5]; merely obtaining specializations in Q would not suﬃce here. The
second result is Kollár’s theorem [ 4, Theorem 2]: a geometrically integral cubic hypersurface of
dimension at least two over a perfect ﬁeld is unirational over that ﬁeld if it has a rational point
and is not a cone. This is a special case of his statement for irreducible cubics. We apply it only
over Q, and remove every vertex direction before applying it. In particular, we do not assume that
a cone over a plane cubic is unirational.
Lemma 18.2. A nonconstant rational function R∈ Q(U1,...,U m) cannot take only integer values
at the rational points of a nonempty Zariski-open subset of AmQ on which it is deﬁned.
Proof. Shrink the open subset to a nonempty principal open subset and choose a connected real
open ball in it, avoiding the denominator of R. Rational points are dense in this ball. If their images
belong to the closed set Z, continuity puts the image of the entire ball in Z. Connectedness then
makes R constant on the ball. Its numerator minus that constant times its denominator vanishes
on a real open set, hence is the zero polynomial. This contradicts nonconstancy.
157Consequently, if an integral variety X/Q is unirational and f ∈ Q(X) is nonconstant, the
rational values of f cannot all be integers. Indeed, compose f with a dominant rational map from
projective space to X, and restrict to a nonempty aﬃne open subset of its domain. Dominance
keeps this composite nonconstant, so Lemma 18.2 applies.
Lemma 18.3. If degF≤ 2 and SF⊆ Z, then |SF|≤ 2.
Proof. Otherwise choose rational zeros a,b,c with distinct input coordinates. If they lie on an aﬃne
line, the restriction of F to that line has three zeros and degree at most two, so vanishes identically.
The input is nonconstant on the line and takes every rational value, a contradiction.
If the points are not collinear, let L be their rational aﬃne plane. If F|L = 0, a line in L with
nonconstant input gives the same contradiction. If F|L factors over Q into aﬃne linear polynomials,
its zero set consists of at most two rational lines. One contains two of the three selected points and
again has nonconstant input. A nonzero polynomial of degree at most one cannot vanish at three
noncollinear points.
It remains to consider an irreducible quadratic on L. Its projective conic has a smooth rational
point: a reduced irreducible quadratic in characteristic zero has at most one geometric singular
point, while the three chosen aﬃne points are distinct. A conic with a smooth rational point
is rational. Explicitly, intersecting it with the pencil of lines through that point gives a rational
parametrization by taking the second intersection point. The input function is nonconstant because
it takes three diﬀerent values. Composing it with this parametrization contradicts Lemma 18.2.
Proof of Theorem 18.1. Suppose SF = Z and degF ≤ 3. The polynomial is neither zero nor a
nonzero constant. Lemma 18.3 excludes degree at most two. It also excludes a reducible cubic:
every nonconstant irreducible factor then has degree at most two, and the ﬁnite union of their
projections cannot equal Z. Thus F is irreducible of degree three.
HomogenizeF toH on a rational vector space E, using X0 as the homogenizing coordinate and
X1 for the input coordinate. Thus on X0⁄= 0 the input is X1/X0. The homogeneous polynomial
H is irreducible over Q. If it is geometrically reducible, its geometric irreducible factors form a
single Galois orbit with equal degrees. In characteristic zero they occur without repetition. Since
the total degree is three, there are exactly three conjugate linear factors. Every rational zero lies
on each of their hyperplanes. Take rational zeros normalized by X0 = 1 with inputs zero and one.
Their whole aﬃne joining line lies on all three hyperplanes and its input takes every value in Q, a
contradiction. We may therefore assume H is geometrically integral.
Deﬁne the rational vertex subspace
W ={v∈E :DvH = 0 identically}, D vH(x) = ddsH(x +sv)⏐⏐⏐⏐s=0.
These are linear conditions on v. In characteristic zero, DvH = 0 is equivalent to H(x +sv) = H(x)
for all x,s . Thus H descends to a cubic H on E/W . After a basis of W is extended to one of E,
this simply deletes the coordinates on which H does not depend. The quotient has no remaining
vertex direction. This assertion holds over the algebraic closure as well, since the kernel of a matrix
over Q commutes with extension of scalars. Hence its projective hypersurface is not a cone.
For v∈ W and a rational zero x with x0 = 1 , translation by sv gives rational zeros whose
inputs, whenever deﬁned, are
x1 +sv1
1 +sv0
.
Unlessv1 =x1v0, this is a nonconstant fractional linear function of s, with coﬁnite image in Q. Its
image cannot be contained in Z. Applying the resulting equality ﬁrst at input zero and then at
158input one gives v0 =v1 = 0 for every v∈W . Therefore X0 and X1 descend to independent linear
forms on E/W .
Choose quotient coordinates beginning with these forms. The aﬃne chart X0 = 1 ofH = 0 has
exactly the same input projection as F = 0. To see both inclusions, project rational points from E
to E/W , and in the reverse direction use a rational linear section of that projection. This section
preservesX0 and X1, and H is constant along each ﬁber.
If the quotient projective hypersurface has dimension at least two, Kollár’s theorem applies.
It is geometrically integral, has a rational point in X0 = 1 , is not a cone, and the ground ﬁeld
is perfect. It is therefore unirational. Its input function is nonconstant, since its rational values
include all integers. The consequence of Lemma 18.2 then contradicts that its input projection is
contained in Z.
If the quotient has dimension one, its aﬃne chart is deﬁned by an irreducible polynomial
G(T,Y ) ∈ Q[T,Y ]. If its degree in Y is zero, its input projection is ﬁnite. If that degree is
one, solving the linear equation for Y gives a rational point above every rational T outside a ﬁnite
set, also impossible. If its degree in Y is at least two, Gauss’s lemma makes it irreducible in Q(T )[Y ].
Integral Hilbert irreducibility supplies an integer t for which G(t,Y ) remains irreducible of that
same degree. It has no rational root, contradicting SG = Z. Finally, a zero-dimensional projective
quotient has only ﬁnitely many rational points and cannot have input projection Z. These exhaust
the possibilities and prove the theorem.
Remark 18.4. This lower bound concerns a single polynomial equation and counts degree in every
variable. It allows systems of quadratic equations; over Q, their sum of squares is quartic. It puts
no lower bound here on the number of variables in a degree-four deﬁnition.
A Dependency order in the arithmetic argument
The even coeﬃcient bound uses stationary-base selection as well as integrality. The odd minimum
uses a ﬁxed nonzero witness and the prior genus-index lower bound. Support removal is applied
only to the actual unramiﬁed specialization. The functional bound and determinant clearing are
proved separately, and the same corrected coordinate is used at every address. These constructions
are given in Sections 7–9 and Appendices E–G.
The conversion to ordinary integers uses the canonical model and total multiplication, fol-
lowed by the exact computation relation. Only the shared-shift separation argument in Section 11
identiﬁes the external rational variable with the ordinary value of its integer index. The literal
substitution and size calculation then apply to the same expression P∗.

The supplementary ﬁle ordinary-integer-definition.json is part of this speciﬁcation. It is a

one polynomial from its bytes. Diagnostic labels and intended-theorem strings add no equations.
The root is integers. Variable zero is the external input and variables one through
292890231904 are its rational witnesses. A nonnegative point reference r means Xr,...,X r+4.

Every model recipe conjoins 8g0− 1641 = 0 , 4g1 + 15465 = 0 , 32g2− 87483 = 0 .
C.1 Scalar nodes and ﬁxed expressions
Read nodes in their stored topological order. A const is its decimal integer; var is its local variable;
add and mul are ordinary polynomial operations. For a call to a template with p parameters,
substitute the speciﬁed node polynomials for variables 0,...,p − 1, and Xw+j for variable p +j,
where w is the witness start. The value is the callee’s entire root polynomial.
A fixed_integer evaluates a ﬁnite expression using literals, references to preceding deﬁnitions,
addition, multiplication, nonnegative powers, maximum, factorial, the catalogue product or max-
imum, and the least formal multiple. The catalogue uses the ﬁrst 22 primes in increasing order
in each unit class. The searches are deterministic and terminate by Section 12. They produce
constants, not quantiﬁed witnesses.
A psi_component uses the division-polynomial recurrence in Section 2, pair multiplication
(a,b )(c,d ) = (ac + 2bd,ad +bc) and A = (−11243, 7950). It returns component zero or one; every
index is ﬁxed. The positive ﬁxed point codes use
xn =x(P0)− ψn+1(P0)ψn−1(P0)ψn(P0)2 , y n = ψ2n(P0)2ψn(P0)4.
Nontorsion makes these ﬁxed denominators nonzero. Negative indices negate both ordinate com-
ponents.
A finite_guard_product ranges over (a,b,c )∈ [−T,T ]3∩ Z3 withab⁄=c, with ﬁxed T . Write
the ﬁfteen coordinates of the three codes as reduced Aj/Bj,Bj > 0, and put D =∏jBj. Its factor
is
15∑
j=1
(DXj− (D/Bj)Aj)2.
Multiply these factors, with empty product one. This is an integer polynomial with precisely the
ﬁnite false-triple zero set used above.
C.2 Recipe constraints and witness scope
A model-evaluation recipe sums squares of the generator equations, listed natural calls, scalar-
constant calls, add/multiply/power-value calls, and the ﬁve coordinate diﬀerences of rootEquality.
A source-variable row with a non-null natural witness start contributes exactly that natural call.
Operation kinds zero, one and two select addition, multiplication and power value. Source-node
and provenance ﬁelds add no equations.
A sum-of-squares composition sums squares of its callee polynomials; a product composition
multiplies them. Parameter [var,i] means Xi. Each private block uses its recorded start and
161length. The checked ranges are disjoint from parameters and other private blocks. These rules
include globally conjoined ﬁxed equations in active callees.

their two rational components in the basis (1,

changing the relation it deﬁnes.

The composition recipes make the remaining identiﬁcations explicit. power_value translates
the source power template with its three input codes and output code; precision translates the
ﬁve-natural-input precision polynomial, in the order (n,κ,a,𝓁,j ) after signed encoding. The recipe
close introduces A,L,J , forms those ﬁve signed encodings, conjoins precision, and calls value.
close_coordinates. Thus it is the full Close. The recipe main introduces one shared κ and con-
joins close and value.test at exactly that same code. The product recipe value_union joins this
branch with value.exceptions; value also imposes member onN . Finally integers existentially
introducesN and calls value with external coordinate t =X0. These are precisely Val and its input
projection. The ordered calls and private ranges are checked by auditOrdinaryIntegerPolynomial
in construction/ordinary-integer-polynomial.js. scripts/check_construction.py regen-
erates the saved expression byte for byte. The dictionary supplies the mathematical identiﬁcation;
those ﬁnite checks verify the stored syntax and wiring, not the arithmetic theorems used to prove
the predicates.

The source package includes the speciﬁcation, natural precision polynomial and construction
sources in SUPPLEMENT_MANIFEST.json. The larger proof corpus is identiﬁed by hashes and stable

E Fixed-form arithmetic and the even coeﬃcient source
This appendix supplies the constructions used in (3.1)–(3.3). Throughout, E = E𝓁 is ﬁxed, with
the lattice, parametrization and period convention of Section 7. Constants may depend on these
data and on the prescribed ﬁnite list of local types. They do not depend on the number or sizes of
the later good conductor primes.
E.1 One retained class, including discriminant 4h
All numbered primary references in this subsection are to Kato [ 55].
Fix a non-CM elliptic curve E/Q of conductor N , its weight-two newform f , T = T2E, and
V =T⊗ Q2. In the application E =E𝓁 is ﬁxed ﬁrst. Put Λ = Z2[[Γ]] = Z2[[t]], where Γ is the real
cyclotomic Galois group. Let κ :GQ→ Γ⊂ Λ× be the quotient map, and set A = Λ[1/2]. We use
arithmetic Frobenius throughout. The action on the cyclotomic group-ring coeﬃcient is the inverse
deck action.
The precise output.
Proposition E.1 (One retained Kato system and its two comparisons) . Use the ﬁxed curve, lattice
and cyclotomic convention just speciﬁed. Fix e≥ max{3,v 2(N )} large enough for the ﬁxed initial
local data; the smoothing choice is (K5). Let G be a ﬁnite elementary abelian two-group, let ψ :

of quadratic characters with one ﬁxed real sign and ﬁxed local squareclasses at the primes dividing
2N . Since the characters of G separate its elements, ψ(c) = 1 for complex conjugation c, and
ψ|GQv = 1 at every v| 2N . All varying conductor primes are good odd primes for E. Let F be
the squarefree least common multiple of the odd parts of the primitive conductors. There is a single

Proof.
The retained route and its comparison. All classes in this subsection retain the smoothing
integers of (K5). The comparison used below is the exact identity (E.1), obtained by integral
weighted corestriction and the norm relation of [ 55, Proposition 8.12]. We do not divide by the
smoothing operators, or compare a chosen generator with a separately normalized zeta class for
the varying newform. In particular the central factor is sF of (K6), whose valuation is 2e for every
F . The use of power-series zero ﬁniteness in paragraph 7 establishes nonvanishing of the supplied
Euler system in both generic sign components; it is not a comparison of two Euler systems.
1. One original lattice and one symbol combination. Kato (8.1.3), p. 180, constructs
retained-smoothed classes
c,dszm(f, 1, 1,ξ,S m)∈H 1(Z[ζm, 1/2],Lf (1)), S m = prime(2mN ), (K4)
where Lf = VOλ(f ) is the image lattice of the ﬁxed form. For ξ∈ SL2(Z), section 8.9, p. 185,
explicitly constructs the trace homomorphism into the integral induced lattice
Z2[Gal(Q(ζm)/Q)]⊗V2,Z2(Y1(N )).
Section 8.11 projects this lattice onto Lf . Auxiliary modular curve levels used in the trace do not
change its target lattice. Choose a rational identiﬁcation Lf (1)⊗ Q2 =V . One ﬁxed 2cE sends its
lattice into T .
By Kato 13.6–13.7, p. 227, the symbol span has a nonzero projection to each Betti sign line.
The union of the two rational kernels does not contain the whole span. Choose an integral ﬁnite
linear combination ξ with both projections nonzero, clearing its ﬁxed denominators once. Norm
relations and reciprocity are linear, so the same combination works at every m. Although (K4) is
written with one symbol, it henceforth denotes this ﬁxed combination.
2. Retain one smoothing choice for the whole family . Fix e≥ max{3,v 2(N )}, enlarged
only for ﬁxed initial data, and take
c =ds = 1 + 2e 3NoddF. (K5)
These integers exceed one, are prime to 6NF , are one modulo N and F , and are one modulo
every dyadic quadratic conductor under consideration. Distinctness or primality of c,ds is not a
hypothesis of Kato’s construction. The exponents (u,v ) in (4.2.4), p. 143, are (1, 1) when k =
2,r =r′ = 1. The retained-smoothed version of Theorem 6.6, p. 163, therefore has central scalar
sF = (c2−c)(d2s−ds) = c(c− 1)ds(ds− 1), v 2(sF ) = 2e. (K6)
No smoothing factor is divided out. The prime factors of cds exclude some Euler-system indices in
Kato 13.1; they are not added to the inverted arithmetic support S, as is explicit in (8.1.3) and in
the deﬁnition of Hq in section 8.2. In particular their number creates no local H 2 correction.
1663. The integral j∗ condition survives ramiﬁed transfer. For a number ﬁeld K, let XK =
SpecOK[1/2] and jK : Spec K→ XK. At ﬁnite coeﬃcients M = T/ 2aT , the low-degree Leray
sequence gives an injection
H 1(XK,jK,∗M )−→H 1(K,M )
whose image is the kernel of all inertia restrictions outside two. Indeed the higher-direct-image
stalk formula and Leray sequence [ 43, Tags 03Q9 and 03QA] identify the stalks of R1jK,∗M at a
ﬁnite place with H 1(Iv,M ); the edge map is restriction to these stalks. The generic-point map
jK is aﬃne, hence quasi-compact and quasi-separated, as required by the stalk formula. Kato’s
section 8.2, pp. 180–181, deﬁnes the integral groups by the inverse limit of these ﬁnite-coeﬃcient
groups, so this assertion applies to the classes (K4) before rationalization.
Let L/K be ﬁnite, with absolute Galois groups H⊂ GK. For a class z∈ H 1(H,M ) and an
inertia subgroup I⊂GK, the Mackey formula is
resGKI corGKH z = ∑
g∈I\GK/H
corII∩gHg −1 resgHg −1I∩gHg −1(gz). (K7)
The intersection in each summand is the upper inertia subgroup for the corresponding place of L.
If all upper inertia restrictions vanish, every summand is zero. This does not require L/K to be
unramiﬁed. It is a ﬁnite double-coset identity at each ﬁnite coeﬃcient precision. Uniqueness under
the Leray injection makes the extensions across XK compatible in a. Thus the integral j∗ condition
survives corestriction, including at primes ramiﬁed in a quadratic twisting ﬁeld.
4. Primitive twisting is an actual Euler system. Fix a constituent χ = χh of primitive
conductor D =|∆h| = 2ad, d odd, and a basis eχ of Z2(χ). Put Σ = prime(2Ndcds). For every
positive integer m with (m,cds) = 1 , set
Km = Q(ζm), L m = Q(ζDm), y m = corLm/Km(zDm⊗eχ). (K8)
The character is trivial over Lm, so tensoring is an integral map there. Formula (K7) places (K8)
in the precise j∗ group required by Kato 13.1 on its index set prime(m)∩ Σ = {2}. The larger
deﬁnition is permitted by (8.1.3), Sections 8.9–8.11 and Proposition 8.12: SDm = prime(2NDm ) is
disjoint from prime(cds), since d|F and (K5) holds. Each class uses the original f,ξ,c,d s. We will
also use this same construction on the diﬀerent index set required by [ 73, Section 0.1], as checked
below.
Form| m′, transitivity of corestriction reduces the norm of (K8) to Proposition 8.12, p. 186.
For a deck automorphism g, equivariance gives
cor((gz)⊗eχ) = χ(g)−1g cor(z⊗eχ).
Consequently the factor at a newly introduced good prime q is
Eq(χ) = 1− aqχ(q)q σ−1q + 1qσ−2q . (K9)
In Kato 13.1, pp. 224–225, the polynomial is Pq(X) = det(1 − FrqX| Tχ ) and the norm factor
is Pq(q−1σ−1q ), exactly (K9). Repeated prime powers introduce no factor because Proposition 8.12
has a product over newly added support primes. Thus the subfamily on Kato 13.1’s index set is
an Euler system of the stated lattice, not a class inferred from proportionality of rank-one Iwasawa
modules. The required norm relations on the 1999 index set are veriﬁed separately below.
167Form = 2n with n≥ a, the ﬁeld Lm can be replaced by Q(ζd2n): apply repeated-two norm
compatibility from exponent n +a to n. This introduces no factor 2a. The construction therefore
covers discriminant 4h on the same coﬁnal tail. At the coeﬃcient level it retains the full character
χ2χodd. A dyadic character nontrivial on Γ changes its action by the integral automorphism 1+t↦→
−(1 +t), or t↦→− 2−t. A dyadic sign character instead changes the ﬁnite real-sign component.
Neither operation is a division by a sign projector.
5. One coeﬃcient map produces every constituent. For n large enough to contain the
dyadic characters, put AF,n = Gal( Q(ζF 2n)/Q). Apply Shapiro to zF 2n, then the map on group
bases
Φn : Z2[AF,n]−→ Z2[Γn×G], [s]↦−→χ0(s)[¯s][ψ(s)]. (K10)
With inverse deck actions, both sides transform under g∈ GQ by the inverse of its group image.
On the target the extra scalar is χ0(g); since χ−10 = χ0, (K10) is equivariant. This is an integral
coeﬃcient homomorphism. Shapiro restriction to an inertia subgroup is the direct sum of the upper
restrictions; the same zero restrictions used in (K7) therefore remain zero after (K10). In particular
the map acts on actual integral classes. The map in (K10) is deﬁned on this ﬁnite group for the
following reason. The constituent indexed by the trivial character of G isχ0, and x◦ψ =χxχ0 for
everyx∈ ˆG. All these quadratic Dirichlet characters have conductor dividing F 2n once n is large
enough. If an element of GQ acts trivially on Q(ζF 2n), every character of G is therefore one on its
image under ψ. Character separation makes that image the identity. Thus both χ0 and ψ factor
through AF,n. Explicitly, for g∈GQ,
Φn([g−1s]) = χ0(g) [¯g]−1[ψ(g)]−1Φn([s]).
This veriﬁes equivariance on each integral group basis before evaluation at any character. The
quotient to the real tower sends each basis element to its image; its matrix has entries zero and
one. The transition maps and character evaluations are also maps of integral coeﬃcient modules.
None of these operations is the idempotent |G|−1∑gx(g)[g].
The maps Φn commute with the norm transition maps. Proposition 8.12 has no new prime when
n increases, so their images deﬁne the single class ZF in (K1). The ﬁnite-coeﬃcient degree-zero
groups are ﬁnite and satisfy Mittag–Leﬄer. The inverse-limit exact sequence [ 44, Corollary 4.1.3,
p. 96] therefore has zero lim←−1H 0 term, so it gives this degree-one class in completed cochains. Thequotient from the full cyclotomic tower to Γ is the integral quotient of group bases, equivalently
corestriction to the real subﬁeld. No factor (1 +τ )/2 occurs.
After evaluation at x, (K10) is the coeﬃcient form of the unnormalized corestriction weighted
by χx. Factor that corestriction through the primitive conductor ﬁeld in (K8). Transitivity and
Proposition 8.12 give the exact identity

Identifying this generator with the chosen local generator for Eh at two has bounded valuation:
odd squarefree h belongs to one of ﬁnitely many classes in Q×2/Q×22 , and changing a representative
by a unit square scales the quadratic twist diﬀerential by a two-adic unit. The local factors at 2N
also form a ﬁxed ﬁnite nonzero list. At a good active prime the local L-factor is one; at an unused
good prime (K9) at t = 0 is #Eh(Fq)/q. This proves (K3), including all its uniformity claims and
the assertion about zero values.
7. Every hypothesis of Kato 13.4(2). Apply the theorem to the integral system (K8), after
multiplying by the ﬁxed lattice comparison if needed. Its hypotheses are:
• Each generic sign component is nonzero. For a ﬁnite-order cyclotomic character ρ, core-
striction and the ρ-sum combine into the single (χρ)-weighted sum on the original class.
Theorems 6.6 and 9.7 apply with the same ξ,c,d s. Their smoothing factors are nonzero,
since c,ds > 1 cannot be roots of unity. Theorem 13.5(2), p. 227, gives only ﬁnitely many
zero L-values among characters supported on the ﬁxed ﬁnite set prime(2Nd ). There are in-
ﬁnitely many ρ of each parity. A torsion Iwasawa class can have a nonzero specialization at
only ﬁnitely many such characters, since a nonzero annihilating power series has only ﬁnitely
many cyclotomic zeros. Hence both generic sign components are nonzero.
• The two real eigenspaces of Tχ have rank one.
• The theorem uses inverse Frobenius in its purity condition. On Tχ its good eigenvalues have
complex absolute value q−1/2, so its integer w is−1.
• Vχ is irreducible; twisting preserves irreducibility of the non-CM rational Tate representation.
• The image over Q(µ2∞) contains an open subgroup of SL2(Z2). Intersecting with kerχ
keeps an open subgroup, which contains a nonidentity unipotent. It ﬁxes a one-dimensional
subspace and gives condition (v).
These are all rational hypotheses. The residual irreducibility, integral cokernel and p⁄= 2 conditions
of part (3) are not used. Theorem 13.4(1) gives torsion H2(Tχ ), and part (2) gives, at every height-
one prime away from two,
𝓁H2(Tχ )0,p≤𝓁(Ap/Jp), H20 = ker(H2→ H2loc). (K15)
The ﬁniteness and Euler characteristic (12.2.1)–(12.2.2), p. 220, give generic rank one in H1.
Its localization away from two is torsion-free. Indeed a torsion element for a scalar prime would
come from invariants of the corresponding ﬁnite-dimensional rational coeﬃcient algebra at that
prime; its coeﬃcient action is abelian, whereas the open commutator subgroup on Vχ has no ﬁxed
vectors. The coeﬃcient exact sequence excludes these invariants. The scalar specialization need
not be a ﬁnite-order character. Thus H1p is free of rank one. Localization commutes with Hom for
this ﬁnitely presented module. Writing y2∞ =ae in a local basis gives Jp = (a). Formula (K15) is
exactly the index bound for the supplied class, not for an arbitrarily rescaled generator.
The published prime-two Euler-system input. Forp = 2 we use the rational, away-from-
two case of [ 73, Theorem 0.8, pp. 317–318], with the stronger hypothesis in its condition 0.6(ii), to
justify the application of [ 55, Theorem 13.4(2)]. For our non-CM elliptic representation the image
ofGQab contains an open subgroup of SL2(Z2), as stated in [ 73, Example 0.6.2, pp. 317–318], using
170Serre’s open-image theorem. A quadratic character χ is trivial on GQab. Thus the same subgroup
acts on Vχ and contains a matrix
( 1 b
0 1
)
withb⁄= 0. Its ﬁxed space over Q2 has dimension one. This
supplies the unipotent in GQab, not merely in the cyclotomic subgroup. The latter subgroup also
contains this open SL2 subgroup, so Vχ remains absolutely irreducible over Q(µ2∞). Indeed, a line
invariant under both upper and lower nonidentity unipotents cannot exist, even over an algebraic
closure. No inference from irreducibility over GQ alone is needed.
We check the index set and the supplied class in that theorem explicitly. Put NK = 2Nd and
N ′K =cds. These integers are coprime by (K5), since d|F . Section 0.1 of [ 73] requires every index
m with NK| m and (m,N ′K) = 1 . The enlarged deﬁnition (K8) supplies all these classes. They
originate in the integral j∗ group over Z[ζm, 1/2] and map to full cohomology over Z[ζm, 1/NK].
The varying construction support SDm is not an instruction to invert all its primes in this last
scheme. Apply the ﬁxed lattice map 2cE from (K4) if needed, identically at every level.
For a prime 𝓁 - N ′K, if 𝓁| m then SDm𝓁 = SDm, so Proposition 8.12 gives norm factor one. If
𝓁 - m, the condition NK | m implies 𝓁 - 2Nd , and hence SDm𝓁\SDm ={𝓁}. The equivariance
identity before (K9) gives the factor P𝓁(𝓁−1σ−1𝓁 ) = E𝓁(χ), with P𝓁(X) = 1−a𝓁χ(𝓁)X +𝓁X 2. These
are exactly the two cases of (0.1.1) in [ 73]. Smoothing primes are excluded by N ′K and need not be
inverted.
For the class in Section 0.7 of that source take its parameter d = 1 ; this parameter is distinct
from our odd conductor part d. For every n≥ 1,

No Euler factor occurs because the supports are equal. No ﬁeld degree occurs because this is
corestriction of the actual upper class, not corestriction of a restriction. These equalities commute
with transition maps, and n≥ 1 is a coﬁnal tail. Write ξK for the Section 0.7 class, to distinguish it
from the modular-symbol combination ξ. It is exactly the full-cohomology image of y2∞, multiplied
by 2cE if the ﬁxed lattice map was needed. That factor is a unit at every height-one prime under
consideration, so its coordinate ideal is precisely Jp. The preceding nonvanishing argument supplies
a nontorsion class on each sign component, purity gives w =−1, and oddness gives condition
0.6(ivp). At the height-one primes used here, 2 is invertible and the localized Iwasawa algebra is
a DVR. Theorem 0.8 then bounds the coordinate ideal of this actual retained class. Its strong
residual and integral-cokernel assumptions concern primes containing 2, which are not used here.
We also account for the diﬀerence between the two published cohomology conventions. Write
Cj for the j∗ Iwasawa complex of [ 55, Section 12.1], and CS for full cochains on the open scheme
with the odd support inverted, as in [ 73, Section 0.5]. Work in one rational sign component and
localize at p. Their quotient is the direct sum of the odd local complexes (K16). Each diﬀerential
has nonzero determinant, so its degree-one cohomology vanishes. Hence H 1(Cj)≃ H 1(CS) and
H 2(Cj) ↪→ H 2(CS). At an odd prime q, the unramiﬁed local term has cohomological dimension
one after inverting 2. The localization triangle identiﬁes the degree-two cohomology of (K16) with
full local H 2(Qq,Tχ ) in Iwasawa cohomology. The map from H 2(CS) is the usual local restriction.

At a good active prime, ramiﬁed quadratic inertia acts by −1; its rational cohomology vanishes

9. Uniformity and the boundary of this proposition. The only lattice denominator is
ﬁxed before the family. The smoothing integers may grow, but their central valuation is 2e. The
multiplier FE depends only on the original local types. Transfer, coeﬃcient push, real quotient
and norm compatibility are integral. The proof never divides by a conductor degree, |G|, a class
number, or a Fourier projector. It keeps one class through (K1), (K2), and (K3).
We now use this same class in the positive complex. The preceding argument supplies its integral
construction, its rational height-one divisibility and its exact central reciprocity normalization.
Proposition E.2 (One integral even scalar for the family) . Retain Proposition E.1 and assume, as
for the present curve, that E[2](Q)≃ (Z/2Z)2. Let C+ be the ﬁber of the ordinary global cochains
to the ordinary real cochains, with real degree zero retained. It has a compatible ﬁnite free model
under the coeﬃcient changes used here. For B =O[G], it satisﬁes C+⊗B≃ B2[−1]. The ﬁxed
real invariant generator, the positive lift of the retained class, and the original determinant basis
deﬁne the single scalar uev by (E.4), with
uev∈ Λ[1/2][G], 2cEuev∈ Λ[G].
Here cE may be enlarged for the ﬁxed data but is independent of G and the later good conductor
primes. The assertion requires the common real sign and local squareclasses at 2N in Proposi-
tion E.1.
Proof. Let C+ be the ﬁber of ordinary global cochains to ordinary real cochains. Real H 0 is in-
cluded. Over a ﬁnite Artin quotient, continuous cochains are ﬁltered unions of ﬁnite free function
modules, hence ﬂat and therefore free. A residual splitting into ﬁnite cohomology and disks lifts
by the nilpotent perturbation series to a ﬁnite free model with actual homotopies. Minimal models
at successive quotients are related by invertible matrices: a homotopy equivalence reduces to an
isomorphism of minimal residual complexes, and Nakayama applies. Lifting these matrices and
comparison homotopies makes the models compatible. Their acyclic, termwise surjective compar-
ison cones have acyclic inverse limit. Here is the compatibility argument. At successive ﬁnite
coeﬃcient quotients, reduction of a minimal ﬁnite free model is a minimal model of the reduced
173cochains. Two such models are homotopy equivalent; reducing a homotopy inverse modulo the
maximal ideal shows that the comparison is degreewise invertible. Lift these invertible matrices
to the next quotient and change bases. The model diﬀerentials then reduce to the preceding dif-
ferentials exactly. The comparison maps to the original cochains agree after reduction up to chain
homotopy. Since reduction of continuous cochains is termwise surjective, lift that homotopy and
adjust the upper comparison by its boundary. The comparison maps are now exactly compatible
and remain quasi-isomorphisms.
LetKn be their acyclic comparison cones. Their transition maps are termwise surjective. If s
denotes the transition shift on the product, successive lifting gives the exact sequence of complexes
0−→ lim←−n Kn−→
∏
n
Kn 1−s−−−→∏
n
Kn−→ 0.
Products of complexes of modules preserve acyclicity, so the long exact cohomology sequence makes
lim←−nKn acyclic. The inverse-limit comparison is therefore a quasi-isomorphism from the bounded ﬁ-nite free limit model to the continuous coeﬃcient complex. This also identiﬁes its derived coeﬃcient
changes with the ﬁnite-quotient constructions. This proves the needed perfection and coeﬃcient
change.
The residual positive complex. WriteKn = Q(ζ2n+2)+, and let Sn consist of the places above
two and a ﬁxed ﬁnite set of odd rational primes. Put rn = 2n and sn = #Sn. We ﬁrst compute
with the scalar coeﬃcient F2 =µ2.
The ordinary two-class group of Kn is trivial. Indeed this holds for K0 = Q. Assuming it for
Kn−1, let H be the Hilbert two-class ﬁeld of Kn, with the real places split. The canonical extension
H/Kn−1 is Galois with a ﬁnite two-group GH . Only the unique dyadic prime can ramify, and its
inertia I has order two. The quotient of GabH by the image of I gives an everywhere unramiﬁed
abelian two-extension of Kn−1 with real places split, hence is trivial by induction. Thus GabH is
cyclic, generated by an element of order at most two. The Frattini quotient of GH has one generator,
soGH is cyclic and has order at most two. Since it contains Kn, we obtain H =Kn. The Sn-class
group is a quotient, so its two-part also vanishes.
The signatures of units span all rn real signs. For n≥ 1 put m =n + 2, ζ =ζ2m, and
u = 1 +ζ +ζ −1 =ζ −1ζ 3− 1ζ− 1 .
This is a unit: the numerator and denominator generate the same dyadic prime ideal in the cyclo-
tomic ﬁeld. Its real conjugates are 1 + 2 cos(2πa/2m) for odd 0 < a <2m−1. The negative ones
have 2m/3<a< 2m−1, and their number is

Kummer theory now gives dimH 1(GKn,Sn, F2) = rn +sn, and its signature map to Frn2 is
onto. The map in degree zero is the diagonal injection. The positive complex is the ﬁber of
ordinary global cochains to the sum of ordinary real cochains, so its long exact sequence gives
dimH 1(C+n ) = rn +sn− 1. The vanishing two-part of the Sn-class group identiﬁes global H 2
with Br(OKn,Sn)[2]. The local invariant description has rn +sn coordinates with one sum relation.

vanishes by the diagonal injection.
For each ﬁxed support, sn is bounded. The dyadic prime is unique; each ﬁxed odd prime
has open decomposition group in the real cyclotomic Z2-extension, since its image in Z×2/{±1}
has nonzero two-adic logarithm. Use now the ﬁnite free model and coeﬃcient change already
constructed. Its scalar reduction is a bounded ﬁnite free F2[[t]]-complex, and its ﬁnite cyclotomic
specializations are derived quotients by t2n. Smith decomposition shows that torsion disks and their
Tor terms contribute bounded dimensions, while a free cohomology generator contributes 2n. The
displayed dimensions therefore give generic rank one in degree one and zero in all other degrees.
For the present curve, E[2] is the direct sum of two trivial F2-representations. Thus the residual
positive complex has rank two in degree one only. The residue ﬁeld of the local ring B =O[G]
is F2((t)); the augmentation ideal of the ﬁnite two-group lies in its maximal ideal. A minimal
ﬁnite free B-model has zero residual diﬀerential, so the just-computed dimensions force exactly two
generators in degree one and no others. Consequently
C+⊗B≃B2[−1]. (E.3)
This uses the local ring B, not semisimplicity of F2[G].
The common real sign is needed here. The real cyclotomic character and ε are trivial on complex
conjugation, so the real action is the ﬁxed action on T2E⊗χ0, extended to the group ring. Its
invariant module is the ﬁxed free rank-one Z2-lattice extended to Λ[G], and then to O[G]. Thus
a generator can be chosen before G varies. Real H 1 is killed by two over these coeﬃcient rings,
so the positive lift below costs the same single factor two for every binary dimension. Choose a
generator of the ﬁxed real invariant lattice and its connecting class a. Since real H 1 is killed by
two, 2cE +1FEZF has an integral positive lift b. For a basis θ of the original inverse determinant
line, deﬁne

The wedge does not depend on the choice of the positive lift. Indeed, the diﬀerence of two lifts is
in the image of real degree-zero cohomology by the cone exact sequence. That module is generated
by the chosen real invariant generator, so the diﬀerence is a multiple of a and wedges to zero with
a. After rationalization set z =FEZF and choose any lift ~z of z. Formula (E.4) then reads
uevθ = 2a∧~z.
Thus the factor 2cE used to make the lift integral cancels exactly. Increasing the ﬁxed clearing
exponent does not change the rational scalar. Character evaluation and derived central specializa-
tion preserve this equality and its cone maps. The class whose dual exponential occurs in (E.7) is
consequently the same FE(ZF )h as in (K3), and the single remaining factor two is exactly the +1
in (E.7).

Proposition E.4 (An actual even form with rational normalized phase) . Fix a sign σ, unit square-
classes at a ﬁnite support S0 containing 2N , and a positive squarefree reference D0 in that ﬁlter
with L∗(σD0)⁄= 0 . For every ﬁxed ﬁnite extra exclusion set B prime to D0, there is an algebraic
holomorphic cuspidal form of weight 3/2, normalized by a(D0) = 1 , with the scalar characters ψ,η
constructed below and ψ/η = χ−σ. It has the prescribed coeﬃcient support. For every retained
positive squarefreeD in the original S0 ﬁlter,
a(D) = zDrD, z D∈µ2∞, r D∈ Q, r 2D =L∗(σD)/L∗(σD0).
It satisﬁes the all-index good-prime recurrence (E.12) outside S0∪B. Nonzero retained squarefree
coeﬃcients have integral v2a(D), and v2a(D)−ω(D) has a ﬁnite attained minimum. The form and
the initial sieve are ﬁxed before this minimum is chosen. Additional exclusions do not authorize a
new minimum.
Proof. Fix a sign σ and unit squareclasses at a ﬁnite support S0 containing two and every prime
dividing N . Let D0 > 0 be squarefree in these prescribed classes, with L∗(σD0)⁄= 0 . In (E.10)–
(E.11),D ranges over positive squarefree parameters in these same unit squareclasses at every prime
of S0. Fix also a ﬁnite extra exclusion set B with gcd(D0,∏p∈Bp) = 1 .
A nonzero reference functional and its scalar local type. Use the global Shimura–
Waldspurger packet with underlying PGL2 representation E(σ) in the normalization of Wald-
spurger’s Corollary 2. The nonzero central value at σD0 supplies the member with a nonzero
D0-Whittaker functional. Equivalently, take the nonzero theta lift associated with E(σD0) and
the additive character of frequency D0; the change of additive character identiﬁes its underlying
packet with the preceding one. The nonvanishing and the local identiﬁcation are the content of
Waldspurger’s global theta theorem and its Fourier-functional calculation [ 67, Theorem 1 and
Proposition 1] and [ 63, Proposition 18 and its proof, pp. 466–467]. The positive real frequency
selects the holomorphic member of lowest weight 3/2. The lift is in the unary-orthogonal cuspidal
spectrum.
Here is a local construction of a scalar type that retains the reference functional. At p∈ S0,
write 𝓁 =𝓁D0 and choose v with 𝓁(v)⁄= 0. Normalize Haar measure in

The integral is a ﬁnite sum on this smooth vector.

The vector PRv, and then w, is ﬁxed by upper unipotents n(Zp), because D0 is a unit. Smooth-
ness makes w ﬁxed by lower unipotents at suﬃciently deep level. Together with the unit eigenchar-
acter, the elementary decomposition of K0(pm) gives a scalar K0 type for suﬃciently large m. The
Weil normalization contributes only two-power roots of unity, so the resulting classical character
has two-power order. The forced values at −1 give the global parity required for a weight- 3/2 scalar
character: the product of the ﬁnite central actions is the inverse of the holomorphic archimedean
action, and the theta-cubed multiplier has the same product relation on rational −I. Thus the
quotient character satisﬁes ψ(−1) = 1 . At 2 one may, if desired, choose α of arbitrarily large
two-power conductor before increasing R; this does not enlarge the prime support.
Apply the ﬁnite additive Fourier idempotent for the prescribed unit-squareclass subset modulo
a suﬃciently high power of p. This idempotent keeps the D0 coeﬃcient. Its support condition is
stable under multiplication by unit squares, so it commutes with the unit action and retains a scalar
type, at a possibly deeper level. At every prime outside S0 take the spherical vector. The packet
there is unramiﬁed; its standard squarefree coeﬃcient c0p is 1 at both valuations zero and one [ 63,
Section I.3, pp. 377–378]. This includes the primes dividing D0. Local Whittaker uniqueness and
the tensor-product factorization show that the resulting global reference coeﬃcient is still nonzero.
The exact full-space comparison. Let the resulting scalar character be ψ and put η =ψχ−σ.
Both have two-power order. Let V be the full good-Hecke eigenspace of this scalar character, at

This also ﬁxes the signs in the packet convention: Waldspurger’s positive index D twists the last

The nonzero space implies hypothesis (H1) by Waldspurger’s Proposition 2 [ 63, Section I.2,
p. 377]. In explicit terms, at a principal-series place its two inducing characters take value 1 at−1.
Corollary 2 [ 63, pp. 483–484] applies to every f∈V and any positive squarefree pair whose ratio is
a square in every Qp at a prime dividing the level. Our D,D 0 satisfy this condition because the level
has prime support S0. The ﬁnal paragraph of that proof removes (H2) for this same- Q2-squareclass
comparison. No assumption of non-supercuspidality at two is required.
The printed coeﬃcient identity and its dyadic hypothesis. For clarity, write LD =

The passage from the automorphic central argument 1/2 to the classical argument 1 is the same for
both terms. Dividing only by the nonzero reference value gives (E.10). In particular this is a literal
square identity, with neither complex conjugation nor an unspeciﬁed absolute-value normalization.
Every prime of NW belongs to S0 at this point of the construction. The prescribed local classes
make D/D0 a square in Q×p at each such prime, including p = 2 . In the proof of the corollary,
the local coeﬃcient functions at these primes have equal values at D,D 0. Outside the level, the
squarefree arguments have valuation zero or one and the unramiﬁed coeﬃcient function has value
one in both cases. The real coeﬃcient contributes exactly the power (D/D0)(k−2)/4 before squaring.
The quotient of epsilon factors is computed on pp. 483–484: its Hilbert-symbol product disappears
by reciprocity and its remaining character is χ(D/D0) = ψ(D/D0). Thus these local terms do not
leave an additional prime-dependent factor in the displayed identity.
The ﬁnal paragraph on p. 484 explicitly explains the use of (H2): it is needed in that argument
only to compare Fourier indices in diﬀerent squareclasses of Q×2 . Our pair is in one squareclass.
Hence the corollary as stated requires (H1) here, but does not impose (H2), a nonsupercuspidal
condition at two, or a new dyadic conductor restriction. The nonzero scalar eigenspace supplies
(H1) by [ 63, Proposition 2, p. 377]. Moreover the proof on p. 483 extends its linear relation to every
vector in the full eigenspace before squaring. The resulting identity therefore applies simultaneously
to old multiplicities, and later to both Galois-conjugated and character-twisted vectors at a common
level. At that later level the same squareclass test is repeated; its prime support is still S0. Putting
n1 =D0,n 2 =D in the corollary, with weight 3/2, gives

An algebraic vector and the Galois comparison. Ramsey’s section model [ 64, Deﬁnition 3.1
and Theorem 4.2] realizes the half-integral-weight space as sections of the indicated divisor sheaf on
X1(4Naux). Here Naux is supported on S0 and includes the chosen local depths; it need not equal
the conductor N of E. Over characteristic-zero ﬁelds, ﬂat ﬁeld extension commutes with these
sections. Cuspidality, scalar character, support conditions and prescribed good-Hecke eigenvalues
are algebraic linear conditions. Although inﬁnitely many coeﬃcients or primes can occur in their
description, a ﬁnite subset cuts out the same subspace, by ﬁnite dimensionality.
One need not impose Petersson orthogonality as an additional transcendental linear condition.
The elliptic good-Hecke eigenspace is automatically orthogonal to unary theta series: at a good
prime, the unary eigenvalues have absolute value p + 1, whereas |η(p)ap(E)|≤ 2√p < p+ 1. The
unary space and its orthogonal complement are stable under the Hecke operators and their adjoints.
Thus their elliptic eigenspaces are disjoint. The nonzero reference functional on the complex space
is consequently nonzero on its algebraic model. Choose such an algebraic g and scale it to a(D0) = 1 .
This uses ﬂat ﬁeld extension only, not Ramsey’s arbitrary-ring base-change assertion in weight 3/2;
see [ 64, Remark 4.4]. Here the ﬁeld-extension assertion can be checked without a cohomology-
vanishing theorem. Work over a number ﬁeld K containing the character values and the roots of
unity specifying the cusps and the support idempotents. A ﬁnite aﬃne cover of the modular curve
computes global sections as the kernel of the diﬀerence of restriction maps to pairwise intersections.
Tensoring with a ﬁeld extension is ﬂat and preserves that kernel. Thus the sections in Ramsey’s
Deﬁnition 3.1 commute with K ⊂ Q⊂ C, also in weight 3/2. Impose vanishing at the cusps,
the diamond character, the support idempotents, and the kernels of T (p2)−η(p)ap(E) inside this
ﬁnite-dimensional space. The span in its dual of all the deﬁning linear equations has a ﬁnite basis.
These conditions therefore commute with the same ﬁeld extensions. Since the reference functional
is nonzero after extension to C, it is nonzero before extension; a vector in a K-basis has nonzero
reference value and may be divided by that value. This also places every coeﬃcient of the chosen
form in one number ﬁeld. No assertion about base change to a residue ﬁeld, nor about vanishing
of H 1 in weight 3/2, is used.
Let τ∈ Aut(Q/Q). Choose an odd integer e describing its action on all values of η and ψ, and
putν =η(e−1)/2. Coeﬃcientwise conjugation gives a form gτ with characterψe and good eigenvalues
η(p)eap(E). One can reduce this conjugation assertion to integral weight by multiplying by the
ordinary theta series: it has rational coeﬃcients, and its fourth power has trivial scalar character.
The algebraic divisibility condition at the cusps is precisely the one in Ramsey’s section model,
so division after conjugation is again a half-integral-weight form. The coeﬃcientwise twist g⊗ν
has character ψν 2 = ψe and exactly these good eigenvalues. The identities hold because ψ/η is
quadratic. The twist is modular at a level still supported on S0; this follows as well by writing it
as the ﬁnite additive translation sum using the Gauss sum of the primitive character inducing ν.
Pass to a common such level. Both forms lie in its full good-Hecke eigenspace, so the propor-
tionality of its D and D0 functionals gives
τ (a(D)) = ν(D)a(D)ν(D0)a(D0) =ν(D/D0)a(D).
There is no vanishing reference denominator: D0 is prime to S0 and a(D0) = 1 . No rationality of

Recurrence, later exclusions, and valuations. Only now impose the extra ﬁnite coprimality
sieve B. The zero-or-retain Fourier sieve is a ﬁnite additive translation operator. It preserves
cuspidality, keeps every coeﬃcient whose index is prime to B, and commutes with good T (p2) for
p /∈S1 =S0∪B. In particular it keeps a(D0) = 1 . The squarefree comparison (E.11) was proved
before this enlargement, so it requires no extra squareclass hypotheses at the primes of B. After
the sieve it is asserted at the retained indices. The standard good T (p2) formula, with ψ2 = η2,
gives
a(p2n) ={η(p)ap(E)−ψ(p)(−n/p)}a(n)−pη(p)2a(n/p2), p / ∈S1. (E.12)
For squarefree D, write a(Dp2r) = η(p)rbr. The ﬁrst multiplier is ap(E)− (ψ/η)(p)(−D/p); subse-
quent multipliers satisfy br+1 =ap(E)br−pbr−1. All are integers, including when p|D. Iteration

η phase. More explicitly, for p /∈ S1 set Ap,0(D) = 1 , Ap,1(D) = ap(E)− (ψ/η)(p)(−D/p), and
Ap,r+1(D) = ap(E)Ap,r(D)−pAp,r−1(D) for r≥ 1. Then, for every retained index Dm2 with D
squarefree,

The formula includes p|D, when the initial Legendre symbol is zero. When applying the recurrence
at one prime, the other prime-square factors do not alter that Legendre symbol. This proves the
formula by induction on the prime factors of m, with a(n/p2) = 0 when p2 -n. All factors Ap,r(D)
are integers. Thus every coeﬃcient used by the extraction and shell operators of Proposition F.5
is a rational number times a two-power root of unity, including coeﬃcients with non-squarefree
index. In the chosen residue ﬁeld such a root reduces to one. A rational coeﬃcient of valuation
zero reduces to the element 1 of its prime ﬁeld F2. Consequently the fresh coeﬃcient-one reading
of that proposition detects precisely equality in the ﬁxed minimum-depth bound. It uses the same
g, D0 and normalization throughout.
183Combining (E.9) and (E.11) makes the values v2a(D)−ω(D) at nonzero retained squarefree
coeﬃcients a bounded-below subset of Z. It is nonempty because D0 is retained, and therefore has
an attained ﬁnite minimum. The form and the initial ﬁnite sieve are ﬁxed before this minimum
is chosen. Restricting later fresh primes by further exclusions does not authorize choosing another
minimum.

F.1 A ﬁxed witness and one compatible sequence
Proposition F.1 (One witness and a compatible detecting sequence) . For the ﬁxed E =E𝓁 under
the full-corank-one hypothesis of Theorem 5.3, use the prepared positive unit ﬁlter containing 1.
There exist a negative odd fundamental discriminant h∗ with L(Eh∗, 1)⁄= 0, a positive fundamental
discriminant Dw in that ﬁlter with L′(EDw, 1)⁄= 0 , and a ﬁxed integer L such that LPD belongs

Proof. We use the positive unit-squareclass ﬁlter containing one, with its ﬁxed support enlarged
before choosing the witness. The rational two-isogeny parity theorem [ 49, Theorem 1.8] gives
root number −1 for E under the full-corank-one assumption. Apply clause (ii) of [ 56, pp. 543–
544] to its weight-two newform, with every old prime in the splitting set, to obtain a negative
fundamental discriminant h∗ with L(E(h∗), 1)⁄= 0 . Including two makes h∗ odd and excludes the
extra-unit ﬁelds. Now add the primes of h∗ to that set and apply clause (i) to obtain a positive
fundamental discriminant Dw with L′(E(Dw), 1)⁄= 0 . Splitting at every old prime puts Dw in the
ﬁlter containing one and makes it prime to h∗. These choices precede every detecting prime and
every theta precision.
The conductor-one genus character for KD = Q(√Dh∗) cuts out FD = Q(√D,√h∗). Thus the
unaveraged sum PD in (O.1) belongs to E(FD). Its rational character components are the D- and
h∗-components. In the degenerate case D = 1 these are the trivial and h∗ characters of the actual
quadratic group. The h∗-component has rank zero by the forward rank-zero theorem. For Dw, the
unaveraged height formula [ 59, Theorem 1.1] has Rankin derivative L′(E(Dw), 1)L(E(h∗), 1)⁄= 0, so
184PDw is nontorsion. Here the conductor is one, all primes of N split, and the extra-unit ﬁelds have
been excluded.

Choose compatible division points Qn with 2nQn = Q and 2Qn+1 = Qn, and let cQ(g) =

OnH, this cocycle is a homomorphism. Its image is compact, and hence a closed Z2-submodule of

The curve A is non-CM. Open image [ 69] supplies both irreducibility of its rational Tate repre-
sentation and a central scalar z⁄= 1 in its Tate image. If cQ(H) = 0 , the rational cocycle descends
to that image. Commuting z with g gives

Since z− 1 is invertible over Q2, this is a coboundary. It contradicts the Kummer injection

makes its rational span all of V2A. It consequently contains 2a0T2A for some a0.

is onto: F 2− 1 is a nonzero separable isogeny, so for S∈ E[n] one can solve (F 2− 1)R = S and
takeP =nR. Also κM (FP ) = FκM (P ), by choosing FR as a division point. Therefore λM =bκM
has Frobenius sign +1.
Good reduction identiﬁes the reduction of the global division cocycle with this exact map. The
chosen place makes the twist identiﬁcation deﬁned over F𝓁0, since (Dw/𝓁0) = 1 . Thus the ﬁxed
witness satisﬁes
λM :E(F𝓁20)−→ Z/2M Z, v 2λM (Q) = cw <M. (O.2)
Choosing increasing M and excluding previous primes gives one sequence of actual stages. Subse-
quent ﬁnite prime exclusions do not alter cw. New ﬁeld prescriptions require a compatible common
lift and, if added after this choice, are not asserted to preserve this particular constant.

The ﬁnite-ﬁeld evaluation map. Writek0 = F𝓁20 and let F denote its arithmetic Frobenius.

It kills 2ME(k0), and its kernel is exactly that subgroup: a zero value means that QP itself lies

modulo four. If 2 -N there is no root character at two.

F.3 Transformation law and recurrence
Use the standard global additive character, self-dual measures, and the Gaussian e−2πQB at inﬁnity.

All the speciﬁed local functions have such a translation lattice. At an odd level prime the
unit-root condition and its character depend on a ﬁnite residue quotient. At two the square-unit
condition is a congruence modulo eight; the integral projectors selecting the cyclic quotient, and the
ﬁnite root character, again depend on a ﬁnite residue quotient. At a prime dividing m, valuation
one and the nonzero-nilpotent sign depend on ﬁnite residue data. The division-prime function is
already a residue function. Each support is contained in a lattice and is a ﬁnite union of cosets
of a suﬃciently small lattice. This proves the Schwartz–Bruhat and translation assertions for the
actual weights.

The metaplectic comparison at two and the extracted character. We specify how the
preceding index calculation ﬁxes the multiplier, including its dyadic part. Let

Use the same additive character and metaplectic lifts for the rank-three Weil representations of
QB and Q0. The latter theta series is literally θ(τ )3; this ﬁxes the meaning of the ordinary theta
multiplier cubed, including its eighth roots of unity. For either representation the nontrivial central
element of the metaplectic cover acts by −1. Consequently the ratio of two eigenvalues computed
using the same lift is independent of that lift. In particular a central factor in multiplying the three
191lifts in the displayed triangular factorization cancels in this ratio. We compare eigenvalues, and do
not choose a group section of the cover over Γ0(2A2).
Here is the comparison on the three factors. Both upper unipotents act trivially on the indicated
vectors. Choose Ap large enough that both lower unipotents do also. This is the Fourier-support
argument above for fp; for φ0,p it requires no positive depth at an odd prime and depth two at
two, since the bilinear dual of Z32 for Q0 is 12 Z32 and 4Q0( 12 Z32)⊂ Z2. The Weyl scalar cancels
in conjugating an upper unipotent to a lower one, so it introduces no additional eigenvalue. On
a diagonal unit u, the scaling part acts by αp(u) on fp and by one on φ0,p. The quotient of the
Weil-index parts, by the preceding calculation, is
γ(uQB)/γ(QB)
(γ(u)/γ(1))3 = (u, detQB)p. (O.5a)
If the inverse Weil-index convention is used for the diagonal operator, both numerator and denom-
inator are inverted; the quotient is unchanged because the Hilbert symbol has order two. Thus
this computation does not leave an undetermined inverse or a dyadic root of unity. These opera-
tor comparisons are precisely the scaled pullback, Fourier conjugation and scalar relations of [ 68,
I.13–I.14 and II.25–II.28].
Here detQ means the determinant of the matrix in Q(x) = xTAx. The associated bilinear
Gram matrix is 2A. If that convention is used instead, both Q and Q0 acquire the same factor 23
in their determinants, which cancels in the comparison. In particular no extra squareclass of two
is introduced at the dyadic place.
For a general ternary form Q in this argument, at each prescribed prime take Ap ≥ 1 and
increaseAp also to the conductor of u↦→ (u, detQ)p. The eigenvalue relative to the ordinary theta
multiplier, on a matrix whose upper-left entry is a, is then
αp(a)(a, detQ)p. (O.5b)
Indeed the triangular factorization has just the two trivial unipotent factors and this diagonal
factor, and its lift ambiguity cancels as explained above. This also veriﬁes multiplication: the
upper-left entry of a product is congruent to the product of the upper-left entries modulo pAp, so
both local characters in (O.5b) multiply. The argument therefore applies to every matrix in the
indicated congruence subgroup at a prescribed prime, not only to a choice of generators. At an
unramiﬁed prime outside the support, the full spherical invariance established below treats matrices
with nonunit upper-left entry as well.

1 + 4Z2. At every odd prime outside m the factor on units is one. There is consequently no new
level prime, and there is no omitted dyadic character.

There are no additional level primes hidden in this argument. Outside the prescribed support,
p is odd, Bp is split, and R0p = sl2(Zp) is self-dual for the bilinear form of QB =−a2−bc. Its
determinant is a unit. Hence the Fourier transform of 1R0p equals itself; the unramiﬁed Weil constant

diagonal units, and the Weyl element ﬁx it. These generate SL2(Zp), so the exponent there is zero.
At the ﬁnitely many prescribed primes the preceding Ap suﬃce. Conjugation by any z∈ ˆB× is an
isometry of QB, commutes with the Weil representation, and preserves the self-dual measure. The
same level and character therefore work for every summand indexed by z.
[68, III, Theorem 6] applies to this Schwartz–Bruhat function on V (A) and proves invariance
of its rational theta sum. The normalized archimedean expression is ∑xf (x)e2πiτQ B(x): the factor
y3/4 in the Weil action is removed in passing to the classical form. It has holomorphic weight
3/2. At a rational cusp, the slash operation replaces the ﬁnite Schwartz function by its ﬁnite
Weil transform. Its support meets V (Q) in a ﬁnite union of rational lattice cosets, with bounded
denominators. Positive deﬁniteness gives nonnegative rational exponents of bounded denominator
and normal convergence for ℑτ > 0. This proves holomorphy at every cusp. Theorem 6 alone
asserts automorphic invariance; this last argument supplies the holomorphy conclusion used here.

This directly constructs the extracted form without a half-integral-weight extraction operator. The
inverse in this classical passage can also be checked before any character notation is introduced. Put
193gτ =n(x)m(√y) forτ =x +iy, so gτi =τ . For γ∈ SL2(Q) writeγgτ =gγτk∞ withk∞∈ SO2(R).
In the adelic group,
(gγτ, 1f ) = γ (gτ,γ −1f )(k−1∞, 1f ).
This equality also holds with consistent metaplectic lifts. The global rational lifts are compatible
for the two forms: [ 68, II.30, Proposition 5] gives ∏vγv(Q) =∏vγv(aQ) = 1 for rational Q,a , and
hence the local normalizing factors in WQ andMQ(a) have product one. The product formula also
gives∏v| det(2A)|1/2v = 1, so the product of the stated self-dual measures is the measure for which
the rational lattice has covolume one. Apply the rational invariance of [ 68, III.41, Theorem 6]
to the displayed adelic identity. The archimedean Gaussian for either positive ternary form has
the same weight 3/2; therefore its factor from k−1∞ is the same as that for θ3. The relative ﬁnite
factor is evaluated at γ−1f , whose upper-left entry is d. Thus the relative classical character is∏
pαp(d)(d, detQ)p, over the prescribed primes. This proves the inverse-idele convention directly.
It compares the transformation identities and does not divide by a value of a theta function.
For the extracted form there is likewise no measure or degree normalization in its coeﬃcients.
With exactly the same ﬁnite weights, replace only the quadratic form and archimedean Gaussian
by QB/m and e−2πQB/m. The summand indexed by x now has exponent QB(x)/m. The original
support makes QB(x) an integer with valuation one at every prime dividing m, so these exponents
are nonnegative integers. Coeﬃcient n is exactly the original sum over QB(x) = mn, namely
HM (mn). The self-dual measure changes by |m|−3/2v locally, but a theta coeﬃcient is a discrete
sum and contains no Haar measure; this change is used only in the Fourier operators. Outside the
original support it is a unit rescaling of the same unimodular local form, and at primes dividing m
the lower-unipotent depth can be increased within the existing support. The determinant changes
bym−3, whose squareclass is m, so (O.5c) supplies exactly (m/·). In particular no new level prime,
factor of m, or dyadic root of unity is introduced by extraction.
All-index recurrence and its phases. Letp avoid the full support and put s =ηB(p). Locally

The actual weighted coeﬃcient comparison.
Proposition F.3 (The unaveraged weighted theta comparison) . Putm =|h∗| andF = Q(√Dh∗),

The root of unity belongs to the ﬁeld generated by the ﬁxed characters at the primes of N . No class
number is divided out.
Proof. The ﬁlter makes Dh∗ an odd fundamental discriminant, with F split at every prime dividing
2N . Its unit group is {±1}. The condition QB(x) = mD is equivalent to x2 =Dh∗. Together with

hold by admissibility.
First classify the local embeddings. Away from N𝓁 0 there is one local orbit: an OF,p-stable

Dh∗. At pe‖N , the algebra is split. The two integral idempotents decompose both lattices of the

one summand. The two possibilities give exactly two orientations. This applies at two using the
integral spectral projectors speciﬁed in the construction. At 𝓁0, the maximal order of the division
algebra has residue ﬁeld F𝓁20. The two residue-ﬁeld embeddings of the unramiﬁed quadratic order
give the two orientations. Consequently there are 2ω(N )+1 local orientation tuples.
Fix one tuple. Choose a rational embedding ι : F ↪→ B and write x0 = ι(√Dh∗). Such an
embedding exists because F is a ﬁeld at the two ramiﬁed places of B, namely 𝓁0 and inﬁnity [ 82,

Take a source Eichler order Rsrc = R′∩R′′ of level N . In [ 62, §3.2] the source subgroup is

Both ﬁne conductors are one. To check this assertion using the deﬁnition in [ 62, §3.3], an em-

the target torsor for that full orientation tuple. Corollary 3.2 gives the same bijection after ﬁxing
any supersingular pair in the target.

The weights are constant on each embedding orbit used in the following count. Indeed a unit

It remains to account for the other orientations; they are not all in the image of the one sign
component of the reduction map. At pe‖N , use the local Atkin–Lehner normalizer. It reverses the
selected root, so multiplies the Schwartz weight by
αp(−1) = wpeχh∗(p)e.
197It multiplies the norm-weighted parametrization value by the same sign: its reduced norm has
valuation e, and the actual parametrization transforms by wpe after L kills the ﬁxed cuspidal
translation. The two signs multiply to one. This normalizer has trivial components at the other
primes, so it changes no other orientation or local weight.
At𝓁0, a division-algebra uniformizer exchanges the two residue embeddings. It sends the trace-
zero residue β to−β, multiplying its weight by (−1/𝓁0) = −1. Its reduced norm has valuation
one and hence contributes χh∗(𝓁0) =−1. Its action on supersingular pairs is Frobenius. Since λM
has Frobenius sign +1 and the parametrization is deﬁned over Q, this introduces no further sign.
Again the two factors multiply to one. The uniformizer operation used here may be described with
arithmetic or geometric Frobenius: they have the same action on the supersingular pairs and their
parametrization values over F𝓁20. The identity λM (FP ) = λM (P ) from (O.2) consequently appliesto the very operation that exchanges the residue embeddings. Its norm has valuation one, and
χh∗ is unramiﬁed at 𝓁0, so its norm-character value is exactly χh∗(𝓁0) =−1, independently of the
uniformizer’s unit part. Likewise, at pe‖N , the norm of the Atkin–Lehner normalizer has valuation
e and χh∗ is unramiﬁed at p. Its norm-character value is exactly χh∗(p)e. These observations ﬁx
the norm signs in both orientation changes.
It follows that every one of the 2ω(N )+1 tuples has the same contribution as the reference tuple.
Multiplying by their number gives precisely c02ω(N ), as asserted. This counts individual embeddings
with their stabilizer; a conjugate-pair formulation of the geometric correspondence must not be used
to insert or remove another factor of two.
Finally,c0/|R×z| is an integer for every z. Thus a change of the chosen integer lift of PM (z) by 2M
changes the actual coeﬃcient by an element of 2MO. Every preceding equality is consequently valid
at the original retained precision. No denominator depending on the class number, the discriminant,
or the stage has entered.
Precision in the orientation count. The factor 1/2 in the orbit count does not require dividing
a congruence modulo 2M by two. Indeed, −1∈ R×z implies 2|| R×z|, and the chosen integer c0
is divisible by |R×z|. Hence c0/2 is an integer. Choose lifts of all PM (z) before summing. Each
orbit of embeddings contributes (c0/|R×z|)(|R×z|/2)PM (z) = (c0/2)PM (z), with its prescribed unit
weight. Replacing any lift by a multiple of 2M changes this expression by a multiple of 2M . The
comparison with the reduced point may therefore be made at precision M , separately for each
orientation. Summing the 2ω(N )+1 orientations gives the stated multiplier c02ω(N ) without any loss
of precision. In particular, write cθ = 9 + ω(N ), the valuation of the ﬁxed integer c02ω(N ). At the
ﬁxed witness Dw, (O.2) and (O.7) imply
v2aM (Dw) = cθ +cw wheneverM >cθ +cw.
The phase is a unit and the congruence error has strictly larger valuation, so this is an equality, not
merely a lower bound. Discarding the ﬁnitely many earlier stages is suﬃcient. Thus the visibility
used in choosing the odd minimum retains a ﬁxed witness and a ﬁxed valuation; neither the class
number nor the changing prime 𝓁0 introduces a new loss of precision.
Proposition F.4 (Visibility and the native genus index) . Use the actual odd form and unaveraged
point of Propositions F.2 and F.3. There is one constant Cdet, independent of the actual stage
and admissible D, for which (O.8) holds. Explicitly, for a known simple-zero D, v2aM (D) ≥
min{M,j gen(D)−Cdet}. For every admissible D, the strict inequality v2aM (D) < M implies
ords=1L(ED,s ) = 1 and jgen(D)≤ v2aM (D) +Cdet. If an admissible odd-sign D is not a simple
198zero, its coeﬃcient vanishes modulo 2M . No genus index of a torsion point is deﬁned or used in
this assertion.
Proof. For a known simple-zero D, write PD = mDG +T in its native rank-one free lattice. The
same L kills T . An integral ﬁnite-ﬁeld Kummer map cannot decrease v2(mD), so (O.7) gives the
lower bound by the native index. The rational free line has index at most two in that native lattice,
by conjugation and norm. Absorb this and the ﬁxed initial convention in Cdet. The ﬁrst inequality
below holds for a known simple-zero D; the visibility implication holds for every admissible D:
v2aM (D)≥ min{M,j gen(D)−Cdet},
v2aM (D)<M =⇒ ords=1L(ED,s ) = 1, j gen(D)≤v2aM (D) +Cdet. (O.8)
If an odd-sign D is not a simple zero, the height formula gives a torsion genus point, killed by L,
and its coeﬃcient is zero modulo 2M . Combined with the ﬁrst inequality on simple-zero parameters,
this gives the all- D truncated bound used below, without assigning a rank-one lattice index to a
torsion point.
F.5 Integer divisions and actual trace operators
Proposition F.5 (Arithmetic realization of the coeﬃcient rules) . Use the rational full-two-torsion
graph conventions of Deﬁnitions 7.4 and 7.5. For an even source of Proposition E.4, use its ﬁxed
minimum depth. For an odd source, ﬁrst ﬁx a ﬁnite operator budget |U|≤ W , take R≥ 2, and
choose an actual stage satisfying the depth inequalities of Proposition G.3. Then the integer divisions
(O.9)–(O.11) are valid at every Fourier index, with the stated truncation for the odd source. The
fresh coeﬃcient-one reading is (t(Frq)θHU )[1] = A(Uq ). On all tested nonempty conﬁgurations,
these actual symbols depend only on their typed graph and satisfy the rules of Lemma 7.6: a pair
diﬀerence contracts the endpoints and adds their types and every external incidence; whole-prime
omission uses the same source, phase, depth and stage; and an isolated primary vertex of neutral
type and neutral external incidences makes the symbol zero. Every contraction has nonempty output.
Stationary character tests are carried along in the same product rule. This statement asserts neither
values at untested odd-stage weights nor an identity at an empty contraction output.
Proof. All new primes below avoid the full coeﬃcient support S, including every late sieve and the
current 𝓁0. Here S is the actual coeﬃcient support. The coeﬃcients of g vanish at all indices not

extraction at |h∗|. At every additional coeﬃcient-sieve prime it is imposed by an exact zero-or-
retain Fourier sieve, which commutes with the good recurrence outside the enlarged support. This
is the vanishing used in the condition (m,S ) = 1 in the unary series below. Later stationary prime-
selection tests may enrich the common label alphabet without resieving the intrinsic odd source;
such tests do not enlarge this coeﬃcient-support condition. Use the minimum-depth inequalities
proved in Appendix G. The general bookkeeping assigns weight one to a split prime of Q(E[2])
and one-half to a prime with transposition Frobenius, called simple. In our actual full-two-torsion
family every prime is split, w(U ) =|U|, and the depth δ is an integer. Thus only matching parity,
meaning eU = 0 , is needed here; the displayed general formula also records where the unused
simple-prime term would enter. For a ﬁnite set U , put

bs+1 = bs−1, with b0 = 1 and b1 = 0 . Thus bs−bs−1 is odd for all s≥ 0, including s = 0 with

modulo four gives ds≡ pds−1 (mod 4) for s≥ 1. Each ds is even, and multiplication of an even
residue modulo four by an odd number ﬁxes that residue. Consequently
ds≡d0 =ap−κ− 1 (mod 4) ( s≥ 0).
This proves (O.10) without dividing a residue-ﬁeld identity by two. For an index Jrm 2, each p∈J
therefore supplies an actual factor two, whereas the factors for p∈U\J are integral units modulo

gives δ +ω(D) +|J| =nU +ω(r) before division by 2nU . All removed character factors are units.

Write mcoef for the maximal ideal of the chosen coeﬃcient valuation ring Ocoef . In the following

The diamond correction above is a sum of integral multiples of θΘJ ; its fresh coeﬃcient q is

For any ﬁxed Fourier index n, Chebotarev approximation lets one test a ﬁnite product of trace
operators using distinct fresh primes q1,...,q a avoidingn and the level. The good Hecke formula
then reads the coeﬃcient at nq1··· qa of the original vector. Diﬀerent indices may use diﬀerent
fresh primes; the conclusion is an identity of coeﬃcientwise reduced expansions.
The determinant identity uses the actual diamond formula, not merely the reduction of its
eigenvalues. For a good prime r, d(Frr) = r⟨r⟩. The formula above shows that (d(Frr)− 1)FU
reduces to a sum of integral multiples of the undivided corrections θBU [J]ΘJ : the scalar rψU (r)− 1

cannot have squarefree part J, and θ≡ 1 (mod mcoef ). This remains true after further fresh traces.
Continuity and the closed integral module therefore give, on the reduced trace orbit of FU ,

The middle equality follows from the characteristic-zero matrix identity tr(A) tr(B) = tr( AB) +

after reduction. Applying the middle identity to t(gh)t(h) gives t(gh2)FU = t(g)FU . Invariance
202under all squares, and continuity, give factorization through the elementary quadratic quotient
unramiﬁed outside S∪U . Over Q this quotient is ﬁnite: its squareclasses are generated by −1, 2,
and the primes in that support. This does not assert abelianity of the characteristic-zero Galois
representations.
At p∈ U , ﬁrst keep the summands of FU separately. The block θBU and each unary block
with p∈ J have level exponent at most one and primitive quadratic character at p. The unary
blocks with p /∈ J are degeneracies of forms at a level omitting p. This distinction is preserved
when retaining all old multiplicities. In particular, the latter blocks cannot acquire a Steinberg
constituent merely by being viewed at a larger level.
Enlarge the characteristic-zero coeﬃcient ﬁeld to realize all the cuspidal newform constituents
and the Eisenstein character pairs of these blocks. On a constituent and all its degeneracies, deﬁne
t(g) and d(g) to be the scalar trace and determinant of its two-dimensional Galois representation,
acting as the identity on its multiplicity space. Distinct characteristic-zero constituents with the
same good eigenvalues have the same trace and determinant by Chebotarev, so these deﬁnitions
agree on coinciding summands. In particular they give well-deﬁned operators on their sum. No
decomposition modulo two, and no residual irreducibility assertion, enters this construction.
For a cuspidal eigenform f , let Πf denote its unitary adelic normalization: at a good prime
q its Satake parameters αq,βq satisfy aq =√q(αq +βq) and αqβq = χ(q). Apply [ 66, §§0.3–0.7,
Theorem A, pp. 409–411] to Π∨f , and set ρf =σ(Π∨f )∨. Here F = Q, the real weight is 2, and the
coeﬃcient place lies above 2. The degree of F is odd, so the additional ﬁnite discrete-series-place
hypothesis for even degree does not apply. The local prime p is odd. Carayol’s reciprocity sends
geometric Frobenius to a uniformizer, and his ωp is|·| p. His normalized-induction formula is
σ(Ind(ξ1,ξ 2)) = ξ−11 ω−1/2p ⊕ξ−12 ω−1/2p .
Thus if Πf,p = Ind(µ1,µ 2),ρf on arithmetic Frobenius has eigenvalues√pµ 1(p),√pµ 2(p) whenever
these characters are unramiﬁed. In particular its good polynomial is X 2−aqX +qχ(q). Both the
automorphic contragredient and the dual on the Galois side are part of this convention.
For a block with ramiﬁed character, its local conductor is exactly one: it is at most the level
exponent and at least the conductor of its determinant. Casselman’s newvector theorem and
conductor calculation [65, Theorem 1, pp. 302–306] show that a special representation has conductor
one only for an unramiﬁed twist; a ramiﬁed twist has conductor at least two. A supercuspidal
representation has conductor at least two. Consequently Πf,p = Ind(µ1,µ 2) with µ1 unramiﬁed
and µ2 of conductor one, after interchanging the characters if necessary. Its newvector space is a
line. On inertia the ramiﬁed character is the prescribed quadratic character.

On a block coming from level prime to p, the representation is unramiﬁed and both diﬀerences

These are identities on characteristic-zero forms before any reduction. To relate them to the
integral trace module above, choose ﬁnitely many Fourier coordinates that inject its ambient space.
Every generator of that module has integral coordinates; hence the module is a submodule of a ﬁnite
free module over the complete coeﬃcient DVR. It is ﬁnite and closed. Simultaneous Chebotarev
approximation in the ﬁnitely many Galois representations makes t(g) andd(g) limits of good Hecke
and diamond operators on this space. They preserve this integral module, so the identity can be
evaluated on the integral FU and then reduced. This does not require integral projectors onto
constituents. In particular no division by the two in the displayed inertia diﬀerences is used.
Apply (O.12) at coeﬃcient one. Put V = U\{p}, v =∏r∈V r, so nU = nV + 1. For a fresh
prime r set
Cp(vr) = ap− (ψ/η)(p)(−vr/p)− 12 ∈ Z, c p(vr) = Cp(vr) mod 2.
204The two surviving operator terms at pr and the good recurrence give
HU [pr]≡η(p)Cp(vr) 2−nV −1a(vr) (mod 2 Ocoef ).
For the even source this is an equality before reduction. For the odd source the error is in
2Mθ−nU −1Ocoef , which is contained in 2Ocoef at our chosen stage. Choose fresh q,qτ,r 0,r 1 rep-
resenting respectively g,gτ,gφ,gτφ in one suﬃciently precise joint quotient for the U andV forms
and all retained characters. The omitted form uses the same g,η,ψ,δ and stage, and its level omits
the entire rational prime p. Thus the two V symbols at vr0,vr 1 agree. Their Legendre signs at

Here the subscript records the operator set, not a new coeﬃcient source or minimum. The factor
