unramiﬁed at p, the representative of the omitted vertex satisﬁes
χ(gφ) = χ(g)χ(φ) = χ(Frq)χ(Frp).
For a ﬁxed-support character this is the product of the two vertex labels. For the character of a
third vertexr∈V , it adds the two bits (r/q) and (r/p) in F2. Reciprocity then gives the reverse bits
from the same endpoint types. All additional stationary tests were included in the joint quotient,
so they obey this formula as well. This proves the full product-label omission rule, including every
external incidence.
A fresh vertex with trivial label in the elementary extension for S∪U has the same reduced
reading as t(1), hence gives zero. An isolated primary vertex has precisely this ﬁxed label and these
zero incidence bits. A contracted cluster of primary vertices has the same property when its external
incidences are neutral. The result is still a nonempty conﬁguration, including the one-vertex case;
no contraction deletes the last vertex.
Finally the ﬁxed- U factorization gives dependence on the entire ﬁnite typed graph. For two
disjoint prime realizations of the same graph, choose a fresh bridge copy with the prescribed types
and matching incidences to both realizations. CRT and Dirichlet provide the primes successively,
and reciprocity determines their reverse bits. Replace the vertices of the ﬁrst copy by bridge vertices
one at a time, then the bridge vertices by those of the second copy. At each step the other vertices
form the same ﬁxed U , and the changed vertex has the same elementary Frobenius data for S∪U ;
its symbol therefore agrees. If the original realizations overlap, ﬁrst replace one by a fresh copy
avoiding both original prime sets, with incidences matching that ﬁrst realization. A vertex and its
own replacement are never tested together, so their mutual bit can be chosen freely. The resulting
two disjoint realizations admit the preceding bridge argument without conﬂicting prescriptions.
Only graphs of the original bounded size are evaluated; extra bridge incidences are prime-selection
conditions. Thus these are rules for the actual arithmetic graph symbols, at the already chosen
valid stage. The simultaneous ﬁnite graph budget and stabilization are treated below.
G Nonvanishing, normalization and the ﬁnal family
The uses of the coeﬃcient and address propositions have the following order. First apply only
their even-source case, whose depth is already ﬁxed by Proposition E.4. This gives the rough
companion and the genus lower bound. These in turn establish the odd minimum and its ﬁnite-
stage inequalities. Only then apply the odd case of Proposition F.5 and the two-symbol address
205construction. In particular the proof of the rough companion does not use the later odd minimum.
We give the choice order underlying (3.3)–(3.9). This also separates the two valuation arguments:
the even upper transfer uses the common Kato scalar, while the ﬁnal contradiction uses the common
Heegner coordinate of Section 9.
G.1 An absolute bound of ﬁve new primes
The theorem of Hoﬀstein–Luo [ 57, p. 436] supplies fundamental discriminants with nonzero central
value and at most four prime factors. Its ﬁnite splitting set is arbitrary. The bound four is absolute;
no uniform size threshold for those discriminants is needed.
Proposition G.1 (Nonvanishing with ﬁve primes and ﬁnite local prescriptions) . Let A/Q be a
ﬁxed modular elliptic curve for this application, let S contain 2, 3 and all its bad primes, and let
B be a ﬁnite prime exclusion set. In every nonempty signed unit-squareclass ﬁlter at S of even
root number, there is a signed odd squarefree parameter u, prime to S∪B, with L(Au, 1)⁄= 0 and
ω(u)≤ 5. Only the intermediate parameter supplied by Hoﬀstein–Luo is required to be fundamental;
the ﬁnal ﬁeld discriminant may be 4u. The number ﬁve is independent of the prescribed ﬁnite set.
No uniform bound on the sizes of the selected primes is asserted.
Proof. Let A/Q be any ﬁxed modular elliptic curve for this application, S contain two, three and
all its bad primes, and B be any ﬁnite exclusion set. Fix a nonempty signed unit ﬁlter of even root
number. By CRT and Dirichlet choose one signed prime q0 in its classes, outside S∪B. Apply the
theorem to the actual curve Aq0, putting
S′ =S∪B∪{|q0|}∪{ p :p|NAq0}
in its splitting set. The theorem gives a fundamental d with χd(p) = 1 for all p∈S′ and at most
four prime factors. The functional equation forces d> 0, since Aq0 has even root number and every
conductor prime splits. Splitting at two gives d≡ 1 (mod 8) . Thus q0d has all original unit classes,
is prime to B, is squarefree, and has at most ﬁve prime factors. If q0d≡ 3 or 7 (mod 8) , its ﬁeld
discriminant is 4q0d; only d was required to be fundamental in the theorem. This is compatible
with the coeﬃcient construction (E.2).
Here h is restricted to the prepared positive odd-sign unit ﬁlter containing one, as in (3.3), so
h≡ 1 (mod 8) . The multiset of dummy labels and its size are ﬁxed before h; its realizing primes
are chosen after h and avoid its support.
For the rough companion the curve itself varies: write a for the bounded dummy product and
take A = Eha. Choose q0 so k0 = aq0 is negative, 1 modulo eight and a square at every prime of
2Nh , with the required exclusions. Then k0 is odd fundamental, prime to NEh, and
w(Ehk0) = w(Eh)χk0(−NEh) = +1.
This proves the sign directly for the actual application. Apply Hoﬀstein–Luo to Ehk0 with every
conductor, old, h, dummy and forbidden prime in its splitting set. The resulting k′ =k0d adds at
most ﬁve new primes. The constants and sizes in the analytic theorem may depend on this varying
curve and the whole ﬁnite set; the bound ﬁve does not.
G.2 The even upper transfer
Proposition G.2 (A uniformly bounded partitionable companion) . Fix E and the ﬁnitely many
prepared even sources, local types and unit seeds used for (3.3). There are constants A,CE, ﬁxed
206beforeh and before any additional ﬁnite prime exclusion set, with the following property. Let h be
positive squarefree in the prepared odd-sign unit ﬁlter containing 1, prime to its ﬁxed support; in
particularh≡ 1 (mod 8) . There is a negative odd fundamental discriminant k′, prime to 2Nh and
the additional exclusions, square at every place dividing 2Nh , for which hk′ is partitionable in the
sense of Deﬁnition 7.8, L∗(hk′)⁄= 0, and
ω(k′)≤A, v 2L∗(hk′)≤ 2ω(h) + 2ω(k′) +s(hk′) +CE.
Here A is the ﬁxed dummy-label count plus ﬁve. Dummy types and their number are ﬁxed ﬁrst,
but their distinct prime realizations are selected after h and the exclusions. The proof uses the
one-symbol stationary-address conclusion of Lemma 7.11, whose proof is given in Proposition G.6.
Proof. For one ﬁxed even source write (E.11) as
v2L∗(σD) = 2v2a(D) +κe.
The lower bound (E.9) and a nonzero reference coeﬃcient imply that the nonempty set of integers
v2a(D)−ω(D) has a ﬁnite attained minimum δe. A unit of the normalized symbol means precisely
equality to this minimum. It therefore gives
L∗(σD)⁄= 0, v 2L∗(σD) = 2ω(D) +Ce,unit, C e,unit = 2δe +κe. (F.1)
This constant belongs to the ﬁxed source, not to a curve later introduced solely to obtain nonvan-
ishing.
For the negative types used in the rough companion, the unsigned index is 7 modulo eight. Its
total (−1/D) label is nonzero, so every unit seed is nonempty. A seed of least positive size has no
dependence on internal edge bits: a nonzero edge derivative would be a smaller nonempty unit. Its
labels therefore suﬃce. If a list with the same total label contains the ﬁrst r− 1 seed anchors and
a nonempty remainder, contract that remainder into the last anchor. Its total label is forced to be
the remaining seed label. This proves partitionability regardless of how many old h-primes are in
the remainder.
For a partitionable d already known to have Selmer corank zero, the one-symbol address con-
struction keeps every prime of d stationary and gives arbitrarily large cubes dx, d0 =d, with unit
symbol at each x⁄= 0. The scalar in (E.5) is common to that cube. By (E.8) and (F.1),
v2uevx (0)≤Be :=Ce,unit +Cloc (x⁄= 0).
ChooseM−cE >B e. The constant coeﬃcient of 2cEuev∈ Λ[Gb] has sign-value bits of total Boolean
degree at most 2M −1− 1. For a larger cube dimension, the Boolean comparison in Section 9 gives
x⁄= 0 with v2(uevx (0)−uev0 (0))≥M−cE. If the base were zero or had valuation greater than Be,
this would contradict the upper bound at x. Therefore
v2L∗(d)− 2ω(d)−s(d)≤Ce,unit + 2Cloc. (F.2)
The required dimension may depend on d; this bound does not. It precedes the odd minimum and
uses no Heegner transfer.
Prepare these sources and nonempty seeds for every total type at the ﬁxed support. There are
ﬁnitely many. A bounded supply of dummy anchors and reserves works for all of them. For each h
realize that supply by distinct primes avoiding the entire ﬁxed support and h. Put every dummy
and every other already ﬁxed forbidden prime in the exclusion set of the preceding nonvanishing
construction. It gives k′ with ω(k′)≤ A, where A is the ﬁxed dummy count plus ﬁve. For the
207total type that actually occurs, hk′ is partitionable. Nonvanishing gives corank zero by the forward
theorem, so (F.2) applies. Taking the maximum over the ﬁnitely many prepared constants proves
v2L∗(hk′)≤ 2ω(h) + 2ω(k′) +s(hk′) +CE. (F.3)
No new even minimum is chosen after h or its exclusion set. Those excluded primes are not added
as relaxed places in a Selmer complex.
G.3 The odd minimum is chosen after its lower bound
Proposition G.3 (The odd minimum and every ﬁnite operator budget) . Fix the original odd
ﬁlter, the detecting sequence of Proposition F.1, and one nonprincipal ultraﬁlter on its stages. The
arithmetic lower bound (3.5), the native comparisons (3.4), and Proposition G.2 give (F.4)–(F.5)
before any odd minimum is chosen. The bounded-weight visible depths therefore have an attained
ﬁnite integer minimum δo. For every ﬁxed weight cutoﬀ, the simultaneous inequality (F.6) holds on
a set in this same ultraﬁlter. For every ﬁnite operator budget and R≥ 2, one may choose a stage
where all the integer divisions and residual errors required in Proposition F.5 are valid at every
Fourier index. On a ﬁxed squarefree-output range the unit symbol deﬁned before (F.7) is exhaustive,
and its value 1 implies (F.7), with the one constant Co = δo +Cdet. No minimum is changed on
omitting a vertex or adding stationary tests, and no single ﬁnite stage is claimed to serve every
unbounded weight range.
Proof. The arithmetic result (3.5) and the two native-lattice height comparisons (3.4) give, using
(F.3),
2jgen(h)≥ 2ω(h) +s(h)−Cgen, C gen = 2A +CR +CE−v2L∗(h∗) +CH. (F.4)
The variable s(hk′) cancels. This estimate precedes the theta prime, precision, odd minimum, ﬁnal
ﬁeld and binary dimension.
Combining (F.4) with (O.8) gives on all admissible D
v2aM (D)≥ min{M,ω (D)−Ccoarse}, C coarse =Cgen/2 +Cdet. (F.5)
At visible coeﬃcients (O.8) ﬁrst gives a simple zero, allowing (F.4); at the other odd-sign parameters
the coeﬃcient is zero modulo 2M . This avoids a circular use of the future minimum.
Fix one nonprincipal ultraﬁlter on the actual stages with M =Mθ→∞ . Consider the integers
r for which a bounded-weight sequence of visible DM hasv2aM (DM )−ω(DM ) = r on a set in the
ultraﬁlter. The ﬁxed witness Dw and (O.2),(O.7) make this set nonempty. Equation (F.5) bounds
it below. Let its attained minimum be δo. For every ﬁxed W , on a set in the same ultraﬁlter,
v2aM (D)≥ω(D) +δo for every visible D, ω(D)≤W. (F.6)
Otherwise choose a violating DM at each bad stage. One of the ﬁnitely many integers between
−Ccoarse and δo− 1 would occur on an ultraﬁlter-large set, a contradiction. Invisible coeﬃcients
satisfy the required truncated inequality once M >W +δo.

G.4 Nonempty rational seed and a common ﬁnite stage

This expression is integral because full rational two-torsion gives ap≡p + 1 (mod 4) . The positive-
twist character convention is essential in this computation. Under the assumed absence of a
nonempty unit seed, the trace identity makes this coeﬃcient zero in the residue ﬁeld. Since its
phase is a unit and ap− 1 is odd, it follows that ap≡p + 1 (mod 8) . The contradiction is obtained
only after this conditional deduction. Choose p also with (k/p) = 1 ,p≡ 1 (mod 4) and (𝓁/p) =−1,
outside the ﬁnite support. These prescriptions are compatible: K has a ramiﬁed prime outside the
ramiﬁcation of Q(i,
√
𝓁), so K∩ Q(i,
√
𝓁) = Q. The corresponding quadratic residue classes are
therefore nonempty and Dirichlet’s theorem supplies inﬁnitely many such primes. Since k is a
square modulo p, the reductions of E(k) and E𝓁 are isomorphic. A half of a nonzero two-torsion
point with root ei on a split cubic requires both diﬀerences ei−ej to be squares. For the roots
0,𝓁,−3𝓁, one of these required diﬀerences is, respectively, −𝓁,𝓁,−4𝓁. All three are nonsquares under
the chosen conditions. Thus none of the three nonzero points of order two has a half over Fp. If
the two-primary subgroup had order greater than four, its structure as a ﬁnite abelian group with
exactly four elements killed by two would give an element of order four, and hence such a half. Its
order is consequently exactly four. This gives v2(p + 1−ap) = 2 , contradicting the congruence
modulo eight above. This proves the required nonempty even seed. If the intrinsic odd symbol
already has a unit at the empty conﬁguration, its detection formula gives a simple zero for E and
the converse is ﬁnished. Otherwise take a least nonempty odd unit seed. Thus the remaining
two-symbol address argument has two nonempty seeds.
Proposition G.5 (Compatible ﬁnite graph tables on one ultraﬁlter) . Use δo and the ultraﬁlter
of Proposition G.3. First ﬁx the abstract type of the bounded elementary label groups and their
projection data on a set in this same ultraﬁlter. For every ﬁnite cutoﬀ W , the valid odd stages give
ﬁnitely many possible symbol tables on the compatible typed graphs with at most W +1 vertices. One
table occurs on a set in that ultraﬁlter. These selected tables agree on their common restrictions,
and their union deﬁnes the intrinsic odd symbol on all compatible ﬁnite typed graphs. It retains its
bounded-weight witness and the nonempty-output coeﬃcient rules. After ﬁxing k, its stationary tests
may be included in a common alphabet with separately surjective projections on the same ultraﬁlter,
without changing δo. Every speciﬁed ﬁnite collection of tests has a common actual stage. No stage
common to all cutoﬀs is selected.
Proof. Here the ﬁnite graph argument has a deﬁnite arithmetic order. The moving theta prime
contributes only a bounded number of quadratic label coordinates. The root characters at the old
212level were ﬁxed before Mθ; growing Kummer precision introduces no growing-order character on
the variable primes. Thus the elementary label groups, their projections and their ﬁnite addition
tables have bounded size. Choose their abstract type on the same ultraﬁlter as δo. For each ﬁxed
weight cutoﬀ W , restrict ﬁrst to the ultraﬁlter-large set of stages where the preceding budget and
precision conditions hold for every |U|≤ W , with R = 2 . On those stages the coeﬃcient reading,
factorization, contraction and isolation rules hold on all conﬁgurations of at most W + 1 vertices,
with the stated nonempty-output restriction. They therefore deﬁne actual symbol truth tables on
the compatible typed graph data. There are ﬁnitely many such tables; select the one occurring on
a set in the same ultraﬁlter. Two selected tables agree on their common restriction by taking the
ﬁnite intersection of their valid-stage sets. Their union deﬁnes the odd limit symbol on compatible
ﬁnite typed graphs of every size; it is not a total valuation symbol at any one ﬁnite stage. Every seed
or contraction identity uses only ﬁnitely many such graphs and is inherited at a cutoﬀ containing
all of them. The bounded-weight witness remains a witness in these tables. No stage common to
all cutoﬀs is selected. After ﬁxing k, include its tests and choose the common projection data on
the same ﬁlter, without redeﬁning the intrinsic odd minimum. Stabilize the ﬁnite alphabet and its
two projections in this way.
Proposition G.6 (Stationary and simultaneous address constructions) . Use the deﬁnitions and
realization freedoms in Deﬁnitions 7.4, 7.5, 7.10 and Lemma 7.7, and the coeﬃcient rules of Propo-
sition F.5. Fix b≥ 1. Either take one symbol on a nonempty set of always active terminals with a
nonzero polynomial in their pair bits, or take one or two symbols on terminals with nonzero linear
activations, including a terminal of every nonzero activation, such that each symbol separately has
a nonzero terminal polynomial at every x⁄= 0 . In the latter case the symbols may read diﬀerent
compatible projections of the common alphabet and ﬁxed stationary external data. Require the ac-
tive data to satisfy each prescribed ﬁlter at every nonzero address. Starting from ﬁxed compatible
typed terminal and stationary data, one can adjoin ﬁnitely many primary auxiliaries with nonzero
linear activations and choose only incidences involving them so that all the symbols equal 1 at every
x⁄= 0. All original data stay ﬁxed, no auxiliary is present at zero, and the required neutral external
incidences and splitting conditions are retained. Only nonempty conﬁgurations and nonempty con-
traction outputs are used. A nonzero iterated diﬀerence, hence a partitionability certiﬁcate, suﬃces
for the required nonzero terminal polynomial. For the two ﬁnal symbols the zero-total packets below
supply these terminal hypotheses using only separate projection surjectivity. For the even station-
ary application, the original prime representatives may be ﬁxed at the outset. For a stabilized odd
symbol, ﬁrst ﬁx the abstract data and the ﬁnite budget, then choose an actual stage carrying them,
and only then choose prime representatives. Previously ﬁxed representatives may be retained when
compatible with the full type at that stage; arbitrary earlier primes are not asserted to satisfy a later
moving condition.
Proof.
T otal labels of the ﬁnal seeds. Both ﬁnal symbols have projected total label zero. The intrinsic
odd ﬁlter contains 1: its ﬁxed unit squareclasses, positive sign, and moving condition (D/𝓁0) = 1
are those of 1. The even source for Ek is likewise normalized in the unit ﬁlter containing 1. Every
nonzero coeﬃcient, and hence every unit seed, lies in its respective ﬁlter. Additional stationary tests
at primes dividing k reﬁne the common alphabet but are forgotten by the intrinsic odd projection.
They do not add conditions to that odd symbol.
Take repeated packets with total zero in the common elementary label group, including suﬃ-
ciently many copies of every label. For either projection separately, keep the ﬁrst r− 1 anchors of
213its unit seed and contract the nonempty remainder into the last anchor. Since the total projected
label is zero, the remainder has the last seed label. This argument uses surjectivity of each projec-
tion separately. It does not require that a ﬁxed old odd seed admit lifts whose total is zero in the
common alphabet. The whole packet already has that total, and contractions preserve it. Thus the
ﬁnal twists satisfy the stationary tests as well as both coeﬃcient ﬁlters. Concretely, choose a packet
with an even number of copies of every common label, and enough copies to provide the anchors
of either seed under its respective projection. Its total label is zero. For every nonzero activation
λ, take a disjoint copy of this whole packet and give all its vertices activation λ. At every x⁄= 0
there are exactly 2b−1 active packets. The active list is therefore nonempty, has total common label
zero, and contains enough anchors for each projected seed separately. Its terminal polynomial for
each symbol is nonzero by the contraction certiﬁcate, regardless of the initially ﬁxed bits. It also
contains a terminal of each required activation. This constructs the terminal input of the address
lemma explicitly. No simultaneous lifting of a prescribed pair of seed labels is needed. Every added
network vertex is primary, so the total common label remains zero at each address.
Network operators and their degree. We spell out the simultaneous argument, including the
product over all addresses. Write ∂e for the ﬁnite diﬀerence in a terminal pair bit e. Products
of these operators commute and ∂2e = 0 . The contraction rule identiﬁes a forest product with its
contracted graph. A cycle product is zero: after a spanning path is contracted its last edge is
internal and changes neither a type nor any external incidence. No contraction removes the last
vertex.
A network of primary auxiliary vertices acts on any of the symbols by an operator in the ∂e. To
see this directly, expand in the bits incident to the auxiliary vertices. Contract the chosen forest
edges. A component with no terminal becomes an isolated primary vertex, and its contribution
vanishes by primary isolation. A component meeting several terminals gives their forest diﬀerence.
IfQ is the grounded auxiliary Laplacian and ca is its attachment column at terminal a, the degree-
zero and degree-one terms are
det(Q) 1 +
∑
a<b
cta adj(Q)cb∂ab.
All other terms have diﬀerence degree at least two. The displayed coeﬃcients are the rooted-
forest minors, by Cauchy–Binet on the incidence matrix. They are polynomial identities, so apply
to singular Q as well. Distinct networks have no edges between their auxiliary vertices. Their
operators multiply: expanding both forests gives their union, and unions containing a terminal
cycle give zero by the preceding observation. This also shows that the operator is the same for
both projected symbols.
Fix a target x∗⁄= 0 . Choose a basis λ1,...,λ b of the dual address space with λi(x∗) = 1 for
all i. Such a basis is obtained from one functional taking value one by adding it to a basis of
the space of functionals vanishing at x∗. Group i has three primary vertices, each activated by λi.

orthogonal complement. The vector ei has norm one. Prescribe the symmetric matrix Q by its

For a selected terminal pair a,b active at x∗, let their activations be α,β . Attach an extra

it is absent at x∗. The extra columns sum to zero at every address since α +β + (α +β) = 0 . If

For the singular network set T = I +P , where P is the cyclic shift, and give B one diagonal

The total degree available to all terminal polynomials is D =∑y⁄=0(d1,y +d2,y).
Each singular network at x has zero constant at x, so its action costs at least one diﬀerence in
each of the two factors there, hence at least two in (G.1a). For a varying network, summing over
itsε cancels the all-constant term. Its ﬁrst-degree terms are independent of ε away from its target,
and their diﬀerence at the target is ∂e. Thus every surviving term costs at least one diﬀerence, and
a cost-one term acts at its target on exactly one of the two factors. The sum of all these minimum
costs is exactly D. Any higher-degree action, or any additional action at a wrong address, exceeds
D and annihilates at least one terminal polynomial. Hence only the minimum-cost terms survive.
215At address x, a variable with exponent two is therefore diﬀerentiated once in each factor; a
variable with exponent one is diﬀerentiated in exactly one factor, summed over the two possibilities.
The resulting scalar is precisely [Mx](H1,xH2,x) = 1 . Lower-degree parts cannot contribute. Taking
the product over all x⁄= 0 proves that (G.1a) equals one. At least one choice of the ﬁnitely many ε
consequently makes every factor in that product one. This is one graph for both symbols at every
nonzero address. Boolean reduction of H1,xH2,x before selecting Mx would destroy this exponent-
two accounting and is not used. Here is the stationary and one-symbol specialization, so that the
application in Proposition G.2 uses the same proved construction. If all terminals are always active,
attach each column hi = Qei to any one ﬁxed terminal. Since hi is supported on its own group,
their restricted sum is QJ 1J at every address. The two additional columns c at a selected terminal
pair cancel directly; no balancing terminal of activation α+β is needed. The same singular program
therefore isolates that pair diﬀerence at its target address. For one symbol, choose a coeﬃcient-one
top monomial of each nonzero Px and put a separate singular program at x for each variable in that
monomial. Its own programs already spend degPx contraction degrees at x; any extra degree kills
the term. Thus exactly the selected top coeﬃcient survives, and it is one. A constant nonzero Px
needs no program. This proves the stationary one-symbol case as well as the moving one-symbol
case. In all cases the primary realization of Lemma 7.7 changes only incidences with the newly
adjoined vertices. These are ﬁnite constructions for each b, with no assertion of one inﬁnite prime
array.
Proposition G.7 (One actual stage for the ﬁnal family) . In the nonempty-seed case of Proposi-

H De Rham comparison and ﬁnite descent in the height argument
Lemma H.1. LetK/Q𝓁 andF/K be ﬁnite extensions, let A/F be an abelian surface, and let C𝓁/Q𝓁
be a ﬁnite coeﬃcient ﬁeld. Suppose a continuous representation r :GK−→ GL2(C𝓁) satisﬁes
V𝓁(A)⊗Q𝓁C𝓁≃r|GF⊕r|GF.
Then r is de Rham. At every coeﬃcient embedding its Hodge–Tate weights are 0,−1, with Q𝓁(1)
assigned weight−1.
Proof. The smooth proper variety A has a smooth proper rigid analytiﬁcation. Apply [ 15, Corollary
1.8], with the corrections in [ 16]. The passage from algebraic to analytic cohomology uses [ 15,
Theorems 9.1 and 9.3]: Theorem 9.1 identiﬁes coherent cohomology, and hence the ﬁltered de
Rham hypercohomology, while Theorem 9.3 identiﬁes the Z/𝓁nZ-cohomology, compatibly in n. Its
invertibility condition holds because F has characteristic zero. Taking inverse limits and then
inverting𝓁 identiﬁes the algebraic and analytic 𝓁-adic cohomology, compatibly with the action of
GF . Thus H 1et(AF, Q𝓁) is de Rham.
The canonical, Galois-equivariant identiﬁcation [ 18, Part I, Theorem 12.1(a)]
V𝓁(A)≃H 1et(AF, Q𝓁)∨
gives the same property for the Tate module. De Rham representations are closed under duals and
subquotients [ 17, Theorem 5.2.1 and Proposition 6.3.3]. Extension of the coeﬃcient ﬁeld is, on
underlying Q𝓁-representations, a ﬁnite direct sum. The displayed decomposition therefore makes
r|GF de Rham.
Here ﬁnite-extension descent requires no semistable descent assertion. To see it explicitly, pass
to a ﬁnite Galois extension F ′/K containingF , and let W be the underlying Q𝓁-representation of
r. The vector space
DF ′ = (BdR⊗Q𝓁W )GF ′
has its natural semilinear Gal(F ′/K)-action, and its invariants are DK = (BdR⊗Q𝓁W )GK . Finite
Galois descent gives F ′⊗K DK ≃ DF ′, also on each ﬁltration step. Since W|GF ′ is de Rham,
dimKDK = dimF ′DF ′ = dim Q𝓁W . This proves the assertion over K; it is the ﬁnite-extension case
of [ 17, Proposition 6.3.8].
The two Hodge numbers of H 1dR(A/F ) are 2, 2. Consequently the Tate module has weights
0, 0,−1,−1. At each coeﬃcient embedding, its two isomorphic summands have the same weight
multiset, so each summand has weights 0,−1. The ﬁltration-compatible descent preserves these
weights.
The Hodge–Tate comparison in [ 34, Chapter III, Theorem 4.1] alone would not prove the de
Rham assertion. The preceding argument uses the de Rham comparison itself and does not infer
de Rhamness from Hodge–Tate weights.
218I Simultaneous primes with coprime adjacent norms
This appendix gives the prime-pattern argument needed for the construction. Its strategy is due
to Sun [ 7]. We supply the local calculations, the two ranges of the sieve, the normalization of the
ray-class densities, and the order in which the parameters are chosen. For the analytic input we
prove the special case for ﬁxed ray class ﬁelds of Q(i) directly. The ideal-norm method follows Frei
and Tinková [ 10]; no theorem from that work is assumed. The proof uses the published ﬁxed-ﬁeld
prime estimate of Mitsui, the one-parameter equidistribution and factorization results of Green–
Tao, and published ordinary-prime transference results. The ﬁeld and its ray modulus are ﬁxed
before every asymptotic limit. The exact source versions checked are identiﬁed in the bibliography
and the accompanying theorem-application inventory.
Write N(z) = z ¯z for z∈ Z[i], and put d−(z) = N(z− 1) and d+(z) = N(z + 1). An Artin or
Frobenius element in this appendix is arithmetic Frobenius: on residue ﬁelds it sends x to xNp.
Theorem I.1 (The simultaneous prime input) . Letc1,...,c k be distinct integers, where k≥ 2. Let
q≥ 1, let M be an odd squarefree positive integer with 7 -M , and let H,Y > 0. There are positive
integersu,w such that
q|w, u/w 2>H, p j =u +cjw2>Y (1≤j≤k),
where every pj is prime. Each pj has a Gaussian prime generator πj of norm pj such that
πj≡ 9 (mod 16) , π j≡ 1 + 3i (mod 7), π j≡i (mod M ).
No odd prime divides both d−(πj) and d+(πj). For j⁄= h, no odd prime divides both dϵ(πj) and
dη(πh), for any ϵ,η∈{−, +}. Moreover,
v2(d−(πj)) = 6, v 2(d+(πj)) = 2, gcd(d−(πj),d +(πh)) = 4 for all j,h,
and all these norms are coprime to 7M .
The quantiﬁers concern ﬁxed data. No estimate uniform in q,M,k or the cj is asserted. We
ﬁrst prove the result for q = 1. Replacing the coeﬃcients by q2cj and then replacing the parameter
v by w = qv proves the general case, since the ratio before this substitution can be made greater
than q2H.
I.1 Local counts and the medium primes
Fix a nonzero integer κ and write
Qκ =a2 +b2−c2−d2−κw2, G ϵ = (a +ϵ)2 +b2, H η = (c +η)2 +d2, ϵ,η ∈{ 1,−1}.
We use χ(𝓁) = (−1/𝓁) for odd primes 𝓁.
Lemma I.2 (Finite-ﬁeld counts) . If 𝓁 - 2κ, then Qκ = 0 has 𝓁4− 1 nonzero solutions over F𝓁. At
most 4𝓁2 of its solutions satisfy Gϵ =Hη = 0. If χ(𝓁) =−1, the latter set has exactly one element.
Letγ1,...,γ k be distinct integers, including 0 and−κ, and put Lj =a2 +b2 +γjw2. Outside a
ﬁxed set of primes containing the divisors of 2κ∏j<h(γj−γh), the proportion g(𝓁) of nonzero zeros
of Qκ on which some Lj vanishes is
g(𝓁) = (𝓁− 1)(𝓁−χ(𝓁))[2ν0 + (k− 2)(𝓁−χ(𝓁))] +ν20− 1𝓁4− 1 , ν 0 =𝓁 + (𝓁− 1)χ(𝓁). (58)
Consequentlyg(𝓁) = (k + 2χ(𝓁))/𝓁 +Ok(𝓁−2).
219Proof. The norm x2 +y2 hasν0 representations of zero and 𝓁−χ(𝓁) representations of every nonzero
element. This follows either by factoring over F𝓁 when χ = 1, or by the norm map from F𝓁2 when
χ =−1. Summing these ﬁbres for Qκ gives𝓁4 solutions including the origin. In the split case, each
of the equations Gϵ = 0 and Hη = 0 is a union of two lines. On each of the four pairs of lines the
remaining equation is −2ϵa + 2ηc−κw2 = 0 , leaving at most 𝓁2 choices. In the inert case these
equations force (a,b,c,d ) = (−ϵ, 0,−η, 0), and then w = 0.
Forw⁄= 0, the conditions Lj = 0 are disjoint. For the two distinguished coeﬃcients one norm is
zero and the other is nonzero, giving 2ν0(𝓁−χ) choices. Each other coeﬃcient gives (𝓁−χ)2 choices.
There are 𝓁− 1 possible nonzero w. For w = 0, both norms are zero, giving ν20− 1 nonzero vectors.
This proves ( 58). In particular, when k = 2 and χ =−1, its value is 2/(𝓁2 + 1), not zero.
Lemma I.3 (Primitive congruence count) . Let ψ be a smooth nonnegative compactly supported

Proof. Apply Browning–Loughran [ 8], Theorem 4.1, to Qκ in ﬁve variables. It is isotropic, since

modulus and in these subsets. Impose primitivity by ∑h|(a,b,c,d,w)µ(h). If (h,m )> 1 there are no
permitted vectors. Otherwise the subsets become h−1Ω𝓁, of the same cardinalities, and the scale

local factors are positive because the form has a nonsingular rational zero. Empty subsets give the
assertion directly.

from zero. The constants in error terms may depend on derivatives of this majorant, and hence on
τ .

For a ﬁxed pair of positions, the vector (a,b,c,d,w ) determines u = a2 +b2−cjw2; at one

many pairs of positions and signs. Each split rational prime has eight Gaussian elements of that
norm, so counting all generator choices changes the bound by at most a ﬁxed factor. Every vector
used in the medium-prime count is primitive, since a common integer divisor greater than one
would have its square divide a prime norm. All logarithmic factors in the remainders are absorbed
for suﬃciently large B, with thresholds depending only on the ﬁxed data and τ . Weakening the
error to B3−1/400 =N 3/2−1/800 proves the claim.

on rational primes which split in E.

Choose a ﬁxed integer W divisible by the conductor of Kab. It is in particular divisible by 4.
For a unit b (mod W ) put

Corollary I.9 (Centered sums on progressions) . For every D> 0,
∑
n∈P
hC,b,W (n) = OH,W,D(N (logN )−D)
uniformly over arithmetic progressions P contained in [N ].
Proof. Write P as a residue class modulo q intersected with an interval. If q > (logN )D+3, its
cardinality is at most N/(logN )D+3 + 1, and the pointwise logarithmic bound proves the claim
directly. Otherwise the condition n∈P becomes one residue class a (mod Wq ) and an interval for
Wn +b.
If (a,Wq ) > 1, every prime in this residue class divides Wq . There are at most O(log(Wq ))
such primes, with total logarithmic weight polylogarithmic in N , which is negligible here. Suppose
instead that (a,Wq ) = 1 . Since Q(ζWq ) is abelian and already contains Kab, one has K∩Q(ζWq ) =
Kab. Also a≡b (mod W ), so the lemma’s main coeﬃcient is
µC(b)
ϕ(Wq ).
For the ordinary prime weight, the Siegel–Walﬁsz main coeﬃcient is 1/ϕ(Wq ). The two terms of
(71) therefore have equal main terms. Apply the progression lemma at both interval endpoints,
with an error exponent larger than D and with X = OW (N ), and subtract. This proves the
claimed bound. All moduli used are at most a ﬁxed power of logN , and W is ﬁxed throughout
this argument.
227I.4 Transfer from Gaussian ideal norms to polynomial nilsequences
Throughout this subsection E = Q(i),OE = Z[i], andH0 is a ﬁxed positive integer. A ray character
χ modulo H0OE is extended by zero to ideals not coprime to H0OE. The trivial character on all
ideals is included by taking H0 = 1. We use the conventions of [ 2]: G is connected, simply connected
and nilpotent, Γ is a discrete cocompact subgroup, and the ﬁltration consists of closed connected
rational subgroups. Lipschitz norms include the supremum norm.
Lemma I.10 (Gaussian norm transfer) . Fix positive integers m,d . There are κ,σ > 0, depending
only on m,d , and constants C,X 0,δ 0 > 0, depending on H0,m,d , with the following property.

We prove the lemma using only the one-parameter quantitative Leibman theorem [ 2, Theorem

Lemma I.12 (Small horizontal character obstruction) . There are c0,σ 0 > 0, depending only on
m,d , such that the following holds for suﬃciently large X and suﬃciently small δ≥X −σ0. Suppose

Proof. Choose representatives of the nonconstant binomial coeﬃcients of η◦g satisfying|αj|≤
TX −j. On the initial interval of length ⌊X/(100dT )⌋ its values lie in an arc of length less than
1/10, translated by its constant coeﬃcient. Choose a Lipschitz function on R/Z which equals one
on that arc, has mean zero and bounded Lipschitz norm. Its composition with η has Lipschitz
norm at most TC(m,d), by the Mal’cev metric estimates in [ 2, Appendix A]. Its Haar mean is zero:
a nontrivial horizontal character maps Haar measure onto Haar measure on the circle. Choose
c0 with C(m,d )c0 < 1/4 and c0 < 1/4. The initial interval is longer than δX , while the discrep-
ancy one exceeds δTC(m,d). Taking σ0 suﬃciently small absorbs the ﬂoors. This contradicts total
equidistribution.

Remark I.13. For Type II, apply the lemma in dimension 2m to (g1(n),g 2(n)) and F1⊗F2, when
this entire product orbit is totally equidistributed. Mean-zero factors ensure that the product test
function has mean zero; they do not imply equidistribution of the product orbit. Use the smaller
exponents for dimensions m and 2m. In dimension zero mean-zero test functions vanish, so the
assertion is immediate.
The constraints rn≡ b (mod q) and r′n≡ b (mod q) either are inconsistent or specify one
residue class modulo a divisor of q. Intersecting their interval restrictions only changes A,B . Thus
all Type I and Type II truncations are covered by the lemma. If δ = (log X)−B1 and Q,q ≤
(logX)C1, any desired saving X(logX)−A1 follows once B1κ> max{C1,A 1}, with H0 ﬁxed.
230I.5 Gaussian ideal Vaughan decomposition and Type I/II estimates
We give the ideal decomposition and the subsequent integer-parameter recombination in full. The
architecture is that of [ 39, Section 3], with the Gaussian ideal-norm adaptation attributed to Frei–
Tinkova. The analytic inputs used here are the separately proved Lemma I.10 and the published
one-parameter results [ 2, Theorem 2.9 and Lemma 4.5]. In particular, no theorem of Frei–Tinkova
is an assumed input.
Fix the Gaussian ray modulus H0. All ideals in this subsection are nonzero integral ideals of
Z[i]. Write Na for the absolute ideal norm, µE for ideal Mobius, and ΛE(pj) = log N p for j≥ 1,
with ΛE zero on other ideals. A ray character χ is extended by zero on ideals not coprime to
H0. It is completely multiplicative and satisﬁes |χ|≤ 1. We use a Lipschitz norm that includes
the supremum norm. All nilmanifolds have connected, simply connected nilpotent covering group,
a cocompact lattice, and an adapted rational Mal’cev basis, with the ﬁltration and polynomial-
sequence conventions of [ 2].

cutoﬀs. Finally ∑c|d ΛE(c) = log N d and every summand is nonnegative. This proves the coeﬃcient
bounds, with no discarded endpoint or error term.

233Lemma I.17 (Integer-parameter recombination) . For each m,d there are Crec,K rec≥ 2 with the

product orbit (g(rn),g (r′n))n≤B is not totally η-equidistributed.

fails. Apply [ 2, Theorem 2.9], in dimension m or 2m. A nonzero horizontal character has magnitude
and restricted smoothness norm at most η−C1, after shrinking η0 to absorb ﬁxed factors.
Here is the required removal of a possibly large shift. Write the scalar phase p(a +qt) in
monomials. Multiplication by d! clears conversion from its binomial coeﬃcients. Inverting t =
(n−a)/q and multiplying by qd clears every remaining denominator. The error in the coeﬃcient
of ni is bounded by

The integer parts disappear modulo one. Thus a nonzero integer multiple of the character has
magnitude and C∞[M ] norm at most η−C2. This argument does not treat a =O(M ) as a bounded-
height rational parameter. There are at most η−C3 possible resulting characters, since their lattice
has rank at most 2m. Pigeonholing therefore ﬁxes one character on a set of density at least ηC4.
In Type I write ψ(g(n)) =∑dj=0βjnj modulo one. For the selected set S⊆ (R/2,R ], coeﬃcient
conversion gives
‖d!βjrj‖R/Z≤η−C5(R/N)j, 1≤j≤d. (84)
If R≤η−C6 for a suﬃciently large ﬁxed C6, choose one r∈S and multiply the character by d!rd.
Multiplication of the jth bound by rd−j gives magnitude and C∞[N ] norm bounded by a ﬁxed
power of η−1. This treats every small dyadic scale, including R = 1.
Otherwise apply [ 2, Lemma 4.5] to r↦→ d!βjrj on [⌈R⌉]. The selected set has density at least
a ﬁxed power of η, and its values lie in an arc of length at most 2η−C5(R/N)j. Taking Krec large,

For Type II the pigeonholed character is ψ1⊕ψ2, not both zero. Write the two scalar phases

Finally let Ψ denote the nonzero character just obtained. Its binomial expansion shows that
for 1 ≤ n ≤ cη2C9+4N the phase Ψ(g(n)) stays within 1/10 of Ψ(g(0)). Choose a mean-zero
Lipschitz circle function equal to one on that arc and compose it with Ψ. Its mean is zero because
a nonzero horizontal character pushes Haar measure to circle Haar measure. Its Lipschitz norm
is bounded by a ﬁxed power of η−1 by [ 2, Appendix A]. Choose Crec larger than the length and
Lipschitz exponents, and then enlarge Krec so the initial interval is nonempty. Its average one
contradicts total ηCrec-equidistribution. This proves both assertions. All exponent constants are

I.6 Fixed-modulus aﬃne interpolation
Fix integers W≥ 1 and 0≤b<W . The modulus W remains ﬁxed as the size parameter tends to
inﬁnity. We ﬁrst justify the aﬃne interpolation needed to apply ideal-norm estimates to a W -tricked
sequence.
Lemma I.20 (Linear coeﬃcient extrapolation) . Let X≥ 1 and let p : Z→ R/Z have degree at
most d. Suppose P (t) = u +vt with u,v ∈ Z, v⁄= 0 ,|u|≤ CX ,|v|≤ E, and L≥ X/E, where

‖kp‖C∞[X]≤Cd(C +E)Cd‖p◦P‖C∞[L].
Proof. Expand

is vj, and the coeﬃcient in row 𝓁 is Od((C +E)jXj−𝓁). Starting in degree d, clear the nonzero
diagonal entries successively. Their product is a nonzero integer bounded by EOd(1). At degree
j use Xj/Lj ≤ Ej; a higher-row error is multiplied by Od((C +E)hXh−j), canceling its X −h
factor. Multiplication to the common product of diagonal entries proves the claim, exactly as in
Lemma I.11.

Remark I.24. Fix the arithmetic moduli ﬁrst, then the positive exponents in this subsection. They
do not depend on the later factorization exponent B. Proposition I.25 chooses B large enough
to satisfy the resulting upper complexity condition. Its output M is polylogarithmic in N , so its
equidistribution parameter also obeys δ≥ N −σ1 for suﬃciently large N . The aﬃne interpolation
argument is motivated by the corresponding step of Frei–Tinkova, but uses no theorem from that
work as an input.
I.7 Quantitative factorization for centered weights
We isolate the analytic implication needed after the arithmetic progression main terms have been
established. Throughout, L = log(2N ), all nilpotent groups are connected and simply connected,
and all ﬁltrations are by closed connected rational subgroups. Constants may depend on ﬁxed
arithmetic moduli and on the ﬁxed dimension and degree bounds.
Proposition I.25 (Factorization implication) . Fix dimension and degree bounds m,d . Let wN :
[N ]→ C satisfy the following three hypotheses.
1. For a ﬁxed Kw≥ 0,|wN (n)|≪ LKw .
2392. For every A0,C 0> 0, uniformly over all interval progressions P⊆ [N ] of common diﬀerence
at most LC0, ⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐∑n∈PwN (n)⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐≪A0,C0 NL −A0. (95)
No lower bound on |P| is imposed here.
3. There are ﬁxed c > 0, C1,K 1≥ 0 such that if a nilmanifold of dimension at most m and
ﬁltration degree at most d has a Q-rational adapted Mal’cev basis, h is a polynomial sequence
totally δ-equidistributed on [N ], and F0 is Lipschitz with Haar mean zero, then

Then, for every A,C init> 0, uniformly over initial nilmanifolds of dimension at most m and degree

Proof. Normalize‖F‖Lip = 1 and put

Leave a factorization exponent B unspeciﬁed for the moment. By [ 2, Theorem 1.19], there is an

and a factorization g =εg′γ into polynomial sequences. Here ε is (M,N )-smooth,γ(n)Γ has period

For each residue j modulo q, choose a reduced rational representative γj of γ(j)Γ. Split that
residue class into ⌈MLD⌉ consecutive blocks of nearly equal cardinality. For suﬃciently large N
after B is ﬁxed, the resulting progressions Pj,k satisfy
#{Pj,k}≪ M 2LD, |Pj,k|≫ NM 2LD, diam(Pj,k)≪ NMLD. (99)
Their common diﬀerence is q. Indeed each original residue class has cardinality comparable to N/q,
and rounding the balanced block sizes changes these estimates only by an absolute factor, since all
powers of M are polylogarithmic in N once B is ﬁxed.

We use the quantitative conjugation claim proved in [ 39, p. 547], whose inputs are the rational-

We have enlarged C2 to absorb ﬁxed multiplicative constants. These exponents do not depend on

from rational conjugation; conjugation is not asserted to be an isometry of the original quotients.
Its ﬁnite-index and metric losses are included in a,C 2.

Remark I.26 (Order of parameter choices) . The order in the proof is
(A,Kw,C init,c,C 1,K 1,a,C 2,κ,σ )−→ (D,U )−→B−→E∗−→ (A0,C 0)−→N0.
Thus there is no requirement that B dominate the factorization upper exponent E(B,m,d ). The
centered progression estimate allows every logarithmic saving, so its exponent can be chosen af-
ter E∗ is known. The minor-arc input must have a ﬁxed power form such as ( 96); unspeciﬁed
polylogarithmic specializations alone would not justify this argument.
241Corollary I.27 (Fixed-modulus centered nilsequence estimate) . Fix the Gaussian ray ﬁeld and a
conjugation-invariant union C of ray classes as above. Let W be a ﬁxed multiple of the conductor
of Kab/Q, and let b be a unit residue modulo W . For every A,C init > 0 and ﬁxed dimension and
degree bounds, uniformly over the nilmanifolds and polynomial sequences in Proposition I.25 with
an (log(2N ))Cinit-rational adapted basis,
⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐∑n≤NhC,b,W (n)F (g(n)Γ)⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐≪H,W,A,Cinit,m,dN (log(2N ))−A‖F‖Lip.
The constants are uniform in b and in the polynomial orbit. No uniformity as W varies is asserted.
Proof. We check the three hypotheses of Proposition I.25. The pointwise inequality |hC,b,W|≤ λb,W
gives its ﬁrst hypothesis with Kw = 1 . Corollary I.9 gives its second hypothesis for every A0,C 0,
in fact for every interval progression.
For the third hypothesis, take H0 =H in Corollary I.23. Its character expansion is ( 66), with
the factor 1/2 and the prime-power error supplied by ( 65) and the bound following that equation.
Its two assumed minor-arc estimates are Proposition I.18 and Corollary I.19. Apply that corollary
to the ﬁxed linear combination deﬁning hC,b,W in (71). It gives ( 96) withc =cW ,C1 = 1 andK1 = 7
in the regime δ≥ N −σ1, Q≤ δ−a. The aﬃne-interpolation result initially restricts δ to a ﬁxed
suﬃciently small interval. For larger δ, the bound |hC,b,W|≪ W log(2N ) gives ( 96) directly after
enlarging its ﬁxed constant. For a dimension-zero quotient the mean-zero test function vanishes.
For all other dimensions and degrees within the ﬁxed bounds, take the minimum of the ﬁnitely
many positive exponents cW,a,σ 1 and the maximum of the ﬁxed constants. Thus the restricted
regime ( 97) holds with ﬁxed positive κ,σ , uniformly throughout the required class.
Proposition I.25 now proves the displayed estimate. There are only ﬁnitely many unit residues
modulo the ﬁxed W , so their constants can be bounded by a single constant. In particular, division
byN and the relabeling W =V , N =L give the ﬁxed-complexity estimate ( 102).
I.8 From Gaussian nilsequence estimates to the polynomial count
Fix the Gaussian ray ﬁeld K/Q(i) and the conjugation-invariant ray classes used in the preceding
argument. Let D be divisible by the conductor of Kab/Q. For each relevant union C of classes,
let Λ′C be its rational prime weight and let µC(b) be the conditional density in ( 108). In particular
0≤µC(b)≤ 1. We use the following conclusion of the preceding Gaussian norm, progression and
factorization arguments, as assembled in Corollary I.27: for every ﬁxed multiple V ofD, every unit
b (mod V ), every ﬁxed bounded-complexity nilsequence F (g(n)Γ), and every B >0,
|En≤LhC,V,b(n)F (g(n)Γ)|≪ V,B, complexity (logL)−B,
hC,V,b(n) = ϕ(V )V (Λ′C(Vn +b)−µC(b)Λ′(Vn +b)). (102)
The bound is uniform in the polynomial orbit and functions with the stated complexity bound.
The ﬁeld, its ray modulus and V are ﬁxed in this statement. We do not assume any uniform rate
as V varies.
Published transference inputs. We use Green–Tao, Linear equations in primes [38], Propo-
sition 6.4 and Appendix D for the ordinary prime majorant, Proposition 7.1 and Appendix C for
its generalized von Neumann bound, and Lemma 4.4 with its subsequent normal-form reduction.
These are statements about the ordinary prime majorant and arbitrary functions dominated by it;
242they do not assert an Artin-prime theorem. We also use Dodos–Kanellopoulos [ 11], Theorem 5.1, in
the following form, checked in arXiv:1610.00487v4, pp. 12–13. The ﬁnal typeset statement has not
been compared. For target interval norm Us[L], s≥ 2, its hypothesis on a nonnegative majorant
on a prime cyclic group of size L′∈ [CL, 2CL], C≥ 20, is
‖ν− 1‖U 2s(Z/L′Z)≤η. (103)
For a real function |f|≤ ν with‖f‖U s[L]≥δ, the conclusion is a correlation bounded below with
a bounded Lipschitz linear nilsequence on an (s− 1)-step nilmanifold. Its nilmanifold, Lipschitz
bound and positive correlation threshold depend only on s,C,δ . The interval norm is the normalized
zero-extension norm. In particular a target Uu+1 requires majorant norm U 2u+2.

the current Vz.
Here are all normalization and limit details. First choose the complexity of the ordinary prime
majorant larger than both the requirements of the intended generalized von Neumann bound and
max(4s, 4s). Choose its ambient constant C≥ 20 large enough for both published majorant and
generalized von Neumann statements. If λV,b(n) = ( ϕ(V )/V )Λ′(Vn +b), then |hC,V,b| ≤λV,b.
The majorant simultaneously dominates a ﬁxed number of such ordinary weights on [L3/5,L ],
with a multiplicative constant depending only on the chosen complexities. Consequently there
is α >0, depending only on the ﬁxed complexities and the ﬁxed factor in the modulus, such that
α1[L3/5,L]hC,Vz,b is dominated in absolute value by ν on [L]. The same α works for all cutoﬀs and
unit residues. In applying the inverse theorem to a lower bound δ for the unscaled norm, use the
lower bound αδ/2 for this truncated, scaled function, once the truncation error is at most δ/2.
For suﬃciently large z, Vz = E∏p≤zp with E ﬁxed. The ordinary divisor-sum construction
works with this Vz: at primes dividing E, the unit residue excludes every divisor term containing
that prime, and at all other primes its local factors are unchanged. The same observation applies to
the correlation upper bounds in Appendix D. Thus the stated pseudorandomness conditions remain
valid, uniformly in unit residues, with constants allowed to depend on E. Expand the centered (2s)-
cube of ν− 1. It contains at most 4s distinct forms, in 2s + 1 variables, with coeﬃcients zero or
one. Every subproduct has mean 1 +o(1) by the linear-forms condition; inclusion–exclusion gives
(103) with a left side tending to zero. This estimate is on the full cyclic group.
The removed initial interval contributes o(1) to the interval Us norm. Indeed the weights are
O(logL) ifVz =Lo(1), and a cube with its initial vertex in this interval has relative count O(L−2/5).
The 2sth power of the norm of the removed function is at most O((logL)2sL−2/5). Its correlation
with a bounded nilsequence is also o(1).
If ( 104) fails by some ﬁxed δ >0, Theorem 5.1 gives a ﬁxed-complexity nilsequence correlation
bounded away from zero, contradicting ( 102). There is no illicit varying-modulus use of that input.
243For each integer z and each of the ﬁnitely many residues modulo Vz, ﬁrst take a size threshold
for the required nilsequence estimates. Diagonalize these thresholds also over δ = 1, 1/2, 1/3,...
and over the ﬁnite list of norm orders needed. Impose in addition z(L)≤ log log logL and all
size thresholds of the majorant. At stage j, choose the cutoﬀ zj large enough for the limiting
majorant errors to be below half the inverse-theorem tolerances needed for the ﬁrst j tests; only
then choose their size threshold Lj. The resulting increasing step function tends to inﬁnity and
proves the assertion for all suﬃciently large L. The ﬁxed smooth metric in the inverse theorem is
comparable to a ﬁxed Mal’cev metric, and a linear orbit with arbitrary base point is a polynomial
orbit, so the nilsequence input applies to exactly the supplied correlations. When this construction
is used at the reﬁned scale L≍N/Vzj below, choose the physical threshold Nj so large that every
constant multiple of N/Vzj used by the normal-form reduction is at least Lj for N≥Nj. Impose
the same growth constraints there. Thus the cutoﬀ is constant on each physical-scale stage; no
implicit equation z =z(N/Vz) is being assumed.

The same conclusion holds for a mixed product in which any selected factors have already been

Proof. We can discard points where some ψi≤ N 9/10. For a nonconstant ﬁxed linear form their
number is OΨ,L0(Nd−1(N 9/10 + 1)); the product of weights is O((logN )t). This is o(Nd), uniformly
in the constant terms. Apply the auxiliary Vz-trick to the variables: n =Vzm + r. If one output
residue is not a unit modulo Vz, its prime weight vanishes on the remaining range, since the output
is larger than every prime dividing Vz. For an admissible residue vector write
ψi(Vzm + r) = Vz~ψi,r(m) +bi,r, 1≤bi,r≤Vz.
The new linear coeﬃcients are the original ones. Its normalized constant terms remain bounded at
scale N/Vz. Because D|Vz, the factor µCi is constant on this residue vector.
Telescope the diﬀerence of the two products. After normalization by (ϕ(Vz)/Vz)t, each term
has one factor hCi,Vz,bi; all other factors are nonnegative and at most λVz,bj . Introduce a common
ordinary-prime majorant for these at most t weights and truncate as above. Multiply every factor
by a common ﬁxed α0 > 0 so that each has absolute value at most that majorant, and apply
the normal-form extension of Green–Tao Lemma 4.4 and Proposition 7.1. Here α0 depends only
on the ﬁxed system, the chosen complexities and the ﬁxed conductor, not on z or the residue
vector. The small factor has the required norm by ( 104). Dividing the resulting estimate by αt0
changes only a ﬁxed constant. The normal-form extension adds only a ﬁxed number of variables
with ﬁxed coeﬃcients; its convex-region reduction is the one following Lemma 4.4, and divides out
their box volume. The error on each residue vector is therefore o((N/Vz)d), uniformly in the vector
and convex region. The discarded initial segment stays outside the retained output range since
Vz =No(1).
One can make this reduction explicit. For each pair i⁄= j choose an integer vector vij with
˙ψj(vij) = 0 and ˙ψi(vij)⁄= 0; ﬁxed nonproportional linear parts ensure such vectors exist with ﬁxed
size. Replace m by x +∑i⁄=jyijvij and allow each new yij to range over an integer interval of
244length comparable to N/Vz. The block yij for ﬁxed i certiﬁes normal form. The region deﬁned
by the original convex constraint on this sum and the new box is convex. The integer change
(x, y)↦→ (x +∑yijvij, y) is bijective, so its sum is exactly the original sum times the number of
points in the new box. Division by that number gives the asserted error. If there is only one form,
choose an integer vector v with ˙ψ1(v)⁄= 0 and replace m by x +yv. The same box and bijection
argument gives the reduction in this case.

I.9 Fixed Artin conditions in quadratic patterns

We now use the arbitrary-function dense-model theorem of Tao–Ziegler [ 12]. Their published
Theorem 11 and Corollary 3 give a single bounded model accurate in both global Gowers norms
and the local polynomial box norms. Their Theorem 9 is the bounded-function concatenation
statement, and their Theorem 13 bounds the polynomial average by one of those local norms. All
majorants here are the ordinary prime majorants, since 0≤ Λ′j≤ Λ′. The ﬁxed factor D in W is
allowed in their divisor-sum construction: for suﬃciently large v, W = E∏p≤vp with a ﬁxed E
whose prime factors are at most v. A unit residue makes every small-prime divisibility condition
identically false, including at the extra prime powers. At the remaining primes the divisor-sum
local factors are unchanged. Polynomial coeﬃcient bounds absorb the ﬁxed factor E. Thus the
polynomial-forms estimates for the majorant have the same 1 +o(1) conclusion.
The following choice records the size hypotheses of these results. First choose their local box
order d′ and a coeﬃcient exponent C; choose an integer B0 > C + 2 , then a suﬃciently large
global order a, and ﬁnally a suﬃciently small ρ. Set ρ∗ =ρ/B0 andA =AB0. Put M0 =⌊T/W⌋,
M1 =⌊M0/A⌋. For T/

Remark I.30 (Published numbering) . The labels in the published 2018 paper diﬀer from those in
arXiv 1603.07817v1. The relevant dictionary is
arXiv Theorem 1.4 Theorem 2.5 Theorem 3.1 Corollary 3.9 Theorem 4.1
published Theorem 4 Theorem 9 Theorem 11 Corollary 3 Theorem 13
The published locations are respectively pages 4, 21, 28, 38, 41. The preprint’s equations (3.5) and

for an arbitrary function bounded by the prime weight. The special model 1 for the ordinary prime
weight is not an assertion about Artin weights.
I.10 The ray-class lower bound
For ray class ﬁelds we use the published existence and reciprocity statements in Kopp–Lagarias [ 13],
Theorems 1.1, 1.3 and 7.2, and the modulus exact sequence of Theorem 6.5, specialized to the
maximal order Z[i]. There are no real places. This replaces an appeal to unpublished course notes.
In this principal ideal domain the ray group for the ﬁnite modulus HZ[i] is
(Z[i]/HZ[i])×/{1,−1,i,−i}. (112)
It also follows directly by sending a residue r to the ideal class of (r): surjectivity is principality,
and the kernel consists exactly of unit residues. The Artin map sends this ideal class to arithmetic
Frobenius. Conjugation acts by r↦→ ¯r.
Choose a ﬁxed L1 larger than all coeﬃcient-diﬀerence primes and larger than 11. Let H0 be
divisible by 16· 7M and by all odd primes at most L1. Prescribe a unit residue r (mod H0) which
is 9 modulo 16, 1 + 3i modulo 7, and i modulo every other odd prime dividing H0. Compatible
249higher powers can be chosen arbitrarily. The associated rational-prime Frobenius condition is the
two-element conjugacy class C0 ={hr,h ¯r} in the Galois group G0 of the ray ﬁeld K0 over Q. The
two elements are distinct: an equality of their ray classes would require r = ϵ¯r (mod H0) for a
Gaussian unit ϵ; modulo 16 this forces ϵ = 1 , contradicted modulo 7. The ﬁeld is Galois over Q,
because conjugation preserves the modulus and the deﬁning ray subgroup.
Let D0 be the conductor of Kab0 and choose a unit b0 (mod D0) inducing the restriction of hr.
The value in ( 108) is then µ0 =|C0|/|G′0|> 0. For L≥L1 let TL =∏L1<𝓁≤L,𝓁 -H0𝓁 and let KL be
the ray ﬁeld for H0TLZ[i]. For each new prime write

Proof. In the CRT presentation of ( 112), the unit group embeds in the H0 factor, because it already

J The odd-product quartics in 523 and 540 variables
The following construction incorporates the November 7 contribution of Gabriel Conant, developed
with ChatGPT. We give its arithmetic argument, including the connection to the results proved
above. Variable counts include the input. The older quartics remain as source and proof baselines.
251Theorem J.1 (The Version 9 quartics) . There are speciﬁed integer polynomials P523 and P540, of
total degree four in respectively 523 and 540 variables, such that
q∈ Z ⇐⇒ ∃v∈ Q522 :P523(q,v ) = 0 ⇐⇒ ∃w∈ Q539 :P540(q,w ) = 0.
The ﬁrst has 35,163 nonzero monomials and largest coeﬃcient magnitude 64· 3227. Its coeﬃcient
table uses the ﬁxed integer S0 = 3 226. The second has 35,213 nonzero monomials and largest
coeﬃcient magnitude 32048+1005072314631333< 23247. Its complete decimal table occupies 708,848
bytes. Both have content one.
The proof is the composition of the source equivalences, local and global arguments, and exact
polynomial certiﬁcates below. The intermediate 554-variable construction is included to separate
the odd-product argument from the additional formal-depth reﬁnement. It is not a further claimed
improvement over the two polynomials in the theorem.

p >76176 and the three roots 0,±d are distinct. Thus their doubling images have order d±(p)/4.

factor Π19.

Proof. The ring O = Z[

√

Proposition J.4. The charts (114) and (115) have exactly the same projected pairs (x,y )∈ K2.
Every solution of the new chart has h,j,x,y ⁄= 0, and x is positive under both real embeddings.
Proof. Given ( 114), put j =t2/2−h2. Then
j2 =h4−t2h2 +t4/4 = h4−D,

The proof is speciﬁc to this ﬁeld and this value of D. It does not use the generally false

254J.3 Using the chart without ordinate variables
All nine point modules use the new chart. It has six rational coordinates: the two components of
each ofh,j,x , subject to four quadratic component equations. The old module had ten coordinates,
including the components of t,h,u,x,y . Thus the change saves four coordinates per point, provided
each downstream ordinate is consumed as the expression hj.
For the diﬀerence of chart points Q,R , retain the existing inverse equations for xQ−xR and
deﬁne the slope by
(xQ−xR)m =hQjQ +hRjR.
Its numerator and deﬁning equation are quadratic in the available coordinates. Deﬁne the diﬀerence
abscissa by the existing square of m, and its ordinate by
yQ−R =m(xQ−xQ−R)−hQjQ.
This again is a quadratic assignment. The retained inverse ensures that the slope has exactly its
old value.
For a chart point,
z =−x/y =−h/j.
Thus the local parameter is deﬁned by jz = −h. When a logarithm value is combined with
its parameter, replace y𝓁 =−xG by j𝓁 =−hG, where G is the unchanged truncated logarithm
polynomial. At the scalar ratio replace
xHρ =−yHzS by hHρ =−jHzS.
All cancellations are reversible by Proposition J.4. Diﬀerence-point denominators and all other
guards remain unchanged. The ﬁxed target has
h =−1 + 74√2, j = 398 − 92√2;
these give exactly its previous abscissa and ordinate.
Together with Proposition J.4, these identities give rational witness maps in both directions.
To reconstruct the old chart, choose the square root supplied by Lemma J.3; then evaluate all old
assignments. The choice of sign does not alter (x,y ) or any downstream value.
J.4 Shorter weight circuits
For each of nine detectors, write e for its nonzero weight and q for its order-one weight. The
required outputs are q12 =qe22 and q40 =qe78. The old construction uses seven pure powers of e
and two ﬁnal quotients. Instead construct
a1 =e−1, a 3 =a21/e, a 7 =a23/e, a 14 =a27, a 29 =a214/e, a 55 =a229/a3,
and impose
a29q12 =qa7, a 55q40 =q12e.
These are six power coordinates and two output coordinates. They give the same outputs and save
one coordinate per detector.
The nonzero condition is inherited, rather than presumed from a quotient notation. In the
source, w0 has a retained inverse and
w0n = (w20− 2w21)/2, (e∆)b =n2.
255Since w0⁄= 0 and 2 is a nonsquare in Q, the ﬁrst equation gives n⁄= 0 . The second gives e∆⁄= 0 .
Therefore every Laurent denominator above is nonzero. Conversely all Laurent coordinates are
uniquely deﬁned for each source solution. The changes introduce no new zero branches and exclude
no source solution.
J.5 Scalar preparation with one fewer coordinate
The scalar pole uses a nonzero input b and a preparation witness t, with
U = b(1−bt2)1 +bt2 .
The old circuit stores b−1,t 2 =t2,bt 2,U . Replace these four coordinates by t2,T,V , with
t2 =t2, b (T−t2) = 1, TV = 2,
and use the aﬃne expression U = V−b. The equation b(T−t2) = 1 forces b⁄= 0 , and TV = 2
forces T⁄= 0. Consequently T =t2 +b−1, 1 +bt2 =bT⁄= 0, and
U = 2/T−b = b(1−bt2)1 +bt2 .
Conversely every old solution extends uniquely by these values of T,V . Thus the modiﬁcation saves
one coordinate and preserves all downstream quantities. It also supplies the nonzero condition
formerly imposed by the separate coordinate b−1.

256positive valuation of x(Q) is equivalent to Q +T0∈E1. The image of T0 in this quotient has order

J.8.2 Completeness
FixX∈ Z, and set n =β. Perform the old scalar preparation: choose its ﬁnite correction and then
a positive shared index h0 divisible by 64 and suﬃciently deep at the ﬁxed small primes. Choose a
positive scalar index s suﬃciently divisible to satisfy the scalar leaves and physical anchor for [s]P .
We may require 6| s and take s arbitrarily large. Set a = s + 1. Then a is odd, a≡ 1 (mod 3) ,
and S−P = [s]P is exactly the prepared scalar point.
Deﬁne
U0 = 0, U 1 = 1, U j+1 =aUj−Uj−1, k = 2(a− 2),
and take b = Uk/(a− 2) and t = Uk+1. Modulo a− 2, induction gives Uj≡ j, so b∈ Z. Modulo
two the sequence has period 0, 1, 1; since k≡ 1 (mod 3) , both Uk and Uk+1 are odd. The positive
odd denominator a− 2 shows that b is odd. The Lucas identity gives
t2−a(a− 2)tb + (a− 2)2b2 = 1.
Thus all four root indices n,a,t,b are positive and odd. Proposition J.7 supplies their chart wit-
nesses.
Fix these roots and the three scales of Corollary J.6. Local continuity of the scalar and com-
parison functions at the prepared shared point gives the same ﬁxed modulus L, with 64|L. Only
then choose the contact primes by the inherited simultaneous-prime theorem. The direct-target
construction chooses a common index h satisfying the primary and opposite reduction-order con-
gruences and h≡h0 (mod L), with the required real bounds. Its comparison points are H = [h]P
and Ui = [hni]P . Their shifted points have indices h− 1 and (h− 1)ni.
All quotient-domain arguments remain valid. The product scales are ﬁxed positive squares,
so the original pole witnesses apply unchanged. The scalar block is preserved by the congruence
modulo L. This gives a rational solution for every integral X and completes the arithmetic proof
of the primary construction.

J.10 Polynomial construction and exact veriﬁcation
The generators apply three changes to the earlier point-chart source: the four odd-coset charts, the
product and three scales, and the shifted scalar point with β = 2X 2 + 3. The depth variant ﬁrst
changes the logarithms and test orders as in Section J.9. Each ﬁeld equation is represented by two
rational component equations. Intermediate products are assigned coordinates as needed to keep
every residual quadratic.
The full-log source has 577 raw coordinates; the pinned upstream compiler reduces this to
561. Seven further certiﬁed eliminations give 554. The depth source has 540 raw coordinates;
compilation gives 532 and nine certiﬁed eliminations give 523. Every elimination is justiﬁed by
exact polynomial identities and reversible substitutions preserving the input. Clearing residual
denominators and taking the sum of their squares gives the displayed integer quartics. Over Q, the
sum vanishes exactly when every residual does.
The constant-chain versions add c0,...,c 16 with
c0 = 3 1024, c i+1 =c2i (0≤i< 16).
These witnesses have unique rational values and c16 =S0. Substitution for S0 leaves every residual
quadratic. Direct expansion gives the 3,247-bit maximum coeﬃcient bound.
The accompanying archive contains the complete polynomial tables, residual systems, coordi-
nate maps, elimination certiﬁcates, pinned source generators, arithmetic notes and independent
checkers. Each ﬁnal package has a standard-library-only verify_all.py. It replays all elimina-
tions, veriﬁes the unique constant lift, independently expands the sums of squares, and compares
every coeﬃcient. Both full packages have passed fresh independent runs. A separate source replay
rebuilds the two compiled precursors and checks the match to their frozen residual systems.
These ﬁnite checks certify the counts, degrees and stated algebraic transformations. The arith-
metic assertions in Sections J.6–J.9 received separate adversarial AI review; they have not received
independent human peer review or a complete formal proof check. The inherited arithmetic inputs
are the results of this manuscript identiﬁed in Section J.1; no theorem from the contribution archive
is assumed as an additional arithmetic input.
Proof of Theorem J.1. The chart identities and Lemma J.2 identify the exact source used by the
generators with the arithmetic system described here. Proposition J.7 enforces the odd root indices,
so Corollary J.6 supplies the required height bound. Section J.9.1 veriﬁes the local implications for
the ﬁnal test orders. Section J.8.1 then forces the input to be an integer. Conversely, for an integer
input, Sections J.8.2 and J.9.3, using Lemma J.8, construct witnesses for every source equation and
retained guard. The certiﬁed substitutions preserve this projection in both directions. The sum of
squares vanishes over Q precisely when all its residuals vanish. Finally the 17-coordinate constant
chain has exactly one rational solution and ends at S0, so it preserves the same projection. This
proves the statements for both polynomials.
263The complete tables are in the ﬁles
quartics/quartic-523-symbolic-coefficients.json
quartics/quartic-540-fully-expanded.txt .
Their SHA-256 digests, respectively, are

The reproduction command is pythonscripts/check_odd_product.py; the complete source, cer-
tiﬁcates and repaired, non-mutating veriﬁers are in quartics/quartic-523-540/.
K A quartic in 498 variables
We now reduce the 523-variable construction of Theorem J.1 to 498 variables. The input remains
one rational variable X; every other variable remains existentially quantiﬁed over Q. The changes
below use the same normalized curve, the same integer labels, the same three height detectors,
and the same published and manuscript inputs as Section J. In particular, they do not replace the
restricted converse, the exponent-nineteen height theorem, or the simultaneous-prime argument by
new assumptions.
Theorem K.1. There is a speciﬁed integer polynomial P498 of total degree four in 498 variables
such that
X∈ Z ⇐⇒ ∃ v∈ Q497 :P498(X, v) = 0.
The polynomial has 24,669 nonzero monomials, content one, and largest absolute coeﬃcient 16·3226.
Its symbolic coeﬃcient table uses the ﬁxed integer C = 3 225, not an additional variable. There is
also a speciﬁed integer quartic P514 with
X∈ Z ⇐⇒ ∃ w∈ Q513 :P514(X, w) = 0.
It has 24,716 nonzero monomials, content one, and largest absolute coeﬃcient 32048+1005072314676622<
23247.
The proof distinguishes two sorts of changes. Some are rational changes of private witnesses
with a rational inverse. Others alter the arithmetic certiﬁcate while preserving both soundness and
the existence of witnesses for every integer input. For the latter we prove those two assertions
separately; we do not claim equality of all intermediate witness assignments.

normalize v(p) = 1 . The coeﬃcient basis is always (1,ρ ) and conjugation sends ρ to−ρ.
Retain the four odd-coset root charts of Proposition J.7, with labels

and the input label is β = 2X 2 + 3.
We retain the pole obstruction and pole-completeness statements identiﬁed in Section J.1, specif-
ically Lemmas 14.4, 14.6, and 17.4, including their existing source dependencies. We also retain
the three scales of Corollary J.6 and its product-height estimate. Write H =H19, so that
1≤H < 22093966, B 2− 6< 7H|N|19/k (121)
whenever the three contact radicals divide a nonzero integer N to orderk, andB = max(|n|,|a|,|t|,|b|).
This is exactly ( 116); none of its scales is replaced by a ﬁeld norm in the present construction.
The local formal-group inputs are the integral formal group in z =−x/y, its multiplication
series, and its logarithm. The integral construction is the one in [ 48, IV§1, Example IV.2.2.3 and
Proposition IV.2.3(a)]. At the unramiﬁed places used below, p >76176, and the logarithm is an
isometry on depth at least one by [ 48, Theorem IV.6.4, p. 132]; its hypothesis is 1 > 1/(p− 1).
The coeﬃcient and truncation estimates are those of Lemma 15.4 and its unramiﬁed extension in
Lemma 17.2. We spell out their new applications below. No minimal-model assertion at the prime
above two is needed.

Let f = 1/x and z =−x/y, as before. Deﬁne

265Lemma K.2. At every consumed unramiﬁed place above p> 76176, the tests (123) imply
L1≡n (mod p12), L i≡ni (mod p40) (2 ≤i≤ 4), (n2,n 3,n 4) = (a,t,b ).
The primary points Ui have depth at least twenty after integrality of the ratios has been established.
Proof. The order-one tests put the indicated points in the formal group. The test on f (H) gives
v(z(H))≥ 20. For a formal point Q of depth r≥ 1, the unchanged logarithm expansion gives
logE(Q)/z(Q)∈ 1 +p4rO.
Its truncation after G2 has relative error of order at least twelve, and its truncation after G9 has

are respectivelyj = 3 andj = 10 in the source recurrence. For 4j+1<p the coeﬃcients are integral.
For the remaining indices the source factorial bound vp(aj)≥− (4j + 1)/(p− 1) is dominated by
4jr and gives the same stated lower bounds. Both truncating factors are units.
The full ratios are the rational numbers
ri = uih, r ′i = ui−nih− 1 .
At least one of h,h−1 is a p-unit, so at least one of these two ratios is integral. Before any improved
depth is inferred for Ui, the unit factors just described show v(Li) = v(ri) andv(L′i) = v(r′i). Each
cross-test has positive order, so integrality transfers to both approximate ratios and both full ratios.
This ﬁrst step is essential.
NowLi integral gives v(z(Ui))≥v(z(H))≥ 20. The primary relative errors have order at least
eighty. The alternate relative errors have order twelve for i = 1 and forty for i≥ 2: in the ﬁrst case
the denominator still has order forty, so it does not reduce the numerator’s order twelve. Since the
ratios are integral, these relative errors give absolute congruences. Together with the cross-tests
they yield Li≡ ri≡ r′i to the asserted order. Multiply by h and h− 1 and subtract. The result
is Li≡ ni, without division by either integer. At an inert prime conjugation preserves the single
local valuation, so this argument has the same conclusion there.

K.5 One coeﬃcient in the physical anchor
Put C = 3 225 and write the actual scalar value as f (R) = f0 +f1ρ. The previous ﬁxed parameter

Lemma K.3. For every nonzero even integer s, write f ([s]P ) = f0 +f1ρ. Both coeﬃcients are
2-integral, and
v3(f1) = 2 + 2v3(s), v 3(f0)≥ 3 + 2v3(s).
In particular f1⁄= 0, and
f1/C∈ Z(2)∩ Z(3) ⇐⇒ f0/C,f 1/C∈ Z(2)∩ Z(3).
Proof. At 3 the ﬁeld is unramiﬁed quadratic with residue ﬁeld F9 = F3[ρ]/(ρ2− 2). The exact

Its square is ρ in F9. Write s = 2· 3ku with 3 -u, allowing negative u. The integral formal group
has inverse−z, and hence odd multiplication series. Thus
[u]z =uz +O(z3), [3]z = 3z +O(z3)
with integral coeﬃcients. At depth r≥ 1, division of the second identity by 3r+1 leaves an error
of valuation at least 2r− 1 > 0. Multiplication by three therefore preserves the leading residue.
Multiplication by u multiplies it by the nonzero residue of u. Consequently
z([s]P )/3k+1≡u(2 +ρ) (mod 3) .
268The exact curve relation is f =z2(1−Df 2). On the formal branch its second factor is one modulo
three, and u2 = 1 in F3. Therefore
f ([s]P )/32k+2≡ρ (mod 3).
Independence of 1,ρ over F3 proves the two asserted coeﬃcient valuations, including the possible
zero value of f0.
At the place above two normalize v(2) = 1 . The literal point has v(x(P )) = −3, v(y(P )) =
−9/2, and v(z(P )) = 3 /2. It lies in the integral formal subgroup, which contains all its integral

The three-witness criterion in the proof of Lemma 17.3 applies to every rational t:
t∈ Z(2)∩ Z(3) ⇐⇒ ∃u,v,w ∈ Q : u2 + 6v2− 2w2− 3t2 + 2 = 0. (126)
To recall its exact input, (2, 3)Q ramiﬁes precisely at two and three. The embedding criterion [ 82,
Proposition 14.6.7, pp. 223–224] tests the nonsquareness of 1− 2/(3t2) at those places when t⁄= 0.
At two, positive valuation of t gives odd negative valuation of the expression, valuation zero gives
residue three modulo eight, and negative valuation gives a square in 1 + 8Z2. At three, nonnegative
valuation gives odd negative valuation of the expression, whereas negative valuation gives a square

and exceptional case already present in that source proof.

It uses the same three private rational witnesses and removes the old coordinate for f 20 +f0f1−f 21 .

new anchors have the same projection onto all their nonprivate coordinates. In each direction use
(126) to choose the private witnesses; they need not be the same witnesses in both anchors. The
aﬃne translation of w is invertible.

s∈ 2 3224−1Z\{ 0}, log2|s|≥ 1 + (224− 1) log2 3> 25165823.5. (128)

Lemma K.4. The new scalar certiﬁcate implies M5(β)32|s and h(β)≤H|s|19/32.
Proof. Let p > 76176 be a prime of the same ﬁve-contact radical as in Lemma 16.1, and put

At a split prime vp(q) is the sum of the two valuations of f (H); at an inert prime it is twice the
single valuation. Positivity of vp(q) therefore supplies at least one place v abovep withv(f (H))> 0.
We claim only existence of one such place. At that place H is formal and v(z(H))≥ 1. By the two
coeﬃcient bounds,
v(z(R))≥v(z(H)) + 32> 0.
This need not make R itself formal: it may lie near (0, 0). We handle both branches explicitly.
Put m =v(x(R)). The retained point domains give x(R)y(R)⁄= 0. Since D is a unit and
z(R)2 = x(R)x(R)2−D,
positive valuation of z(R) excludes m = 0 . For m >0 its valuation is m/2, and for m <0 it is

then gives, for either sign of m,

BothH and [2]R are now in the formal subgroup where the logarithm is a homomorphism and
isometry. Apply it to the exact global identity
[h]([2]R) = [2s]H.
The points are nontorsion, so their nonzero formal logarithms permit comparison of valuations.

For completeness use the old scalar preparation with its stronger six-leaf conditions. For integral
X, r is a nonzero integer. Choose c, a suﬃciently divisible positive h0, and then a still deeper
positives satisfying the anchor, exactly as in the proof of Lemma 16.1. At primes dividing r, both
coeﬃcients of f (H) are divisible by r locally, so q/r is integral; the two retained ratio leaves are
integral. At primes dividing c but not r, the same leaves are divisible by the prime. The constant-

at those primes. Thus crW 2 is positive, has square reduced denominator, and has the same local
squareclasses as cr at two, three, and seventeen. The original pole-completeness lemma supplies
fresh scalar pole witnesses. The new norm and ratio functions are continuous at the chosen points,
so the same ﬁnite-neighborhood argument provides a congruence class of h0 preserving all required
conditions.

K.8 A shorter weight circuit with both outputs retained
In each detector let e be its weight e, q = q1, and keep both outputs q12,q 40. The grouped
evaluations already contain Q =q3 and T =q212. Replace the six old intermediate powers of e by

eα = 1, eγ =α2, γu =eq, γv =u2, Qq 12 =v2, qτ =eT, vq 40 =Tτ. (136)
These are seven quadratic equations for seven coordinates when the two retained outputs are
counted. The old circuit had eight.
The ﬁrst equation gives e⁄= 0 . The unchanged deﬁnition of q is qδ 3 =e, with the powers of δ
represented by their retained coordinates, so q⁄= 0 as well. The equations now determine, in order,
α =e−1, γ =e−3, u =qe4, v =q2e11, q 12 =qe22.
The division by Q = q3 is legitimate. The retained equation for T then gives T = q2e44, and the
last two equations give
τ =qe45, q 40 =qe78.
In particular v⁄= 0 whenever it is used as a divisor. This is a successive computation, not a circular
speciﬁcation involving T .
The forward map from the old circuit assigns these ﬁve Laurent monomials and retains
q12,q 40, Q,T , and every other coordinate. For the reverse map restore the old six powers as
e−1,e −3,e −7,e −14,e −29,e −55. The old output equations then give the same qe22 and qe78. The
replaced internal powers have no outside consumers. Thus these maps preserve every outside
coordinate, not merely the input. Applying this equivalence to all three detectors saves three
coordinates.

K.10 Soundness of the combined construction
Let a rational solution of the ﬁnal system be given. The inherited odd charts identify the four root
points with odd integer labels. The reciprocal chart supplies the actual R =S−P , is nonidentity,
and, together with the anchor, excludes S =−P . The explicit maps above therefore restore all
unchanged point domains and quotient guards needed by the earlier arguments. The removed scalar
norm coordinate also has its unique rational value.
Put κ =|s| =|a− 1|. By ( 128) and Lemma K.4,
log2κ> 25165823.5, h(β)≤Hκ19/32.
These are the exact bounds consumed in the Version 9 height argument. For completeness we
repeat the numerical deductions. Since
10(7 + 2093966)< 25165823.5, 80< 27,
we have
κ> (80H)10, |a|≥ κ− 1≥κ/2> (40H)10,
and
h(β)<κ/ 80≤|a|/40, log(40H)<κ/ 80≤|a|/40. (138)
The ﬁrst inequality uses κ13/32> 80H; the logarithmic inequality is weaker. In particular all labels
are in the large range needed below.
Set
