274K.11 Completeness with all replacements at once
Fix an integer X and put n = β = 2X 2 + 3, a positive odd integer. First perform the scalar
preparation with the original six-leaf bounds, which, as shown above, suﬃce for the new scalar
tree. Choose its ﬁnite correction and a suﬃciently divisible positive shared index h0, with 64|h0.
Choose a positive scalar index s still more divisible, large enough for the physical anchor, and with
6|s. Put a =s + 1. The actual prepared scalar point is R = [s]P =S−P . The new anchor has
exactly the old allowed subgroup, and the new scalar tree has the required unit root. Fresh scalar
pole witnesses therefore exist.
To choose the two other root labels, deﬁne
U0 = 0, U 1 = 1, U j+1 =aUj−Uj−1, k = 2(a− 2), b =Uk/(a− 2), t =Uk+1.
Induction gives Uj≡j (mod a− 2), so b is an integer. Modulo two the sequence has period 0, 1, 1.
Since a≡ 1 (mod 3) , k≡ 1 (mod 3) , and both Uk,Uk+1 are odd. The positive odd denominator
a− 2 makesb odd. The Lucas identity gives F (a,t,b ) = 0 . Thus all four root indices are positive
and odd, and Proposition J.7 supplies their chart witnesses.
Fix these root points and the three positive rational square scales of Corollary J.6. The new
scalar functions and the mixed alternate numerator are rational functions deﬁned at the prepared
points. Their required ﬁnite local conditions persist in a suﬃciently small neighborhood. As in the
original argument, choose a common continuity modulus L, with 64| L, so that h≡ h0 (mod L)
preserves them. All these choices precede the contact-prime selection. Increase the ﬁnite prime
cutoﬀ to include the ﬁxed root denominators needed for the ﬁve half-trace guards.
Choose the contact primes using the same simultaneous-prime theorem and Lemma J.8. Put
M± = lcmp(d±(p)/4), Π =

Use the inherited real-progression argument to choose a suﬃciently large positive h in this pro-

At every primary place the points H,Ui have depth at least twenty: the doubling-image order
ﬁrst makes them formal, and multiplication by p19 raises depth by nineteen under the same formal
logarithm isometry. At opposite places the points Vi are formal. Hence Li agrees with its integer
label to order at least eighty; the alternate ratio agrees to order twelve for i = 1 and forty for the
other three labels. All tests ( 123) hold. The same primary/opposite reductions prove that all ﬁve
half-traces are nonzero, by the argument following ( 124). The chosen scalar label has S⁄=±P , so
its reciprocal chart has the ordinary rational lift described above.
The pole-realization estimates are valid for these ﬁxed orders 1, 12, 40. In the source notation
their large-real-parameter decay is still λ−3; the small-prime lower bound is 3ν + (10m− 8)η− 2c,
which tends to inﬁnity under the same preparatory divisibility. No new prime-pattern assertion is
used. Obtain the detector pole witnesses and apply the rational normalization ( 125). Apply the
275weight-circuit maps ( 136), the reciprocal denominator cancellation, and the norm-square elimina-
tion. These exact maps preserve all outside coordinates. They therefore give one simultaneous
rational solution of the ﬁnal system for the chosen integer X.
This proves completeness in the required order of choices. In particular, it does not combine
separately chosen primes or incompatible continuity moduli from diﬀerent optimizations.
K.12 Exact polynomial, counts, and coeﬃcient lift
Each equation above is represented by its rational component rows; intermediate products in the re-
tained straight-line program are represented by their speciﬁed coordinates. Normalize each nonzero
rational residual by clearing denominators and dividing its integer content, and let P498 be the sum
of the squares of those 467 normalized residuals. This is an integer polynomial. A sum of rational
squares vanishes exactly when every residual vanishes. The soundness and completeness just proved
therefore establish Theorem K.1.
The following count records the actual transported, fully reduced systems; it does not count
unreduced formulas in isolation.

The mixed-depth compilation uses only exact quadratic identities, constant-pivot substitutions,
and invertible shears in shared coeﬃcient coordinates. A constant-pivot substitution inserts its
unique polynomial value in the reverse direction; a shear inserts its inverse linear transformation.
Both maps ﬁx the input. The ﬁnal norm-square elimination is the explicit identity ( 137). Thus
no compiler reduction introduces a variable denominator or alters an arithmetic guard. The exact
certiﬁcates also check that private coordinates deleted in the other replacements have no undeclared
consumers. In particular, the half-trace witnesses occur only in their guard equations; the removed
weight powers occur only in their old weight circuit; the removed scalar d2 coordinates occur only in
their deﬁnitions and k equations; and the removed norm occurs only in its deﬁnition and constant-
node equation.
The frozen residual ﬁle is candidate498-ir.json, with SHA-256

coeﬃcients are independent of C or linear in C and are smaller in absolute value. The coeﬃcient

The exact checks establish the residual identities, the witness counts, and every coeﬃcient of the

For one root write Q = (c,ab ), c = a2 and b2 = c2−D. The reciprocal comparison chart above
usesr,J,f,g,k,u,F , with f =r2,F =g2. Its retained squared outputs are YU =f 2 andYV =F 2,

280Every displayed equation is quadratic in retained coordinates. In particular c is an existing coordi-
nate, so cY is a quadratic product.
Here is an algebraic proof including all zero branches. Put d =c +Dr2,e =c +Dg2, and deﬁne
T =c(r2 +g2) +Dr2g2 + 2brg− 1,
G =dg−aJ +br, E ′ =ak +bg +er, H ′ =dk−bJ + 2a(cr +bg).
Modulo only c =a2 and b2 =c2−D, direct expansion gives

dE′ =aH ′ +DrT−bG.
The factors a,c,D are nonzero, and d,e are positive at both real embeddings. An old solution has

setr =V/a ,g =Q′/a,J =I/a,k =K′/a; the product equation forces W=rg. The new equations

Dividing the ratio equations by a restores the old ratios. Thus these are inverse rational maps,
ﬁxing every outside coordinate.
There is no division by r,g,J,k,W,L,z or either test value. When r=0 the ﬁrst point is O and
its ratio is zero; when g=0 the diﬀerence is O and its parameter is zero. The biquadratic equation
prevents both from vanishing. These are exactly the already accepted identity branches. This
transformation replaces the intermediate inverse-addition change; it is not composed with a second
copy of that elimination.
L.5 Enlarging the auxiliary H point domain
After the exact H.j rescaling, the two coordinates H.h occur only in the two component equations
x=H.h2. Delete them and keep
xf = 1, S 2 =x−Df.
They deﬁne the ﬁnite point H=(x,xS) of E(K), with x nonzero, but no longer require H to be a
double. The rank and torsion calculation in the generator proof gives
H = [m]R0 +T, P = 2R0, m ∈ Z, T ∈E[2](K).
Write h=m/2 only for its rational free index. It is not notation for multiplication by a half-integer
on the elliptic curve.
We ﬁrst exclude all ﬁnite points in E[2],P +E[2] and−P +E[2] before interpreting ratios. The
points O and (0,0) are excluded by the ﬁnite chart. Put d0 = 3−ρ, c∗ = x(P ). The exact norm
and translation identities are
Norm(c∗) = 41 2/64, Norm(c∗−d0) = 17 2/64, Norm(c∗ +d0) = 63 2/64,
x(P + (0, 0)) =−D/c∗, x (P + (d0, 0)) = d0(c∗ +d0)/(c∗−d0),
x(P + (−d0, 0)) =−d0(c∗−d0)/(c∗ +d0).
Each of these abscissae and its inverse is integral outside {2, 3, 7, 17, 41}. The same is true for the
nonzero torsion abscissae, and negating P changes none of these abscissae. Thus f(H) is a unit at
281every consumed place. The retained order40 f(H) test would then rule out every contact prime.
All three radicals would equal1, contradicting the root-height bound and scalar anchor exactly as
in the preceding V0 exclusion. This proof needs no ratio involving H. Consequently m is neither0
nor2, H and H-P are nontorsion, S is nonzero, and H is neither P nor -P.
In the rescaled diﬀerence equations put
d =c∗x +D, N =a∗S−b∗, dv =N, T ′ =−2D−a∗b∗S + 2Db∗v.
The former positivity proof for d no longer applies. If d=0, the curve equation gives x =−D/c∗,
S =±b∗/a∗, so H is one of the excluded points ±P + (0, 0). If N=0, it gives (x−c∗)(x +D/c∗) = 0 ,
also excluded. Thus d,N,v are nonzero. Direct subtraction now gives
x(H−P ) = 1/(xv2), y (H−P ) = T ′/(a∗dxv3), z (H−P ) =−a∗dv/T ′.
There is no unresolved equal-abscissa case, since x =c∗ means H=P or -P. Since H-P is nontorsion,
its ordinate and T’ are nonzero. In particular the retained equation f (V0)f (H) = v2 has its intended
interpretation, even if either real abscissa is negative.
The old positivity interval for the rational logarithm is no longer enough. A stronger certiﬁcate
is available: N(w) above has no root modulo71, and Q(w) has no root modulo7. At the respective
prime all coeﬃcients are integral and the leading coeﬃcient is a unit. These assertions are ﬁnite
substitutions through all residues. Both primes split in K, with square roots of2 equal to12,59 mod-
ulo71 and3,4 modulo7. A K-root would give a local root, necessarily integral, and then a forbidden
residue root. Hence N and Q never vanish in K. The actual equation T ′𝓁0 +a∗NR(Df (V0)2) = 0
therefore implies 𝓁0⁄= 0 and gives the correct denominator logarithm. No sign assertion for this
logarithm is needed.
The formal ratio argument changes by using integral group identities:
[m]Ui = [2ui]H, [m− 2]Vi = [2(ui−ni)]V0.
They kill T. Applying the formal logarithm only to formal points gives ratios ui/h and (ui−ni)/(h−
1). At every consumed odd prime h and h-1 are integral and diﬀer by1. The earlier transfer and
subtraction proof therefore applies, including zero numerators. At opposite split places use the
same conjugation as before; at an inert place it preserves the valuation.
The scalar transfer uses the same integral-index convention. For R = [s]P use
[m](2R) = [4s]H.
If the scalar coeﬃcient test has depth ds, formal logarithm isometry gives vp(s/(m/2))≥ds. Since
m/2 is p-integral, vp(s)≥ ds. This includes the selected depth24. The proof that 2R is formal
when R is near O or (0,0) is unchanged. Thus both the comparison and scalar height arguments
survive the larger domain. Completeness uses the original H in 2E(K) and forgets its two square
witnesses. It need not realize any newly admitted half-index or torsion translate.
L.6 A positive scalar input and the depth-twenty-four certiﬁcate
Use the selected label and contact polynomial
τ = 2X + 1, β = 51τ 2 + 2504, r =β(β− 1)(β− 2401)(32β + 343)(2β− 1).
For every rational X, β≥ 2504, hence r > 0. For integral X, τ is odd and β is a positive odd
integer. Conversely, the ﬁnal conclusion must be that β is an odd integer. If τ = u/v in lowest
282terms, integrality gives v2| 51; as 51 is squarefree, v = 1 . Oddness of β then makes τ odd and
X = (τ− 1)/2 integral. Mere integrality would not suﬃce: X =−1/2 gives the even integer
β = 2504 . The unchanged odd chart for Q1 = [n]P supplies the required oddness once n =β has
been proved.
At a consumed prime p> 76176, 51 is a unit. A negative valuation of β is 2vp(τ ), hence even.
The ﬁve contact factors then have the same valuation, so an odd valuation of r must be positive.
This argument makes no assertion at the exceptional primes3 and17.
For integralX the following simultaneous local calculation holds. Since τ is odd, β≡ 3 (mod 8) ;
the two even contact factors each have valuation one and equal odd parts modulo8. The other
factors have residues3,7,5. Therefore v2(r) = 2 andr/4≡ 1 (mod 8) . At3, β≡ 2 and the ﬁrst four
factors have product1 modulo3. Furthermore
(2β− 1)/3 = 34τ 2 + 1669≡ 1 +τ 2 (mod 3),
which is nonzero. Thus v3(r) = 1 . At17, the contact residues are 5,4,1,10,9 with product15, a
nonzero square. Thus v17(r) = 0 .
An intermediate one-choice construction used c = 33033 when 3|τ andc = 33 otherwise. Both
are square units at2 and17, and their unit parts at3 are respectively1 and2, correcting the two
possible classes of r. The selected construction removes even this choice and sets
c =r + 4896, 4896 = 2 5· 32· 17.
At2 the quotient c/r = 1 + 4896/r belongs to 1 + 8Z2; at3 and17 it belongs to 1 +pZp. Hence cr
is a square in all three local ﬁelds. Also c,r are positive integers. At a consumed scalar contact
prime, positivity of the odd valuation vp(r) was proved before using c; since4896 is a unit there,
c is a unit there too. We do not assert that c is a unit at every large prime. Its additional ﬁnite
prime support is included in the preparation before the continuity modulus is chosen.
The physical input square Y =X 2 is retained. In each other consumer, the old occurrence of
Y is replaced by 102Y + 102X + 76, since 2(102Y + 102X + 76) + 2403 = 51(2 X + 1) 2 + 2504.
This substitution must include every scalar test and height-input consumer, not the deﬁning row
Y =X 2 itself.

the correction is deﬁned to be r + 4896; the removed ﬁnite-choice equation is not reimposed.

Here is the changed local application. At a consumed prime let η = vp(r) > 0 be odd. The

The scalar coeﬃcient guard may be imposed on f (R)1 instead of f (R)0. In the reciprocal chart

on every prepared scalar orbit point. This is an arithmetic replacement of the guard, not an

L.7 The two ﬁnal height contradictions
The local order-forty tests force the contact radicals to divide F =t2−a(a− 2)tb + (a− 2)2b2− 1
to order40. If F ⁄= 0 , then |F| < 5B4 and ( 139) contradicts the retained lower bound B ≥
|a| > (40H)10. Thus F = 0 . The nonzero odd root label b and the retained Lucas lemma give
logB >|a|− 3. If n⁄=β, its nonzero reduced numerator N satisﬁes|N|≤ 2eh(β)B and is divisible
by the three contact radicals to order10. Since B2− 6> (5/8)B2 for B >4,
B1/10< 48He(19/10) h(β), logB <10 log(48H) + 19 h(β)< 29|a|/40<|a|− 3.
This contradiction proves n = β. The unchanged root chart makes n odd, so the parity-sensitive
input argument above gives X∈ Z. The ﬁrst contradiction uses order40; lowering only the scalar
depth does not weaken that test.

For retained ﬁeld values Yi, each order block evaluates ∏i(1 +qYi). In order1 group the nine factors

L.10 Specializing the scalar preparation to zero
Writeq = Norm(f (H)), and write the actual scalar ratio as z(R)/z(H) = z0 +z1ρ. The retained
expressions are
B = (z20 +rz21)/r32, W = (B2 +rq2)/r32, b =crW 2.
Here r,c are positive and q⁄= 0 , so W, b > 0 at every rational solution, independently of a pri-
vate preparation inverse. The earlier preparation-zero lemma applies when b < 17, its reduced
denominator is a square, it is locally square at 2, 3, 17, and its valuations there are at least 3, 1, 2,
respectively. We now arrange these conditions at the same points used in the ﬁnal construction.
LetG be the closure of the simultaneous real orbit of P and its conjugate, let G0 be its identity
component, and put m0 =|G/G0|. The real-progression lemma says that a progression with both
residue and modulus divisible by m0 has dense positive tail in G0. The functions f,z extend
continuously to the identity with value zero. Choose a multiple M of 64m0 whose multiples give all
desired formal depths at primes in S = supp(cr)∪{ 2, 3, 17}. In particular require coeﬃcient depth
8η for f (H) when η =vp(r)> 0, and positive coeﬃcient depth at17. Density permits H0 = [h0]P ,
h0 > 0, M|h0, as close to the two real identities as needed to make q(H0)2/r31 arbitrarily small.
Its real z values are nonzero.
With H0 ﬁxed, choose a multiple N0 of 6m0 imposing the anchor and all required scalar ﬁnite
depths. At p|r require coeﬃcient depth 24η forz(R)/z(H0); impose positive depths also at17 and
at primes dividing c but not r. The formal subgroups have ﬁnite index, so arbitrary further depths
can be imposed this way. Density of the positive progression s≡ 0 (mod N0) permits s arbitrarily
large and R = [s]P arbitrarily close to both real identities. The two real ratios, hence their rational
coeﬃcients z0,z 1, become small. Choose them so that b(H0,R )< 17/4. This also permits the size
part of the anchor; set a =s + 1 as before.
At p| r these depths give vp(q)≥ 16η, vp(B)≥ 16η and vp(W )≥ 0. At p| c, p - r, the
imposed positive depths again give vp(W )≥ 0. Outside this support vp(b) = 2 vp(W ), so the
reduced denominator of b is a square. Its squareclasses at 2, 3, 17 are those of cr. Since v2(cr) = 4 ,
v3(cr) = 2 , the ﬁrst two required bounds hold. At17 both r,c are units; the separately imposed
depths give v17(q),v 17(B)≥ 2, hence v17(W )≥ 4 and v17(b)≥ 8. Inclusion of17 does not depend
on it belonging to the support of cr.
Keep R ﬁxed. A nonempty real neighborhood U ⊂ G0 of H0 has 0 < b(H,R ) < 17. A
suﬃciently divisible continuity modulus L, divisible by 64m0, preserves all the ﬁnite conditions
under h≡ h0 (mod L). Choose the remaining labels, root points and height scales before the
286contact primes, and enlarge L for their ﬁnite continuity conditions. The ﬁnitely many comparison
pole sets have measure zero in G0 by the real-progression lemma. Choose a nonempty open U ′,
with compact closure in U , avoiding them. All comparison functions are now uniformly bounded
on that closure, by bounds ﬁxed before any contact prime is chosen.
The original simultaneous-prime and separation argument is valid for this enlarged ﬁxed L. Its
compatible congruences are
h≡ 0 (mod Π 19M−), h ≡ 1 (mod M+), h ≡h0 (mod L).
Their solution progression has residue and modulus divisible by m0. Its positive tail is therefore
dense in G0 and meets U ′ at arbitrarily large indices. Choose the ﬁnal H there. It has every
required primary and opposite-place depth, all scalar ﬁnite depths, the real comparison bounds
and 0 < b < 17 simultaneously. The preparation-zero lemma supplies fresh pole witnesses with
prepared value exactly b. A zero factor in the factored pole is handled by the zero lift already
proved above.
For clarity, the physical elimination is also exact. With its ﬁxed positive normalization K, the
semantic input is b =−Kx and the consumed square is K2n. The old rows are
U 2 =K2n, 1 +KxJ = 0, Kx +K2Jn +V 2 = 0.
Replace all three by n =x2 and forget U,J,V . At any new solution b> 0 as proved independently
above; restore U = b, J = 1/b, V = 0 . Each old row is then zero, and every outside value is
unchanged. The opposite direction for the integer input projection is the simultaneous construction,
not a claim that an arbitrary old preparation witness can be specialized.
L.11 Normalizing the remaining scalar pole
After the preparation-zero replacement, let x again denote the physical scalar input coordinate, so
b =−Kx> 0 andx⁄= 0. This nonvanishing was proved from the unchanged point and scalar root
rows; it does not depend on the preparation square or on the private pole equations. Assign weight
one to the ﬁve private slots (e,h,p,q,v ) and weight two to their square slots (p2,q 2). Replace each
old slot of weight j by xj times its new slot, and discard the preparation-square slot n, setting it
equal to x2. Every one of the ﬁve remaining pole residuals is then x2 times its new residual. For
example the ﬁrst conic and the h equation have respectively their ex and x2 terms replaced by e
and1, and
17ex +Ken− 17hv = 0 ↦−→ 17e +Kxe− 17hv = 0.
The square rows remain p2 =p2,q2 =q2, and the factored second-conic coeﬃcient list is unchanged.
In the physical indexing of the reviewed component, the weight-one slots are 51, 52, 53, 54, 532, the
weight-two slots are 527, 534, and 531 is n. These are implementation indices, not mathematical
constants.
From an old solution divide each private slot by its indicated power of x. From a new solution
multiply by that power and restore n =x2. These maps are inverse and ﬁx every outside coordinate.
The sign of x is immaterial, and no division uses a private pole value. In particular all zero branches
of p,q,v or h are preserved. These seven slots occur only in the ﬁve displayed pole rows, and n has
only its former deﬁnition and these consumers. Thus there is no untransformed outside use of a
rescaled slot.
287L.12 Rescaling the scalar anchor
The odd scalar root S has the unchanged chart S = (x,hj ), x =h2, j2 =x2−D, hence S = [a]P
for an odd integer a. Write the retained reciprocal diﬀerence equation as
(c∗x +D)g =a∗j−b∗h, g =g0 +g1ρ.
Its denominator is positive in both real embeddings. We replace the old inverse of the irrational
coeﬃcient of f(S-P) by g0τ = 1. This implies g⁄= 0 before using the anchor. The subtraction chart
identiﬁesg2 =f (S−P ): at S =P the quotient would be zero, which is excluded, and at S =−P
the explicit duplication formula gives the same value f (−2P ). Thus S−P = [s]P has a nonzero
even index s =a− 1 without any prior anchor divisibility assertion.
The earlier scalar coeﬃcient lemma applies to every such s and gives
v3(f1([s]P )) = 2 + 2v3(s).
Its hypotheses contain no anchor equation. Indeed z(2P )/3≡ 2+ρ (mod 3) ; multiplication by units
and then by powers of3 in the integral formal group preserve the stated leading term. The curve
equation makes the leading residue of f/ 32+2v3(s) a nonzero rational multiple of ρ. Independence of1
andρ in the residue ﬁeld proves the displayed coeﬃcient valuation, also for negative s. Consequently
F = f1([s]P ) = 2g0g1⁄= 0 , in particular g1⁄= 0 . This is the noncircular justiﬁcation for restoring
the old inverse.
The old three-row subsystem is
F = 2g0g1, Fσ = 1, u 2 + 6v2− 2w2− 3F 2− 4Cw = 0.
The new subsystem is
g0τ = 1, U 2 + 6V 2− 2W 2− 12g21− 4CτW = 0.
Forward put (U,V,W ) = (u,v,w )/g0,τ = 1/g0 and forget F. Backward put (u,v,w ) = g0(U,V,W ),
restore F = 2g0g1 and σ = 1/F . If qold,q new denote the anchor residuals, the exact identity is
qold|(u,v,w)=g0(U,V,W ), F=2g0g1−g20qnew = 4Cg0W (g0τ− 1).
These inverse maps ﬁx the input and all other coordinates. They permit zero values of U,V,W.
They are equivalences for the full point system, not for an isolated anchor with arbitrary g: that
isolated equation would admit g1 = 0, which the coeﬃcient lemma just excluded.
When C remains a ﬁxed integer coeﬃcient, both new residuals are quadratic and their squared
sum contributes 16C2τ 2W 2. For the ordinary companion compute C by the quadratic chain c0 =
31024,cj =c2j−1 for 1≤j≤ 15, so that c15 = 3 225 =C. Introduce a separate multiplication slot TC
and equation
TC =Cτ.
Use−4TCW in the anchor. Treating a computed C as a coeﬃcient would instead make CτW
cubic, so this extra slot is necessary for that companion. Both directions restore TC uniquely;
no nonvanishing or point-domain argument is changed. This slot and its gate, together with the
sixteen constant-chain slots, are included in the ordinary companion.
288L.13 Completeness and ﬁnite certiﬁcation
Fix an integer X and compute the positive deterministic scalar correction. Choose the preliminary
shared point and then the deep nonzero scalar point by the simultaneous real and ﬁnite-place
construction just proved. Choose the odd Lucas labels, then their actual root points and positive
root scales. Fix a continuity modulus for all the new rational functions. Only after these choices
select the contact primes and solve the retained primary/opposite congruences. Take a suﬃciently
large common index h in the prescribed real open set, avoiding the ﬁnitely many old exceptional
point loci. This gives Ui = [hni]P and Vi = [(h− 1)ni]P , all nonidentity in this completeness
construction. The new rational logarithms have the required local precision, and the original real
pole estimates and arbitrary ﬁnite preparation depths give positive semantic pole inputs. Obtain
fresh pole witnesses with scalar preparation equal to its input, apply the factored-conic maps, and
reconstruct each ﬁnite private circuit. These are simultaneous choices; no claim is made that an
arbitrary old pole witness survives a changed logarithm or changed scalar root.
L.14 The speciﬁed polynomials and coeﬃcient tables
Clear denominators and divide each nonzero quadratic residual by its integer content. Deﬁne P394
to be the sum of the squares of the 364 resulting integer residuals. Over the rational numbers this
sum vanishes exactly when every residual vanishes. The soundness and simultaneous completeness
arguments above therefore prove its stated integer projection. The sixteen constant-chain equations
and the TC =Cτ equation give the ordinary companion: its 381 normalized residual squares deﬁne
P411, with the same integer projection.
Expansion of the speciﬁed residual lists gives respectively 6,338 and 6,388 distinct nonzero
monomials. Both polynomials have total degree four and content one. For the symbolic polynomial
the largest absolute coeﬃcient is 16C2 = 16· 3226; for the ordinary polynomial it has 3,247 bits and
is strictly less than 23247. These counts and bounds are computed from the complete coeﬃcient
lists, not by adding component savings. Every listed coordinate occurs.
The coeﬃcient artifacts use consecutive coordinate indices, with index one for X. The symbolic
JSON coeﬃcient table has 973,221 bytes, and the ordinary JSON coeﬃcient table has 982,235
bytes. Their plain text tables have 212,067 and 214,957 bytes, respectively. The tables, residual
lists, coordinate maps and exact maximum coeﬃcients specify the two polynomials independently
of their factored construction.
M A quartic presentation with 299 variables
We retain the arithmetic inputs, qualiﬁcations and source editions of Sections K.1 and L. The
polynomial of the main construction is unchanged. The following are smaller presentations of the
same rational input projection.
Theorem M.1. There is a speciﬁed integer polynomial P299 of total degree four in 299 variables
such that
X∈ Z ⇐⇒ ∃ v∈ Q298 :P299(X, v) = 0.
It has 6,630 nonzero monomials, content one, and largest absolute coeﬃcient 186624· 3226. Its
coeﬃcient artifact writes C = 3 225 as a ﬁxed integer, not a quantiﬁed variable. There is also a
speciﬁed integer quartic P314 with
X∈ Z ⇐⇒ ∃ w∈ Q313 :P314(X, w) = 0.
289It has 6,672 nonzero monomials and content one. Every coeﬃcient has absolute value strictly less
than 26496; its largest coeﬃcient has exactly 6,496 bits. The compact ordinary decimal coeﬃcient
table occupies 264,430 bytes in its speciﬁed ASCII format. The separate table retaining source
coordinate identiﬁers occupies 222,890 bytes.
The ordinary coeﬃcient bound here is diﬀerent from the bound for P411. We prove the new
bound for the new artifact. Some changes below are inverse changes of private witnesses; others
change an arithmetic certiﬁcate or point domain. For the latter we prove soundness and complete-
ness separately.

height/anchor contradiction. No division by UQ is used to prove its own legitimacy.
M.3 Root parity and the shifted anchor

M.7 Auxiliary point domains and the H-depth bootstrap
We give the domain argument for all four pairs simultaneously. At a good consumed place D is a
unit. On h2 = f−Df 3, a nonzero f has even valuation. If v(f ) > 0, 2v(h) = v(f ); if v(f ) < 0,
2v(h) = 3v(f ); and if v(f ) = 0 there is nothing to prove. The identity has f = 0 and every positive-
depth condition is automatic. Consequently the valuations of f 2,f 4,f 8 have periods 4, 8, 16 on all
of E(K) represented by this chart. This proves the periods used in the group calculation before H
has been restored. The low tests exclude the ﬁnite points of order two, whose reciprocal abscissae
are units at these places.

297M.8 Simultaneous completeness of the common scale
We check that a single choice of witnesses meets all the new tests. Start with the original si-
multaneous construction for an integer input, with the same positive progression and the same
contact-prime restrictions. Use its depth19 preparation. The resulting primary formal depths are
at least20, the high tests have valuation at least40, and the middle tests have valuation at least12.
The ﬁxed H/V0 exclusions remain part of this construction.
At each contact prime α = v(R) = 1 . Prescribe v(γ) = 1 . In the three groups the high-term
valuations are at least1 or2 and the middle-term valuations at least9 or10. The primary terms
have lower bounds159,3,83,76 in root order; the guard has lower bound8. The alternate low terms
are unchanged and have positive valuation. Every group is therefore one plus an element of the
maximal ideal at the successful place. At the other place above a split contact prime, the inverse
of each group has residue zero or one. Thus the trace of the product of inverses has residue one or
two and is a unit. The pole value has positive odd valuation one.
At each preparation prime p = 2, 3, 17, put ηj = vp(Aj), prescribe vp(γ) = vp(λ) = −νp, and
enlargeνp before choosing the contact primes. Since vp(R) = 5(ηj−νp), the coeﬃcients multiplying
the bounded test values have valuations

Each tends to +∞ withνp. These are all the new coeﬃcient forms. The previous ﬁnite preparation
therefore works after increasing its bounds. At the real places impose λ<γ < 2λ. The correspond-
ing powers of λ are−155,−150,−145,−35,−30,−65,−100,−24. They all tend to zero uniformly
in this interval. The groups tend to one, their trace to two, and the pole value tends to 1/(4R)
through positive values. This gives the real conditions required by the unchanged local-global pole
certiﬁcates.
Away from the contact support, R has even valuation. Since the other factors in its pole value
are squares, new primes in the denominator of γ create no odd pole support. This also explains why
γ may be chosen on the anchor quadric below rather than independently. The argument requires
its speciﬁed local valuations and real interval, not an absence of other denominator primes.
The choices have a ﬁxed order. First prepare the scalar and root witnesses and all ﬁnite and
real bounds. Choose the contact primes and then λ. Choose the anchor point and hence γ with the
required local conditions. Finally choose H in the original progression, deep enough at the ﬁnitely
many selected places and in the retained real neighborhoods. The bounds above are uniform in the
anchor choice. Thus this last H choice meets all three groups simultaneously.

Finally approximate the three direction coordinates simultaneously by rationals. After clearing

chooses A for each coordinate. Continuity of Φ on the chosen local neighborhoods now supplies a
rational point in all the required open sets. Its u gives precisely the valuations and real interval
required for γ. This proves simultaneous completeness of the coupled anchor.
M.10 Exact private changes and the ﬁnite polynomial
We relate the preceding arithmetic argument to the literal quadratic presentation. Coordinates
represent elements of K by their rational components; each displayed ﬁeld equation contributes its
two component equations. All rational coeﬃcients are cleared and each equation is divided by its
integer content before taking the sum of squares. These operations do not change its zero set.
For the reciprocal replacement, an old root chart with scaled private coordinates Vold,Iold,Qold,
Kold has c =a2i⁄= 0. Its actual reciprocal coordinates are
f =V 2old/c, h =−IoldVold/c, F =Q2old/c, k =−KoldQold/c.
The old identities I 2old = c(1−DY ) and K2old = c(1−DB), together with IoldL = VoldN0 and
Koldz =−Qold, give the new curve and both regular ratio equations, including the identity cases.
The actual equality U−V =Qi supplies a rational secant slope or its rational tangent limit. For the
scalar pair it also supplies ( 159). No new local neighborhood, root sign, anchor choice or H choice
is needed for this change. Conversely ( 158) restores the deleted root ordinates and the preceding
soundness proof applies to the enlarged auxiliary domain. We assert equality of input projections,
not a bijection to all the old private square-root ﬁbers.
The subsequent deletion of each alternate ordinate is an exact change on the full zero set. Its
inverse isk =s(f−F )−h, and ( 156) and the globally justiﬁed ( 162) recover every deleted equation.
This accounts for six rational coordinates in the A, T and Z pairs. The scalar pair saves two further
coordinates while retaining its root ordinate and adding the orientation equation.
The remaining private replacements are exact power chains or polynomial changes. For a local
inverseER = 1, a shorter chain may specify
EJ =R2, E 4J =E, E 8 =E24, E 19J =E28,
RE39 =E219, RE 79 =E239, E 158 =E279, E 157 =RE158.
It forces J = R3 and exactly the indicated powers of E. There is no zero or sign branch because
ER = 1. The ﬁnal common-scale presentation needs only the local coordinates for E,R 2,R 3,E 4,E 8,
with shared powers speciﬁed above. Its equivalence as an arithmetic certiﬁcate was proved in the
common-place and completeness arguments, rather than inferred from coordinate counting.
The sparse Lucas change replaces its private coordinate Cold =AZ byCnew =Cold− 2Z. The
inverse is Cold =Cnew + 2Z, and the deﬁning polynomial becomes
F + 1 = T 2 +C2new−Cnew(AT ).
Both substitutions are aﬃne in the private coordinates. The cached product AT makes the displayed
equation quadratic. Every external Lucas value is unchanged. This change reduces terms, not the
number of witnesses.
300For reference the successive total-variable counts of the selected construction are
394, 392, 390, 372, 369, 366, 326, 319, 318, 315, 314, 313, 307, 301, 299.
The early exact eliminations, aﬃne H chart and squared-H replacement are justiﬁed above. The
count326 replaces separate pole blocks by the three groups; the ﬁnal group argument proves their
required arithmetic properties directly. The count315 precedes common scaling. Common scaling
removes twelve local coordinates, adds nine shared coordinates and two scaled test coordinates,
giving314. The coupled quaternion anchor replaces those nine by eight, giving313. Reciprocal A, T
and Z root charts give307, alternate-ordinate elimination gives301, and the oriented scalar auxiliary
chart gives299. Intermediate alternatives with diﬀerent depth requirements are not needed for this
selected proof.
The literal ﬁnal system has279 quadratic component equations in299 active rational variables,
including the input. Its polynomial is their unweighted sum of squares. Over Q it vanishes exactly
when every component equation vanishes. Exact expansion, collection by exponent vector and
removal of zero coeﬃcients give6,630 monomials, total degree four, content one, and maximum
absolute coeﬃcient 186624C2 = 186624· 3226. Every coordinate is active. The coeﬃcient artifacts
specify the exponent-vector order and the integer coeﬃcient representation, so these ﬁnite assertions
refer to a single polynomial, not just to its zero set.
M.11 The ordinary coeﬃcient presentation
The large ﬁxed coeﬃcient can be represented with fourteen constant coordinates. Put
(a0,...,a 13) = (2049, 2048,..., 2048/bracehtipupleft /bracehtipdownright/bracehtipdownleft /bracehtipupright
11 entries
, 0, 0)
and impose c0 = 3a0 andcj = 3ajc2j−1 for 1≤j≤ 13. If cj = 3ej thene0 = 2049 andej = 2ej−1 +aj.
After the eleven2048 steps e11 = 2 23, and the two last squares give e13 = 2 25. Thus c13 =C. Each
coordinate is uniquely determined; no square-root sign is introduced.
Add one more coordinate
Q = 48v283 + 48v287 + 432v289.
Only two rows of the symbolic system contain C. Replace them by
v255 + 6v256− 2v257− 4c13v57− 3v2903 = 0,
v903− 2v543v544−c13Q = 0.
Both are quadratic. Substitution of the unique constants and Q gives the exact symbolic rows.
Projection and unique extension therefore give inverse maps on full rational solution sets and ﬁx
the input. There are 299 + 14 + 1 = 314 variables and 279 + 14 + 1 = 294 quadratic equations.
Apply the same primitive integer normalization and unweighted sum of squares. The ordinary
polynomial has 6,672 nonzero monomials and content one. Let K0 denote the constant coeﬃcient
of the symbolic sum of squares, an explicitly tabulated positive integer of1,514 bits. The exact
largest absolute coeﬃcient of the ordinary expansion is
34098 +K0, 26495≤ 34098 +K0< 26496.
This proves the asserted ordinary coeﬃcient bound. The source-coordinate table retains the original
coordinate identiﬁers and has222,890 bytes. A compact table renames the active variables consecu-
tively by an explicit bijection ﬁxing the input; its byte count is a separate artifact property. That
renaming changes neither the equations nor the coeﬃcient statistics. The proof and the coeﬃcient
tables above establish Theorem M.1.

308AI usage statement
The Version 9 odd-product and asymmetric-depth improvements were developed by Gabriel Conant
with ChatGPT and incorporated with additional source, convention, and ﬁnite-certiﬁcate checks.
This manuscript was developed primarily by OpenAI Codex agents (Astra 6 and Sol 6.1) and
Anthropic Claude agents (Opus 5.5), with auditing from Fable 5.1. Their contributions included
mathematical exploration, construction and analysis of the polynomial, proof drafting, literature
searches, computational checks, internal review, and drafting and revision of the exposition. Re-
views by multiple AI agents formed part of this process; they do not constitute independent human
peer review.
